BY THE COMMISSION: For nearly 50 years, the Commission has engaged in the
integrated resource planning (IRP) process in accordance with N.C. Gen.
Stat. § 62-110.1(c) (IRP Statute). Consistent with the IRP Statute, that process generally
involves estimating future electric load, determining the appropriate level of generating
reserve margin for planning purposes, and identifying the mix of generation and
demand-side resources needed to meet the projected needs of the electric system. The
process results in a plan for each electric public utility subject to the Commission’s
jurisdiction that addresses the long-term needs for the generation of electricity in
North Carolina.
The General Assembly declared that energy planning and ratemaking should
assure adequate, reliable utility service and result in the least-cost mix of generation and
demand-reduction measures that is achievable, including appropriate consideration of
conservation, load management, and efficiency programs. Enacted in 2021,
N.C.G.S. § 62-110.9 (Carbon Plan Statute) added carbon-planning targets to the
Commission’s long-term planning exercise with Duke Energy Carolinas, LLC (DEC), and
Duke Energy Progress, LLC (DEP, and together with DEC, Duke).
In the Order Adopting Initial Carbon Plan and Providing Direction for Future
Planning issued on December 30, 2022, in Docket No. E-100, Sub 179 (Initial Carbon
Plan Order), the Commission recognized the overlap between the planning and execution
components of the Carbon Plan Statute and the planning requirements of the IRP Statute,
consolidated the Carbon Plan and IRP processes for Duke, and directed Duke to file its
first proposed biennial consolidated Carbon Plan and Integrated Resource Plan (CPIRP).
Commission Rule R8-60A now governs the biennial CPIRP process, including the
development of resource portfolios and a Near-Term Action Plan (NTAP).
The General Assembly has since modified the Carbon Plan Statute through
Session Law 2025-78 (S.L. 2025-78). As modified, the Carbon Plan Statute now directs
Duke to model the least-cost path to target carbon neutrality by 2050 while maintaining
or improving upon the adequacy and reliability of the existing grid.
This proceeding has been conducted in accordance with the Carbon Plan Statute,
the IRP Statute, N.C.G.S. § 62-2(3a), Commission Rule R8-60A, and the Commission’s
orders in this docket. The findings of fact, supporting evidence, resulting conclusions, and
directives presented in this Order (2026 CPIRP Order) represent the Commission’s
2025 biennial CPIRP. The Commission has developed this CPIRP based upon
competent, material, and substantial evidence derived from careful and thorough
consideration of the entire record, including the sworn testimony of public and expert
witnesses and public comment. Determining the most reasonable, least-cost resource
plan that maintains or improves the adequacy and reliability of the existing grid while
taking all reasonable steps toward carbon neutrality by 2050 remains a complex and
iterative process. In developing this CPIRP, the Commission has endeavored to identify
an NTAP first and foremost focused on customer affordability and reliability while also
considering executability and the flexibility to check and adjust as conditions change.

6

The CPIRP encompasses several distinct but related planning timeframes and
components. The Base Planning Period is the 15-year period beginning the year following
the filing of the CPIRP and serves as the principal planning period for evaluating
forecasted load, resource needs, planned additions and retirements, and system
reliability. See, generally, Commission Rule R8-60A. Beyond the Base Planning Period,
the Carbon Neutrality Planning Horizon (together with the Base Planning Period referred
to herein simply as the “planning horizon”) provides a longer-term planning framework
designed to ensure that Duke remains on a least-cost path toward achieving the carbon
reduction requirement of the Carbon Plan Statute. Recognizing the greater uncertainty
associated with this longer-term horizon, the Commission’s rules permit the use of
simplifying assumptions and analytical approaches. Within this broader planning
framework, the NTAP identifies the specific planning and development activities that Duke
proposes to undertake before Commission approval of the next CPIRP. Thus, while the
CPIRP evaluates resource needs and potential portfolios over a multi-decade planning
horizon, the NTAP identifies the nearer-term actions proposed for execution while
preserving the Commission’s ability to reassess longer-term resource decisions in
subsequent biennial proceedings.
PROCEDURAL HISTORY
On February 12, 2025, the Commission opened this proceeding to consider Duke’s
2025 Biennial Carbon Plan and Integrated Resource Plan (2025 CPIRP or 2025 Carolinas
Resource Plan). On July 14, 2025, Duke filed a motion for waiver of Commission Rule
R8-60A(e)(1) and a 30-day extension of time to file the 2025 CPIRP. On July 22, 2025,
the Commission granted Duke’s motion and directed Duke to file the 2025 CPIRP no later
than October 1, 2025.
Petitions to Intervene were filed on various dates by the following parties and were
granted by orders of the Commission: (1) Avangrid Power, LLC (Avangrid); (2) the
Carolinas Clean Energy Business Association (CCEBA); (3) the Carolina Industrial Group
for Fair Utility Rates II and the Carolina Industrial Group for Fair Utility Rates III
(collectively, CIGFUR); (4) Carolina Utility Customers Association, Inc. (CUCA); (5) the
City of Asheville, North Carolina (Asheville) and Buncombe County, North Carolina
(Buncombe); (6) the City of Charlotte, North Carolina (Charlotte); (7) Corporate Energy
Buyers Association (CEBA); (8) Environmental Defense Fund (EDF); (9) Google LLC
(Google); (10) Microsoft Corporation (Microsoft); (11) the North Carolina Attorney
General’s Office (AGO); (12) the North Carolina Electric Membership Corporation
(NCEMC); (13) the North Carolina Sustainable Energy Association (NCSEA); (14) NC
WARN; and (15) the Southern Alliance for Clean Energy (SACE), the Sierra Club, and
Vote Solar (collectively, SACE et al.).
On August 6, 2025, the Commission issued an order providing superseding
direction to Duke regarding pursuit of the interim target. In accordance with the Carbon
Plan Statute, as modified by S.L. 2025-78, the Commission directed Duke to cease
modeling scenarios designed to achieve the interim target by the earliest possible date
but to continue modeling scenarios to achieve the least-cost path to carbon neutrality by
2050 while maintaining or improving upon the adequacy and reliability of the existing grid.

JURISDICTION AND STANDARD OF REVIEW
The Commission has jurisdiction over Duke’s CPIRP petition pursuant to the
Carbon Plan Statute, the IRP Statute, and N.C.G.S.§ 62-2(3a).
When fulfilling its resource-planning duties, the Commission acts, in part, in a
legislative capacity. In State ex rel. Utilities Commission v. North Carolina Electric
Membership Corporation, the North Carolina Court of Appeals described least-cost
planning proceedings as resembling a legislative hearing in which facts and opinions are
gathered so that informed decisions may be made later. 105 N.C. App. 136, 144, 412
S.E.2d 166, 170 (1992).
At the same time, for purposes of conducting hearings, making decisions, issuing
orders, and conducting formal investigations where a record is made of sworn testimony,
the Commission exercises functions judicial in nature under N.C.G.S. § 62-60.
When acting in a judicial capacity, the Commission applies the rules of evidence
insofar as practicable and may exclude incompetent, irrelevant, immaterial, unduly
repetitious, or cumulative evidence. N.C.G.S. § 62-65(a). Evidence, including records and
documents in the Commission’s possession that it desires to use, must be made part of
the record by definite reference at the hearing. Id.
In developing the record, the Commission may admit evidence offered by the
parties and may take judicial notice of its decisions, published reports of federal regulatory
agencies, the decisions of state and federal courts, state and federal statutes, public
information and data published by official state and federal agencies and reputable
financial reporting services, generally recognized technical and scientific facts within the
Commission’s specialized knowledge, and such other facts and evidence as may be
judicially noticed by justices and judges of the General Court of Justice.
N.C.G.S. § 62-65(b).
The Commission determines the weight to give the evidence of record and the
sources of which it takes judicial notice. Ultimately, the Commission must base its
decision upon competent, material, and substantial evidence derived from consideration
of the entire record, consistent with N.C.G.S. § 62-65(a).
The Commission notes that participation in this proceeding has been
robust — from formal parties that participated in the twelve-day evidentiary hearing,
9

public witnesses who testified at the five public witness hearings held across the State
and virtually, and members of the public who filed statements of position in the docket. In
summarizing the evidence in this Order, the Commission did not attempt to restate every
detail but instead focused on the evidence it found most persuasive. A lack of direct
reference to a certain recommendation or concern should not be taken as an indication
that the Commission failed to consider that perspective. In developing this 2026 CPIRP
Order the Commission has attempted to give careful and due consideration to the entire
record.
Determining the reasonable steps along a least-cost path to carbon neutrality by
2050 while maintaining or improving the adequacy and reliability of the existing grid
remains a complex and iterative process. Consistent with the prior CPIRP orders and the
current statutory framework, the Commission balances near-term action with long-term
flexibility, customer affordability, reliability, executability, and the need to check and adjust
as conditions change.
STAKEHOLDER INPUT AND PUBLIC PARTICIPATION
The Carbon Plan Statute requires the Commission to receive and consider
stakeholder input when developing the Carbon Plan, and the IRP Statute provides for
public witness hearings in biennial integrated resource plan proceedings.
Duke’s prefiling stakeholder engagement process for the 2025 CPIRP began in
early 2025 and included four public engagement sessions and four technical engagement
sessions over a four-month period, drawing more than 350 individuals representing more
than 180 organizations. 2025 CPIRP App. A at 1.
The stakeholder meetings addressed the resource planning process, changing
market and policy conditions, load forecasting, demand-side modeling assumptions, grid
edge and customer programs, supply-side modeling assumptions, solar and wind
resources, nuclear development, transmission planning, fuel price forecasting, coal
retirement modeling, effective load carrying capability (ELCC), Inflation Reduction Act
assumptions, Long-Duration Energy Storage (LDES), the analytical framework, and
potential sensitivities for planning. Id. at 5–9.
Stakeholders could participate by attending public meetings, offering written
comments, asking questions during meetings, submitting written questions, or sharing
information with other participants. Participants submitted more than 230 questions
through the meeting Q&A process, and stakeholder feedback informed the development
of modeling assumptions, portfolio sensitivities, and other planning considerations
reflected in the 2025 Carolinas Resource Plan. Id. at 5.

In addition to the testimony received at public witness hearings, more than
600 statements of position were filed in Docket No. E-100, Sub 207CS. Notably, the North
Carolina Conservation Network filed a consumer statement of position as a petition
supported by 1,327 North Carolina residents. The full text of the petition is as follows:
We, the undersigned, urge the NC Utilities Commission to reject Duke
Energy’s Carbon Plan proposal and instead direct the utility to implement a
plan which maximizes clean energy generation to keep energy costs
reliable and affordable while meeting the state’s 2050 carbon neutrality
goal. The Utilities Commission should approve a Carbon Plan that
prioritizes reliable, affordable clean energy generation, instead of extending
the lifespans of dirty coal plants, and investing in costly methane gas
infrastructure.
As in all proceedings, the Commission prioritizes consideration of impacts to
customers in determining the appropriate action to take. In this proceeding, public
witnesses and those who filed consumer statements of position repeatedly addressed
affordability, reliability, energy burden, customer bill transparency, and the allocation of
costs associated with new generation, fuel, large-load additions, and data-center growth.
Several public witnesses expressed concern that new fossil-fuel generation,
natural gas price volatility, and construction work in progress could increase customer
costs, particularly for residential, low-income, rural, and energy-burdened customers.
Witnesses also raised concerns that forecasted data-center and other large-load growth
could cause costs to be socialized across residential bills if appropriate protections are
not adopted.
Other public witnesses urged expanded reliance on solar, storage, virtual power
plants, distributed energy resources, and demand-side management (DSM) and EE
programs as ways to reduce bills, mitigate fuel-price volatility, improve resilience, and
meet growing demand in a lower-cost manner.
The public testimony also reflected concerns about health, environmental justice,
storm impacts, and the long-term consequences of infrastructure choices, including
testimony connecting fossil-fuel infrastructure, air pollution, climate impacts, and the
experience of recent extreme-weather events in North Carolina.
At the same time, some witnesses acknowledged customer benefits associated
with existing programs and utility service, including praise for the PowerPair program,

13

statements that customer-sited solar and storage reduced individual bills and contributed
to grid support, and appreciation for Duke’s restoration efforts following Hurricane Helene.
The Commission has considered these customer-impact comments as part of the
entire record and, in evaluating the 2025 CPIRP, will take those steps necessary to
maintain reliable service through a least-cost, reasonable, and executable resource plan,
while also closely monitoring bill impacts to ensure continued affordable rates.
MAINTAINING A LEAST-COST, RELIABLE SYSTEM WHILE MEETING GROWTH
Pursuant to § 4 of the Carbon Plan Statute, the Commission is required to develop
the most-reasonable, least-cost resource plan that ensures reliability is maintained or
improved while taking all reasonable steps to meet the carbon dioxide emissions
reduction target set forth in the Carbon Plan Statute. The 2025 CPIRP proceeding is the
first CPIRP proceeding following enactment of S.L. 2025-78, also referred to as Senate
Bill 266 (S.B. 266), the Power Bill Reduction Act which, among other things, eliminated
the interim target of a 70% reduction in emissions of carbon dioxide from electric
generating facilities owned or operated by electric public utilities by 2030.
The Commission is considering the 2025 CPIRP during a time of unprecedented
economic development growth, combined with strong residential customer growth and
increasing electrification in the Carolinas. In its initial filing in October 2025, Duke
forecasted that energy needs in the Carolinas would grow eightfold over the next fifteen
years. At the time of filing its rebuttal testimony in this proceeding, Duke witness Kendal
Bowman reported that the pace of economic development has accelerated and that Duke
now projects that its load forecast will exceed the High Load scenario beginning in 2032.
Nevertheless, witness Bowman asserted that “[Duke] remain[s] confident that this load
growth — including the data center load growth — can be served in a manner that
benefits all customers and results in a stronger and more resilient system over time.”
Tr. vol. 22, 419. Other Duke witnesses confirmed that Duke has in place a detailed
process to ensure that Duke is able to both fulfill its obligation to provide electric service
that facilitates the economic development objectives of the State and local communities
while also ensuring that the commitments made to serve do not impair reliability or impose
risk on other customers. Tr. vol. 20, 539–40; tr. vol. 21, 89–90; tr. vol. 25, 27. The Public
Staff, on the other hand, “sees an emergent and growing threat to the system presented
by the continued addition of new load[,]” tr. vol. 13, 13, while at the same time
acknowledging that the expected increase in large, high load factor customers “may
provide ratepayer benefits, as those same system costs would be spread over greater
energy sales.” Tr. vol. 16, 23. Within this dynamic landscape, the Commission must set
a path for the future.
As discussed in greater detail in this Order, the modeling performed by Duke is
intended to identify the least-cost mix of generating resources that will maintain reliability
and achieve other policy objectives, including taking all reasonable steps towards carbon
neutrality by 2050. More specifically, to achieve this outcome, the model identifies the
economically optimal mix of resources by minimizing the revenue requirement for the
specific model run. By modeling different combinations of generating and other
14

supply-side resources, implementation timelines, and assumptions through this
“least-cost” lens, Duke, the Public Staff, and other parties that conducted modeling have
provided the Commission with an understanding of the difference in the financial and
reliability implications of the different portfolios, each of which is designed to be the lowest
cost under its unique set of assumptions. At the time of issuing this Order, Duke and the
Public Staff are fully aligned on the near-term actions appropriate to serve Duke’s existing
and future customers with both parties supporting all of the resources outlined in Duke’s
NTAP (Duke Recommended NTAP). The Commission also notes that determining an
overall least-cost portfolio is the only appropriate way to address a problem of this scale;
a simplistic comparison of one resource against another does not capture the operational
realities of operating a power system with a diverse mix of resources, each with its own
operational characteristics and challenges. The Commission emphasizes this dynamic,
as highlighted by Duke witnesses Glen A. Snider, Michael Quinto, and Thomas Beatty
(IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel) in their supplemental direct testimony:
Q. Why is it necessary to determine an overall optimal least-cost portfolio
of resources rather than simply comparing one resource against
another?
A. Planning for a future system that will provide reliable energy 24/7,
365 days of the year is a complex endeavor and must account for a wide
range of real-world considerations, including the fact that system
conditions and needs and generation resource performance change
over the course of every hour, day and season throughout the year.
These annual dynamics are in addition to changing system
requirements that occur from year to year over the planning horizon as
customer demand and energy requirements grow and older resources
retire.
Putting aside the risk-reducing benefits of resource diversity, no system
can be served by a single generation type, particularly given real-world
constraints. For instance, it would be unreasonable to assume an
unlimited amount of new nuclear would be available prior to 2040 given
the known and real-world constraints on siting, permitting, and
construction timelines.
Further, simply comparing the cost of one resource type to another in
isolation also fails to recognize that the value a resource might provide
to the system varies significantly depending on system conditions at any
point in time. A resource that provides little or no capacity and reliability
benefits could still be part of a least-cost portfolio if it is able to provide
sufficient low-cost emission-free energy that reduces the need for
higher-cost energy. Therefore, while such a resource might not provide
as much reliability as another resource, it nevertheless can be one
component within an overall least-cost plan.

15

The true (and much more complex) question is what set of
resources collectively results in the most robust, lowest-cost,
reliable system taking into account a wide range of real-world
considerations
including
projected
resource
availability
limitations, changing planning parameters such as changing load
requirements, technology costs and fuel prices as well as evolving
energy policy.
In summary, a simple technology cost comparison cannot replace the
extensive scope and breadth of an IRP proceeding that
comprehensively addresses these complex issues in order to determine
the “least-cost” portfolio that ensures long-term system reliability, while
also accounting for numerous technical, economic and policy issues that
drive optimal resource selection within the integrated planning
framework.
Tr. vol. 24, 149–50 (emphasis added). The Commission concurs.
In this proceeding, the Commission has relied on two metrics to evaluate the
customer financial implications of different portfolios. The first metric, the present value
of revenue requirement (PVRR) is calculated by assessing future capital costs including
costs for new generation resources, transmission system upgrades, and DSM/EE
program costs. The PVRR metric also captures fixed and variable production costs, which
are the costs to operate the system, including fuel. 2025 CPIRP App. C at 79, 87. As the
PVRR metric is a present value calculation, costs that are incurred farther out in time are
more highly discounted and have a smaller impact.
IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel Figure 17 shows the PVRR across the range of
Sensitivity Analysis Portfolios developed under the Advancing Development Load
Forecast, including the Duke Recommended Portfolio.

16

As explained by Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel, Duke’s Recommended
Portfolio is near the middle of the range of PVRRs. While there are portfolios that have a
lower PVRR, they generally either take advantage of lower cost assumptions for
resources or lower fuel price assumptions in achieving a lower cost and generally do not
reflect a significant divergence in resource selection. Tr. vol. 24, 111–12, 125–27. The
Commission has relied on the PVRR metric as a benchmark to compare and better
understand the relative cost implications of the various resource portfolios. The PVRR
metric is an important tool for comparing costs across portfolios but reflects only costs
considered in resource planning, and therefore, the PVRR metric is not intended or useful
as an estimate of “all-in” cost.
The second metric which has guided the Commission’s understanding of the cost
implications is the residential bill impact analysis. Duke conducted a bill impact analysis
by converting the annual incremental revenue requirements into an estimated bill impact
for retail customers, which is then translated into monthly bill increases over a set period.
2025 CPIRP App. C at 87. The analysis calculates the average incremental retail impact
(total change in retail energy costs over all rate classes) and applies that average retail
impact to a typical residential customer bill using 1,000 kilowatt-hour (kWh) of energy per
month. Duke presents bill impact both in terms of dollars per month and compound annual
growth rate (CAGR). Id. The bill impact analysis attempts to quantify how much a
residential customer could expect to see their bill change over the planning horizon, as
impacted by the resource changes contemplated by the various portfolios. Id. The bill
impact analysis uses cost of service factor assumptions at the time of the calculation to
allocate total system revenue requirements to retail customers. Id. The bill impact analysis
for the Duke Recommended Portfolio in this proceeding projects an average annual
increase of 2.1% by 2035 and 2.4% by 2040. Id. at 102; tr. vol. 12, 171.
IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel Figure 18 shows the compound annual
customer bill impacts across a range of Sensitivity Analysis Portfolios developed under
the Advancing Development Load Forecast, including the Duke Recommended Portfolio.

17

Like the PVRR analysis, the Recommended Portfolio is near the middle of the
range of customer bill impacts. While customer bill impacts for the Recommended
Portfolio are slightly higher in 2035 compared to the Preliminary Base Portfolio, by
2040 the adjustments to the portfolio result in a lower customer bill impact CAGR due to
the risk mitigating actions earlier in the portfolio. PVRRs of portfolios with different load
forecasts are generally not appropriately comparable since the portfolios require different
amounts of resources and resource utilization to serve the system.
While the Commission acknowledges the broader benefits of economic
development success for the State, the Commission is also closely assessing the effect
of large-load growth on customer bills and affordability and will take all steps necessary
to ensure that all large load customers pay for their costs and bring benefits to the system,
ensuring that cost to serve such customers are not borne by other customers. The
Public Staff noted the risk to customer affordability if a large load customer ultimately does
not materialize or is delayed in interconnecting to the electrical system, a risk that, if not
mitigated, could shift costs onto other customers. Duke's Electric Service Agreements
(ESAs) require large load customers to make performance and financial commitments
intended to protect customers from that risk.
The Commission has considered the evidence presented by Duke, together with
the concerns raised by the Public Staff and intervenors, in evaluating the extent to which
large load growth may mitigate or exacerbate the bill impacts borne by residential and
other customer classes. The Commission remains focused on protecting existing
customers from adverse impacts of large load customers and will take such actions in
other regulatory proceedings that are needed to ensure that such large load customers
provide system benefits and do not shift costs to other customers.
In addition to cost considerations, the Commission is focused on ensuring that
Duke can maintain or improve the reliability of the system during this period of rapid load
growth. The Commission recognizes that Duke must invest in its system to ensure reliable
service, particularly as load grows in North Carolina and South Carolina. Duke integrates
reliability considerations into the CPIRP planning process by incorporating realistic and
executable technology assumptions; incorporating reasonable resource adequacy inputs,
including the 22% planning reserve margin target approved by the Commission, and
ELCC assumptions; performing reliability verification modeling to ensure achievement of
the 0.165 loss-of-load-expectation (LOLE) reliability threshold; and risk-adjusting the
Recommended Portfolio. 1
As discussed later in this Order, to ensure that Duke — and
North Carolina — continue this trajectory of rates that are at or below the national average
and to ensure the continued reliability of the system, the Commission maintains its primary
focus on near-term actions, recognizing the need for aggressive but balanced actions
while monitoring conditions and circumstances that emerge over time. This approach
ensures that Duke is positioned to take immediate action necessary to ensure sufficient
For more detail on how Duke established the LOLE reliability metric for this CPIRP, see
2025 CPIRP App. C at 70–77.
1

18

generating resources are available to serve load reliably, and the Commission is able to
monitor Duke’s progress, as well as other conditions such as forecasted load, and adjust
as necessary.
The Commission continues to closely assess the forecasted load growth to identify
those resources needed to maintain and improve reliability and also to ensure that such
large load customers will pay for the costs required to serve them.
IMPLEMENTATION OF THE CPIRP ORDER
The Commission notes that the CPIRP is a biennial process conducted in an
inherently dynamic environment. As such, while the CPIRP provides a longer-term
outlook on generation planning paired with the most pragmatic near-term actions,
implementation of the specific mechanics of the Commission’s CPIRP orders necessarily
requires a certain degree of flexibility for Duke. It is not the Commission’s intention, nor
should it be, to micromanage the day-to-day operations of the utility. However, the
Commission’s responsibility under the regulatory compact is to ensure that the utility’s
actions remain aligned with the public interest. When there are significant deviations from
the CPIRP order, it is reasonable to expect Duke to provide notice and request a waiver
of the applicable provisions of the Commission’s order. 2
One such significant deviation, however, of which the Commission was not given
any notice or request for waiver, was Duke’s unilateral decision to withdraw Bad Creek II
from the 2022 Definitive Interconnection System Impact Study (DISIS). The evidence
discussed below demonstrates that the decision itself had a reasonable basis. The
Commission emphasizes, however, that Duke’s decision to withdraw Bad Creek II only a
few months after the Commission approved near-term development actions, including
authorizing Duke to incur up to $165 million in project development costs through 2026,
was a clear violation of the 2024 CPIRP Order. Despite being given multiple opportunities
during the hearing, Duke could not provide any explanation that established a rational
basis for failing to inform the Commission of a decision of this magnitude concerning a
resource that had been affirmed in two successive planning cycles.
Going forward, the Commission expects Duke to provide notice and seek a waiver
from the Commission if it wishes to significantly deviate from the Commission’s approved
CPIRP planning actions. The Commission does not see the need at this time to draw a
bright line delineating what constitutes a significant deviation from a CPIRP order. It is
plainly evident, however, that pulling a resource of such magnitude, the need for which
has been affirmed in two successive planning cycles, from the interconnection queue,
falls well beyond that line.

2
For example, Duke requested such waiver on September 12, 2025, and the Commission granted
the waiver from a provision of the Commission’s 2024 CPIRP order three days later. See Order Granting
Motion to Hold Energy Infrastructure Reinvestment Program Evaluation Directive in Abeyance, Biennial
Consolidated Carbon Plan and Integrated Resource Plans of Duke Energy Carolinas, LLC, and Duke
Energy Progress, LLC, Pursuant to N.C.G.S. §§ 62-110.9 and 62-110.1(c), No. E-100, Sub 190
(N.C.U.C. Sep. 15, 2025).

19

IMPLEMENTATION OF S.B. 266
The Commission finds that the modeling conducted by Duke is compliant with
S.B. 266. In reaching this conclusion, the Commission places significant weight on the
unrebutted testimony of Duke witness Snider:
Q. Mr. Snider, has the impact of S.B. 266, including the removal of the
interim target, been fully considered in Duke's modelling of this CPIRP?
A. Yes, sir.
Q. To your knowledge, has any party stated that Duke's modelling violates
the requirements enacted in S.B. 266?
A. No, not to my knowledge.
Tr. vol. 25, 105. The Commission concurs with this unchallenged assessment.
Under N.C.G.S. § 62-110.1(e), the Commission must find that other resources
identified in § 1 of the Carbon Plan Statute would not establish a more cost-effective and
reliable generation system before issuing a certificate of public convenience and
necessity (CPCN) for the Proposed Facility. The Commission also finds that any resource
that has been identified in Duke’s NTAP and approved by the Commission as reasonable
for planning purposes satisfies that standard introduced in S.B. 266 by comparing it to all
the other selectable resources. In reaching this conclusion, the Commission again places
significant weight on the unrebutted testimony of witness Snider:
Q. Okay. I recognize you are not an attorney, so I am not asking for a legal
interpretation of S.B. 266. I also recognize this is not a CPCN
proceeding. With those caveats, SB 266 added a requirement that the
Commission can only grant a CPCN if the resources listed in North
Carolina General Statute § 62-110.9(1) wouldn't establish or maintain a
more cost effective and reliable generation system consistent with NC
General Statute § 62-110.9. Those listed resources are power
generation, transmission and distribution, grid modernization, storage,
energy efficiency measures, demand side management, and the latest
technological breakthroughs. Is it Duke's position that when a facility is
selected as part of the Near-Term Action Plan, that resource has already
been compared with all those listed resources within the modelling
framework?
A. Yes, sir.
Id. at 105–06. The Commission concurs with this unchallenged assessment. Consistent
with prior CPIRP Orders, the Commission will consider and give appropriate weight to the
planning approval of generation resources in future CPCN proceedings but will consider
that factor in addition to all other evidence required by law. To clarify how the Commission
will implement the CPCN provision of S.B. 266, however, absent material escalations
20

from assumed modeling costs, the Commission deems the inclusion of a resource in the
Commission-approved NTAP sufficient to satisfy the requirement to find that the other
resources identified in N.C.G.S. § 62-110.9 (1) would not establish or maintain a more
cost-effective and reliable generation system. This determination does not relieve Duke
of the obligation to satisfy all other applicable requirements for the issuance of a CPCN.
WHEREUPON the Commission makes the following
FINDINGS OF FACT
Load Forecasting and Planning for Economic Development
1.
The load forecast for the 2025 Carolinas Resource Plan follows the same
approach used in the 2023 CPIRP. It is developed in four steps: (1) a service area
economic forecast is obtained; (2) an energy forecast is prepared by estimating statistical
models based on these economic conditions; (3) certain ex post modifications that
account for the growth in large economic development sites, electric vehicles (EVs),
behind-the-meter (BTM) solar and Utility EE (UEE) programs must be considered; and
(4) summer and winter peak demand forecasts are developed using the modified energy
forecast.
2.
Consistent with the 2023 CPIRP cycle, the Carolinas are continuing to
experience a period of rapid load growth, driven by economic development growth as well
as population growth and electrification. The load forecast accounts for economic
development, as economic growth is part of the baseline projection included in the
economic forecast.
3.
To capture multiple potential future load forecast scenarios, Duke
developed four load forecast portfolios for the 2025 CPIRP: Low, Moderate Development,
Advancing Development, and High. Duke manually applied adjustments to the forecast
for economic development projects, considering their continuing significant increases in
Duke’s service areas. To avoid double counting, Duke adjusted the load forecast
including applying reduction factors to load associated with projects in earlier stages of
its economic development pipeline. Projects that have signed ESAs were counted at their
full expected load and ramp rates.
4.
The 2025 CPIRP relied on the Spring 2025 load forecast, which projected
notably higher energy and peak demand needs relative to prior load forecasts, largely
attributable to continued and sustained economic development in the Carolinas. From
June 2024 to the date of filing this 2025 CPIRP, the amount of planned large load
additions to the DEC and DEP systems that were in an advanced stage of development
(Advanced Development Projects) increased by approximately 50%, representing an
additional approximately 2,000 megawatts (MW) on a full load projection basis.
5.
On April 15, 2026, Duke filed its Semi-Annual Economic Development
Report on Large Load Additions (Spring 2026 Large Load Report) in Docket
No. E-100, Sub 208A, reporting approximately 2,000 MW of further growth in the amount
of Advanced Development Projects since filing the 2025 CPIRP.
21

6.
To assess the impact of this continued large load growth, Duke prepared
an update to the Advancing Development Load Forecast (Updated 2026 Advancing
Development Load Forecast) incorporating the most recent information from the Spring
2026 Large Load Report.
7.
The Updated 2026 Advancing Development Load Forecast is aligned with
the Advancing Development Load Forecast (prepared in Spring 2025) through 2029 and
then begins trending toward and eventually surpasses the High Load Forecast scenario
in 2032.
8.
The Public Staff and some intervenors expressed concern over how to most
appropriately capture economic development activity in the load forecast. Some also
expressed concern that Duke’s load forecast may overstate future load. The Public Staff
ultimately took the position that the Advancing Development Load Forecast represents a
reasonable baseline for planning purposes and made recommendations on how to
improve the load forecast.
Modeling
Overview
9.
Consistent with prior CPIRP cycles, Duke relied on capacity expansion and
production cost modeling using least-cost planning principles, along with a portfolio
reliability verification analysis, to develop a preliminary base portfolio (Preliminary Base
Portfolio) using baseline planning assumptions. Duke also developed 30 sensitivity
analysis portfolios and 12 informational portfolios examining a range of potential future
outcomes, and resulting cost and performance, with respect to uncertainty in various
model assumptions such as resource costs, fuel commodity prices, availability of
resources, and load forecast scenarios. Duke used the EnCompass platform for its
capacity expansion and production cost modeling and the Strategic Energy and Risk
Valuation Model (SERVM) platform for its reliability verification analysis.
10.
Duke developed a “risk-adjusted” NTAP (Recommended NTAP) based
primarily on the Preliminary Base Portfolio, with reasonable and appropriate risk
adjustments considering the totality of the modeling results across all the sensitivities
along with execution considerations.
11.
Duke used its Recommended NTAP to prepare the Duke Recommended
Portfolio, a model portfolio which is optimized beyond the NTAP horizon, to quantify the
reliability, cost, and operations of the portfolio, providing an additional analytical step to
assess Duke’s Recommended NTAP.
12.
The Public Staff perform capacity expansion and production cost modeling,
also using the EnCompass platform. Based on its investigation, the Public Staff modified
certain of Duke’s resources, modeling assumptions, load forecasts, and other inputs to
generate base portfolios and sensitivities.

22

13.
In developing its recommended portfolio (Public Staff Recommended
Portfolio), the Public Staff introduced a change to the Duke’s modeling by allowing Public
Staff’s model to select large load curtailment provisions agreed upon in certain ESAs as
a firm capacity resource to meet capacity planning requirements. Inclusion of the large
load curtailment provisions in the Public Staff’s modeling had the largest impact of any of
the Public Staff’s adjustments to Duke’s modeling assumptions.
14.
NCSEA, SACE et al., and EDF conducted independent modeling to
evaluate Duke’s proposed 2025 CPIRP. Each party made multiple changes to the
modeling inputs used by Duke, resulting in differences reflected in their respective
recommended portfolios and NTAPs.
Maintaining System Reliability
15.
Duke integrated reliability considerations into the CPIRP planning process
by: (1) incorporating realistic and executable resource assumptions based upon
operational experience and expertise; (2) incorporating reasonable resource adequacy
inputs, including the planning reserve margin target of 22% approved by the Commission
and resource-type specific ELCC assumptions; (3) performing reliability verification
modeling; and (4) risk-adjusting the recommended portfolio.
16.
The 2025 CPIRP continues to plan to achieve the 22% planning reserve
margin by 2031 as approved by the Commission in its Order Accepting Stipulation,
Granting Partial Waiver of Commission Rule R8-60A(d)(4), and Providing Further
Direction for Future Planning, Biennial Consolidated Carbon Plan and Integrated
Resource Plans of Duke Energy Carolinas, LLC, and Duke Energy Progress, LLC,
Pursuant to N.C.G.S. §§ 62-110.9 and 62-110.1(c), No. E-100, Sub 190 (Nov. 1, 2024)
(2024 CPIRP Order).
17.
The 2025 Solar & Storage ELCC Study, included as Attachment I to the
2025 CPIRP, establishes the capacity values of solar and BESS within the DEC and DEP
systems.
18.
Duke’s Recommended Portfolio passed the SERVM reliability verification
analysis under the Advancing Development Load Forecast as initially filed, achieving the
0.165 LOLE reliability threshold. The Recommended Portfolio failed to pass the SERVM
reliability verification analysis under the High Load Forecast.
19.
Duke did not conduct an updated SERVM modeling on the load forecast
presented in its rebuttal testimony.
20.
Duke completed SERVM reliability validation on the Public Staff Recommended
Portfolio and demonstrated that it failed the SERVM reliability verification step.
21.
The Public Staff expressed concern that the number of large load ESAs Duke
has executed could have a significant impact on system reliability and recommended that the
Commission adopt safeguards to ensure Duke maintains reliability while continuing to
support integration of large loads in a reasonable and controlled manner.
23

Retirement of Coal-Fired Generating Units
22.
Duke prepared an updated coal retirement analysis including the targeted
extension of operations for certain coal units beyond the retirement schedule approved in
the 2024 CPIRP Order. Duke represented these extensions as necessary to maintain
resource adequacy while enabling a disciplined and strategic transition to new generation
sources given the known and significant supply chain and operational risks associated
with the indefinite operation of aging coal units.
Planned Combination of DEC and DEP
23.
Duke performed modeling that treats DEC and DEP as two separate utilities
and legal entities with their own load, resources, and transmission limits.
24.

Duke also modeled sensitivities assuming a fully combined utility.

25.
Now that the business combination has been approved in all three
jurisdictions – North Carolina, South Carolina, and federal 3 – future IRPs will be
developed assuming a fully combined DEC and DEP.
26.
Based on the now-approved business combination between DEC and DEP,
Duke has reduced its requested NTAP by 200 MW of battery storage consistent with the
settlement reached in Docket Nos. E-2, Sub 1383 and E-7, Sub 1332 (Combination
Proceeding).
Grid Edge Resources
27.
Duke’s CPIRP modeling maintained a UEE savings assumption of 1% of
eligible load as an annual floor or load modifier.
28.
Over time, as the adoption of EVs grows, there may be material differences
for modeling purposes between UEE forecasts depending on whether the forecast
includes a projection of EV sales.
29.

Duke’s BTM solar forecasting method is based on outdated data and trends.

Near-Term Action Plan and Execution Activities
Overview
30.
Duke submitted a recommended NTAP, as required by Commission Rule
R8-60A(d)(8).
31.
Duke and the Public Staff are entirely aligned on both the type and volume
of each resource included in the Duke Recommended NTAP, as well as the target dates
for bringing each resource online.
32.
Other intervenors proposed modifications to the Duke Recommended
NTAP which primarily supported increased reliance on demand side programs and
solutions.
Near-Term Development and Procurement Activities for New Standalone Solar,
Solar Plus Storage, and Standalone Battery Energy Storage Systems
33.
The modeling performed by Duke and by the Public Staff economically
selected solar and BESS resources as part of the least-cost plan, and these resources
are consistent with a resource plan that reduces fuel cost volatility and mitigates price
and other risks for customers. Duke’s and the Public Staff’s modeling considered all costs
and impacts of solar resources, including through implementation of the Solar Integration
Service Charge (SISC).
34.
Both Duke and the Public Staff supported adjusting the near-term execution
pace for new controllable solar resources relative to prior plans and recommended
procuring a total of 1,540 MW through the 2026 and 2027 solar resource procurement
requests for proposals (RFPs). Duke recommended procuring an additional 770 MW
through the 2028 RFP. The Public Staff agreed that Duke should pursue an incremental
amount of solar through the 2028 RFP, but recommended Duke set a 2028 RFP target
upon filing its pre-issuance proposal. The total procurement target is reduced compared
to the target amount approved by the Commission in the 2024 CPIRP Order due to the
reduced near-term need for solar resulting from changing market and policy conditions
including the enactment of S.L. 2025-78, expiring federal energy tax credits, and tariffs
impacting the cost effectiveness of these resources.
35.
Duke recommended adding 2,900 MW of BESS resources in its
Recommended Portfolio, incremental to the 2,700 MW previously approved by the
Commission, resulting in in a total of 5,600 MW of BESS to be online by 2034. This
amount is inclusive of both standalone and storage paired with solar resources. Following
the approval of the business combination, Duke reduced this request by 200 MW, for a
total incremental request of 2,700 MW. The Public Staff recommended the Commission
approve an incremental 1,200 MW of standalone BESS through 2032 and 400 MW of
paired BESS through 2033, stating that it is premature for Duke to request procurement
of BESS resources with expected in-service dates beyond 2032. These volume targets
are aligned with Duke’s proposed targets for the same time period.
25

Near-Term Development Activities for Onshore and Offshore Wind
36.
The comprehensive modeling performed by Duke did not robustly support
onshore or offshore wind resources as needed during the Base Planning Period.
37.
In the 2024 CPIRP Order, Duke was authorized to incur up to $65.6 million
for the development of three annual tranches of onshore wind through 2026 for the
purpose of putting 1,200 MW of onshore wind generation into service by 2033. Duke had
spent approximately $1.3 million of the authorized amount as of July 31, 2025.
38.
Duke’s recent onshore wind development efforts indicate that the viability
and scalability of this resource is more constrained than previously anticipated due to lack
of availability of suitable sites and other land acquisition challenges. Coupled with a
challenging policy environment, these constraints delay resource availability and increase
execution risk.
39.
The Public Staff recommended that Duke continue to advance development
activities for onshore wind resources given that the Public Staff’s model continues to
select minor to moderate amounts of the resource.
40.
Duke completed an Acquisition Request for Information (ARFI) to evaluate
offshore wind resources. The ARFI results, along with details provided by North Carolina’s
Kitty Hawk and Carolina Long Bay Wind Energy Area leaseholders, informed updates to
Duke’s generic offshore wind costs and availability assumptions. Offshore wind was not
economically selected in the modeling until after the Base Planning Period.
41.
Duke considered build-own-transfer and joint ownership proposals
submitted through the ARFI in the 2025 CPIRP modeling by performing a Low Wind Cost
sensitivity that encapsulated the lower cost of those proposals. The Low Wind Cost
sensitivity likewise did not economically select offshore wind during the Base Planning
Period.
42.
The Public Staff took the position that Duke’s proposed delay of offshore
wind resources and the removal of offshore wind from the Duke Recommended NTAP to
be reasonable for planning purposes at this time.
Near-Term Development Activities for New and Existing Natural Gas Resources
43.
The comprehensive modeling performed by Duke economically selected
new natural gas generation as part of the least-cost resource plan. Natural gas resources,
which are dispatchable, flexible, and capable of providing baseload, intermediate, and
peaking capacity, play an important role in planning and operating a reliable generation
fleet that can meet the future capacity and energy needs of the system. These resources
also play an important role in replacing the function of Duke’s long-serving coal-fired
generating units as they retire.
44.
Following Duke’s submittal of its Updated 2026 Advancing Development
Forecast in rebuttal, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel witnesses testified that
26

Duke’s Recommended NTAP is no longer a broadly risk-adjusted set of resources but is
now the floor of what is required to keep the lights on for customers.
45.
Duke and the Public Staff are aligned in support of the new natural gas
resources selected in Duke’s Recommended NTAP. Specifically, Duke and the Public
Staff recommended adding approximately 1,111 MW of additional combustion turbine
(CT) generating units to achieve commercial operation by 2032, including one new
255 MW F-class CT (CT5) 4 and two 856 MW advanced class CTs (CTs 6 and 7)
incremental to CTs 1–4 which were approved for planning purposes by the 2024 CPIRP
Order. In addition, Duke and the Public Staff recommended adding approximately
2,730 MW of combined cycle (CC) generating units (CCs 4 and 5) to achieve commercial
operation by 2033. The Public Staff initially opposed approval of CT5. However, the Public
Staff later updated its testimony in Docket No. E-2, Sub 1395, to support the development
of CT5.
46.
Duke sought recognition that relying on enhanced liquefied natural gas
(ELNG) is a reasonable fuel security planning assumption to ensure fuel security for
proposed CCs 4 and 5.
47.
The Public Staff and other intervenors expressed concerns with Duke’s
proposed fuel supply strategy for these new units, including concerns about Duke’s
planning assumption that ELNG would provide fuel security for CCs 4 and 5.
48.
The Public Staff recommended that the Commission require Duke to hire
an independent, third-party evaluator to assess ELNG and file a report in this docket prior
to any investment in ELNG as a fuel security strategy. Public Staff witnesses Blaise C.
Michna, Jordan A. Nader, and Evan Lawrence (Thermal Resources Panel) provided a
minimum scope of topics for this independent evaluation, in Appendix D of their prefiled
direct testimony.
49.
The Public Staff recommended that the Commission direct Duke to examine
potential natural gas firm transportation (FT) and deliverability constraints and assess its
requirements ranging from 1.5 to 2 billion cubic feet (BCF) or more for power generation
within the DEC and DEP service territories through approximately 2040, taking into
account current and future CC facilities and advanced turbine fuel supplies.
50.
The Public Staff recommended that Duke identify hydrogen market and
blending milestones, specify its sourcing and associated load or transportation impacts,
The filings in this proceeding contain inconsistent alphanumeric designations for the combined
cycle and combustion turbine natural gas-fueled electric generating facilities referenced in the 2025 CPIRP.
CT5 is sometimes referred to as the Smith CT while CTs 6 and 7 are sometimes referred to as CTs 5 and
6. Except where quoting testimony or reproducing tables from the 2025 CPRIP, the Commission uses the
naming conventions set forth in the Commission’s Order Establishing Procedure for Shorthand
Designations of Natural Gas-Fueled Generating Facilities, issued in this docket on June 9, 2026. Thus, in
this Order the Commission refers to the proposed combustion turbine facilities as CT5, CT6, and CT7 rather
than as Smith CT, CT5, and CT6.

and estimate the pipeline, storage, and equipment costs needed to achieve those
milestones for meeting the 2050 carbon neutrality requirement.
51.
Consistent with the Commission’s direction in past CPIRP orders, Duke has
continued execution of planned uprates at existing natural gas units to expand the
flexibility and efficiency of the existing natural gas fleet while increasing the capacity of
the existing fleet to meet near-term reliability needs.
Near-Term Activities for Bad Creek II
52.
In the first quarter of 2025, Duke made the decision to withdraw Bad Creek II
from the 2022 DISIS cluster study process to reduce network upgrade costs and accelerate
in-service dates for other, more crucial, near-term projects in the same DISIS cluster. Duke
continued to consider the long-term role of the project in its resource portfolio.
53.
Parties expressed concerns regarding Duke’s decision to remove Bad
Creek II from the 2022 DISIS and described follow-on consequences of such a decision
on the timeline for further development.
54.
Due to the extended development timeline and updated 2040 resource
availability, Bad Creek II was not economically selected by Duke’s modeling in the current
Base Planning Period. Nevertheless, Duke’s Recommended NTAP plans to: (1) progress
limited near-term actions that preserve optionality for 1,760 MW by 2040; (2) safe harbor
federal energy tax credits; and (3) allow Duke to further assess the project in future
planning cycles.
55.
Efforts to safe harbor federal energy tax credits in the coming years are
likely to provide significant customer savings if Bad Creek II is selected in a future
least-cost portfolio.
56.
The Public Staff recommended continued evaluation of Bad Creek II
in future CPIRPs but did not believe further development of Bad Creek II to be in the
public interest at this time due to minimal PVRR impacts, declining EnCompass modeling
usage, potential project delays, increasing costs, and the development of alternative
LDES options.
Near-Term Development Activities for New Nuclear
57.
The comprehensive modeling performed by Duke and the Public Staff
economically selected new advanced nuclear technologies, 5 such as small modular
reactors (SMRs) and large light water reactors (LLWRs), throughout the planning horizon
as part of the least-cost path to achieve the targeted reduction in carbon dioxide
5 As explained by the Duke Nuclear Panel, “[a]dvanced nuclear is the term used to encompass SMRs
(which are light-water cooled reactors), non-light-water cooled reactors (Non-LWRs), and a select group of
LLWRs that are considered Generation III+ advanced reactors (such as the Westinghouse AP1000®). The
terms advanced nuclear and new nuclear are used interchangeably throughout the [2025 CPIRP], since any
new nuclear will also be an advanced nuclear technology. Different advanced nuclear technologies, along with
examples of each, are provided in detail in Appendix J.” Tr. vol. 6, 158.

28

emissions required by the Carbon Plan Statute.
58.
Duke’s Recommended NTAP plans to develop 600 MW of SMRs or
1,117 MW of LLWRs by 2037 through a disciplined pursuit of licensing activities in the
near-term and a technology selection in 2027.
59.
Duke and other parties recommended that the Commission open a
dedicated new nuclear docket to address technology selection and update the
Commission on Duke’s near-term development activities.
60.
The Public Staff recommended that Duke proceed with its proposed
near-term actions to develop new advanced nuclear resources. The Public Staff further
recommended that the Commission approve Duke’s request to extend the prior
authorization to incur $440 million in development costs for new advanced nuclear
resources through 2028.
Near-Term Development Activities for Existing Nuclear
61.
The baseload capacity provided by Duke’s existing nuclear fleet will become
increasingly important to provide a foundation of reliable capacity and achieve long-term
reductions in carbon dioxide emissions.
62.
In addition to extending the operating licenses at each of Duke’s existing
nuclear sites as approved by the 2024 CPIRP Order, Duke continues to leverage its
existing infrastructure and optimize the use of extended power uprates, where cost
effective, to increase the total MW of clean baseload nuclear capacity.
Transmission
63.
Duke has complied with the directives of the 2024 CPIRP Order regarding
transmission planning as well as interconnection optionality and reliability considerations
while otherwise taking additional steps to enhance its transmission planning processes.
64.
Duke is on track to complete construction of the 15 Red Zone Expansion
Plan (RZEP) 1.0 transmission projects approved in the 2022 Carbon Plan Order and is
progressing construction for the RZEP 2.0 projects approved through the Carolinas
Transmission Planning Collaborative (CTPC) process.
65.
The first Multi-Value Strategic Transmission (MVST) Study, completed in
2025, identified five projects for inclusion in the 2025 Collaborative Transmission Plan,
two of which are in the “planned” stage, ready to proceed through the project management
process, and three of which are “conceptual” and will be monitored for addressing
transmission needs identified in subsequent MVST Studies.
66.
Duke has continued to evolve interconnection policies to remain in
compliance with federal interconnection regulations while adopting new timeline
improvement options.
29

67.
The Public Staff expressed support for Duke’s transmission planning
processes, including incorporation of the MVST planning process as providing for a more
robust, holistic study process, and Duke’s interconnection study process and timelines.
Customer Affordability
68.
The bill impacts of the Duke’s Recommended Portfolio are significantly
reduced as compared with the prior CPIRP. Adjusted for inflation, Duke calculated that
the average CAGR for the combined DEC/DEP system Recommended Portfolio is
reduced by 0.5% in 2035 and reduced by 0.1% in 2040.
69.
The Public Staff noted customer affordability risks if large load customers
ultimately do not materialize or are delayed in interconnecting to the electrical system.
70.
Duke’s ESAs require large load customers to make performance and
financial commitments intended to deter attrition and protect customers.
71.
In rebuttal testimony, Duke provided two analyses (Large Load Impact
Analysis and Bill Impacts) intended to show net potential benefits from connecting large
load customers to the system. While it is appropriate for the Commission to consider the
impacts of large load customers for the planning purposes of this docket, the more
detailed consideration of how to allocate the costs and benefits of connecting large load
customers to the system is more appropriate for the Commission to take up in other
proceedings.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS
As noted above, the Commission bases its findings of fact upon competent,
material, and substantial evidence derived through consideration of the entire record. In
providing the following evidence and conclusions in support of its findings of fact, the
Commission does not exhaustively summarize the entire record.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 1–8
Load Forecasting and Planning for Economic Development
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Chapter 1,
Chapter 2, and Appendix D of the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke
witness Bowman; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Load Forecast and Economic
Development Panel (Benjamin Passty, Phillip Stillman, and Andrew Tate); the testimony
and exhibits of Duke’s Bill Impacts and Large Load Benefits Panel (Laura Bateman and
Morgan Beveridge); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff’s Load Panel (Patrick
Fahey and Jeff Thomas); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff witnesses Blaise
Michna and Dustin Metz; the testimony and exhibits of AGO witness Edward Burgess;
the testimony and exhibits of EDF witness Robert Patrylak; the testimony and exhibits of
NC WARN witness William Powers; the testimony and exhibits of SACE et al. witness
Maria Roumpani; the initial comments of the City of Charlotte; the initial comments of the
City of Asheville and Buncombe County; and the entire record.
30

Duke Direct Testimony
Economic Development Growth
Chapter 1 of the 2025 CPIRP explains that the Carolinas are continuing to
experience a critical period of sustained and rapid load growth, driven by population
growth, electrification, and significant and sustained economic development. The
Carolinas continue to rank in the top 10 fastest growing states, and North Carolina was
again named America’s Top State for Business in 2025. In other words, as Duke witness
Bowman testified, “North Carolina is booming. People want to live here, work here.
Businesses want to come here[,]” and because it’s “an attractive place, . . . there’s a lot
of large load looking to come here.” Tr. vol. 6, 103, 107.
Appendix D to the 2025 CPIRP explains that the number and size of new
businesses seeking to locate in Duke’s service territories in North Carolina and
South Carolina continues to be dynamic and unprecedented. 2025 CPIRP App. D at 9.
Between June 2024 and the date of filing the 2025 CPIRP, the amount of planned large
load additions (i.e. exceeding 20 MW) to DEC’s and DEP’s Advanced Development
Projects increased by approximately 50%, representing an additional 2,000 MW on a full
load basis. Id.
Duke witness Tate explained that Duke’s Economic Development and Large
Account Management teams devote significant resources toward engaging and tracking
potential development projects in the DEC and DEP territories at various stages of site
exploration, negotiation, and buildout. Tr. vol. 8, 172. To ensure Duke is apprised of future
load growth, witness Tate explained, the Economic Development team engages with
prospective customers on an ongoing basis to refine their load ramp schedules to align
with both the prospective customers’ needs as well as Duke’s ability to serve. Id.
Developing the Spring 2025 Load Forecast
Appendix D to the 2025 CPIRP presents Duke’s 2025 Spring Load Forecast. To
develop the load forecast for the 2025 CPIRP, Duke followed the same methodological
approach as it used to prepare the 2023 Fall Load Forecast that informed the final
approved 2023 CPIRP. Tr. vol. 8, 160–61. First, Duke obtained economic forecasts for
the DEC and DEP service territories from Moody’s Analytics. Duke then prepared an initial
energy forecast by estimating statistical models based on the economic conditions
contained in the Moody’s forecast. Id. at 164. Next, Duke incorporated certain ex post
modifications that account for the growth in large economic development sites, EVs, BTM
solar and UEE programs. Id. at 165–66. Finally, Duke developed summer and winter peak
demand forecasts using the modified energy forecast. Id. at 163. The result allows
analysis of the impact of varying inputs on sales, including substitution of different
economic or weather conditions. Id. at 164; 2025 CPIRP App. D at 4–5.
As Duke’s Load Forecast and Economic Development Panel testified, the
economic development adjustment is the most significant adjustment to the load forecast
for the 2025 CPIRP. Tr. vol. 8, 166. Duke witness Passty noted that Duke began
31

calculating and incorporating an economic development adjustment in the 2023 CPIRP
and continued this practice in the 2025 CPIRP to ensure that rapid growth observed but
not otherwise captured by traditional modeling is integrated into Duke’s resource plan.
Id. at 175. The economic development adjustment considers the prospective load of
Advanced Development Projects — which includes the projects of potential customers
that have executed an ESA or letter agreement (LA) as well as projects that have
progressed to advanced discussions prior to signing an LA or ESA (Late-Stage Pipeline)
— and certain projects that have demonstrated significant siting interest with negotiations
underway that are earlier in the large load pipeline (Mid-Stage Pipeline). Id. at 175–77.
Before incorporating the projected incremental sales from these economic development
projects into the load forecast, Duke applied a range of discounts to attempt to address
project location uncertainty, project timing uncertainty, and the possibility of double
counting the growth expected in the base forecast. Id. at 180; 2025 CPIRP App. D at 10.
Ultimately, Duke developed four load forecast scenarios for the 2025 CPIRP to
capture multiple potential future load forecast scenarios: the Low, Moderate
Development, Advancing Development, and High scenarios. Id.; 2025 CPIRP App. D at
20. Witness Passty testified that these scenarios provide Duke with the ability to evaluate
potential resource needs and planning outcomes with varying levels of economic
development, EV integration, and BTM solar adoption over the planning horizon.
Tr. vol. 8, 160. The table below describes the characteristics of each load forecast
scenario:

Id. at 186; 2025 CPIRP App. D at 20.
With respect to economic development, these four load forecast scenarios
represent a spectrum of large load project certainty: the Low scenario includes only
contracts with signed ESAs and does not include any discount, or reduction factor.
Tr. vol. 8, 160. The High scenario includes all projects with a signed ESA or LA or that
are in the Late-Stage Pipeline, as well as all Developing Pipeline projects at a
50% reduction factor. Id. The Moderate Development and Advancing Development
32

scenarios are substantively similar, applying no reduction factor to projects with a signed
ESA, a 25 reduction factor to projects with a signed LA, and a 30% reduction factor to the
Late-Stage Pipeline. Id. at 181. The Moderate Development scenario adopts a similar
methodology to the economic development adjustment applied to the load forecast
developed for the 2023 CPIRP. Id. at 181. The Advancing Development scenario
recognizes that a portion of projects in the Developing Pipeline may become Advanced
Development Projects as negotiations mature and accordingly adds approximately 2,000
MW from the Developing Pipeline. Id. at 181–82. Duke witness Stillman explained that
Duke’s approach to developing its economic portfolio “involves placing an emphasis on
understanding the energy requirements of each individual project separately, categorizing
those projects based on their maturity, and applying a discount to the portfolio to capture
project location, project timing, and to mitigate the risk of double counting uncertainties
as the Carolinas’ economic development pipeline progresses.” Id. at 183–84.
Overall, Duke identified the Advancing Development scenario as the most
appropriate for planning purposes. Id. at 160. Witness Passty testified that the “Advancing
Development Load Forecast captures high-confidence Advanced Development projects
as well as additional future economic development pipeline growth by assuming the
additional materialization of approximately 2,000 MW by 2040, allowing the Companies
to attract and serve future economic development projects.” Id. at 186. He further stated
that the Advanced Development load forecast appropriately plans for future growth. In
contrast, should Duke rely on the Moderate Development Forecast, it would not plan
appropriately to develop the resources necessary to serve future economic development
project opportunities and support industry retention. Id. at 187. At the evidentiary hearing,
witness Stillman defended Duke’s use of the Advancing Development Load Forecast
scenario, stating “energy historically has not been a constraint in North Carolina’s
economic development value proposition. And we don’t want it to become a constraint on
that opportunity for the communities that we serve and the projects they choose to
pursue.” Tr. vol. 9, 23–24.
Duke witnesses Tate and Stillman also addressed questions about the attrition risk
for large load customers. Witness Stillman testified that the pipeline of large load
customers is “robust” and that, for every project that leaves the pipeline, there is another
one ready to join. Id. at 74–75, 83. He further explained that the discounting methodology
Duke applies to projects in the economic development portfolio also protects against the
risk of attrition. Id. at 83. Witness Tate added that Duke determined the appropriate
discount based on the observed progression of projects that were in the portfolio in 2023.
The overall discount applied to economic development projects in the Advancing
Development scenario was around 23% and in line with the observed historical reduction
in forecasted large load realized over the past year. Id. at 89–91. On cross examination,
witness Tate acknowledged that load growth in the Carolinas is dynamic and that this
word implies that load can either increase or decrease based on additions, delays, or
cancellations tied to economic changes and other factors. Id. at 67–68. Witness Stillman
testified that forecast trends demonstrated that a small number of customers were
becoming a substantial portion of expected system load. Id. at 73–74. He also
acknowledged that cancelations or changes in plans from just one or two large load
customers could impact the load forecast overall. Id.
33

Finally, Chairman Brawley asked witness Tate about the likelihood that projects
with executed ESAs would eventually take service and achieve their projected demand
ramps. In response, witness Tate stated that the contractual commitments in the ESA are
firm and “help encourage accuracy in load ramp planning processes.” Id. at 84. He
explained that before getting to the contracting phase, a large load customer must undergo
transmission infrastructure and generation resource adequacy reviews, which serve as
gating requirements that have a culling effect on the number of large load projects. Id. at
85. Once these studies are complete, large load projects progress to the LA stage where
they must make a cash payment to cover the estimated cost of interconnection. Id. at 85,
103. According to witness Tate, these costs range from $20 million to $80 million and test
the financial wherewithal of a project to make a commitment. Id. at 85. Thereafter, an ESA
must be executed within 90 days and the large load customer must contemporaneously
provide security that relates to the termination damages if a project failed to materialize.
Id. at 86. The ESA also requires a minimum billing demand of 75% of the contract demand.
Witness Tate explained that “[a]ll of those features together culminate in features that
press a customer to be accurate in their load planning projections, and to be willing to put
financial resources behind that commitment.” Id.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
Public Staff witnesses Thomas and Fahey emphasized the importance of load
forecasts and highlighted the increasing national focus on load forecasting due to
escalating load growth across the country. Tr. vol. 16, 13. Witness Fahey noted that both
over- and under-forecasts create risk, which can manifest in multiple forms, such as
stranded assets and inefficient asset use, system unreliability, limited economic
development, or higher costs. Id. at 17–18. Witness Thomas testified that Duke has
improved its load forecasts since the 2023 CPIRP proceeding. Id. at 13. However, he
testified that the Public Staff had “concerns in several areas” of Duke’s load forecasts,
including Duke’s forecasted load from large customers, and characterized the forecast
for large load customers’ as “uncertain and likely overstated.” Id.
Witness Fahey testified that Duke created a range of load forecasts titled High,
Advancing Development, Moderate Development, and Low, which he thought was
prudent because it accounted for uncertain load growth. Id. at 24. He opined that the load
forecast is highly uncertain, and that use of Duke’s Advancing Development forecast
better protects system reliability and allows for greater economic development, although
it potentially results in increased future rates. Given this uncertainty and the impact on
customers’ rates, witness Fahey recommended that the forecast should be reevaluated
frequently. Id. at 16, 67. Witness Fahey also supported Duke’s change in forecast
methodology from the 2023 CPIRP for commercial forecasting due to increasing load
from data centers. Id. at 26.
Throughout his testimony, witness Fahey pointed to numerous concerns with
Duke’s current forecast methodology and made several recommendations designed to
improve the load forecast. Id. at 71–72. Witness Fahey recommended that the
Commission: (1) require Duke to reevaluate its cost of electricity assumptions used as an
input to the econometric models, and to vary those assumptions to reflect the differences
34

in expected ratepayer costs across different sensitivities; (2) require Duke to develop an
alternate method for forecasting industrial class load, using a combination of historic
meter data and expectations of future customers in addition to its current method both to
provide an alternate forecast and to identify any double counting produced by the
combination of the econometric drivers and industrial large load additions; (3) require
Duke to provide an annual update to the load forecast outside of the CPIRP, to provide
load forecast updates for the base econometric and economic development data during
future CPIRPs, and to continue to provide forecast sensitivities; (4) direct Duke and the
Public Staff to work together to determine the scope of a 15-year financial plan; and
(5) further direct Duke to provide a 15-year financial plan with the filing of its next
proposed CPIRP. Id.
Witness Fahey stated that the economic development project loads included in
the Advancing Development Load Forecast may be overstated for a variety of reasons.
Id. at 33–34. Specifically, witnesses Fahey and Michna both noted that the economic
development forecast included in the Advancing Development scenario reflects more
economic development load than Duke reported in its semiannual Fall 2025 Large Load
Report filed in Docket No. E-100, Sub 208 (Large Load Proceeding) and adds an
increasing load factor that is not tied to specific projects. Tr. vol. 13, 52–54. Witness Fahey
testified that the reductions applied by Duke do not explicitly address delays. Tr. vol. 16,
34. He further testified that just one individual customer can materially change the
forecast, and large load customers often decrease their requested load, citing the net
decrease requested by the 35 large load customers presented in the 2023 Supplemental
Planning Analysis (SPA) filing. Id. at 48–49. Additionally, on cross-examination, witness
Fahey expressed concerns with Duke’s inclusion of approximately 2,000 MW of demand
in its base forecast because uncertainty exists regarding both the customers’ identity and
the customers’ ramp period. Id. at 157–58.
Duke currently uses an inflation index-based price inflator to reflect future
electricity pricing within its econometric load forecasts. Witness Fahey testified that the
rate of inflation of retail electricity has recently been greater than that of general inflation
used in Duke’s index, with much greater increases requested in their pending rate cases,
and that electric rate increases have increased national awareness on the issue of
electricity prices. Id. at 34–37. He opined that using a lower rate than reasonable to
estimate price increases used within the econometric forecasting may result in future
overestimation of electric load for the various customer classes. Id. Witness Fahey
additionally noted that separate retail rates are modeled for the two utilities in all
scenarios, which does not reflect the reality of a future merged utility. Id. at 38. He
contended that revising this cost of electricity input will help better understand the
implications of increasing electricity prices on the load forecast. Id. at 37.
Witness Fahey further expressed that Duke’s econometric model captures some
of the industrial growth that is attributable to large load customers, which likely results in
double counting them in both the econometric model and through the load forecast.
Id. at 50. He explained that Duke did not explicitly adjust for the risk of double counting
and instead assumed that the reduction factor applied to its large load customers
accounted for the double counting concern. Id. at 52. He recommended that the
35

Commission require Duke to develop an alternate method for forecasting industrial class
load, using a combination of historic meter data and expectations of future customers to
provide an alternate forecast and to identify any double counting produced by the
combination of the econometric drivers and industrial large load additions. Id. at 71.
Witness Fahey shared his concerns regarding the financial impact on Duke of
pursuing its NTAP. Id. at 68. He testified that the large amount of generation needed to
serve the forecasted increase in load will result in substantial additional costs to Duke in
the future as well as additional revenues. Id. at 68–69. Witness Fahey further noted that
while revenues from potential large load customers will offset some of these costs, but
that if load does not materialize as expected, future ratepayers could face significant rate
impacts. Id. While Duke is planning its generation over a more than ten-year horizon and
beyond, corporate planning does not match that horizon. Id. To address this timing and
planning mismatch, witness Fahey recommended that Duke work with the Public Staff to
determine the scope of a 15-year financial plan, and that the Commission further direct
Duke to provide a 15-year financial plan in its next CPIRP. Id. at 72.
Witness Fahey also presented the Public Staff’s alternative load analysis and
explained the process he used to generate it. The Public Staff used different large load
projections as well as an additional adjustment for industrial double counting to modify
Duke’s large load additions. Based on these methods, the Public Staff’s analysis was
slightly lower than Duke’s Moderate Development forecast but closely followed the same
trajectory. Id. at 65, fig. 10. Witness Fahey explained that the Public Staff’s load analysis
was used to provide a comparison to the range of Duke’s load forecasts but was not used
in the Public Staff’s Encompass modeling. Id. at 65. Public Staff witness Michna further
stated that the Advancing Development Load Forecast “does ultimately represent the
general trajectory of load in the Carolinas and provides a reasonable baseline for the
modeling of a rapidly expanding system.” Tr. vol. 13, 53. The Public Staff therefore used
the Advancing Development Load Forecast in the Public Staff’s Encompass modeling.
Id. at 57. At the evidentiary hearing, witness Fahey confirmed that the methodologies
Duke used in preparing the load forecast are generally consistent with industry practice,
