stating that “[t]he use of econometric drivers on what Duke does in their econometric
forecasting is fairly common across a number of utilities” as is the use of a 15-year
planning horizon. Tr. vol. 16, 217.
Duke currently provides the Commission with an economic development update
twice a year. The Public Staff recommended that the Commission require Duke to provide
an annual update to the load forecast outside of the CPIRP, to provide load forecast
updates for the base econometric and economic development data during future CPIRPs,
and to continue to provide forecast sensitivities. Id. at 71–72. Duke currently creates an
updated forecast twice a year for internal purposes as noted by witness Fahey. Id. at 39.
Witness Fahey explained at the expert witness hearing that there is a lot of uncertainty
within the load forecasts which is driven by a customer-specific adjustment to represent
a large portion of the forecast. Id. at 175. He further noted that with the changes in the
load forecast from large load customer additions, what happens in the next few years in
terms of large customer development and loads is critical to system planning and must
be monitored closely and updated regularly. Id. at 30. Witness Fahey argued that
36

continued review of load updates is critical in a CPIRP proceeding because changes in
load forecasts can have a material impact on the outcome of the CPIRP as well as any
pending or upcoming CPCN applications. Id. at 39. He further recommended that
because forecasting accuracy generally improves with more recent data, Duke should file
an update to its load forecast in future CPIRPs to demonstrate whether changes to the
plan are necessary, and should provide updated analyses demonstrating the need for
generation resources when it files applications for CPCNs. Id. at 42.
In addition, at the hearing, witness Michna acknowledged the dynamic nature of
the load forecast, noting that Duke had experienced “significant changes in
circumstance[s]” since March 2026, when the Public Staff filed their direct testimony.
Tr. vol. 13, 9. Witness Michna stated that the updated forecast presented with Duke’s
rebuttal testimony reflects the “rapid pace at which Duke has entered into ESAs with large
load customers since the filing of its [2025 CPIRP] application,” with the result that “its
load forecast has increased drastically.” Id. at 10. Public Staff witnesses Michna and Metz
further testified that “the Public Staff sees an emergent and growing threat to the system
presented by the continued addition of new load.” Id. at 13.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
Several intervenors questioned the load forecast and highlighted the uncertainties
of predicting future load growth and associated risks of potential overbuilding.
AGO witness Burgess stated that although most analysts anticipate a projected
period of load growth, perspectives on the magnitude of this growth vary widely within the
industry. Tr. vol. 22, 53. He further raised concerns about the adequacy of Duke’s four
load forecast portfolios and stated that he “do[es] not believe that the scenarios presented
accurately portray the full range of uncertainty associated with the LA portion of the
forecast[.]” Id. at 56. Witness Burgess stated that the most reasonable forecast may be
an alternative case that falls between the Moderate and Low forecasts. Id. at 57. He noted
that approximately 75% of Duke’s projected load increase was driven by large loads.
Id. at 52. Witness Burgess further expressed concern that Duke's load forecast "was
significantly higher than the firm demand Duke had contracted through ESAs[.]” Id. at 58.
He noted high project attrition rates and argued that, based on historical data, Duke’s
discounting factor for project attrition was too low. Witness Burgess testified that
“including large loads that are speculative or uncertain places significant risk on existing
Duke customers who must still bear the costs if too much of this projected load fails to
materialize or ramps up more slowly than initially forecasted.” Id. at 62–64. He
recommended the Commission evaluate the magnitude and timing of the new contract
additions represented in Duke’s load forecast. Id. at 53. Witness Burgess further
recommended that the economic development load forecast include only signed ESAs,
with loads reduced to reflect curtailment provisions. Id. at 65.
Similarly, SACE et al. witness Roumpani testified that Duke’s forecast
methodology is “overly optimistic” and argued that it does not apply an adequate reduction
factor to customers that have not entered binding agreements. Tr. vol. 20, 446. She stated
that Duke’s Advancing Development forecast, “does not adequately account for ordinary
37

attrition among customers who have expressed interest but have not made binding
financial commitments,” noting that Duke’s data shows load contractions within
successive pipeline updates, and the Mid-Stage Pipeline appears significant but includes
projects that, if delayed or discontinued, would result in a significantly smaller forecast.
Id. at 446–48. Witness Roumpani presented her analysis of the contraction experienced
within Duke’s pipeline, finding that a significant number of customers who appeared in
earlier filings as LOI-executed or Late-Stage Pipeline were later reclassified as
Discontinued or Delayed. She also found that the projected load for several
customers decreased, and others remained in the LOI/LA stage with reduced demand.
Id. at 451–52. Witness Roumpani testified that, “Duke’s recommended portfolio is sized,
sequenced, and priced against a specific load trajectory. If that trajectory is overstated,
then the generation additions Duke proposes may not be needed on the timeline or at the
scale presented.” Id. at 453–54. At the evidentiary hearing, she remarked that the Spring
2026 update includes the trend of attrition from projects with ESAs and LAs. Id. at 505.
Witness Roumpani noted that some projects have been discontinued, and she
recommended that ESAs should be discounted to some degree. Id. at 505–06. She added
that in every update from Duke, there is a delay in how large load materializes despite
overall load increases. Id. at 510.
EDF witness Patrylak emphasized the uncertainty of load forecasting, stating that
“projected load growth beyond the near term is uncertain in both timing and magnitude[,]”
which can lead to the “risk [of] overbuilding.” Tr. vol. 22, 143. NC WARN witness Powers
noted that Duke’s electric sales have been relatively flat over the past 10–20 years and
claimed the projected 22% load growth in four years is unrealistic. He argued that data
center growth is speculative, as some communities have introduced moratoriums,
developers may promote the same project in multiple jurisdictions, and data center
energy demand forecasts do not account for future processor EE gains. Id. at 190–97.
Witness Powers further argued that Duke’s past demand forecasts were inflated and
inaccurate, and recommended using historic demand growth rates. Id. at 198–200.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
Duke witnesses Bowman, Stillman, and Tate addressed the continuing economic
development in their respective rebuttal testimonies. Witness Bowman stated that the
pace of economic development has accelerated since the filing of Duke’s CPIRP in
October 2025, tr. vol. 22, 418, while witness Stillman testified that the economic
development growth Duke is experiencing in the Carolinas “is real, substantial, and
continuing to progress.” Tr. vol. 20, 536. Witness Tate stated that CNBC recently named
North Carolina the number one state for business for the third time in four years, Site
Selection magazine ranked North Carolina as the top state for workforce development,
and Business Facilities magazine named North Carolina the top state for economic
development. Id. at 538.
Witness Tate further explained that Duke’s semiannual Spring 2026 Large Load
Report, which was filed on April 15, 2026, in the Large Load Proceeding shows that the
portfolio of Advanced Development Projects increased by approximately 35%, or more
than 2,000 MW on a full load basis, since Duke filed its 2025 CPIRP. Id. at 540, 564. He
38

explained that in addition to growth in the size of the Advanced Development Project
portfolio, the Spring 2026 Large Load Report shows that a substantial number of large
load projects earlier in the pipeline progressed to execution of a LA and/or ESA. Id.
Since filing the Spring 2026 Large Load Report, Duke has executed additional ESAs
totaling more than 2,600 MW. Id. In terms of MW, ESAs make up more than 50% of the
Advanced Development Projects in the Spring 2026 Large Load report, a substantial
increase compared to less than 10% in Spring 2025. Id. at 537, 551. Duke implemented
a new large load queue process in Fall 2025 to bolster project readiness and viability.
Id. at 545–46, 568. The large load process requires execution of an ESA within 120 days
following execution of the LA. Id. at 543. Finally, since Duke filed the SPA to the
2023 CPIRP, four of the original Advanced Development Projects have progressed to
in-service, meaning that they are no longer considered Advanced Development Projects
and are now part of the base load forecast. Id. at 536–37, 540.
To assess the impact of this continued economic development growth on the load
forecast, witness Stillman testified that Duke prepared an updated version of the
Advancing Development Load Forecast (Updated 2026 Advancing Development Load
Forecast), incorporating the most recent economic development information from the
Spring 2026 Large Load Report as well as the progression of projects in the Developing
Pipeline. Id. at 553, 564. Witness Stillman explained that the Updated 2026 Advancing
Development Load Forecast is not a comprehensive load forecast update; rather, it shows
the impact of continued large load economic development growth when incorporated into
the initial Advancing Development Load Forecast. Id. at 553, 565. The Updated 2026
Advancing Development Load Forecast largely tracks the initial Advancing Development
Load Forecast to 2029, then continues to grow, surpassing the High Load Forecast by
2032. Id. at 554, fig. 2, 565.

39

Responding to the Public Staff’s and other parties’ claims that the Advancing
Development Load Forecast overstates expected load growth, witness Stillman testified
that the Duke load forecasting team takes seriously the risk of both over- and
under-forecasting future loads. Id. at 556, 565. Witness Stillman testified that the recent
growth in the large load pipeline indicates that the Advancing Development Load Forecast
underestimates future load growth, rather than overstates it as some parties have
claimed. Id. at 537, 555–56. Regarding the certainty of the load forecast, witness Stillman
agreed that there is uncertainty and testified that Duke discounts expected load to
address the uncertainty of the large load additions. Id. at 557. In response to AGO witness
Burgess’ recommendation to include only executed ESAs in the load forecast, witness
Stillman testified that a Spring 2026 forecast relying solely on executed ESAs would have
understated the economic development growth in ESAs by nearly 6 Gigawatts (GW) on
a full load basis. Id. at 558.
Witness Stillman testified that the load forecast sufficiently minimizes double
counting, and an alternative methodology is unnecessary. Id. at 558–60. According to
witness Stillman, Duke’s practices already align with the Public Staff’s recommendation
that Duke use a combination of historic meter data and expectations of future customers
in addition to their current industrial forecasting method. Id. at 558–59. Witness Stillman
explained that, in addition to the forecasting process, Duke performs additional final tests
to minimize double counting. Id. at 559. However, when questioned at the hearing witness
Stillman could not quantify how much load is being removed from the forecast to
40

address double counting and noted that Duke seeks to improve its forecasts each year.
Id. at 621–23. He further acknowledged that Duke expects that a 10% increase in the
price of electricity would reduce customer electricity usage by 1%, and that DEC’s and
DEP’s requested rate increases over a two-year period exceeded 10%. Id. at 631–33.
In rebuttal responding to Public Staff witness Fahey’s 15-year financial report
recommendation, Duke witnesses Bateman and Beveridge noted that Duke currently
does not prepare 15-year financial forecasts and were unclear how a 15-year financial
plan would provide useful data beyond what is currently provided within modeling and bill
impact analyses. Tr. vol. 21, 61–62. Witnesses Bateman and Beveridge testified that the
existing regulatory framework provides sufficient transparency and oversight to ensure
Duke is in a good financial position to meet future load growth and stated it is unnecessary
to require the Duke to prepare and file a 15-year financial plan. Id.
Witness Stillman further disagreed with the Public Staff’s recommendation that
Duke should provide an annual update to the load forecast outside of the CPIRP.
Tr. vol. 20, 560–61, 624. Witness Stillman explained that Duke already provides
an update twice a year on economic development in the Large Load Proceeding,
and economic development is currently the primary driver of growth in the forecast.
Id. at 560–61, 625–26. During cross-examination, witness Stillman testified that the
economic development portfolio is “the item that is moving the forecast right now” and
serves as “a good indication of where the forecast is going.” Id. at 626. Witness Stillman
also confirmed that Duke produces a load forecast every six months. Id. at 625. According
to witness Stillman, the biennial filing of the CPIRP provides an appropriate cadence for
review and tracking of the load forecast, and the semiannual large load reports filed in the
Large Load Proceeding convey general mid-cycle updates. Id. at 561. During
cross-examination, he acknowledged that between Spring 2025 and Fall 2025, there were
changes in the load forecast that were characterized in the Public Staff’s testimony and
a reduction in load. Id. at 626–28.
Discussion and Conclusions
The evidence in the record demonstrates that DEC’s and DEP’s North Carolina
and South Carolina service territories continue to experience significant economic
development growth, particularly from large load customers. The Commission is
persuaded that this economic development activity is dynamic and materially impacts
Duke’s load forecast.
The Commission is persuaded that Duke’s development of four load forecast
scenarios reasonably captures a range of potential future load outcomes for planning
purposes and gives weight to the Public Staff’s finding that this range of forecasts reflects
the uncertainty associated with load growth. The Commission concludes that Duke’s
methodology for developing its Advancing Development load forecast, including its use
of the economic development adjustment and associated discounting methodology, was
reasonable for planning purposes in this proceeding. The Commission further finds and
concludes that the input used for load forecasting should be as accurate as possible to
41

reflect current expectations and eliminate potential bias. Duke agreed that improvements
to the load forecasting methodology should be explored and accepted.
The Commission is persuaded that modification to Duke’s load forecast for
industrial loads is appropriate considering the substantial portion of the overall load
forecast represented by these loads. Duke noted that it has already modified its
forecasting methodology for large commercial customers to address the potential for
double counting and to make it more accurate. The Commission acknowledges potential
double counting of industrial loads as a valid concern and is persuaded that potential
solutions identified by the Public Staff can reasonably address potential double counting.
Therefore, as Duke is already implementing a method to remedy this issue with its
commercial customers, it is reasonable to use a similar method to address the issue with
its industrial customers. The Commission finds and concludes that it is reasonable to
order Duke to modify its method of forecasting electric load from its industrial customers
with a method similar to that used for data center customers within its commercial
forecasting to prevent double counting. The Commission is also persuaded that it is
reasonable for the load forecast to reflect rate changes occurring due to the business
combination of DEC and DEP and sensitivities of higher rate increases. Duke conceded
that the rate increases it is requesting in currently pending rate cases could have an
impact on future electricity demand, which would then be expected to have an impact on
the load forecast. Therefore, the Commission finds it reasonable to order Duke to provide
a sensitivity using the 4% cap on MYRP annual rate increases for the expected price
growth of electricity within its econometric forecasting. Duke must also include a merging
of legacy DEC and DEP rates over the next decade to accurately reflect expectations
relating to the business combination.
The Commission gives substantial weight to the testimony of Public Staff
witnesses Fahey and Thomas and SACE et al. witness Roumpani concerning the
evidence of large economic development projects dropping out of the economic
development pipeline, scaling back expected demands, and delays in taking service.
Their analysis demonstrates the dynamic nature of the load forecast and highlights that,
given the size of these projects, even minor changes to the timing or load can have
material impacts on resource planning. It is therefore critical to improve the discounting
methodology used within the load forecasting process. The Commission thus concludes
that Duke should collaborate with the Public Staff to enhance the load forecast for all
scenarios as it relates to economic development projects within the ESA, LA, late-stage,
and mid-stage timeline to include information on: (1) the type of project and likelihood of
the project materializing; (2) changes in load ramp and overall project delays; and (3) final
total load.
The Commission is not persuaded that it is reasonable to require Duke to prepare
and file a 15-year financial plan. The Commission notes that Duke does not currently
prepare 15-year financial forecasts, and it is unclear how a 15-year financial plan would
provide useful data that adds value to the analysis that Duke already provides as part of
the CPIRP filing. Further, neither Duke nor any other party was aware of any other utilities
providing such 15-year financial plans. The Commission finds that North Carolina’s
constructive regulatory tools are designed to enable utilities to raise capital to invest in
42

their systems. Accordingly, the Commission concludes that it is unnecessary to require
Duke to prepare and file a 15-year financial plan.
Finally, the Commission concludes that the concerns raised by the Public Staff and
intervenors regarding forecast uncertainty underscore the importance of continued,
regular monitoring of Duke’s economic development pipeline. As evidenced in the record,
the dynamic nature and pace of the economic development activity materially changes
the load forecast and resource planning. It is evident that the impact of additions,
cancelations, or delays in even a small number of large load customers could have a
material impact on the load forecast. The Commission is persuaded that more frequent
updates to the load forecast would meaningfully assist the parties and the Commission in
planning for this load growth. The evidence in the record shows that Duke completes load
forecasts twice a year. The Commission concludes that given the potential changes in
forecasts and timeline of the proceeding it is reasonable to order Duke to file its updated
load forecast every six months. The Commission concludes that as this requirement
aligns with Duke’s existing internal processes it would not impose a substantial burden
on Duke. The Commission gives significant weight to the testimony of witness Stillman
and witness Fahey that economic development is currently the most significant driver of
change to the load forecast. More frequent updates of the large economic development
pipeline may reduce the possibility of substantial new information being introduced late in
the next proceeding, as happened within this docket. Accordingly, the Commission
concludes that Duke’s semiannual large load reports should now be filed quarterly in
E-100 Sub 208A.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 9–14
Modeling
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in the Executive
Summary, Chapters 2–3, and Appendix C to the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits
of Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel (Beatty, Quinto, and Snider); the testimony
and exhibits of Duke witnesses Bowman and O’Connor; the testimony and exhibits of
Public Staff witnesses Michna and Metz; the testimony and exhibits of NCSEA witness
Devi Glick; the testimony and exhibits of SACE et al. witness Roumpani; the testimony
and exhibits of EDF witness Patrylak; the testimony and exhibits of CEBA witness Ivan
Urlaub; and the entire record.
Duke Direct Testimony
CPIRP Framework and Modeling Inputs
The 2025 CPIRP presents a comprehensive resource plan and update to the 2023
CPIRP, which was approved by the Commission’s 2024 CPIRP Order. The 2025 CPIRP
includes a detailed execution plan identifying near-term and intermediate-term actions for
Duke’s existing generation facilities, as well as proposed new supply-side, demand-side,
and transmission resources. The 2025 CPIRP is supported by 12 detailed appendices,
including the 11-page Appendix C (Quantitative Analysis), which provides detailed
43

analytical support for Duke’s approach to modeling and the key modeling inputs and
assumptions. The 2025 CPIRP also includes an updated Solar & Storage ELCC Study.
2025 CPIRP Ch. 2, App. C, Attach. I.
As explained in the 2025 CPIRP, the core focus of the analysis is to identify the
most reasonable, least-cost plan for Duke’s North Carolina and South Carolina service
territories through the development of resource portfolio options that maintain affordability
and reliability while planning to achieve long-term carbon neutrality by 2050. 2025 CPIRP
Exec. Summary at 10. The overall modeling and analysis framework is intended to satisfy
the primary requirements of maintaining or improving reliability while complying with all
applicable law. The 2025 CPIRP explains that these requirements are met by:
(1) quantifying performance of these portfolios; and (2) considering opportunities and
risks associated with resource diversity, executability, and meeting customer needs for
increasingly clean energy. The 2025 CPIRP explains that this approach informs the
identification of the most reasonable, least-cost portfolio. 2025 CPIRP App. C at 3. 6
The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained that in developing the
2025 CPIRP, Duke conducted three initial evaluations which included incremental,
multi-step modeling to determine the most reasonable base assumptions to be utilized in
subsequent capacity expansion and production cost modeling. These initial evaluations
were: (1) the fuel security evaluation; (2) the representative nuclear technology selection;
and (3) the coal retirement analysis. Tr. vol. 24, 90. The fuel security evaluation was
conducted to determine the most cost-effective fuel security option for future CCs and
CTs and was directed for review by the Commission in the 2024 CPIRP Order. This
evaluation considered enhanced firm transportation (EFT) and ELNG for fuel security
purposes, selecting ELNG as the most economic fuel security option for use as the CPIRP
base planning assumption. Id. at 90–91. The representative nuclear technology selection
evaluation contemplated the use of LLWRs versus SMRs for the CPIRP base planning
assumption, with the analysis selecting LLWRs as the representative technology.
However, Duke made clear that this does not indicate LLWRs to be Duke’s final selected
nuclear technology for execution. Id. at 91–92. Finally, the coal retirement analysis
evaluated the economically optimized retirement dates of eight of Duke’s coal units to
determine the appropriate retirement schedule to include in base planning assumptions.
Id. at 92–97.
The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained that Duke’s modeling process
includes three model types: (1) capacity expansion; (2) production cost; and (3) reliability
verification. Duke used the EnCompass modeling platform to perform capacity expansion
and production cost modeling and the SERVM reliability verification to perform a reliability
verification analysis for select portfolios to ensure the assessed portfolio would maintain
or improve system reliability. Id. at 79; 2025 CPIRP Ch. 2 at 18–22.

Appendix C of the 2025 CPIRP contains two consecutive pages denominated as page 3. This reference
is to the first of the two pages.

6

44

As explained in the CPIRP, capacity expansion modeling is used to optimize
portfolio resources to meet customer energy and peak demand needs, maintain or
improve reliability, and achieve carbon reduction targets over the planning horizon in a
least-cost manner. The model seeks to develop a portfolio of resources that will minimize
overall system costs inclusive of capital costs for new resources as well as ongoing
operation, maintenance, and fuel costs. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 2 at 22.
The portfolio of resources developed using the capacity expansion model is then
evaluated through production cost modeling, which uses detailed, chronological, hourly
granularity over the planning horizon to simulate the commitment and dispatch of system
resources to meet the weather normal hourly load requirements of the system consistent
with least-cost system operations. This step allows for additional analysis of each
portfolio’s cost, reliability, and diversity in order to incorporate risk adjustments or
execution considerations. Id.
Additional reliability modeling is conducted within SERVM for the base portfolios
for study years 2035 and 2040. This verification step is intended to ensure that reliability
is maintained with each portfolio’s particular set of resources identified in the capacity
expansion model. Additional dispatchable resources are added in this step if additional
firm capacity is needed to maintain system reliability. Id. at 22–23.
Preliminary Base Portfolio and Sensitivity Analysis
The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that Duke developed a Preliminary
Base Portfolio using the EnCompass capacity expansion model under baseline planning
assumptions, including the Advancing Development Load Forecast. The Preliminary
Base Portfolio represents a capacity-expansion-optimized least-cost mix of resources.
Duke witnesses Snider, Quinto, and Beatty testified that the Preliminary Base Portfolio
“represents a portfolio optimized entirely by the model, based on a single set of baseline
assumptions” and does not account for alternate planning uncertainty outcomes or
execution risk. Tr. vol. 24, 100, 109.
From the Preliminary Base Portfolio, Duke developed 30 sensitivity analysis
portfolios to address a wide range of planning uncertainties, including load, DSM, natural
gas price, resource cost, resource availability, and utility combination. Id. at 110. By
adjusting inputs and assumptions in the capacity expansion model, Duke evaluated the
impact of key variables on resource selection and identified potential risks and
opportunities that may emerge as Duke continues to balance the planning goals and
regulatory requirements of both states. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 2 at 4. Duke also developed
12 informational portfolios to address specific regulatory requirements or provide
supplemental information of interest to stakeholders. Id. Collectively, the 30 sensitivity
analysis portfolios and 12 informational portfolios enabled a robust evaluation of risks,
uncertainties, and tradeoffs relative to Duke’s planning objectives. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 3 at 4.
In addition, Duke conducted a portfolio performance analysis, assessing cost,
PVRR and bill impacts, operations, reliability, risk mitigation, and CO₂ emissions
45

reductions across the Preliminary Base Portfolio and sensitivity analysis portfolios to
inform the development of Duke’s Recommended NTAP. Tr. vol. 24, 105–06.
Risk-Adjusted NTAP and Recommended Portfolio
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that Duke’s Recommended
NTAP resources are “primarily based on the results of the Preliminary Base Portfolio, with
reasonable and appropriate risk adjustments when considering the totality of modeling
conducted as part of the Plan, including the sensitivity analysis and Portfolio Performance
Analysis.” Id. at 120. Duke witness Bowman described the Duke Recommended NTAP
as representing a “risk-adjusted strategy” that accounts for both the economic modeling
results and real-world execution considerations. Tr. vol. 6, 52; 2025 CPIRP Ch. 3 at 19.
The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained that because the Duke
Recommended NTAP is risk-adjusted rather than purely model-optimized, Duke took the
additional analytical step of developing the Recommended Portfolio. The IRP and
Near-Term Actions Panel testified that the Recommended Portfolio “modeled the
deployment of the recommended NTAP resources and then relied on the capacity
expansion model to optimize the selection of resources beyond the NTAP resources and
through the planning horizon.” Tr. vol. 24, 123. This additional modeling step allowed
Duke to assess whether the Duke Recommended NTAP resources maintain or improve
reliability and to quantify the cost, performance, and emissions trajectory of the
recommended plan. Id.
The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that the Recommended Portfolio
performed well in reliability verification using the baseline assumption of the Advancing
Development load forecast. As compared to the Preliminary Base Portfolio, the
Recommended Portfolio resulted in generally more favorable reliability metrics through
2035, though with slightly poorer results in 2040 while still satisfying reliability
requirements. According to the Panel, the increased reliability of the Recommended
Portfolio by 2035 is wholly attributable to the incremental 2,300 MW of solar and 400 MW
of storage in the risk-adjusted Recommended Portfolio. Id. at 124. With respect to cost,
the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel reported that the Recommended Portfolio trends
in the middle of the range of PVRR and bill impacts across all portfolios. The costs of the
Recommended Portfolio are modestly higher than the cost of the Preliminary Base
Portfolio through the planning horizon, but position Duke to improve reliability in the near
term and provide optionality to serve incremental load growth while mitigating long term
executability risk. Id. at 125–26. In addition, the Recommended Portfolio results in lower
annual emissions compared to the Preliminary Base Portfolio over the near term and
thereafter trends near the middle of the range through 2050. Id. at 127–28.
For these reasons, the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel concluded that the
Recommended Portfolio is the most reasonable portfolio to use as Duke’s reference
portfolio for planning purposes at this time. Id. at 129.

46

Public Staff Direct Testimony
Analysis of Duke’s Modeling
Public Staff witness Michna testified that Duke's presentation of a Preliminary Base
Portfolio which used baseline planning assumptions, from which sensitivities are derived
by introducing and removing datasets, is a reasonable modeling approach that the Public
Staff supports. Tr. vol. 13, 51. He explained that the portfolio spread, while presenting a
tremendous amount of data, greatly reduced the burden required to review the modeling
as compared to the approach used in the last two proceedings and aided in the analysis
and testing of Duke's model runs. Id. Witness Michna further testified that the
30 sensitivities Duke modeled assessing key inputs such as load, DSM, natural gas price,
resource costs, resource availability, and the planned utility combination are reasonable
and help inform the basis of a least-regrets and risk-adjusted NTAP. Id. at 56–57. He
further testified that Duke's three sequential evaluations, the fuel security evaluation,
representative nuclear technology selection, and coal retirement analysis, were
appropriate for modeling purposes and that the Public Staff did not contest inclusion of
the results as base assumptions. Id. at 57–59.
In addition, witness Michna testified that the Public Staff agrees with Duke's
risk-informed approach to developing the Duke Recommended NTAP, noting that it is
similar to the Public Staff’s approach in the 2023 CPIRP proceeding. He stated that
Duke’s methodology is representative of the near-term landscape and adequate as a
baseline for determining necessary actions before the next CPIRP proceeding. Id. at 60.
Witness Michna also testified that the Public Staff agrees with the methodology Duke
used to model the 2050 carbon neutrality target, including the use of non-segmented
model runs and the assumption that up to 5% of the target will be met through carbon
offsets. Id. at 63–65.
Notwithstanding this general support, witness Michna identified several concerns
with Duke's modeling inputs and assumptions. He testified that the Public Staff has
concerns with Duke's use of the Advancing Development Load Forecast as the baseline
for its Preliminary Base Portfolio, noting that this forecast is higher than the one filed in
Docket No. E-100, Sub 208A. He further adds this forecast applies an increasing load
factor not tied to individual projects, so that Duke's sensitivities analyses are measured
against a baseline of aggressive construction and high PVRR. Id. at 52. Witness Michna
also testified that his investigation uncovered several modeling constraints that were not
directly communicated in Duke's filing, which he testified had the effect of "funneling" the
model toward prescribed outcomes rather than allowing it to select the most economic
path forward. Id. at 53. As examples, witness Michna cited an undisclosed constraint
limiting combined offshore wind and nuclear development to one long-lead-time resource
per year, and a second undisclosed constraint prescribing a “2-years-on-1-year-off”
cadence for nuclear energy additions beginning in different years for the two utilities.
Id. at 54. Witness Michna testified that, while these constraints do not affect the Duke
Recommended NTAP, he is concerned that the lack of transparency risks conflating
"economically selected" resources with resources selected under rigid, self-imposed
parameters. Id. at 53–54. He further testified that Duke's methodology for estimating the
47

output of battery storage paired with solar resources, which relies on a pre-prescribed,
externally modeled charge-discharge pattern rather than allowing EnCompass to
dispatch the resource, likely underestimates the resource's contribution to the winter
morning peak by approximately 6,250 gigawatt-hour (GWh) through 2050 and could result
in unnecessary additional resource buildout. Id. at 90-94. Additionally, witness Michna
testified that Duke applied its Large Load Curtailment program only within a single
high-load informational portfolio sensitivity, rather than across all load forecast scenarios,
which he characterized as a missed opportunity. Id. at 82-84.
Public Staff’s Modeling Approach
Witness Michna testified that the Public Staff conducted its independent modeling
analysis using the same EnCompass modeling platform and version employed by Duke.
Id. at 32. He noted that EnCompass version 8.0 was released commercially in February
2025, before Duke’s CPIRP filing, but that use of the older version 7.3.4 did not result in
a material impact on results in this proceeding. Id. at 49.
Witness Michna testified that the Public Staff first reviewed the EnCompass input
files provided by Duke and used them to recreate Duke’s EnCompass database. The
Public Staff then re-ran Duke’s portfolios to validate that similar results were achieved,
confirming the reproducibility of Duke’s modeling. Id. at 80. After validating Duke’s
portfolios, the Public Staff modified those datasets based on its investigation, creating or
modifying certain resources, modeling assumptions, load forecasts, and other inputs. Id.
Witness Michna testified that the Public Staff structured its modeling to test the
impact of several different Duke datasets by introducing them one at a time, allowing the
Public Staff to isolate and identify the incremental impact of each assumption change on
the optimized resource portfolio. The Public Staff’s final Recommended Portfolio
incorporated lessons from these assumption changes and then applied combined
DEC/DEP single-utility (1U) parameters to test Duke’s assumptions under a consolidated
system framework. The Public Staff further included a modified Clean Air Act (CAA) Rule
sensitivity to test the Duke Recommended NTAP under a scenario in which then-current
CAA rules remained unchanged. Id. at 81.
The Public Staff also calculated the PVRRs for its own portfolios using Duke’s
PVRR workpapers on a consistent basis, enabling direct comparison of cost outcomes
across the two parties’ modeling results. Id. at 94.
When discussing the sensitivities completed by the Public Staff, witness Michna
stated the following were modeled: a "CC CF Limits" dataset to cap the capacity factor of
new CC units at a more realistic level than the 93%-plus level produced by Duke's
assumptions; a "CTF Cost Adj." dataset updating CT pricing to reflect the unit specified
in Duke's CPCN application for the F-Class CT in Richmond County, North Carolina, in
Docket No. E-2, Sub 1395; a dataset forcing the Anderson CC unit (CC3) into service in
2031 to test the robustness of the Duke Recommended NTAP; two datasets updating
solar and battery storage additions to reflect projects approved and added to the
interconnection queue since Duke finalized its modeling inputs; and a sensitivity in which
48

battery storage paired with solar is dispatched endogenously within EnCompass to
observe effects on the contribution of storage during winter mornings. Id. at 85–92.
Witness Michna testified that the Public Staff also proposed running the CAA Section 111
rule dataset over the Preliminary Base Portfolio, given that compliance with the rule was
then required. Id.
Witness Michna also testified extensively regarding the Public Staff's introduction
into its modeling of a large load curtailment sensitivity. He explained that Duke's Large
Load DSM program modeled only 750 MW of curtailable load in DEC and 250 MW in
DEP, beginning in 2030, and was applied solely within Duke's high-load Informational
Portfolio sensitivity without associated development or deployment costs. Id. at 82. To
more accurately reflect the large-load curtailment provisions, as written in Duke's current
ESA construct and relevant large load contracts, witness Michna stated that the Public
Staff modified model representation of large load contracts and included the dataset
“Large Load DSM” in its recommended portfolio. Id. at 82–83. Public Staff witness
Thomas in the Public Staff Load Panel testimony outlined key modifications to the Large
Load DSM program, including a 50% higher dispatch cost to ensure it is used only as a
true economic last resort and a revision of available capacity to reflect existing ESA
agreements. Tr. vol. 16, 57–64. Witness Michna testified that this dataset is not a
proposed tariff or program in this proceeding, but rather a model update to reflect the
current language of Duke's executed ESAs. He further testified that in the Public Staff
Recommended Portfolio, the program is called on for one hour of use, and two hours for
use in its 1U Recommended Portfolio. Tr. vol. 13, 83. Witness Michna stated that Duke’s
approach of only applying large load customer curtailment provisions in a sensitivity
analysis represented a missed opportunity, and that the program should be modeled in
all load forecast scenarios going forward. Id. at 82–84.
Regarding the effect of this adjustment, witness Michna testified that inclusion of
large load curtailment provisions has the largest impact of any single dataset introduced
into either Duke's or the Public Staff's modeling. Id. at 84. He stated that the combination
of the first mentioned updates, along with adding the Large Load DSM program, resulted
in the Public Staff’s Recommended Portfolio having a PVRR $1 billion less than Duke’s
over the next decade in both the fully combined utility scenario (1U) and a scenario where
the business combination is not approved (2U). Id. at 95.
Witness Michna explained that, because the model has very limited options for
meeting power needs through the early 2030s, the Large Load DSM program provides
immediate relief to the generation buildout across all sensitivities while reflecting
commercial terms currently in effect, resulting in: (1) delays to proposed CC and CT units
in any portfolio using the dataset; and (2) lower PVRR borne by ratepayers. Given the
significance of this impact, witness Michna requested that Duke explain in rebuttal
testimony why it is reasonable to exclude programs that are elements of executed
agreements from its modeling, particularly where doing so exposes ratepayers to a more
aggressive generation buildout and higher overall costs. Id. at 84–85.
Witness Michna stated that the Public Staff does not have a SERVM license and
requested that Duke complete a SERVM evaluation on at least the Public Staff
49

Recommended Portfolio and Public Staff 1U Recommended Portfolio and provide the
results in their rebuttal testimony. Id. at 96–97. Finally, witness Michna made the following
recommendations regarding Duke’s modeling approach: (1) Duke should update the
parties and the Commission on the status of upgrading to a newer version of EnCompass
in rebuttal; (2) Duke should apply the CAA Rule unit limits in the base analysis if these
rules have not been modified before the filing of the proposed 2027 CPIRP; (3) the
Commission should order Duke to revise the Duke Recommended NTAP as soon as
possible after the outcome of the 1U Business Combination is known; and (4) Duke
should explain in its rebuttal testimony why it is reasonable to exclude programs that are
elements of executed agreements from its modeling. Id. at 106–07.
Revisions to the Public Staff’s Model Recommendations
During the expert witness hearing, when asked if their responses to questions
would differ from those filed in prefiled testimony, Public Staff witnesses Michna and Metz
stated their responses would differ, requiring revisions to their testimony. Following this
statement, witness Michna reviewed their prefiled testimony and struck select material.
Significantly, the Public Staff struck its recommendation that the Commission adopt the
Public Staff’s proposed NTAP, proceed with the Advancing Development load forecast
as filed by Duke, and delay its plan of approaching the Commission for CPCNs for two
CCs and two CTs prior to the filing of its proposed 2027 CPIRP. Id. at 106–09. These
revisions were made following Duke’s filing of the Updated 2026 Advancing Development
Load Forecast within the Spring 2026 Large Load Report, in which Duke projected an
additional 2 GW of growth compared to the Fall 2025 Large Load Report.
When asked why these revisions were made, witnesses Metz and Michna explicitly
referenced Duke’s revised load forecast and stated, “The concern is that the
recommendations that we put forward…[are] the bare minimum that need to be executed
with the information we have.” Tr. vol. 14, 21–22. Witness Michna emphasized this
position, stating “[the model is] building pretty much whatever you allow it to do. It’s
megawatt hungry.” Id. at 20. He further clarified that this alteration of the Public Staff’s
position did not alleviate their concerns surrounding the “funneling” effect present
within Duke’s modeling, but rather served as a recognition that, as Duke had presented
its model, the updated load forecast indicated a more immediate need for power. Id. at
47–49. Witnesses Metz and Michna extensively responded to cross examination
regarding these alterations, reiterating their concern that the aggressive upward revision
of Duke’s load forecast challenged Duke’s ability to accomplish the necessary resource
buildout. Id. at 19–22, 127–28.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
In addition to the Public Staff, three intervenors performed independent modeling
analysis in this proceeding: NCSEA, SACE et al., and EDF.
NCSEA witness Glick of Synapse Energy Economics testified regarding NCSEA’s
independent EnCompass and SERVM modeling analysis. Witness Glick reviewed Duke’s
EnCompass-based modeling framework, describing the progression from the Preliminary
50

Base Portfolio to the 30 Sensitivity Analysis Portfolios, to the manually constructed NTAP,
to the NTAP re-run through EnCompass producing the Recommended Portfolio, and
finally to SERVM reliability modeling. Tr. vol. 17, 125–40.
Witness Glick performed independent EnCompass modeling reproducing Duke’s
Preliminary Base scenario (Reproduced Base) and NTAP scenario ( Reproduced NTAP)
and developed the “NCSEA Alternative” scenario incorporating four changes to Duke’s
inputs: High EE load, High DSM resources, Bad Creek II online in 2037
(versus 2040), and PowerPair as a selectable resource. Witness Glick also developed an
“NCSEA Combined Utility” scenario. Id. at 150–61. She also used SERVM to validate the
reliability of NCSEA’s alternative portfolio through 6,570 iterations across 45 weather
years, 50 outage draws, and three load scenarios. Id. at 169–76. Witness Glick testified
that the NCSEA Alternative Portfolio is $3.9 billion less in PVRR than the Reproduced
Recommended Portfolio over the study period and is reliability-equivalent. Id. at 180–82.
During the expert witness hearing, when asked about the efforts required to conduct
SERVM modeling as an intervenor, witness Glick stated that the licensing was an
expensive acquisition for her consulting firm, Synapse Energy Economics, but that “it is
very important…to have parties besides just the utility able to look under the hood and
understand what assumptions were made, how the model works, what reliability metrics
it verifies, how it does that, and to be able to talk to the model vendor…to understand
some of the background of the model.” Id. at 202–03.
Witness Glick testified that the modeling results of the NCSEA Alternative Portfolio
demonstrated equal cost-effectiveness and reliability compared to Duke’s Reproduced
Recommended Portfolio. With respect to SERVM modeling results, witness Glick stated
that the NCSEA Alternative Portfolio achieved an LOLE of 0.116 and 0.056 for 2035 and
2040, respectively. In comparison, Duke’s Reproduced Recommended Portfolio had an
LOLE of 0.118 and 0.058 for the same years. Id. at 170–71. Since the following were core
adjustments to Duke’s modeling assumptions in the NCSEA Alternative Portfolio, witness
Glick recommended that Duke should pursue greater EE savings, expand its demand
response programs, and expand its pumped hydroelectric storage capacity through
bringing Bad Creek II online by 2037. Id. at 182–83.
SACE et al. witness Roumpani conducted independent EnCompass modeling and
testified regarding her analysis. Witness Roumpani criticized Duke’s sensitivity analysis
methodology, testifying that Duke’s approach of re-optimizing the portfolio for each
sensitivity, rather than holding the NTAP fixed and varying external drivers, masks the
true risk exposure of the recommended NTAP. Tr. vol. 20, 468–69.
To reflect the assertion that Duke’s sensitivity-analysis methodology was flawed,
witness Roumpani provided a different strategic approach when designing SACE et al.’s
alternative portfolio. As an illustrative portfolio, the alternative portfolio first incorporated
Duke’s level of solar and energy storage resources in the NTAP, and then included the
following as adjustments: a combined DEC/DEP system; the Moderate load forecast;
increased solar/wind availability; onshore wind as a selectable resource in 2032; Bad
Creek II in-service in 2034; High DSM; selectable PowerPair; the inclusion of large load
customer curtailments, as configured in Duke’s relevant sensitivity; and 100-hour LDES
51

configurations available for selection in modeling. Id. at 456–57. Witness Roumpani’s
alternative portfolio produces PVRR savings across scenarios, including reductions of
$3 billion, $4.3 billion, and $8.0 billion under the Advancing Development, Moderate, and
Low Load forecasts, respectively. Under the high-gas cost scenario, witness Roumpani
testified that the alternative portfolio produces a PVRR $5.4 billion lower. Id. at 464–65.
EDF witness Patrylak conducted narrower, issue-specific EnCompass-based
modeling focused specifically on the robustness of the timing and need for Duke’s fifth
CC unit (CC5). He performed 17 modeling runs testing variations in load level/timing,
transmission capability, and CC capital costs. Tr. vol. 22, 139. Witness Patrylak testified
that CC5 is not a robust NTAP selection because it was delayed to 2040 in many of the
alternative sensitivities he tested. Id. at 140.
Intervenors who did not perform independent modeling nevertheless put forth
recommendations and/or critiques related to Duke’s modeling across a variety of issues. For
example, the AGO and NC WARN criticized Duke’s reliance on the Advancing Development
Load Forecast as the base forecast for planning purposes. Tr. vol. 22, 56–57; 201. Similarly,
the AGO and SACE et al. suggested use of curtailment provisions in large load ESAs to
support reliability. Id. at 65, 88; tr. vol. 20, 298, 392. Several intervenors advocated for
increased use of EE/DSM programs. Id. at 89, 93; id. at 252, 298.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
In rebuttal, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel responded to the testimony
of Public Staff witnesses Michna and Metz and other intervenors regarding Duke’s
modeling. The Panel testified that after two planning cycles of collaborative engagement
and Commission directives, Duke and the Public Staff agree on the majority of modeling
inputs, analytical methods, and resource planning approaches. Tr. vol. 24, 165–66. The
Panel identified numerous specific areas of agreement, including Duke’s overall modeling
methodology, finding it representative of the near-term landscape and adequate as a
baseline for determining near-term actions. Id. at 174. The Panel testified that the Public
Staff further found Duke’s more than 30 sensitivities to be reasonable and helpful in
informing a least-regrets, risk-adjusted NTAP, and found the three sequential initial
evaluations — the fuel security evaluation, the representative nuclear technology
selection, and the Coal Retirement Evaluation — to be appropriate for modeling
purposes. The Panel also testified that the Public Staff agreed with Duke’s approach to
modeling CAA regulations, the carbon neutrality target methodology, and the
methodology for projecting bill impacts. Id. at 174–75. In addition, the Panel testified that
the Public Staff found Duke’s reliability-related assumptions to be generally reasonable,
including the 22% planning reserve margin, the ELCC values assigned to different
resource types, and the reliability verification step, which the Public Staff itself
acknowledged as a “critical tool to ensure system reliability.” Id. at 175. The Panel testified
that the Public Staff similarly found Duke’s solar production profile methodology, SISC,
BTM solar forecast, planned hydroelectric uprates, and various transmission-related
assumptions to be reasonable for planning purposes. The Panel concluded that this
breadth of alignment should provide the Commission with confidence that Duke’s overall
52

modeling process and the majority of Duke’s inputs and assumptions have led to
reasonable modeling and planning of the system. Id. at 176.
Next, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel critiqued aspects of the Public
Staff’s modeling related to large load. The Panel first noted that since filing the
2025 CPIRP, economic development in the Carolinas has continued at a rapid pace, with
Duke’s Updated 2026 Advancing Development Load Forecast now tracking at or above
the originally filed High Load Forecast scenario. Id. at 166. The Panel asserted that,
because the Public Staff relied exclusively on the Advancing Development Load Forecast
scenario and did not meaningfully consider a load growth scenario, the Public Staff
Recommended Portfolio fails to account for the load growth risk that Duke expressly
considered, and which has now come to pass. Id. at 189–90.
The Panel next explained that, aside from this increased load forecast, the most
material difference between Duke and the Public Staff’s modeling is the Public Staff’s
treatment of large load customer curtailment provisions. Duke’s pro forma ESA allows
curtailment of certain large load customers on an interim basis for up to fifty hours per
year at 95% of actual energized load or up to 100 hours per year at 50% of actual load.
Id. at 192. The Panel testified that these provisions are a temporary, operationally limited
bridge measure to provide flexibility while new resources are constructed and should
neither be considered a firm capacity substitute nor a modification of Duke’s obligation to
serve customers on a firm basis. Id. During the expert witness hearing, Duke witness
Snider characterized these provisions as “an extra layer of depth and defense, not using
them to defer resources but as an operational tool for our operators, that, along with other
mitigants, puts us in a position to have a reliable plan.” Id. at 273.
In the Panel’s view, the Public Staff improperly modeled these provisions by:
(1) assigning them a 100% firm capacity contribution; and (2) representing the provisions
for ten years instead of five. On request of the Public Staff, Duke conducted SERVM
reliability verification modeling and determined that the Public Staff Recommended
Portfolios would result in an unreliable system, reaching a LOLE nearly four times the
expected 0.165 threshold. The Panel asserted that it is the Public Staff’s very reliance on
curtailment provisions, and their firm capacity assignment on a one-to-one basis, which
spurs natural gas resource deferment and the consequent modeling reliability failure.
Id. at 199. They further stated that the Public Staff’s one-to-one representation deviates
from the well-established concept of ELCC, under which energy-limited resources cannot
be credited with their full nameplate capacity for reliability purposes. Id. at 196–97. For
an alternate example, the Panel cited a 2025 industry study finding similar time-limited
curtailment provisions would warrant an ELCC value of only 31% to 36% in 2030. The
Panel concluded that while curtailment provisions can serve as a valuable operational
reliability tool, as Duke intended, the Public Staff's reliance on those provisions to defer
generation resources is unreasonable for planning purposes and results in a plan that
does not maintain or improve the adequacy and reliability of the existing grid as required
by the Carbon Plan Statute and Commission Rule R8-60A. Id. at 200–01.
With respect to the Public Staff’s other modeling updates, including adjustments
to thermal and non-thermal resources, the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that
53

these additional changes do not materially affect the need for the remaining resources in
Duke’s Recommended NTAP. Id. at 210–13.
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel next addressed the modeling of other
intervenors. The Panel testified that, in contrast to the substantial alignment with the
Public Staff, the modeling submitted by other intervenors, including NCSEA, SACE et al.,
and EDF, was limited in scope, relied on selective or simplified representations of the
system, and was designed to illustrate directional outcomes rather than to produce a
reasonable, least-cost, reliable resource plan. The Panel testified that all of the intervenor
modeling relies on artificially reduced load assumptions, thereby changing the "playing
field" by solving for lower system needs than are expected. This disconnect is particularly
pronounced for SACE et al., which planned to the Moderate Forecast, but also applies to
NCSEA's use of unrealistic DSM/EE assumptions. Id. at 218–20. The Panel testified that
intervenors' alternative portfolios also rely on overly optimistic or inexecutable resource
availability timelines for solar, storage, onshore wind, and Bad Creek II; unproven
customer programs at scale; and aggressive coal retirement schedules unsupported by
replacement generation. Id. at 167, 258. The Panel noted, however, that even NCSEA's
alternative portfolio, despite its unreasonable assumption changes, aligns closely with
Duke’s Recommended Portfolio by 2035, which the Panel testified confirms the
robustness of Duke’s planning process. Id. at 226.
Finally, the Panel testified that, consistent with prior orders, the Commission
should give limited weight to recommendations that are not supported by comprehensive
modeling to demonstrate the impacts on resource selection, system reliability, and
customer costs. The Panel asserted that the Commission cannot assess the reliability,
economic, or operational effects of generalized intervenor recommendations to reduce,
defer, or replace specific NTAP resources or determine the most reasonable, least cost
resource plan. The Panel testified that, without supporting modeling, intervenors lack the
technical support necessary to demonstrate that their recommendations would produce
a more reasonable plan than the one proposed by Duke, which is largely supported by
the Public Staff’s independent modeling. Id. at 168.
Duke SERVM reliability verification Results
During the expert witness hearing, counsel for the Public Staff inquired with Duke’s
IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel regarding the effects of the Updated 2026 Advancing
Development Load forecast on Duke’s Recommended NTAP. During this inquiry, the
Public Staff identified and admitted into the record Public Staff Cross Reliability and
Operational Resilience Panel Direct Exhibit 1 (High Load SERVM Exhibit). This exhibit
represented Duke’s response to a Public Staff discovery request for modeling information
relevant to the Updated 2026 Advancing Development Load Forecast. Duke’s response
stated that Duke did not conduct modeling for this load forecast update but rather
completed SERVM reliability verification for its High Load sensitivity portfolio against the
high load forecast. The High Load SERVM Exhibit shows that in 2035 Duke’s High Load
portfolio results in a LOLE of 0.304 as developed, higher than Duke’s stated 0.165 LOLE
reliability threshold. This exhibit additionally provided LOLE results for the High Load
portfolio with ESA curtailments included and 2 CTs of firm capacity as separate model
54

runs, showing LOLE values of 0.182 and 0.131, respectively. When asked by Public Staff
counsel as to whether Duke had conducted the reliability verification step for the Duke
Recommended NTAP against the Updated 2026 Advancing Development Load Forecast,
Duke witness Snider confirmed it did not. Tr. vol. 23, 303–04.
Discussion and Conclusions
The evidence demonstrates that Duke and the Public Staff are generally aligned
on the fundamental modeling inputs, methodology, and portfolio analysis underlying the
2025 CPIRP. Though the Public Staff has some concerns regarding the methodology
underlying Duke’s Reproduced Base Portfolio, it agrees with Duke's overall modeling
methodology and argues that the approach provides an adequate baseline for
determining the NTAP. The Public Staff likewise agrees with Duke’s three sequential
evaluations, its approach to modeling CAA compliance 7 and the carbon neutrality target,
its reliability-related assumptions, and numerous other modeling inputs and assumptions.
Relating to other parties, the Commission notes general alignment between the NCSEA
and Duke portfolio results when considering the appropriate load forecast, which the
Commission finds confirms the robustness of Duke's planning process. 8 The Commission
gives substantial weight to this breadth of alignment, particularly between Duke and the
Public Staff, and finds that Duke’s overall modeling process, and the development of the
risk-adjusted Duke Recommended NTAP, and Recommended Portfolio derived
therefrom, reflects reasonable and appropriate planning for the system.
Duke and the Public Staff employed the same general approach to modeling. Both
used version 7.3.4 of the EnCompass modeling platform to perform capacity expansion
and production cost modeling. Duke additionally used the SERVM model to perform a
portfolio reliability verification analysis for both its own and the Public Staff’s
recommended portfolios under the Advancing Development load forecast. Significantly,
the capacity expansion modeling, which optimizes portfolio resources in a least-cost
manner, and the production cost modeling, which dispatches the resources consistent
with least-cost system operations, are critical components of identifying the least-cost
path to compliance with the Carbon Plan Statute and the IRP Statute’s requirement to
plan for the least-cost mix of generation and demand-reduction measures. The output of
these models reflects a mix of generating resources that can meet system needs under
various scenarios in a least-cost manner. The Commission recognizes that identifying the
least-cost path involves steps beyond the initial modeling (such as understanding the
practical implications of the resource portfolios), but the Commission concludes, given
7 The Commission notes that on September 14, 2026, the EPA finalized its repeal of the majority of

the provisions of the 2024 CAA § 111 Final Rule, permitting new natural gas CTs to dispatch as needed
without any anticipated capacity factor limitation or emissions capture requirements. The final rule is
effective November 16, 2026. See Partial Repeal of the Carbon Pollution Standards for Fossil Fuel-Fired
Electric Generating Units, 91 Fed. Reg. 58, 954, 58, 989–93 (Sep. 17, 2026).
8 The Commission notes that three parties conducted EnCompass modeling in this CPIRP in addition
to Duke: the Public Staff, NCSEA, and SACE et al. Overall, the Commission commends the parties for the
time and effort spent in modeling the CPIRP and finds the multiple modeling efforts extremely helpful in
developing a thorough evidentiary record in this proceeding to converge towards a robust no-regrets NTAP.

55

the least-cost planning principles incorporated into the modeling, that the modeling
conducted provides the necessary foundation for a least-cost path to compliance.
The Commission also highlights that the modeling conducted by Duke has taken
into account the removal of the Interim Target and finds the modeling, as a whole,
consistent with S.B. 266. Tr. vol. 25, 105.
With respect to Duke’s portfolio analysis, the Commission finds that Duke’s
30 Sensitivity Analysis Portfolios and 12 Informational Portfolios examine a range of
potential future outcomes and resulting cost and performance with respect to uncertainty
in various model assumptions such as resource costs, fuel commodity prices, availability
of resources, and load forecast scenarios. Public Staff witness Michna confirmed that
Duke’s 30-plus sensitivities are reasonable and helpful in informing a least regrets and
risk adjusted NTAP. The Commission accordingly concludes that this broad portfolio
analysis provides the Commission and parties with a thorough evaluation of the potential
effects that a variety of future conditions may have on optimal resource selection and
portfolio performance.
The Commission further concludes that Duke’s development of a Recommended
Portfolio based on a “risk-adjusted” NTAP developed from the Preliminary Base Portfolio,
including reasonable and appropriate risk adjustments considering the totality of the
modeling results and real-world execution considerations, is reasonable for planning
purposes. Stated another way, the Commission concludes that Duke’s approach of
developing a risk-adjusted NTAP from the totality of modeling results rather than relying
on a single model-optimized portfolio is a reasonable approach to resource planning
under conditions of significant uncertainty, including uncertainty regarding load growth.
The Commission notes that the principal areas of misalignment between Duke and
the intervening parties are the following: (1) the Public Staff and other intervenors’
reliance on lower load forecasts for the purposes of modeling; and (2) the opposing
positions of Duke and the Public Staff regarding representation of load customer
curtailment provisions within portfolio modeling.
First, with respect to the effect of load forecast assumptions on modeling, the
Commission gives substantial weight to Duke’s rebuttal that intervenors’ modeling relied
on artificially reduced load assumptions, in effect solving for unrealistic and artificially
lower system load. As indicated above, the Commission finds the Advancing
Development Load Forecast reasonable for the purposes of this proceeding.
Second, with respect to modeling of large load curtailment, the Commission finds
the record inadequate to agree with either Duke or the Public Staff. As Public Staff witness
Thomas summarizes, the Public Staff recommends curtailment provisions be represented
within modeling because the “mandatory curtailment provision already exists, is signed
and executed by sophisticated parties… [and] is a tool for Duke to use.” The Public Staff
recommends Duke incorporate these provisions into modeling to understand the effect
they will have on system planning in the near term. Tr. vol. 16, 97–98. On rebuttal, Duke’s
IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel stated explicitly that curtailment provisions were “to
56

provide a temporary real-time operational reliability tool.” This characterization was
reaffirmed by Duke witness Snider from the stand. Tr. vol. 24, 194, 273. Despite this
formal position, Duke presented reliability modeling to the Public Staff within the High
Load SERVM Exhibit which modeled curtailment provisions to improve the LOLE of the
High Load portfolio. Duke witness O’Connor stated during the hearing that these “high
load [SERVM results] taught us a lot of really important things. The first thing is…those
curtailment provisions that we are holding in reserve as part of depth and defense, those
are almost enough on their own with no other action to help keep the portfolio reliable.”
Tr. vol. 23, 305–06. Overall, the Commission finds Duke’s reliance on these curtailments
to maintain a reliable portfolio, while simultaneously dismissing the Public Staff’s
modeling of them, logically inconsistent. Additionally, implicit in Duke’s criticism of the
Public Staff curtailment provision modeling with a 100% ELCC is an acknowledgement
that the curtailment provisions have some ELCC value that is non-zero.
The Commission sees value in further refining the modeling of ESA curtailments
in the next CPIRP. During the expert witness hearing, when responding to Duke’s position
generally, Public Staff witness Thomas agreed that it would be appropriate to assign an
ELCC value to large load curtailment provisions to the extent they are modeled as a
selectable resource. Tr. vol. 17, 60. When Duke witness Snider was asked if Duke was
open to working with the Public Staff to determine reasonable modeling assumptions for
ESA curtailments within EnCompass for the next CPIRP, witness Snider stated, “yes, we
are willing to work with them.” Tr. vol. 25, 109. Thus, the Commission concludes it is
appropriate to direct Duke and the Public Staff to confer and determine the most
reasonable method of incorporating large load customer ESA curtailment provisions into
modeling for the 2027 CPIRP proceeding.
On the independent modeling conducted by NCSEA, SACE et al., and EDF, the
Commission finds that this modeling relies on artificially reduced load assumptions that
change the underlying planning ”playing field” by solving for system needs lower than
those actually expected, a disconnect that is particularly pronounced for SACE et al.'s
reliance on the Moderate Load Forecast and NCSEA's use of DSM/EE assumptions the
Commission finds unrealistic. The Commission concludes that the minimal impact these
lower load forecasts have on the resulting NTAP does not render the portfolios preferred
by NCSEA, SACE et al., or EDF more reasonable than Duke's Recommended Portfolio,
particularly where those alternative portfolios also rely on overly optimistic assumptions.
The Commission further notes that even NCSEA's alternative portfolio (on which NCSEA
conducted independent SERVM analysis) aligns closely with Duke's Recommended
Portfolio by 2035, which the Commission finds confirms the robustness of Duke's planning
process.
Finally, the Commission finds that those parties who did not perform independent
modeling in this proceeding, notwithstanding the recommendations and critiques they
raised regarding Duke's modeling, failed to provide adequate technical support for their
positions. Without supporting modeling to demonstrate the impacts of their
recommendations on resource selection, system reliability, and customer costs, the
Commission cannot assess the reliability, economic, or operational effects of generalized
recommendations to reduce, defer, or replace specific NTAP resources, or otherwise
57

determine that such recommendations would produce a more reasonable, least-cost
resource plan than the one proposed by Duke and largely supported by the Public Staff's
independent modeling. Accordingly, consistent with the Commission's prior orders, the
Commission affords the modeling positions of parties that did not perform modeling
little weight.
The Commission concludes that the modeling conducted by Duke and the Public
Staff, together with the independent modeling analyses presented by NCSEA, SACE et
al., and EDF, provides the Commission with substantial, competent, and material
evidence to support the findings of fact and conclusions set forth herein. The modeling
conducted provides the necessary analytical foundation for a least-cost resource plan,
consistent with the Commission’s obligation to ensure any generation and resource
changes maintain or improve the adequacy and reliability of the existing grid.
N.C.G.S. §§ 62-110.9, 62-110.1(c), and 62-2(3a).
Based on the foregoing competent, material, and substantial evidence, and given
the substantial alignment between Duke and the Public Staff on the vast majority of
modeling inputs, methodology, and portfolio analysis, the Commission approves Duke's
Recommended Portfolio. The Commission further concludes that the Duke
Recommended Portfolio is reasonable and appropriate for use as Duke’s “reference
portfolio,” as that term is used in Rule R8-60A(f)(10), for the purpose of determining
avoided costs between now and Duke’s next CPIRP filing.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 15–21
Maintaining System Reliability
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Appendices C and
L to the 2025 CPIRP; Attachment I (2025 Solar & Storage ELCC Study) to the
2025CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Reliability and Operational Resilience
Panel (John Samuel Holeman III and Patrick O’Connor); the testimony and exhibits of
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel (Snider, Quinto, and Beatty); the testimony and
exhibits of Duke’s Load Forecast and Economic Development Panel (Passty, Stillman,
and Tate); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff witness Michna; the testimony and
exhibits of Public Staff’s System Reliability and Operations Panel (Jethro Ssengonzi and
Jeff Thomas); the testimony and exhibits of CIGFUR witness Colin Fitzhenry; the
testimony and exhibits of NC WARN witness Powers; the testimony and exhibits of SACE
et al. witness Michael Goggin; and the entire record.
Duke Direct Testimony
Duke witness O’Connor testified that Duke mitigates reliability risk in the 2025
CPIRP through a combination of direct incorporation of reliability criteria into the portfolio
modeling process and the use of quantitative and qualitative considerations to help shape
the type and timing of resources available and selected for the future. Tr. vol. 7, 126.
Witness O’Connor explained that Duke integrates reliability considerations into the
CPIRP at every step of the planning process. First, Duke incorporates realistic and
58

executable technology assumptions. As witness O’Connor explained, unrealistic
modeling and technology assumptions can create long-term risks to reliability should
resources not be deployable in the necessary timeframe. Within the Plan, these
considerations include ensuring firm fuel supply for new gas units, preserving
transmission system reliability, balancing reliability and operational risk in coal unit
retirements, and carefully vetting new resource execution timelines and new technology
availability. Id.
Next, Duke incorporates reasonable resource adequacy inputs, including the
planning reserve margin (PRM) and the ELCC of renewable resources and energy
storage resources. These inputs guide the capacity expansion models to choose
resources that will enable the reliable operation of Duke’s system under a variety of
real-world uncertainties. As explained in Chapter 2, the PRM is a critical component for
ensuring the system has sufficient resources to meet the needs of customers. The PRM
functions as a safety net by ensuring there is enough generating capacity available to
serve demand at all times, including during unexpected events such as unanticipated
generating unit outages or periods of high customer demand that stress the system.
2025 CPIRP Ch. 2 at 3. For the 2025 CPIRP, Duke continues to use the 22% PRM
approved in the 2024 CPIRP Order. Tr. vol. 7, 127. According to witness O’Connor, the
22% PRM still supports the reliable planning and operation of the DEC and DEP systems.
However, Duke plans to conduct a new Resource Adequacy Study to review the PRM in
advance of the 2027 CPIRP filing. Id. at 128–29.
Aside from the PRM, the second group of key reliability inputs is the set of ELCC
estimates for variable or energy limited resources such as solar, wind, and storage. The
ELCC of a resource determines how much it is expected to contribute to meeting
customer demand during the most acute periods of system stress. As explained in
Appendix L, because these resources may not be available when needed due to variable
weather conditions (wind, solar), or the system may lack adequate excess energy for later
use (storage), they cannot be expected to count one-to-one toward system reliability.
2025 CPIRP App. L at 5. To develop the 2025 CPIRP, Duke used the 2023 Wind ELCC
Study that was approved by the Commission in the 2024 CPIRP Order and engaged
PowerGEM to conduct the 2025 Solar and Storage ELCC Study. Id.
In addition to planning for an appropriate PRM and incorporating the appropriate
ELCC for renewable resources and storage, Duke performs a reliability verification step
to test the ability of multiple portfolios to reliably serve future loads in the face of key
uncertainties. This modeling step uses the same SERVM model underpinning the ELCC
and resource adequacy steps and tests the LOLE of the portfolios across various
scenarios in years 2035 and 2040. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 2 at 5, 23. If a portfolio does not meet
the 0.165 LOLE target, additional firm capacity (CTs) are added into the model portfolio
to maintain system reliability. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 2 at 23; ch. 3 at 16. For the 2025 CPIRP,
the reliability verification confirmed that the Duke Recommended Portfolio, Preliminary
Base Portfolio, Moderate Development Load, and Accelerated Renewables Portfolios
satisfied the LOLE target without the need for additional resources, while the Low Battery
Cost and High Solar and Storage Availability Portfolios each required the addition of
reliability resources to satisfy the target in 2035. By 2040, however, given the additional
59

capacity and energy resources identified in the capacity expansion modeling, each of the
portfolios was deemed reliable as developed. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 3 at 16, 33.
Duke witness Holeman testified to Duke’s federal- and state-mandated reliability
obligations, and the importance of increased system margin; more diverse resource, grid
and operating options; and increased coordination between impacting parties in
addressing reliability risks identified by the North American Electric Reliability Corporation
(NERC). Tr. vol. 7, 111–12, 121–22. Qualitatively, He testified that Duke’s Recommended
Portfolio plans for the diverse mix of resources necessary to maintain reliability as Duke
plans to meet growing customer demand and replace critical grid capabilities before
retiring aging generation. Id. at 139.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
Public Staff’s System Reliability and Operations Panel, consisting of witnesses
Thomas and Ssengonzi, found Duke’s reliability measures to be reasonable for use in the
2025 CPIRP. They noted that ensuring system reliability is important both to comply with
the statutory mandate to maintain or improve reliability and to ensure that North Carolina
customers have access to reliable and affordable electricity. Tr. vol. 16, 232–33.
Public Staff’ witnesses Thomas and Ssengonzi supported Duke’s continued use
of the 22% reserve margin. They noted that the reserve margin is a critical input to
capacity expansion modeling and can have significant impact on the determination of
needed capacity. Witnesses Thomas and Ssengonzi recommended that Duke’s planned
2026 resource adequacy study and 2027 CPIRP should comprehensively address how
neighboring balancing authority (BA) assistance, coal retirements, grid operations and
maintenance spending, and the business merger of DEC and DEP will impact future
reserve margin calculations. Id. at 229–30, 235–43.
Witnesses Thomas and Ssengonzi further noted that the ability of resources to
contribute to the reserve margin is critical to ensuring long-term system reliability.
Accordingly, they recommended that Duke perform updated ELCC studies for solar,
batteries, and wind in advance of the 2027 CPIRP. According to witnesses Thomas and
Ssengonzi, Duke should also evaluate assigning ELCC values to coal resources given
the planned retirement schedules and the impact coal unit reliability has on the reserve
margin. Finally, they recommended that the ELCC study for battery storage should
evaluate longer-duration batteries (8-hour) to differentiate from shorter duration batteries.
Id. at 230, 282.
With respect to the reliability verification process, witnesses Thomas and
Ssengonzi acknowledged that the reliability verification process is an important
methodological step in portfolio development to ensure system reliability in a variety of
system conditions, complementing other reliability assurance processes. While they
found Duke’s current reliability verification process to be reasonable, witnesses Thomas
and Ssengonzi recommended that Duke should work to ensure closer alignment between
system dispatch inputs from PowerGEM’s SERVM tool and EnCompass in support of its
