resource adequacy study, ELCC studies, and reliability verification process. Id. at 230,
60

282. In addition, Public Staff witness Michna requested that Duke run the SERVM
reliability verification on at least the Public Staff Recommended Portfolio and Public Staff
1U Recommended Portfolio and that Duke provide the results in its rebuttal testimony.
Tr. vol. 13, 96–97.
At the hearing, Public Staff witnesses Michna and Metz provided new testimony
responding to Duke witness Stillman’s rebuttal analysis demonstrating that the continued
economic development activity has grown the load forecast above the High Load
sensitivity. Id. at 10. Witness Michna testified that the Public Staff has concerns of
reliability degradation due to the updated load forecast, noting that Duke’s High Load
Portfolio, which shows additional generation in the coming years beyond the Duke
Recommended NTAP, does not meet the 0.165 LOLE standard of the reliability
verification step. According to witness Michna, the High Load Portfolio fails Duke reliability
screening and does not maintain or improve reliability. Witness Michna testified that the
Public Staff see an emergent and growing threat to the system presented by the continued
addition of new load. Id. at 12–13.
Witness Metz recommended that the Commission implement safeguards to
ensure system reliability is maintained while large load integration efforts continue in a
reasonable and controlled manner. Specifically, witness Metz recommended that the
Commission put in place guardrails to ensure that the interconnection of large load on too
rapid a scale does not negatively impact reliability. For example, witness Metz stated that
the Commission could implement a short-term pause of at least one year in assigning
additional loads exceeding 100–200 MW. Alternately, witness Metz suggested that the
Commission could put in place a requirement that ESAs for all loads exceeding 100–200
MW be approved by the Commission prior to execution. Id. at 14–16.
Intervenor Direct Testimony
Intervenors addressed a variety of aspects regarding Duke’s reliability modeling.
AGO witness Burgess testified that Duke does not apply ELCC consistently across
resources, which creates a double standard favoring natural gas. Tr. vol. 22, 121.
According to witness Burgess, Duke determines the capacity value of solar, wind, and
battery storage using probabilistic analysis, but applies a much more favorable unplanned
outage rate assumption for natural gas resources. Id. at 119–20. Natural gas CTs and
CCs have lower reliability characteristics in probabilistic resource adequacy studies and
in response to specific reliability events such as large winter storms. For example, the
Pennsylvania-New Jersey-Maryland Interconnection regional transmission organization
(PJM) performs an ELCC analysis for all resource types. Id. at 120. During Winter Storm
Elliott, approximately 60,000 MW of unplanned generation outages were reported, with
76% of the capacity lost due to non-fuel-related issues. Id. at 121.
NC WARN and SACE et al. also raised issues related to the ELCC modeling.
NC WARN witness Powers testified that Duke’s ELCC Study should be supplemented
with an independent assessment of the cost and scope of solar paired with storage
(SPSW) resources needed to fully meet new demand. Id. at 187. He specified that the
independent study should not be funded by Duke. Id. SACE et al. witness Goggin testified
61

that the results of the 2025 study indicate that combining solar and storage can increase
the ELCC and yield greater benefits than solar in isolation. Id. at 295–97.
CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry recommended two enhancements to address power
quality in resource planning. First, Duke should incorporate the "value of lost production"
metric into modeling so the Commission can determine whether a least-cost capacity plan
is actually more expensive when broader economic damage is accounted for. Tr. vol. 20,
18. Second, Duke should explicitly model the capital and operating costs of standalone
mitigation equipment (e.g., synchronous condensers, grid-forming inverters) needed to
maintain grid stability as the resource mix shifts from synchronous to inverter-based
generation. Id.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
In rebuttal, Duke witness O’Connor testified that Duke ran the SERVM reliability
verification on the Public Staff’s portfolios as requested by Public Staff witness Michna.
Tr. vol. 23, 239. Duke ran the analysis on the two portfolios referenced by witness Michna
for the test years 2035 and 2040. Witness O’Connor’s Table 2 below presents the results
of that analysis:

Witness O’Connor explained that both Public Staff portfolios significantly exceeded
the 0.165 LOLE reliability threshold in the 2035 test year and therefore would not
adequately maintain system reliability. Duke then ran scenarios adding reliability CTs
back into the Public Staff portfolios to identify the magnitude of dispatchable resource
additions necessary to achieve operational reliability. Id.
According to witness O’Connor, the Public Staff’s heavy reliance on curtailment of
large load customers contributed to the poor LOLE results achieved by its portfolios.
Witness O’Connor noted that the Public Staff assumes an ELCC of 100% for large load
curtailments. Within the EnCompass model, this implies that 1 MW of curtailment is
equivalent to 1 MW of firm capacity in reliability contributions, which results in the deferral
of approximately 2,500 MW of CTs and CCs. Id. at 240. Witness O’Connor explained that
the reliability verification results demonstrate that large load curtailments do not have an
effective ELCC equivalent to that of thermal generators. While Duke has not conducted
a full study to precisely determine an ELCC for large load curtailments, the need to add
back approximately 1,700 MW of firm capacity (equivalent to four reliability CTs) in
2035 strongly indicates that the capacity value of 2,400 MW of curtailment is less than
50% for the Public Staff portfolios in 2035. Id. at 241.
62

Witness O’Connor also responded to the reliability recommendations presented by
Public Staff witnesses Thomas and Ssengonzi. In response to the Public Staff’s
recommendation to more closely align the inputs to the EnCompass and SERVM models,
witness O’Connor stated that Duke generally strives to align inputs as closely as possible.
Witness O’Connor reported that Duke has now aligned the SERVM inputs with the
EnCompass inputs and found no material changes in system reliability when the unit fuel
prices and heat rates were corrected. Id. at 245.
Addressing the Public Staff’s recommendations regarding planned resource
adequacy study and additional ELCC studies for the 2027 CPIRP proceeding, witness
O’Connor agreed that the 2027 proceeding is the appropriate time to revisit the reliability
inputs to the EnCompass model to incorporate operational changes, including the
combination of DEP and DEC, an evolving resource mix with growing contributions from
energy-limited or variable resources, and changes to neighbor capabilities, among other
things. Id. at 246–47. Witness O’Connor stated that Duke plans to assess the utility of
revised ELCC analyses along with the recommendation of the Public Staff on priority
areas of focus and sensitivity analysis for any ELCC studies. For context, witness
O’Connor noted that the 2025 Solar and Storage ELCC study involved more than
3.5 million individual simulations of 8,760 hourly dispatch. Similarly, witness O’Connor
stated that Duke is considering exploring the impact of coal unit performance on the PRM.
However, at this time, Duke asserts that specific study directives would be overly
prescriptive and risk mis-prioritizing limited computational and analytical bandwidth in
advance of the 2027 CPIRP. Id. at 248–49.
Duke witness Holeman reaffirmed the Reliability and Operational Resilience
Panel’s support for the Duke Recommended NTAP and emphasized Duke’s obligation to
plan for and serve customers’ needs dynamically in real time at all hours of the day.
Id. at 219. Witness Holeman generally commented that a benefit of the unprecedented
load growth is the ability to develop a larger, diverse, and resilient generating system with
newer resources. Id. Duke’s Recommended NTAP supports a larger system that is more
reliable and stronger, positioning the Carolinas for success for decades to come.
Id. at 219–20. Witness Holeman noted that the capabilities and characteristics of the new
system that will result from the Duke Recommended NTAP will further enhance the
flexibility and optionality of Duke’s generation fleet. Id. at 220. According to witness
Holeman, if something were to change and economic growth slowed, Duke would be
well-positioned to respond by relying less upon older, less efficient generating units,
including the option to accelerate unit retirements. Id. Witness Holeman also discussed
NERC’s Large Load Action Plan and how Duke monitors the ongoing NERC rulemaking
process that is establishing reliability guidelines for operations and resource adequacy.
Id. at 221. Witness Holeman also testified to the operating capabilities and characteristics
of different generating resources and how Duke’s existing resource mix and the Duke
Recommended NTAP include a balanced and diverse set of resources to ensure the
provision of essential reliability capabilities. Id. at 234. The Reliability and Operational
Resilience Panel disagreed with Public Staff and intervenor claims that Duke should rely
more heavily on curtailment provisions in new large load ESAs in lieu of additional new
dispatchable generation. Id. at 230. Witness Holeman testified that curtailment provisions
are a helpful tool in the system operator’s toolbox but should be left in reserve as they are
63

not a one: one replacement for generation and do not contribute to energy adequacy.
Id. at 231–32. Witness Holeman explained that Duke’s planning and operational reliability
principles are reflected in Duke’s Recommended NTAP and that it is built upon a
foundation of a balanced portfolio of resources that will enable Duke to meet not only
growing customer load, but also grow into the Commission-approved 22% planning
reserve margin. Id. at 222. Witness Holeman reaffirmed the Reliability and Operational
Resilience Panel’s conclusion that Duke’s Recommended Portfolio and NTAP contain the
right balance of resources and operating resources to reliably serve customer needs all
hours of the day, all days of the year, under dynamic load and operating conditions.
Id. at 251–52.
At the evidentiary hearing, Duke witnesses Tate, Snider, and O’Connor responded
to the Public Staff’s new testimony raising reliability concerns related to the continued
large load growth. As a threshold matter, witness Tate took issue with the Public Staff’s
suggestion that Duke acted recklessly in signing large load ESAs. Witness Tate stated
that Duke already has effective guardrails in place to ensure that large-load growth and
system reliability are not in conflict with each other. Those guardrails include a new large
load queue process with a rigorous set of intake requirements, transmission infrastructure
evaluations, and generation capacity evaluations. Tr. vol. 20, 562–64. Witness Snider
explained that the large load queue process is managed by a cross-functional team of
Duke subject matter experts, including transmission planners, grid operators, generation
planners (witness Snider or someone from his team), load forecasters (witness Stillman),
economic development team (witness Tate), and others. Tr. vol. 24, 101. Witness Snider
described a structured, serial process where new customers enter the queue and
progress through increasingly stringent requirements. Through this process, Duke
evaluates when it can have transmission and generation available to serve that customer.
The team also reviews the impact of each project on an ELCC-adjusted basis to see how
large load curtailments can provide real time operational reserves. Id. at 101–02.
According to witness Snider, the process allows for careful monitoring of the large load
queue on a regular basis by a broad cross-functional team with a particular focus on
transmission and generation reliability. Id. at 102.
Witness O’Connor addressed witness Michna’s concern that the High Load
Portfolio did not meet the 0.165 LOLE reliability verification standard in 2035. Witness
O’Connor acknowledged that the High Load Portfolio exceeded the 0.165 LOLE reliability
threshold but explained that when the model considered the large load ESA curtailments
as operational tools to ensure reliability as Duke intended, the LOLE of the High Load
Portfolio was just slightly above the 0.165 standard at 0.182 LOLE. Tr. vol. 8, 61; Public
Staff Cross Reliability Direct Ex. 1. Witness O’Connor explained that the ESA curtailment
sensitivity used the ESA curtailment provisions as a mechanism for maintaining
operational reliability (but not as a mechanism for deferring generation). According to
witness O’Connor, the curtailment provisions bring the High Load Portfolio “very close to
reliability” indicating that, if held in reserve, these curtailment provisions serve as a critical
reliability tool with higher system demand during extreme weather events. Id. Witness
O’Connor further stated that, even though Duke did not run the reliability verification step
on the Updated 2026 Advancing Development Load Forecast, the results from the High
Load Portfolio are sufficient because it shows the directional need. Tr. vol. 23, 304–05. In
64

addition, witness Snider explained that Duke has “ample time” to “check and adjust” in
the 2027 CPIRP for any incremental resources that may be needed to serve expected
load. Tr. vol. 25, 100.
Duke witness Tate opposed Public Staff witness Metz’s new recommendation that
the Commission impose a moratorium on new large load ESAs or require Commission
approval before signing. According to witness Tate, a moratorium would be an extreme
overreaction that would have significant consequences for the State; it would signal to the
economic development community that North Carolina is closed for business, and it would
have an immediate and disruptive effect on large-load projects that are considering
North Carolina today. Tr. vol. 21, 564–65. Witness Tate further noted that many local
communities are actively seeking new large load projects as these customers can increase
the tax base, create new jobs, and make impactful contributions to the community and a
moratorium would obviously prevent such benefits. Id. at 564; tr. vol. 9, 81–82.
Finally, addressing power quality issues, Duke witness Holeman affirmed that
Duke “care[s] deeply about the power quality experience of our customers from a home
to a large industrial customer.” Tr. vol. 8, 114. He noted that Duke has a power quality
team that works with large industrial customers to address power quality issues. Witness
Holeman further explained that the SAIDI metric, which reflects the average duration of
interruption, and SAIFI, which reflects the frequency of the interruption, are key
performance indicators for Duke as Duke seeks to meet its obligation to serve its
customers. However, he explained, an integrated resource plan like the CPIRP
necessarily evaluates the system at a higher level than individual customer experience.
Witness Holeman explained that the CPIRP plans for system-wide reliability, which sets
the stage for reliable service at the customer level, but the CPIRP is not the appropriate
forum in which to evaluate power quality at the customer level. Id. at 113–16. Duke
witness O’Connor similarly explained that the resource adequacy and reliability
verification evaluations performed in developing the CPIRP are focused on ensuring
reliability of the bulk electric system and not power quality for individual customers.
Witness O’Connor acknowledged that the two concepts are inter-related as system
reliability is the foundation of customer reliability. Tr. vol. 24, 27–28.
Discussion and Conclusions
Consistent with the 2024 CPIRP Order, the Commission concludes that it is
reasonable for planning purposes for Duke to build towards a 22% planning reserve
margin by 2031, while filing a new resource adequacy study with the Commission in its
2027 CPIRP application. With respect to Duke’s planned resource adequacy study, the
Commission concludes that it is reasonable for Duke to consider the Public Staff's
recommendations when establishing the parameters of that study, including: (1) how to
more closely align system dispatch inputs between SERMV and EnCompass; and (2) the
manner in which neighboring BA assistance, coal retirements, grid operations and
maintenance spending, and the business combination of DEC and DEP are assessed.
The Commission also concludes that Duke should consider the Public Staff's
recommendations in determining whether to pursue additional ELCC studies for solar,
battery storage, and wind resources, as well as the Public Staff's suggestion that Duke
65

evaluate assigning ELCC values to coal resources and differentiate longer-duration
battery storage before the 2027 CPIRP is filed. The Commission notes that Duke has
represented its willingness to assess the usefulness of revised ELCC analyses in light of
the Public Staff's priorities and available analytical resources and expects Duke to
continue that collaborative evaluation.
The Commission’s conclusions regarding Duke’s future resource-adequacy and
reliability studies are also informed by the disagreement between Duke and the Public
Staff concerning Duke’s application of the SERVM reliability verification process to its
updated load forecast. That disagreement is reflected in Duke’s response to a Public Staff
data request concerning the reliability verification analysis performed on the High Load
Portfolio against the High Load Forecast. Duke provided the following information in
response to a data request from the Public Staff about the reliability verification analysis
run on the high load portfolio against the high load forecast 9. Duke stated in its response
that this analysis served as a “proxy for portfolio reliability under the current load forecast”.
The pertinent results were:
The Companies have indicated in rebuttal testimony that the load forecast
update generally tracks the High Load sensitivity modeled in the 2025
CPIRP, and as such the High Load Portfolio, which includes all of the
Recommended NTAP (with additional incremental resources beyond the
recommended NTAP which can be assessed at the latest in the next
CPIRP) can serve as a proxy for portfolio reliability under the current load
forecast. The SERVM reliability verification analysis on the High Load
portfolio and the accompanying SERVM result files are published in
Datasite with this response. The reliability verification LOLE results for the
High Load portfolio against the High load forecast runs are summarized below:
1.
