3.
4.

2035 (as developed): 0.304
2035 (as developed) + ESA Curtailment: 0.182
2035 (as developed) + 2 CTs of Firm Capacity: 0.131
2040 (as developed): 0.109

Duke ran the standard reliability verification process for 2035 and 2040, and
additional sensitivities on 2035 including the use of ESA curtailment
provisions as a mechanism for maintaining operational reliability (but not as
a mechanism for deferring generation). In the 2035 test year, the portfolio
initially falls above the 0.165 reliability verification threshold without
additional modifications. Deployed in isolation, the curtailment provisions
bring the High Load portfolio to near-reliable levels (0.182 LOLE) indicating
that, if held in reserve for circumstances such as this, that curtailment
serves as a critical reliability tool with higher system demand during extreme
weather events. This is consistent with the Companies' position regarding
9 This exhibit was marked confidential. However, counsel for the Public Staff stated during the

hearing that Duke had removed the confidentiality designation for this exhibit. The Commission treats the
exhibit accordingly. Tr. vol. 8, 56.

66

the appropriate use of ESA curtailments in a planning context, as discussed
in the rebuttal testimony of the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel and the
Reliability and Operational Resilience Panel. In general, without
consideration of ESA curtailments, the reliability verification process would
suggest approximately 2 CTs worth of firm capacity would be necessary to
bring the High Load portfolio at or below the reliability threshold in 2035.
Tr. Ex. vol. 8, 129–130, Public Staff Cross Reliability Direct Exhibit 1, 1–2.
The Commission also notes the following exchange during the cross examination
of Duke witness O’Connor by the Public Staff:
Q. Okay. But the bottom line is that that imperative SERVM modeling that
you testify is required and that you performed on the Public Staff's
NTAP, you have not performed on your own NTAP under your most
current load forecast, you have not?
A. We have not run SERVM on that new load forecast.
Tr. vol. 23, 303–04.
The Commission is concerned with Duke’s approach in presenting the SERVM
results. However, the question before the Commission is the reasonableness of the NTAP
in light of the SERVM results. The Commission generally agrees with Duke witness
Snider’s characterization of this challenge: the reliability shortfall in 2035 is relatively small
in relation to Duke’s overall system, and Duke has time to check and adjust in the next
CPIRP cycle. See tr. vol. 25, 100. The Commission also recognizes that Duke’s proposed
NTAP includes resources that Duke considers necessary to support reliable system
operation and move the Companies towards the Commission approved 22% planning
reserve margin. Thus, the resources in the NTAP, do indeed form the floor for continued
reliable operation of Duke’s system over the planning horizon.
Nevertheless, Duke cannot selectively invoke the 0.165 LOLE standard as a
bright-line measure to criticize the reliability of other resource portfolios while declining to
apply that same standard to its own proposed NTAP using its most up-to-date load forecast.
See tr. vol. 23, 303–04. The Commission also notes Duke’s lack of effort to conduct a
SERVM run between the time of filing its rebuttal testimony in May 2025 and the start of
the CPIRP hearing in June 2025, despite the significant increase in load forecast. Id.
Accordingly, the Commission gives little weight to Duke witness O’Connor’s expert witness
hearing testimony regarding the adequacy of Duke’s proposed NTAP to the extent that
testimony rests on reliability analysis Duke did not perform. Id. However, the missing
SERVM results do not, in the Commission’s view, invalidate Duke’s NTAP as discussed
above. To avoid a similar situation in the next CPIRP proceeding, the Commission is
prepared to require Duke to provide SERVM modeling based on its most up-to-date load
forecast and proposed portfolio at any point – including after the filing of rebuttal
testimony – should Duke fail to provide the relevant analysis as it did in this proceeding.

67

The Commission agrees that addressing the reliability shortfall will require timely
development of the generation resources identified in Duke’s proposed NTAP. The
Commission finds that it is reasonable to require Duke to provide a detailed explanation
of its generation-capacity evaluation process that underlies the execution of the ESAs in
its 2027 CPIRP Application. The Commission agrees with Public Staff witness Metz that
the pace at which Duke has executed ESAs could have serious consequences for system
reliability. However, Duke remains solely responsible for maintaining system reliability
under N.C.G.S. § 62-2 and must comply with federally mandated reliability standards
developed and enforced by NERC. Nothing in the record suggests that Duke views the
execution of ESAs as relieving it of those responsibilities. Furthermore, the Commission
has regulatory tools available to address circumstances in which Duke’s load
commitments or resource decisions fail to support reliable service under the
performance-based regulation framework. Accordingly, the Commission declines to
impose the moratorium recommended by witness Metz.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDING OF FACT NO. 22
Retirement of Coal-Fired Generating Units
The evidence supporting this finding of fact is contained in Chapter 4 and
Appendices C and H of the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Coal
Generation and Supply Panel (Jeffrey Flanagan and John Verderame); the testimony and
exhibits of Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel (Snider, Quinto, and Beatty); the
testimony and exhibits of Public Staff’s Thermal Resources Panel (Lawrence, Michna,
and Nader); the testimony and exhibits of AGO witness Burgess; the testimony and
exhibits of NCSEA witness Glick; the testimony and exhibits of SACE, et al. witness
Roumpani; and the entire record.
Appendix F
In Appendix F, Duke indicated that its coal fleet has served customers across the
Carolinas for decades. In response to growing demand, Duke stated that it has carefully
assessed targeted opportunities to extend the operation of certain coal units beyond the
coal retirement schedule approved in the 2023 Carolinas Resource Plan in a manner that
seeks to maintain resource adequacy and enable a disciplined and strategic transition to
new generation. While the supply chain and operational risks Duke described will
necessarily increase during any extension of coal unit operations, Duke determined it was
a prudent step to evaluate targeted extensions of planned unit retirement dates and
continue to define mitigation actions necessary to manage the increased risks. 2025
CPIRP App. F at 7. The analytical process conducted by Duke to assess coal retirements
is consistent with the process used in previous resource plans, and is reflected below in
Figure F-3 presented Appendix F.

68

2025 CPIRP App. F at 9. Economic coal retirements were modeled against the Moderate
Development Load Forecast and the Advancing Development Load Forecast. Duke
selected the optimal retirement dates after considering the modeling results, as well as
other planning factors. Id. at 11. Table F-2, from Appendix F, excerpted below, presents
the updated coal retirement analysis.

2025 CPIRP at App. F at 12.

69

Duke noted that as they consider extending the operation of certain coal plants,
they must weigh necessary investments in aging facilities to maintain the reliable
operation of each plant against planned retirement dates. Duke identified the following
categories of investments that will be necessary to ensure the reliable operation of the
coal units, consistent with the updated coal retirement analysis, including major
components, operational reliability improvements, repair holds, winterization operations
and maintenance, and staffing/workforce. Id. at 12–13. Duke has experienced challenges
to fuel supply and security for its coal units but has reportedly developed strategies to
mitigate those challenges as circumstances allow for the time being. Id. at 14.
Duke Direct and Supplemental Direct Testimony
In direct testimony, Duke witnesses Flanagan and Verderame, from the Coal
Generation and Supply Panel, presented the practical challenges associated with the
targeted extensions of certain coal units’ operations reflected in the updated coal
retirement analysis. The Panel emphasized that while Duke’s coal units have played a
central role in serving customers reliably for, on average, nearly 50 years and will continue
to provide support to the electric system through the end of their operations, the dynamics
of the coal industry present real and significant challenges to Duke’s operation of these
units and to the balancing of reliability against the risks that the indefinite reliance on coal
units pose. Tr. vol. 10, 84–85.
In the Coal Generation and Supply Panel direct testimony, witness Verderame
explained that as the Senior Vice President, Fuels & Systems Optimization for Duke
Corporation, he oversees all aspects of the overall strategic direction and commercial
management of the purchase, delivery, and storage of fossil fuels that the Duke’s
regulated utilities use for the generation of electricity. Id. at 81. He described how current
coal production dynamics impact fuel security for Duke’s fleet and noted that Duke’s coal
units are designed to burn significant concentrations of Central Appalachian (CAPP)
thermal coal. Id. at 89. Witness Verderame further stated that Duke’s coal demand
currently accounts for roughly half of all CAPP production, which presents a fuel security
risk in its own right. Id. He indicated that the limited number of CAPP coal producers
presents risk to fuel security and market price and explained that as domestic demand for
coal has fallen, production economies of scale have declined, which, combined with rising
inflation, has increased production costs both now and forecasted into the future. Id.
Witness Verderame stated that separate from the impacts of commodity price
volatility, coal producers are contending with structural shifts in investment capital
allocations away from the production of coal as well as declining workforce, threatening
the long-term viability of domestic thermal coal production. He also described the
challenges associated with transporting coal to Duke’s units and indicated that when
viewed comprehensively, the magnitude of volatility in demand for coal continues to be
larger than the coal supply chain can effectively withstand. Id. at 91. Witness Verderame
concluded that Duke expects that degree of volatility to continue. Id.
In the Coal Generation and Supply Panel direct testimony, witness Flanagan, the
General Manager III of the Carolinas Dispatchable Generation — West Zone, described
70

the operational realities of Duke’s coal fleet. He stated that, in his current role, he provides
strategic direction and leadership to station general managers, including day-to-day
operations, business analysis, process development, O&M and capital budget allocation
and implementation, and outage performance. Id. at 83. Thus, witness Flanagan is
reportedly familiar and experienced with the realities of operating the coal fleet under
present conditions and described two challenges in particular: workforce and
parts/equipment. Id. at 93.
He stated that Duke is challenged to maintain a qualified and competent internal
workforce to run the coal fleet and to access a competent external workforce to perform
specialized maintenance and repairs. Id. However, witness Flanagan noted that given the
age of the coal fleet, it is increasingly difficult for Duke to obtain replacement parts and
equipment, as many original equipment manufacturers are no longer in business and/or
that the parts and equipment needed in the coal units have become obsolete. He stated
that materials that Duke could previously secure in days can now take weeks, months, or
even longer to obtain. Witness Flanagan indicated that where Duke was once able to
source parts and equipment competitively from across a number of vendors, Duke no
longer has options. He noted that, in some instances, Duke must resort to looking for
parts and equipment on eBay or other electronic marketplaces as the number of
traditional vendors has dwindled. Id. at 94–95.
The Coal Generation and Supply Panel explained that, these challenges
notwithstanding, Duke carefully assessed targeted opportunities to extend the operations
of certain coal units beyond the coal retirement schedule previously approved by the
Commission, primarily to accommodate projected load growth. They explained that the
strategic extension of the operations of these specific units aims to maintain resource
adequacy and enable a disciplined and strategic transition to new generation while
mitigating the risks and potential customer costs of planning to run a significant
percentage of Duke’s coal generation fleet indefinitely. Id. at 97.
Furthermore, the Coal Generation and Supply Panel explained that additional
investment will be critical for the units’ continued reliability to continue to operate the coal
units beyond the currently proposed retirement extensions, and that level of investment
will likely be materially higher when contemplating extended long-term operation of a unit
versus preparing to retire a unit in the near term. They also presented strategies
developed for the short term mitigation of risk to fuel security and operational reliability.
Id. at 102–04.
Witness Flanagan’s supplemental direct testimony responds to certain requests
for information identified by the Commission in its Scheduling Order. He indicated that
while Duke is forecasting the addition of large load customers that are projected to have
higher load factors and a resulting reduction in reserve margin in the shoulder months
over time, Duke’s projections of the overall system continue to show that there will be
sufficient margin on the system, including in the shoulder periods, to perform the
necessary maintenance on Duke’s generation facilities, particularly as Duke adds new
generation resources and builds towards the 22% planning reserve margin approved by
the Commission. Id. at 107. Witness Flanagan explained that, as has always been the
71

case, Duke will continue to proactively schedule needed generation maintenance through
their outage optimization scheduling practices, which is informed by a range of
considerations and ensures sufficient generation capacity is available at all times to
provide reliability for customers. He also explained that Duke will continue to take steps
wherever reasonable and in customers’ best interests to extend the run cycles of the
plants (i.e., to extend the period between planned maintenance outages) and to reduce
the duration of planned maintenance outages. Id.
At the expert witness hearing and in response to a question from Chairman
Brawley, witness Verderame testified that every time Duke extends the retirement date
of a coal unit, there is a recognition of moving across a “continuum of risk” but that Duke
is comfortable with where the updated coal retirement analysis lands on that continuum.
Id. at 132–33. In response to a question from Commissioner Tucker regarding new
coal-fired generating facilities being constructed in Alaska and West Virginia, witness
Verderame testified that the project in Alaska is being developed as a result of challenges
associated with transporting natural gas into Alaska. He also testified that the projects are
mine mouth projects, which address fuel cost volatility and fuel security, and he
distinguished these projects from North Carolina’s resource needs. Id. at 135–36.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
The Public Staff did not object to Duke’s approach to coal unit retirement planning
in this proceeding. Tr. vol. 18, 27. In joint direct testimony, the Public Staff Thermal
Resources Panel recognized that Duke’s plan to retire coal fired generation units “aims
to balance the need for newer generation with the cost and reliability associated with
retiring eight GW of older coal generation, all while accommodating projected load
growth.” Id. at 20. Ultimately, they concluded that the “Companies’ proposed coal unit
retirement dates and planning cycle are reasonable for planning purposes for this
CPIRP.” Id. at 33.
The Thermal Resources Panel also recommended that Duke provide, in rebuttal
testimony, the “exact date that a point of no return decision has to be made to retire each
coal unit and the timeline required to make an orderly transition.” Id. at 33–34. Lastly, the
Panel indicated that the coal retirement process should be flexible given planning and
operational dynamics. Id. at 22.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
In direct testimony, SACE, et al. witness Roumpani took issue with the targeted
extensions of the retirement dates for certain units and argued that the extensions do not
align with the operational characteristics of the units. Tr. vol. 20, 339–40. She also took
issue with the investments made in the coal fleet relative to the capacity factors of those
units. Id. at 348–50.
In AGO witness Burgess’ direct testimony, he expressed concerns with the coal
unit retirement dates, particularly Mayo. Tr. vol. 22, 97–98.

72

In NCSEA witness Glick’s direct testimony, she criticized the fact that Duke locked
the retirement dates from the updated coal retirement analysis in EnCompass, alleging
that the retirement dates were not optimized by the model as a result. Tr. vol. 17, 142.
Duke’s Rebuttal Testimony
In prefiled rebuttal testimony, the Coal Generation and Supply Panel confirmed its
support for Duke’s coal retirement plans and the Duke Recommended NTAP, suggesting
that both were reasonable and executable plans for replacing less-efficient legacy
generation with highly-efficient dispatchable generation designed to reliably serve
customers. Tr. vol. 26, 111–13. The panel stated that Duke’s plans appropriately balance
the objectives of cost, reliability, commodity price risk, and diversity of generation supply.
Id. at 112. The panel emphasized that Duke’s proposed coal retirement schedule
mitigates the risk of continued coal operations while providing flexibility to adjust should
circumstances change. Id. They indicated that although Duke has presented substantial
evidence in support of their unprecedented load forecasts in this proceeding, should
actual customer load develop at a slower pace, Duke has the optionality, with
Commission approval, to accelerate coal unit retirements to serve customer needs in a
more cost-effective manner. Id. at 112–13.
The Coal Generation and Supply Panel explained that while the Public Staff
requested the “exact date that a point of no return decision has to be made to retire each
coal unit and the timeline required to make an orderly transition[,]” the coal retirement
analysis performed by Duke is intended to provide the appropriate level of detail for a
planning proceeding to assure the Commission that Duke is working toward an orderly
transition. Id. at 113. Furthermore, the panel stated that the Public Staff’s request for an
exact “point of no return” date for each coal generating unit is not possible as it seeks to
tie Duke to a determination that requires near perfect foresight of the numerous
unpredictable factors that must be assumed. The panel indicated that, from an operations
standpoint, dynamic factors such as labor, availability and cost of operational parts and
maintenance, and the fuel supply chain all require flexibility in evaluating retirement
decisions. The panel noted that, most importantly, an operational “point of no return”
cannot be established absent an evaluation of changes in load forecasts or in the timeline
for the development of new resources in order to ensure system reliability. Id. at 114.
On this point, the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel, in their rebuttal testimony,
stated that there are numerous, difficult-to-predict factors that make it impossible for Duke
to specify a definitive “point of no return” for retiring coal generation assets and that the
retirement dates in the updated coal retirement analysis under the Advancing
Development scenario are the most definitive retirement dates Duke can provide at this
time based on the detailed planning analysis conducted in the 2025 CPIRP. Tr. vol. 24,
207. The panel suggested that the Commission’s key takeaway should be that through
the Duke Recommended NTAP, Duke has appropriately planned for and balanced the
reliability, cost, and execution risk of adding the system resources necessary to meet load
growth, maintain reliability, and allow for the orderly retirement of Duke’s coal fleet. Id.

73

Furthermore, as to NCSEA witness Glick’s concerns regarding using the dates
from the updated coal retirement analysis in the EnCompass modeling, the Coal
Generation and Supply Panel stated in their rebuttal that Duke’s coal retirement schedule,
and the targeted extension plans for the Belews Creek, Marshall, and Cliffside units, were
developed to carefully strike the appropriate balance between ensuring resource
adequacy to serve anticipated current and future customers while managing the risks
posed by extending operations of these coal units. Tr. vol. 26, 121–22. The panel
indicated that, from an operational expertise standpoint, Duke has developed over 50 or
more years of operating these assets and has discussed at length the challenges that the
coal industry faces in general, as well as how those challenges specifically impact Duke.
Id. at 122. They further explained that the decision to extend the retirement timelines for
the Belews Creek, Marshall, and Cliffside units was not made to prolong their operations
and thereby preclude any specific resource addition, nor was it made to simply fit with
pre-determined replacement generation timelines without regard to holistic resource
planning considerations. Instead, they argued that the reasonable decision to extend the
retirement dates for the targeted coal units was only made after careful evaluation of the
economic and operational risks and benefits to customers and the system needs. Id.
Finally, with respect to the level of investment Duke must make in the coal units,
the Coal Generation and Supply Panel stated that Duke is mindful of potential concerns
about the level of investment in the coal generation fleet but continues to strike an
appropriate balance between maintaining reliability and minimizing customer cost
increases. Id. at 117. The panel indicated that, from an operational standpoint, Duke’s
coal fleet continues to age and investments are needed to maintain reliability because
these assets remain critical to the reliability of the system, as was demonstrated in recent
extreme weather events in the Carolinas. Id.
In prefiled rebuttal testimony, the Coal Generation and Supply Panel stated that
Duke recognizes that the capacity factors of their coal fleets are declining, an endemic
situation across the U.S. and a large driver to the identified fuel supply and operational
risks discussed by the panel. Id. at 120. However, the panel indicated that Duke requires
dispatchable resources, which operate at lower capacity factors, to provide reliable
service to customers in periods of high demand. Id. at 120–21. They explained that if a
needed coal unit were not online, then Duke would have to start additional CTs and/or
purchase capacity to maintain reserve requirements and energy to serve load, assuming
capacity and energy was available. Id. at 121. In addition, the panel noted that Duke’s
coal units are available to provide the firm dispatchable capacity needed to meet day
ahead planning reserve margins as well as the real time energy needed to support record
customer demand during periods, such as the January 27, 2026, 8:00 a.m. winter peak,
which occurred as solar resources were only just beginning to become available. Id.
The Coal Generation and Supply Panel noted the delicate balance between:
(1) strategically extending the retirement dates of DEC Belews Creek Units 1 and 2,
Marshall Units 3 and 4, and Cliffside Unit 5 in order to serve rapidly growing customer
needs while transitioning to new generation resources; with (2) increasing risk to the
reliable operation of the system. Id. at 112. The Panel also noted Duke’s commitment to
74

continue to reevaluate the best retirement dates for all coal units to ensure that they
reliably and economically serve customer needs. Id.
Discussion and Conclusions
Coal Fleet Outlook
The Commission addresses this issue to avoid any misunderstanding regarding
the condition and maintenance of Duke’s coal fleet. No party in this proceeding has
suggested that the coal fleet has been inadequately maintained. 10 As testified by Public
Staff witness Michna, the record reflects significant depreciation expense associated with
these assets and more than $6 billion in remaining costs to recover across the Marshall,
Belews Creek, Cliffside, Mayo, and Roxboro coal plants:
Q. Are Duke’s coal plants fully depreciated?
A. No. As of its December 31, 2024, depreciation study, Duke has over
$6B of the costs of its coal fleet left to recover, and future
investments will increase that amount and further impact ratepayers.
The below figure details the costs remaining to recovered by plant.

Tr. vol. 18, 31 (emphasis added).
The Commission notes that no party in this proceeding recommended extending
the coal retirement dates beyond the strategic extensions proposed by Duke as evident
by the unrebutted testimony of Duke witness Verderame. Tr. vol. 26, 131–32. The
Commission also notes Duke’s acknowledgment that these strategic extensions are not
intended to permit indefinite operation of the coal units. The Commission is persuaded by
Duke’s and the Public Staff’s contentions that expecting these units to operate until failure
would create serious reliability risks, particularly in light of increasing supply-chain risks
and declining manufacturer and technical support.

While not evidence the Commission relies on for this Order, the Commission notes DEC and
DEP Late-Filed Exhibit No. 3, Application of Duke Energy Progress, LLC and Duke Energy Carolina, LLC
for Adjustment of Rates and Charges Applicable to Electric Service in North Carolina and for
Performance-Based Regulation, Nos. E-2, Sub 1380 and E-7, Sub 1329, at 2 (N.C.U.C. July 14, 2026).
Duke reported approximately $3.4 billion in capital expenditures and $2.4 billion in O&M expenses at its
currently operating coal stations from 2016 through 2025. Capital spending includes CapEx, retirement,
rotable spares, and AFUDC; O&M excludes fuel and fuel-related costs and allowances.
10

75

The Commission further emphasizes the check-and-adjust nature of the
CPIRP cycle as an important tool for revisiting the coal-retirement schedule in future
CPIRP cycles as new facts and circumstances arise.
The Commission is committed to ensuring that Duke maximizes the value of
current coal units for the benefit of customers, while also assessing the material risks
associated with continued operation and maintenance of these units. The coal fleet
provides critical winter peaking capacity on today’s system. The Commission accords
significant weight to Duke’s balancing of risks associated with extending the retirement
dates to ensure system reliability, as well as the Public Staff’s acceptance of the updated
coal retirement analysis as reasonable.
Accordingly, and based on the competent, material, and substantial evidence of
record, the Commission concludes that the strategic extensions of the retirement of
certain coal units in the updated coal retirement analysis are reasonable for planning
purposes.
The Commission recognizes the risks associated with fuel supply and security, as
well as the risks to the reliable operation of these aged units. However, the Commission
is persuaded that, for now, Duke has developed and is executing reasonable strategies
to mitigate those risks. Moreover, the Commission agrees with Duke that the Public Staff’s
request for an exact “point of no return” date for each coal generating unit is not feasible,
as it seeks to tie Duke to a determination that requires near perfect foresight. The
Commission is persuaded by Duke’s expressed need to retain flexibility in light of dynamic
factors that could impact such a determination, such as labor, availability and cost of
operational parts and maintenance, and the fuel supply chain.
Finally, the Commission is persuaded that Duke must continue to make
investments in the coal units and conduct maintenance on the coal units to ensure the
reliable operation of the electric system. The Commission recognizes that these units are
called on to get Duke through the most stressful of grid conditions and that these units
are critical to ensuring the reliable provision of service to customers at this time. Any
issues related to the prudency of these costs will be considered in other proceedings.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 23–26
Planned Combination of DEC and DEP
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Chapters 2 and 4 of
the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Carolinas Utility Operations Panel
(Laura Bateman and Mark Goettsch); the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s IRP and
Near-Term Actions Panel (Beatty, Quinto, and Snider); the testimony and exhibits of
Public Staff witness Michna; and the entire record.
Duke’s Consolidated System Modeling
Chapter 2 of the 2025 CPIRP explains that the planning analytics deployed in the
2025 CPIRP assume the implementation of a Consolidated System Operations (CSO)
76

model where the NERC BA, Transmission Service Provider, and Transmission Operator
functions are consolidated for DEC and DEP. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 2 at 9. In direct testimony,
Duke witness Goettsch stated that this “hybrid approach” allows Duke to assume certain
discrete elements of an integrated system but not an entirely integrated Carolinas 1U
scenario. Tr. vol. 12, 165. The 2025 CPIRP further explains that the CSO approach also
allows for the economic dispatch of the combined system and further allows for
optimization in meeting operating services requirements, such as balancing and
regulating reserves. 2025 CPIRP Ch. at 9. Duke indicated that in the current operations
of the DEC and DEP systems, each utility must meet its own operating requirements with
its own units to meet the system operational needs of its BA area. Id. The CSO model
allows the collective operating requirements to be aggregated at the combined system
level, which Duke suggested improves efficiency by allowing the requirement to be met
by resources from the combined system. Id. Duke noted that the two utilities do, however,
retain responsibility for independently committing resources for meeting forecasted
demand and maintaining long-term capacity planning requirements in the modeling. Id.
In Duke’s last two resource plan proceedings, the Commission encouraged DEC
and DEP to continue to pursue combination of the two currently separate operating
utilities, with a targeted completion date of January 1, 2027. See 2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 32.
As discussed in Appendix C, Duke modeled sensitivities assuming a fully combined utility
(1U) and a scenario where the business combination is not approved (2U), resulting in
DEC and DEP independently planning, executing, and operating in absence of the
combined system benefits. 2025 CPIRP App. C at 10. As witness Goettsch explained in
direct testimony, these modeled sensitivities allow Duke to further quantify projected
savings anticipated from the upcoming merger. Tr. vol. 12, 165–66.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
Public Staff witness Michna stated that the 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke to
provide an information model simulating the impacts of a merged DEC and DEP utility.
Tr. vol. 13, 78. He confirmed that Duke complied with the Commission’s directive and
submitted new datasets into its modeling portfolios that created a single utility BA and
updated the ancillaries (such as grid stability, frequency/voltage control, and spinning
reserves) to allow joint capacity planning and joint commitment of a single utility system.
Id. at 79. Finally, witness Michna recommended that the Commission require Duke to
revise its proposed NTAP as soon as possible once the outcome of the 1U Business
Combination becomes known. Id.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
On May 1, 2026, the Commission approved the Combination of DEC and DEP
effective January 1, 2027, in its Order Accepting Stipulations and Approving Business
Combination, Request for Accounting Order, and Regulatory Conditions and Code of
Conduct (Order Approving Combination) issued in Docket Nos. E-7, Sub 1332 and
E-2, Sub 1383.

77

In their rebuttal testimony, the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel stated that Duke
anticipates conducting additional modeling now that the merger has been approved.
Tr. vol. 24, 212. They indicated that, in the near term, Duke will conduct modeling that
reflects a fully combined DEC and DEP system as part of the 2026 Carolinas Resource
Plan to be filed in South Carolina in August 2026. Id. Consistent with Duke’s
check-and-adjust planning approach, the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel stated that
Duke intends to provide the Public Staff with relevant informational modeling data once
such analysis is available. Id.
In the Order Approving Combination, the Commission noted Duke’s agreement to
remove 200 MW of BESS from the 2025 NTAP in the event the combination received all
necessary regulatory approvals. Order Approving Combination at 12–13. The IRP and
Near-Term Actions Panel confirmed that, as a result of the Combination approval, Duke
is reducing the NTAP to remove 200 MW of BESS resources consistent with the
Combination settlement and the Commission’s Combination Order. Tr. vol. 24, 211.
Discussion and Conclusions
Based upon the competent, material, and substantial evidence of record, the
Commission concludes that Duke complied with the Commission’s directive in the 2024
CPIRP Order to conduct informational modeling to simulate a business combination of
DEC and DEP.
The Commission notes that the Public Staff supported Duke conducting additional
modeling upon final approval of the merger, and because such approval has now been
issued, the Commission concludes it is appropriate to direct Duke to model a fully
combined utility in the 2027 CPIRP.
The Commission further notes that Duke conducted additional modeling to reflect
a fully combined DEC and DEP system as part of its planning process to develop the
2026 Carolinas Resource Plan filed with the Public Service Commission of South Carolina
on August 14, 2026, in Docket Nos. 2026-8-E and 2026-10-E. Accordingly, the
Commission concludes it is appropriate to direct Duke to share relevant results of such
modeling with the Public Staff.
Finally, the Commission concludes that Duke has appropriately reduced its NTAP
target for BESS resources by 200 MW in compliance with the Stipulation and Order
Approving Combination.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 27–29
Grid Edge Resources
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Appendices C and
E to the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke witness Timothy J. Duff; the
testimony and exhibits of Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel (Beatty, Quinto, and
Snider); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff’s Grid Edge Panel (Laura Weaver and
Tommy Williamson, Jr.); and the entire record.
78

Duke Direct Testimony
Witness Duff stated that in Duke’s 2025 CPIRP, Duke refers to “grid edge” as
technologies, programs, and investments that advance a decentralized, distributed, and
two-way grid. Tr. vol. 11, 122. He noted that the “edge” refers to the edge of the electricity
network, or grid, where Duke’s electricity reaches customers’ homes and businesses.
Id. Witness Duff testified that Duke utilizes grid edge and customer programs to “shrink
the challenge” of meeting the increasing demands on the electric system while deploying
increasingly clean energy across the Carolinas. Id. at 128. He further explained that these
programs are intended to help customers manage their energy use and reduce their
electric bills, while simultaneously supporting electric system reliability and flexibility.
Id. at 126–27. In direct testimony, Witness Duff offered support for Duke’s grid edge and
customer programs, explaining that grid edge programs include “dynamic rate designs,
voltage control efforts, and other customer programs, such as DSM and EE programs,
renewable energy programs, electric transportation programs, and voltage optimization”
that allow customers to manage their use of electricity. Id. at 126.
Witness Duff explained that Duke included certain modeling assumptions that
modeled EE as a load modifier, which allows it to be considered before the evaluation
and selection of supply side resources needed to meet load. Id. at 133. He noted that for
purposes of CPIRP modeling, and consistent with the 2024 Carbon Plan Order, Duke
presented a base case assumption of a minimum annual reduction of 1% of eligible load
from EE savings. Id. at 133–34. Witness Duff stated that Duke also provided a low case
forecast that assumed a flat annual achievement of 1.0% of eligible load, excluding IRA
impacts and new forecasted economic development load. Id. He lastly noted that Duke
modeled a high EE sensitivity scenario with energy savings of 1.5% of eligible load. Id.
With respect to eligible load, witness Duff indicated that Duke defined “eligible
load” as load resulting from residential and nonresidential retail customers who participate
in Duke’s EE programs and do not opt out of the DSM/EE rider as permitted by
N.C.G.S. § 62-133.9 and Commission Rule R8-68(d). 11 Id. at 134–35. Witness Duff
explained that, consistent with the direction in the 2024 CPIRP Order, Duke removed
EV load from annual retail sales in the determination of eligible retail sales in all three
EE forecasts and evaluated the impact of such removal. Id. at 134–36. He noted that
removal of EV load remains appropriate because of the “lack of EE opportunities related
to EV load.” Id. Witness Duff also explained that the 2023 market potential study (MPS)
informed both the long-term EE forecasts utilized in this proceeding and the amount of
eligible load needed to achieve the 1% savings floor. Id. at 174–76. He stated that the
MPS is performed every two or three years and involves stakeholder input and reflects a
comprehensive, robust DSM/EE portfolio. Id. Witness Duff indicated that, upon review,
Duke felt that the continued use of the 1% floor is appropriate because, while aggressive,
it represents a reasonable path forward with regard to the long-term EE forecast used in
resource planning. Id. at 136.

11 This term may also be referred to throughout witness Duff’s testimony as “eligible sales.”

79

In assessing the appropriateness of the 1% floor, witness Duff cited certain
challenges to achieving the minimum 1% EE savings floor, noting that “interest rates have
remained elevated, resulting in higher capital costs.” Id. at 138. He also described rising
equipment and material costs, along with continued workforce and trade ally shortages,
as contributing to higher costs and presenting additional challenges in achieving the
1% EE savings floor. Id. However, witness Duff testified that Duke continues to work to
overcome the barriers to achieving the 1% modeling assumption and cited recent
revisions to the DSM/EE Mechanism, along with continued engagement with the DSM/EE
Collaborative, as part of those efforts. Id. at 138–39.
Witness Duff explained that Duke is continually working with the DSM/EE
Collaborative to make its DSM/EE programs more attractive to both residential and
nonresidential customers and has received approval for several significant new program
offerings and enhancements since the prior CPIRP. Id. at 128–33. However, he noted
that the effectiveness of Duke’s DSM/EE programs relies, at the end of the day, on
customer decisions and behavior that are beyond Duke’s direct control. Id. at 139.
Witness Duff also explained that, in accordance with the 2024 CPIRP Order, Duke
modeled a sensitivity that looked at the incremental capacity resource associated with
DR participants in the PowerPair Pilot as a selectable resource. Id. at 140. Witness Duff
noted that the solar component of the PowerPair Pilot is included in the load modification
that is modeled as part of Duke’s net metering forecasts, while the capacity impacts of
battery storage from PowerPair Pilot participants not participating in DR are reflected as
a modification to the system load shape and peaks. Id. Witness Duff noted that the IRP
and Near-Term Actions Panel provided additional details regarding specific modeling
aspects of the PowerPair Pilot program. Id.
Finally, witness Duff indicated that he supports Duke’s request for relief six and
seven detailed in Duke’s Petition, i.e., and that the Commission: (1) approve Duke’s plan
to continue advancing grid edge and Customer Programs and (2) approve and find
reasonable Duke’s continued use of 1% of eligible load annual UEE savings in the CPIRP
modeling as a base assumption and that such target is reasonable and appropriate for
future planning purposes. Id. at 125.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
The Public Staff recommended that the Commission approve Duke’s two requests
for relief. Tr. vol. 21, 191. Specifically, the Public Staff recommended that the Commission
continue to allow Duke to use 1% of eligible load annual EE savings as the annual floor
or minimum load modifier for the CPIRP modeling. Id. In support of this recommendation,
the Public Staff noted that the 1% target aligns with the baseline savings target in the
tiered utility incentive structure under Duke’s most recently approved DSM/EE
Mechanism. Id. at 191–92. Witnesses Williamson and Weaver stated in their direct
testimony that, with recent changes to Duke’s DSM/EE cost-recovery mechanism,
including a new tiered incentive structure and updated values for energy and demand
savings, Duke has sufficient incentive to advance grid edge and customer programs. Id.
80

Concerning the 1% floor, witnesses Williamson and Weaver indicated that it is
appropriate to continue to apply and pursue EE savings associated with the modeling
assumption of 1% of prior year eligible retail sales in the CPIRP. However, they argued
that Duke manipulated the 1% of eligible retail sales floor calculation by removing EV load
from its modeling. Id. at 183. According to witnesses Williamson and Weaver, by
removing EV sales, Duke breaks from its previously-used method for determining eligible
retail sales for the DSM/EE portfolio, which is also used to determine the performance
incentive in both DEC and DEP’s annual rider proceedings starting with Vintage Year
2025. Id. at 183–84. Witnesses Williamson and Weaver contend that the CPIRP should
use the current DSM/EE portfolio, based on the guidelines of the most recently approved
DSM/EE cost recovery mechanisms and influenced by market potential studies, which is
then forecasted throughout the planning horizon. Id. at 184. Furthermore, witnesses
Williamson and Weaver argued that Duke witness Duff’s contention that justification for
removing the EV sales is based on the “lack of EE opportunit[ies]” related to EV load is
not persuasive. Id. at 184–85. For these reasons, witnesses Williamson and Weaver
recommended that in future CPIRP proceedings, Duke provides a UEE forecast that
includes EV sales to remain consistent with EV sales inclusion in the DSM/EE rider
proceedings and consistent with the most recently approved DSM/EE cost recovery
mechanisms. Id. at 188–89.
Witnesses Williamson and Weaver also stated that BTM solar has grown in
popularity since the early 2000s for various reasons, and that BTM storage has grown
more recently as well. Id. at 170. Witnesses Williamson and Weaver discussed the
regulatory and policy changes since the 2023 CPIRP that may affect BTM generation
adoption, including the creation of Duke’s PowerPair pilot program, the reduced federal
tax credits available for residential solar, and Duke’s updating of available net metering
tariff options, which affects the bill credits that customers with BTM resources receive in
exchange for generating their own energy. Id. at 173–74. Witnesses Williamson and
Weaver indicated that Duke was unable to provide the Public Staff with an estimate of
how much BTM storage is connected to the system. Id. at 171. Furthermore, witnesses
Williamson and Weaver contended that Duke’s method of estimating BTM solar was
based on out-of-date data developed in 2015 and did not accurately reflect current
adoption trends in North and South Carolina. Id. at 175. Although the Public Staff
determined that Duke’s filed BTM solar forecast was reasonable for planning purposes in
this proceeding and recommended the Commission’s acceptance thereof, witnesses
Williamson and Weaver recommended that Duke update its BTM solar forecasting
method and consider including observed adoption trends when weighting
location-specific solar production profiles for the 2027 CPIRP. Id. at 170–77.
Lastly, the Public Staff noted that the PowerPair sensitivity consistently selected
PowerPair, demonstrating its potential value and the potential value of a similar program
for non-residential customers. Id. at 179. Witnesses Williamson and Weaver noted that
only incremental additions of BTM storage are included in Duke’s modeling, and unless
customers connect battery storage through PowerPair or a similar program, Duke has
difficulty tracking the amount of BTM storage on the system and forecasting adoption
rates. Id. at 177.
81

Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
Eligible Retail Savings Target of 1%
SACE et al. witness Roumpani provided testimony explaining how Duke is
underutilizing cost-effective demand-side resources like EE, demand response, and load
flexibility. Tr. vol. 20, 383. She emphasized how valuable DSM/EE response programs
are, as they are much faster to build than new generation, have low risk, and incorporate
benefits, such as being scalable as conditions change, improving resilience, and reducing
costs, demand, and emissions. Id. at 384–87. Witness Roumpani stated that Duke’s
analysis demonstrates that the DSM/EE reduce the need for new gas generation and
lower costs for ratepayers, and she recommended that Duke pursue more demand-side
resources. Id. at 387. Witness Roumpani concluded that Duke should have an
EE performance target of 1.5% and should increase EE and DERs. Id. at 395–96.
NCSEA witness Karl R. Rábago stated that given the elimination of federal
incentives and potential for data centers to opt out of EE programs, the 1% goal “is likely
aggressive but achievable absent greater effort by Duke”. Id. at 207. He also provided
testimony enumerating multiple recommendations on how to develop more grid edge and
customer programs that add operational flexibility and capabilities. Id. at 238–54.
PowerPair Pilot
Several intervenors provided testimony regarding Duke’s PowerPair Pilot program,
which provides upfront incentives to residential customers for both BTM solar and
storage. SACE et al. witness Jake Duncan recommended that Duke solicit stakeholder
input and seek Commission approval of a full PowerPair program within four months of
evaluation, measurement, and verification (EM&V) results. Tr. vol. 22, 348. He also
recommended that Duke be required to model the PowerPair Pilot program across all
core portfolios. Id. NCSEA witness Rábago explained that the PowerPair Pilot should be
expanded given the benefits that such a program may provide. Tr. vol. 20, 243–44.
Virtual Power Plants
SACE et al. witness Duncan provided testimony on customer programs utilizing
virtual power plants (VPP) and highlighted several Duke offerings that reflect VPP
concepts, specifically noting the recently approved Active Load Management (ALM)
provisions in the DSM/EE Mechanism. Tr. vol. 22, 387. He recommended that Duke
expand these programs and offerings. Id. at 390–94. Witness Duncan also advocated for
use of the Huels test, developed by EnergyHub, as a metric to determine the maturity of
those VPP offerings and characterized Duke’s current VPP capabilities at a “Level 2, or
‘Enhanced Demand Response’.” Id. at 389. He added that Duke has made meaningful
progress with regards to Duke’s current VPP capabilities but suggested that there is
“significant opportunity for Duke to increase the grid value of these resources through
improved integration and automation”. Id.
NCSEA witness Rábago also recommended deploying Virtual Power Plants
(VPPs) to maximize existing infrastructure and achieve benefits like peak reduction, better
82

grid utilization, rate modernization, and other customer benefits. Tr. vol. 20, 227. He also
recommended the Huels test as a framework for evaluating the maturity of Duke’s VPP
and similarly characterized Duke’s status as Level 2. Id. at 227–31.
Clean Energy Tariffs
CEBA witnesses Priya Barua and Steven Levitas stated that although large
customers are contributing to the significant load growth in Duke’s system, many of these
customers are prepared to deploy private capital to help meet their energy needs.
Tr. vol. 19, 211. Witnesses Barua and Levitas provided direct testimony on how large
customers make investment decisions based on their ability to access clean energy.
Id. at 168. CEBA witnesses Barua and Levitas stated that Duke’s Green Source
Advantage (GSA) program does not adequately meet large customer needs, as
evidenced by the participation numbers in the GSA Standard and Resource Acceleration
Option. Id. at 200. Witnesses Barua and Levitas primarily cited these programs’ lack of
“additionality” as the gap in addressing the needs of large industrial customers, in that
these programs do not add resources to the system that are incremental to Duke’s
planned procurements. Id.
Witnesses Barua and Levitas recommended the Commission direct Duke to
develop and file a new clean customer tariff for large commercial and industrial
customers. Id. at 167–68, 208–11. The clean customer tariff is described in their
testimony as a program under which eligible customers could fund new dedicated clean
energy resources to add to Duke’s system, including both supply-side clean generation
resources and demand-side resources such as EE, aggregated BTM resources, and
other DSM measures. Id. at 167–68, 172–80. Witnesses Barua and Levitas stated that
the program should be designed around customer choice and flexibility. Under their
proposal, participating customers could work directly with a developer or with Duke to
determine the size, location, and generation type of the resource, and would not be limited
to projects that lost a Duke solicitation. Id. at 167. During the expert witness hearing,
witnesses Barua and Levitas testified that the proposal was intended to function as a
voluntary alternative that could help defer the need for Duke to invest in additional
generation. Id. at 218–20.
Google witness Issac Gabel-Frank also recommended that Duke should work with
stakeholders to develop a program allowing large load customers to provide financial
support to bring new resources that are then integrated into the utility’s portfolio via a
Clean Transition Tariff or other mechanism. Tr. vol. 22, 173–77. Other intervenor
witnesses similarly supported the development of a new “bring your own generation”
proposal. During the expert witness hearing, CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry testified that
large customers should be able to work with Duke on bring-your-own generation
arrangements, and that doing so would reduce the need for Duke to build additional
generation to serve growing loads. Tr. vol. 20, 58–60.
Public Staff witness Thomas testified on cross-examination that moving from a
general “clean customer tariff” concept to an actual program design raised several
83

complex issues that must be addressed to protect other customers and the system.
Witness Thomas stated:
And I will say that it is very easy for a large customer to say, I want to bring
my own generation. I want to pay for it. But once you get down and the
rubber hits the road and you're in a stakeholder meeting and you're trying
to decide things about additionality and how do you protect ratepayers from,
you know, large tech companies kind of getting the most cost-competitive
projects before they bid into the RFP at the detriment of other ratepayers.
And once you start talking about timelines for interconnection and how to
model those resources in the IRP and what do they displace to the future,
once you get down to all that and trying to design a program that actually
protects other customers while allowing that pathway, the answer gets a
little more complicated.
…
But what we have striven to as the Public Staff is to make sure that any
program that allows customers to bring their own generation does not hurt
other ratepayers by depriving them of cost-effective system resources or by,
you know, clogging up the queue or allowing these customers to claim
attributes that are not theirs. We had, I think, nine months of debate and
conversation about what additionality means and surplus.
Tr. vol. 16, 112–13
Large Load DR Programs
CIGFUR Witness Fitzhenry stated in his direct testimony that “While Duke has
made progress by expanding certain demand response programs for large customers,
the Companies’ current offerings remain insufficient. Specifically, Duke has fallen short
of providing large customers with the robust direct access programs necessary to
meaningfully offset the significant demand growth currently facing the system.” Tr. vol. 20,
23. Google witness Gabel-Frank highlighted Duke’s agreement with Google to receive
and consider a proposal for a new customer clean energy program and recommended
Duke work with stakeholders to develop a DR program for large load customers.
Tr. vol. 22, 173–74. CEBA witnesses Barua and Levitas recommended that large
customers be allowed to bring customer-funded DSM resources and to fund EE programs
to reduce energy use and peak demand, thus lowering costs. Tr. vol. 19, 175. CCEBA
Witness John Hagerty provided testimony agreeing with the testimony of CEBA
Witnesses Barua and Levitas that large customers should be permitted to bring DSM/EE
measures. Id. at 107. AGO witness Burgess recommended the Commission direct Duke
to develop customer-funded, off-site DSM programs for large load customers. Tr. vol. 22,
92–93.

84

Duke Rebuttal Testimony
Duke Response to the Public Staff Direct Testimony
Witness Duff highlighted the Public Staff’s recommended approval of Duke’s two
Requests for Relief related to grid edge. Tr. vol. 23, 128. Duke did not comment on the
Public Staff’s recommendation concerning BTM forecasting in its rebuttal testimony. In
response to the Public Staff’s recommendation regarding EV load, witness Duff cautioned
that Duke does not agree with the Public Staff’s underlying rationale for this
recommendation – noting that the utilization of eligible retail sales in the tiered incentive
structure of the DSM/EE Mechanism serves a fundamentally different purpose than its use
in resource planning. Id. at 129–30. In the context of the CPIRP, witness Duff explained
that inclusion of EV sales in the eligible retail sales used to establish an annual minimum
in the UEE forecast could underestimate the timing of supply-side resources needed in the
resource plan. Id. However, witness Duff testified that, despite these concerns, Duke will
provide a base case UEE forecast (i.e., 1%) “both with and without EV sales included in
the calculation of eligible retail sales in future resource plans.” Id. at 129.
Duke Response to Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
Overall Theme of Other Intervenor Testimony
At the outset, witness Duff described the overall theme of other intervenor
testimony, noting that several intervenors provided specific tariff or program design
recommendations. Id. at 125–28. He explained that these recommendations are outside
the scope of this resource planning docket and ignore the separate, defined process
established by the Commission expressly for evaluation of new program designs or
modifications. Id. Witness Duff noted that Duke’s DSM/EE Mechanism contains “detailed
processes, procedures, and rules that govern [Duke’s] entire DSM/EE portfolio,” including
the DSM/EE Collaborative. Id. He described the Collaborative as a forum for Duke to
“engage stakeholders, incorporate feedback into program proposals, and report the
results of [Duke’s] existing, Commission-approved programs.” Id. at 126. Witness Duff
explained that any programs or modifications resulting from those processes are
proposed in separate dockets where these proposals undergo careful review by the
Commission and other parties. Id.
Witness Duff reiterated Duke’s commitment to working with stakeholders in the
DSM/EE space but stated that engaging in such a process in this broader resource
planning docket would ignore the processes and procedures from the reforms to Duke’s
DSM/EE program portfolio. Id. at 127. He testified that DSM/EE modifications must be
evaluated for effectiveness and customer attractiveness, and that it “is important for all
programs to mature and produce data so that modifications, or even new programs, are
driven by lessons learned.” Id. at 128. Witness Duff argued that making further program
modifications at this time in a resource planning docket would be premature and restrict
Duke’s ability to meaningfully evaluate the impacts of these reforms. Id.

85

Savings Target of 1%
In response to SACE et al. witness Roumpani’s statement that Duke failed to
achieve the 1% eligible retail sales goal, witness Duff explained that witness Roumpani
was essentially comparing “apples and oranges” because she used total retail sales
rather than eligible retail sales. Id. at 131. When using eligible retail sales, witness Duff
noted that both DEC and DEP exceeded the 1% goal in 2024, with DEC achieving annual
EE savings of 1.34% and DEP achieving 1.04%. Id. He added that DEC and DEP
achieved greater UEE performance in 2024 than the U.S. on average. Id. at 132–33. In
response to witness Rábago’s request to implement an opt-out-specific target, witness
Duff explained that opt-out eligible customers have a statutory right to not participate in
EE programs. Id. at 134. Therefore, including savings associated with these customers
in the UEE forecast would “almost certainly overstate the amount of annual energy
savings” included in the CPIRP. Id.
During the expert witness hearing, witness Duff testified that it is important to
consider the economics and adoption of EE when evaluating the appropriate modeling
assumption for energy savings. Id. at 201. He explained that there is “only so much
efficiency.” Id. Furthermore, witness Duff noted that if Duke exceeds the savings forecast
in a certain year, then “we would need to reduce the future forecast” because Duke
experienced savings earlier than they were forecasted, diminishing the expected savings
in future years. Id. at 201–02. He explained that this supports Duke’s view that the
1% savings floor is aggressive yet achievable over the long term because it is “not
physically possible” to continue to exceed forecasted targets over the long term. Id.
PowerPair Pilot
In response to testimony recommending expansion of the PowerPair Pilot, witness
Duff explained that while the pilot has garnered participation more quickly than expected,
Duke does not yet have the necessary data to perform EM&V. Id. at 136. Witness Duff
cautioned against fully commercializing a program that provides significant incentives to
customers without the necessary data or evidence of cost-effectiveness, which is a
requirement for DSM/EE programs. Id. at 136–37. Therefore, witness Duff stated that fully
commercializing a limited pilot program that has yet to provide any meaningful data
regarding its impacts or cost-effectiveness would be premature. Id. In response to SACE
et al. witness Duncan’s recommendation to model the pilot across other portfolios,
witness Duff reiterated that the “distinct impacts of the solar and storage components of
the PowerPair Pilots are accurately reflected in the resource plan.” Id. at 137–38. Witness
Duff noted that Duke witness Quinto discussed the modeling of PowerPair in greater
detail. Id.
VPPs
Regarding the testimony of intervenors on the topic of programs utilizing VPPs,
witness Duff stated that all intervenors recognize that VPPs must provide “some level of
control or dispatchability” to the utility, but they found it difficult to determine if there was
consensus on the definition of a VPP. Id. at 139. In response to intervenors’ claims that
86

Duke should more rapidly deploy VPPs, witness Duff explained that the CPIRP “includes
hundreds of MW of capacity from DSM programs that allow [Duke] to dispatch
customer-sited resources.” Id. Additionally, witness Duff pointed to additional efforts by
Duke to advance VPPs, including working to develop ALM offerings under the
DSM/EE Mechanism. Id. at 142–43. Regarding the Huels test recommended by
intervenors, witness Duff pointed to Duke’s existing programs, evaluations of new
programs, and the modeling underlying the resource plan as already evaluating the
known utility values of VPPs. Id. Witness Duff testified that requiring Duke to report the
results of a Huels test is limited in usefulness. Id.
Clean Energy Tariffs
In response to CEBA’s testimony regarding the participation levels under Duke’s
GSA Choice Program, witness Duff explained that the design of this program “was the
outcome of a great deal of collaboration with interested parties.” Id. at 145. Under that
design, witness Duff highlighted several different options that customers could select,
ranging from a “plug and play” option under GSA Express to GSA Standard and RAO
options. Id. Finally, witness Duff addressed CEBA’s testimony regarding additionality,
noting that the GSA Choice Program provides additionality by bringing renewable
resources online sooner than they would have otherwise been developed. Id. at 147.
Regardless, witness Duff reiterated Duke’s commitment to providing attractive programs
that do not shift costs to non-participants and are responsive to potential changes in the
energy space. Id. On this point, he noted that Duke has already engaged CEBA to begin
those discussions. Id.
Witness Bateman responded to intervenors’ recommendations for a clean
transition tariff and similar programs for large load customers. Tr. vol. 21, 56. She
explained that Duke’s understanding for such a tariff or program is that participating large
load customers could bring clean energy resources to the utility or otherwise fund such
resources, which are then integrated into the utility’s resource portfolio. Id. Witness
Bateman also explained that Duke is committed to engaging with Google to receive and
consider, in good faith, a more detailed proposal related to a new customer clean energy
program structured similarly to the Clean Transition Tariff cited by Google in its direct
testimony in the Combination Proceeding. Id. She asserted that Duke looks forward to
engaging on this topic but does not expect any proposal to be considered as a part of this
proceeding. Id.
Large Load DR Programs
In rebuttal testimony, witness Duff noted that the existing PowerShare Program
has a 100 MW cap for eligibility to participate, reflecting the need to account for the scale
and scope of customers beyond that threshold. Tr. vol. 23, 143–44. He agreed with
Google Witness Gabel-Frank that a large load demand response program would be
beneficial and that it is appropriate to develop a large load demand response program to
mitigate risks to customers. Id. Duke committed to work with large load customers and
other stakeholders to develop a demand response program that accommodates
customers over the existing cap of 100 MW. Id.
87

Discussion and Conclusions
Based on the competent, material, and substantial evidence in the entire record,
the Commission is persuaded that the CPIRP modeling assumption of a minimum floor
of annual UEE savings of 1% of eligible load represents an aggressive but achievable
target. The Commission determines that reducing load through grid edge and customer
programs — including
DSM,
EE,
customer
self-generation,
and
voltage
management — is a critical component of ensuring Duke can meet increasing demand
for electricity while achieving 2050 net zero carbon dioxide emissions in a least-cost
manner as required by the Carbon Plan Statute. The Commission also recognizes that
the recent comprehensive reform of Duke’s DSM/EE portfolio and updated DSM/EE
Mechanism may provide additional opportunities for EE savings. Additionally, based upon
the persuasive testimony of the Public Staff and Duke, the Commission concludes it is
reasonable to direct Duke to provide a base case UEE forecast (i.e., 1%) both with and
without EV sales included in the calculation of eligible retail sales in future resource plans.
Therefore, the Commission concludes it is reasonable to approve Duke’s continued use
of 1% of eligible load annual UEE savings in the CPIRP modeling as a base assumption
and concludes that such target is reasonable and appropriate for future planning
purposes. The Commission is also persuaded that UEE savings of 1.5% continues to be
an appropriate sensitivity and thus it is reasonable for Duke to conduct this sensitivity in
the 2027 CPIRP with an analysis on the achievability of this savings target.
The Commission is persuaded by the Public Staff’s contention that Duke’s BTM
solar forecasting modeling is outdated and does not take into account solar adoption
trends that have occurred over the last decade or major changes in the regulatory and
policy landscape since then. Accordingly, the Commission concludes it is appropriate to
direct Duke to update its BTM solar forecasting modeling by including observed adoption
trends and weighing location-specific solar production profiles in the 2027 CPIRP.
However, for purposes of this proceeding, the Commission accepts Duke’s BTM solar
forecast as reasonable for planning purposes.
Regarding the proposed clean customer tariff, the Commission gives significant
weight to the testimony of Public Staff witness Thomas and his concerns regarding
program design, additionality, ratepayer protection, and system integration. The
Commission generally finds Public Staff witness Thomas’s concerns well founded and
further finds that key issues remain unresolved. In particular, CEBA witnesses Levitas
and Barua did not sufficiently detail how the proposed tariff would address those issues
in practice. Their expert witness hearing testimony also raised additional questions
concerning design, implementation, and interaction of such a program with Duke’s
existing programs and planning processes. Given the breadth of the near-term actions
currently being undertaken by Duke and the number of vital unresolved questions
regarding the proposed tariff, the Commission declines at this time to direct Duke to file
or propose a clean customer tariff as recommended by CEBA witnesses Levitas and
Barua. The Commission nevertheless recognizes Duke’s representation that it remains
willing to collaborate with interested parties on enhancing existing programs and
developing new opportunities. The Commission encourages Duke to work with the
interested parties to work on a proposal for a Clean Transition Tariff.
88

The Commission gives weight to the testimony of various intervenors on the
potential benefits of a large load demand response program and Duke’s commitment to
engage with interested large load customers to design such a program. The Commission
thus directs Duke to engage large load customers and other stakeholders to design a
large load demand response program that accommodates customer sites with loads over
100 MW, and to file a proposed large-load demand response program within 180 days of
the issuance of this Order.
The Commission also recognizes that the established DSM/EE Mechanism is the
governing document for consideration and development of Duke’s DSM/EE programs and
modifications, including DR programs. Importantly, this mechanism requires engagement
of stakeholders through the DSM/EE Collaborative, which has consistently been the
forum for exploring new programs, modifications, and other issues related to DSM/EE
programs, including cost-effectiveness and customer interests. Witness Duff and others
provided testimony regarding several ongoing efforts under the mechanism aimed at
addressing certain design recommendations made by intervenors in this docket, including
furthering deployment of VPPs. Although several intervenors request that the
Commission approve other new programs or modifications in this docket, the Commission
declines to consider these other issues outside of the process under the DSM/EE
Mechanism. The Commission is persuaded that Duke should continue advancing its grid
edge and customer programs in its continued effort to shrink the challenges associated
with the programs and is satisfied that the modified incentives and values attributed to
energy and demand savings should better enable Duke to do so. The Commission also
encourages Duke to continue meaningful engagement regarding customer programs that
protect non-participating customers and are otherwise compliant with North Carolina law.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 30–32
Near-Term Action Plan and Execution Activities
Overview
The evidence supporting these findings of fact are contained in Chapter 4 of the
2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke witness Bowman; the testimony and
exhibits of Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel (Beatty, Quinto, and Snider); the
testimony and exhibits of Public Staff witnesses Michna and Metz; the testimony and
exhibits of EDF witness Patrylak; the testimony and exhibits of NCSEA witness Glick; the
testimony and exhibits of SACE et. al. witness Roumpani; and the entire record.
The Initial Carbon Plan Order and the 2024 CPIRP Order approved NTAPs
authorizing Duke to procure and develop certain targeted supply-side resources. Initial
Carbon Plan Order at 132–33; 2024 CPIRP Order at 93–94. Duke witness Snider, of
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel, testified that the 2025 CPIRP builds upon and
advances the NTAP and related execution activities approved in the 2024 CPIRP Order.
Tr. vol. 24, 129.
89

As explained in Chapter 4, Duke remains in a critical execution period and must
continue to take decisive and reasonable steps to meet customer needs. 2025 CPIRP
Ch. 4 at 2. Accordingly, Duke’s execution plan represents a two-year progression and
evolution of the 2023 CPIRP’s NTAP, making appropriate adjustments to account for
rapid and sustained load growth and evolving policy, market, and industry conditions. Id.
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Action Panel testified that the Recommended NTAP
resources represent a risk-adjusted and execution-informed selection considering an
extensive range of planning uncertainties and execution strategies. Tr. vol. 24, 121–22.
The resources in Duke’s Recommended NTAP allow Duke to maintain or improve the
reliability of the system, balancing cost impacts with near-term and long-term risks. In
addition, the Recommended NTAP resources provide for a diverse, risk-adjusted energy
mix to meet the needs of current and future customers. These resources build on the
recommended and approved NTAP resources from the 2024 CPIRP Order, while
increasing capacity resources to meet continued growth and adjusting to policy and cost
impacts with regards to the pace of deployment of solar and wind. Id. Witness Bowman
presented Table 4-2 to the 2025 CPIRP (shown below), which presents a consolidated
view of the near-term actions (2026–2028) for supply side resource additions in the Plan.
Tr. vol. 6, 32; 2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 9–11. In-service timing for new resources is presented
on a beginning of year (BOY) basis, meaning that the resource will be online and available
to the system on January 1 of the identified year. In addition, the Recommended NTAP
reflects generic resources and provides flexibility to check and adjust in the near- and
long-terms. For example, the Recommended NTAP for new nuclear resources provides
activities for both SMR and LLWR as Duke evaluates options for implementation in the
late 2030s. The Recommended NTAP also presents near-term actions for long-lead
resources that may be targeted for commercial operation beyond the 2026–2040 Base
Planning Period to preserve optionality in future resource planning.

92

In direct testimony filed on March 30, 2026, Public Staff witness Michna presented
the Public Staff’s NTAP and recommended that the Commission direct Duke to pursue
the near-term actions identified by the Public Staff in lieu of those proposed by Duke.
Tr. vol. 13, 36–37. The Public Staff’s proposed NTAP aligned with Duke’s Recommended
NTAP in both quantity and target in-service date for all resources except new gas
resources. Id. Witness Michna testified that the Public Staff’s modeling suggests that the
majority of Duke’s proposed near-term actions are “directionally correct” and noted that
the only deviation between the Public Staff’s NTAP and Duke’s Recommended NTAP lies
in the pace of new natural gas additions proposed by Duke. Id. at 37–38. Witness Michna
further stated that if Duke filed an update with a material change to the load forecast or
some other information that shows a significant impact on reliability or need, the Public
Staff would reassess the need to pursue the new gas CT and CC units on such an
aggressive timeline. Id. at 72.
On May 14, 2026, Duke filed its rebuttal testimony and witness Stillman presented
the Updated 2026 Advancing Development Load Forecast which demonstrated that
Duke’s load forecast is now tracking at or above the High Load Forecast scenario
identified in Appendix D. Tr. vol. 20, 549. As a result, witness Snider testified that Duke’s
Recommended NTAP is no longer a broadly risk-adjusted set of resources as originally
presented in the Plan but rather is now the minimum set of resources necessary to
maintain the reliability of the system in the near term. Tr. vol. 24, 166, 182. Despite the
increased load forecast, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained that Duke
is not proposing any changes to its NTAP in this proceeding, noting that the same
resources required in its Recommended NTAP are also required in the High Load
Sensitivity Portfolio prepared as part of the 2025 CPIRP. Id. at 182–83. The High Load
Portfolio also identifies the need for a 6th CC and an additional 1,900 MW of battery by
2034, and the panel testified that these resources can be reconfirmed in a subsequent
CPIRP cycle as the execution timeframe for bringing these resources into service occurs
further in the planning horizon. The panel further underscored that each and every
resource included in Duke’s current Recommended NTAP is critical to ensure that Duke
can maintain system reliability for customers. Id. at 182–84.
At the evidentiary hearing on June 12, 2026, Public Staff witness Michna revised
his testimony, noting that, since he filed his testimony, there had been three significant
changes in circumstances that impacted his analysis, including: (1) the update to the load
forecast; (2) the delay of the 2026 Solar and Solar Paired with Storage RFP; and (3) the
approval DEC’s and DEP’s business combination. Tr. vol. 13, 10–11. Witness Michna
stated that the Public Staff now agrees with Duke on the pace of new natural gas additions
for all units except CT5. Id. at 11. Witness Michna prepared a corrected version of the
Public Staff’s NTAP showing the increased alignment with Duke’s Recommended NTAP:

93

Id. at 37.
Testifying at the hearing on rebuttal, witness Snider noted that, in light of witness
Michna’s updated testimony, Duke and the Public Staff are now aligned on the need and
timing for all resources except CT5. Tr. vol. 24, 272. Witness Snider stated that Duke
strongly believes that CT5 is a very important reliability resource and no other resource can
replace it by 2030. Accordingly, witness Snider testified that Duke “struggle[s] to understand
how Public Staff does not support [CT5] at the same time having reliability concerns.” Id.
On June 30, 2026, the Public Staff filed corrected testimony in the
CPCN proceeding for the CT5, then pending before the Commission in Docket
No. E-2, Sub 1395. In its corrected testimony, the Public Staff withdrew its previous
opposition to construction of the CT5 and recommended that the Commission grant
DEP a CPCN authorizing construction of the CT5 to be in service by 2030.
Of the parties that performed Encompass modeling, NCSEA prepared a complete
alternative portfolio and NTAP (NCSEA Alternative Portfolio), while SACE et al. and EDF
recommended modifications to Duke’s Recommended NTAP. As compared to Duke’s
Recommended NTAP, the NCSEA Alternative Portfolio proposes an additional 428 MW of
CTs by 2040, a reduction to the targeted volumes of nuclear capacity (1,117 MW reduction),
utility-scale solar (156 MW reduction), and battery storage (1,400 MW reduction), as well
as deferral of 1,365 MW of CCs from 2032 to 2034. Tr. vol. 17, 162–63. EDF witness
Patrylak proposed to defer CC5 beyond 2033, tr. vol. 22, 150, and SACE et al. witness
Roumpani similarly recommended that the Commission should decline to approve the
construction of CCs 4 and 5 and CTs 5 and 6 at this time. Tr. vol. 20, 297.
In rebuttal, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained that each
intervenor made multiple changes to modeling inputs to develop their respective
portfolios. Tr. vol. 24, 218. According to the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel, many
94

intervenors artificially lowered the load forecast, which effectively changed the playing
field and required fewer resources (and theoretical cost savings) as compared to Duke’s
Recommended NTAP. Id. at 219. The panel also noted that NCSEA’s Alternative Portfolio
aligns very closely to the Duke Recommended NTAP, including the selection of natural
gas resources. Id. at 245–46.
Discussion and Conclusions
As a threshold matter, the Commission gives substantial weight to the alignment
between Duke and the Public Staff on the resource volumes and target in-service dates
identified in Duke’s Recommended NTAP. Both parties acknowledged the criticality of
developing the resources in the NTAP to ensure that Duke can maintain the reliability of
the system while planning to serve all current and future customers. While NCSEA, SACE
et al., and EDF presented recommendations contesting aspects of Duke’s Recommended
NTAP based on their independent modeling, these parties’ modeling relied on lower load
forecast assumptions and included overly optimistic or inexecutable resource availability
timelines in a manner that materially impacts resource selection, and accordingly, the
Commission gives these recommendations little weight. The Commission further gives
significant weight to the recent update to the load forecast. Based on this competent,
material, and substantial evidence, the Commission finds Duke’s Recommended NTAP
to be reasonable for planning purposes.

New Solar & Solar Paired with Storage Resources
Duke Direct and Supplemental Testimony
