The 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke, during the 2025–2026 period, to take
reasonable steps to procure an additional 3,460 MW of new controllable solar generation
and 625 MW of paired BESS to be placed in service by 2031. 2024 CPIRP Order at 176.
The 2025 CPIRP explains that solar is a fuel-free resource that provides low-cost
energy and can also support and augment traditional fuel cost hedging by reducing
95

customers’ overall exposure to periodic volatility in fuel commodity markets. 2025 CPIRP
App. H at 2. The 2025 CPIRP also discusses risks associated with solar and SPSW
development and integration into Duke’s system, noting that careful consideration must
be given to both the commodity risk mitigation and emission reduction benefits provided
by renewables and storage, while acknowledging their variability. Id. The 2025 CPIRP
specifically points to challenges of interconnecting large volumes of solar year after year;
constraints around sourcing materials, equipment and labor for projects; and third-party
contracting risk. Id. at 7–9. Duke is also navigating changing market and policy conditions
that impact solar and SPWS resources, including revisions to existing federal energy tax
credit provisions that require new solar facilities to be placed in service by 2030 to be
eligible. Id. at 3.
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel witnesses explained in their
supplemental testimony how the variability and intermittency of solar is accounted for in
the model when evaluating the least-cost way to meet customer demand each hour.
Tr. vol. 24, 140. Despite the relatively low firm winter capacity contributions of solar, the
panel’s witnesses explained that the model continues to select some levels of solar for its
low-cost energy benefit to the system, while also ensuring reliability is maintained
holistically by the overall resource portfolio. Id. at 142. The model recognizes the impact
solar selection has on the operations of other system resources like natural gas and
storage. Id. at 143. These impacts include fuel consumption, variable operations and
maintenance costs, start costs, marginal storage charging costs, as well as storage
dispatch value. Id. The model considers these impacts through various modeling
sensitivities, such as the low gas price sensitivity, evident by the selection of different
quantities of solar under various sensitivities. Id. Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions
Panel explained that Duke’s modeling fully recognizes solar’s limited winter capacity
value, as well as the impact solar intermittency has on the system, and yet solar continues
to be selected as part of an overall least-cost portfolio given its no fuel, low carriable cost,
and emissions free energy. Id. at 148. In the modeling, solar’s energy attributes and
benefits to the system outweigh its lack of contribution to firm winter planning capacity
and the costs associated with intermittency requiring additional operating requirements.
Id. at 147.
Duke asserts that it worked diligently to complete the 2024 RFP as well as design
and launch the 2025 RFP targeting procurement of 1,700 MW of new solar resources and
300 MW of paired storage in compliance with the Commission’s directives in the
2024 CPIRP Order to procure 3,460 MW of new solar resources and 625 MW of paired
storage during the 2025–2026 period. Tr. vol. 11, 27. The 2025 RFP was designed to
seek only proposals that can leverage the currently available federal energy tax credits,
and included a one-time exception to allow projects with existing Interconnection
Agreements from the 2022 DISIS or earlier legacy serial study that do not have another
offtake agreement to participate in the 2025 RFP. Id. at 28. The 2025 RFP incorporated
new cost control mechanisms to encourage the most competitive bidding and to keep
costs of these resources as low as possible. Id. at 28–30.
Duke requests in its Petition that the Commission select the following additional
solar and SPSW resources: (1) 2,300 MW of solar resources and 420 MW of
96

paired storage through RFPs in the 2026–2028 period to achieve in-service dates in the
2032–2034 timeframe. These in-service dates do not qualify for the federal tax credits,
and thus the model selected less solar in these years. Id. Duke testified that maintaining
moderate procurement levels in the RFPs during the 2026–2028 period will lead to more
competition and lower prices in the long term (as opposed to stopping procurement and
later restarting) and is a risk-adjusted approach for procuring needed solar resources that
lowers risk for customers. Id. at 30–32.
The 2024 CPIRP Order also approved a settlement agreement requiring Duke to
study the interconnection of solar and battery resources at existing solar, coal and
CT sites utilizing surplus interconnection service, and directing Duke to engage with
stakeholders and evaluate potential procurement of energy and capacity from existing
operational qualifying facilities (QFs) with PURPA power purchase agreements 12 (PPAs)
or renewable PPAs expiring within 60 months of issuance of the potential procurement.
2024 CPIRP Order at 177 (Ordering ¶¶ 23–24).
Further, the 2025 CPIRP includes Duke’s evaluation of utility owned gas, solar,
and coal sites to explore the feasibility of surplus interconnection service. 2025 CPIRP
App. K at 25–29. These results are summarized in Table K-8 and Table K-10 of Appendix
K of the 2025 CPIRP:

Power Purchase Agreements governed by the federal Public Utility Regulatory Policies Act of
1978, Pub. L. 95-617, 92 Stat. 3117, 16 U.S.C. 2601 et seq.
12

97

As directed by the Commission, Duke filed a report on the efforts to evaluate
potential procurement of energy and capacity from expiring QFs, which include
developing a new PPA option for QFs that have expiring contracts that choose to add
battery storage. This new PPA option was included in Duke’s 2025 biennial avoided cost
filing and is pending review by the Commission in Docket No. E-100, Sub 211.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver recommended the Commission support
Duke’s solar and SPWS RFPs and approve a procurement target of 1,540 MW of solar
and SPWS through the RFP process in 2026–2027, with an incremental amount for the
2028 RFP to be determined at the time Duke files its pre-issuance proposal for the
2028 RFP. Tr. vol. 18, 221.
Witnesses Hill and Weaver explained that the RFPs are a reasonable and least cost
method, when risk adjusted, to procure new solar and SPWS resources. Id. at 209–10.
Specifically, Hill and Weaver explain the ratepayer guardrails in place within the RFPs that
protect ratepayers from solar at any cost, known as the volume adjustment mechanism,
and that fostering a competitive RFP process benefits ratepayers. Id. at 204–07.
Witnesses Hill and Weaver expressed concern about the execution risk of the
targeted solar for the 2025–2028 RFPs, given the attrition experienced in prior
procurements and the general turbulence in the solar industry. Id. at 314–18. They also
expressed concern that the requested target procurement amount for the 2028 RFP is

98

premature due to rapid changes in the solar industry and the need for flexibility to respond
to future changes in this rapidly evolving landscape. Id. at 216–17.
Witnesses Hill and Weaver explained that the Public Staff’s modeling supports the
economic selection of solar resources in the 2030–2034 time period and agrees with
Duke that additional solar should be built to reduce execution risk. Id. at 218–21.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
No intervenor opposed Duke’s proposed addition of 2,300 MW of solar resources
in the 2026–2028 time period.
AGO witness Burgess and SACE, et al. witness Roumpani advocated for and
requested the Commission approve the additions of solar resources in Duke’s
Recommended NTAP. CCEBA witness Hagerty testified that Duke’s cost and operating
assumptions for solar resources used in the model are reasonable and comprehensive
but opined that Duke’s modeling underestimates the value of solar. Tr. vol. 19, 37. CCEBA
witness Hagerty requested that the Commission increase the solar procurement targets
to at least 1,000 MW per year to ensure Duke has sufficient resources to serve the High
Load case. Id. at 39.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
On rebuttal, Duke’s Solar and Energy Storage Panel testified in support of the
benefits of solar as part of a diverse generation portfolio that mitigates risk and protects
customers from fuel price spikes. Tr. vol. 22, 482. Duke witness Farver testified that few
generation technology types can be constructed as quickly as solar, making it unique in
its ability to add needed energy to the system in the near term. Id. Duke’s IRP and
Near-Term Actions Panel also emphasized the need for solar generation in Duke’s
Recommended NTAP in light of the increased load forecast. Tr. vol. 24, 187. In response
to CCEBA’s recommendation to increase the solar procurement amounts in the NTAP,
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained that while solar is an important
resource, Duke’s Recommended NTAP amounts appropriately balance solar
development with overall system requirements and reflect a reasonable, risk-adjusted
approach. Id. at 261.
Witness Farver provided updates on the results of the 2025 Solar and SPWS
RFP and testified that the RFP resulted in 1,551 MW of solar-only proposals. Tr. vol. 22,
509–10. She testified that at the direction of the Commission in the 2026 RFP Docket,
Duke had paused the 2026 RFP, which was targeting 770 MW of solar and SPWS,
pending the Commission’s review in this proceeding. Id. at 486. Witness Farver explained
that the pause impacts the execution of a 2026 RFP due to the timing of interconnection
cluster studies, but that Duke continues to support the 2,300 MW volume proposed in its
Recommended NTAP over the 2026–2028 time frame, which can be procured through
the 2027 and 2028 RFPs. Id. at 486–87, 516.
Witness Farver rejected the Public Staff’s concerns about setting a procurement
target for the 2028 RFP, explaining that the timing of the RFP and CPIRP cycles
99

necessitate guidance from the Commission on the procurement amounts, which can be
subject to the check and adjust framework established in the CPIRP. Id. at 486. Further,
she explained that establishing a procurement target for the 2028 RFP now will better
ensure future cost savings for customers by maintaining market interest and competition
among developers. Id. at 519.
New Battery Energy Storage System Resources
Duke Direct Testimony
The 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke to take reasonable steps during the 2024–
2026 period to pursue the development of 1,100 MW of incremental BESS, including
475 MW of standalone storage and at least 625 MW of BESS paired with solar generation
to be placed in service by 2031. 2024 CPIRP Order at 176–77.
In Appendix H of the 2025 CPIRP, Duke stated that it had nearly 70 MW nameplate
capacity of standalone BESS in-service as of the beginning of 2025 and is planning to
install 564 MW of standalone BESS by 2028. 2025 CPIRP App. H at 9.
Witness Tompson testified on the progress Duke has made towards fulfilling the
Commission’s directives in the 2024 CPIRP Order. Duke is progressing development,
procurement, and construction activities for the 1,475 MW of cumulative standalone
storage as authorized by the 2024 CPIRP Order. Tr. vol. 11, 38. To that end, Duke placed
two projects totaling 55 MW in service in the fourth quarter of 2024. Id. Additionally, Duke
executed engineering, procurement, and construction (EPC) and battery integrator
contracts for 287 MW of projects, ordered 18 high voltage transformers, executed
interconnection agreements for 365 MW of projects, and submitted interconnection study
requests for 3,172 MW of battery storage projects. Id.
Witness Tompson stated that Chapter 2 and Appendix C of the 2025 CPIRP detail
the 2025 CPIRP modeling, which included 4- and 8-hour duration lithium-ion battery
storage as selectable resources. Id. at 37. Lithium-ion storage is currently the
predominant technology used in the utility-scale stationary storage industry and Duke has
extensive experience developing and constructing this mature technology. Id. The
2025 CPIRP modeling reflects that costs for lithium-ion storage are expected to decline
over the long term. During cross-examination, Witness Tompson testified that “over the
long-term the cost of BESS has gone down and [Duke’s] execution plans and [Duke’s]
planning efforts are taking advantage of that.” Id. at 80. As described in 2025 CPIRP
Appendix G, Duke also included a technology-agnostic 10-hour LDES resource as a
selectable resource during modeling. This resource represents a blend of leading
electrochemical and mechanical LDES technologies that have been demonstrated at
smaller scales. Id.
Witness Tompson testified that Duke has an executable plan to develop and
procure additional BESS as part of its updated Recommended NTAP. Id. at 38–39. The
updated Recommended NTAP for 2026 through 2028 targets a total of 4,330 MW of
standalone battery storage to be placed in-service by 2034. Id. This includes the
100

1,475 MW of standalone BESS previously identified as needed and selected by the
Commission in the 2023 CPIRP proceeding as well as 2,855 MW of incremental
standalone storage to be placed into service by 2034. Id. at 38–39. To meet these
increased storage addition targets, Duke’s standalone BESS development plan now
includes year-over-year activities to achieve 2,855 MW of incremental standalone storage
resources to be placed in-service by the beginning of year 2034. Id.
In response to the Commission’s 2024 CPIRP Order directive that required Duke
to take reasonable steps to procure 1,225 MW of SPWS resources by the beginning of
2031, Witness Tompson explained that the 2023 RFP targeted 260 MW of paired storage,
and the 2024 and 2025 RFPs targeted a combined 700 MW of storage resources to be
placed in-service by 2031, with the expectation that the 2026 RFP would procure the
remaining 265 MW to meet the 2024 CPIRP Order’s directive. Id. at 42. These
SPWS amounts were a portion of the overall approved cumulative storage amount of
2,700 MW (including both SPWS and standalone configuration) to be in service by 2031.
Id. Duke witness Farver identified challenges the Companies have encountered in
procuring the 1,225 MW of SPWS resources, including attrition due to tariffs, increased
cost uncertainty, and the sheer volume of work being conducted. Id. at 34–36. To provide
a backstop in case paired storage through the procurement does not materialize as
planned, Duke has taken early actions to develop a flexible and robust portfolio of
utility-developed standalone storage project opportunities. Id. These actions include
interconnection studies, limited procurement of real estate, ordering of long-lead high
voltage equipment, engineering of preliminary designs, and stakeholder engagement. Id.
As was the case with the 2023 CPIRP, the 2025 CPIRP continues to rely upon both
standalone-configuration storage and RFP-procured SPWS resources (4,330 MW and
1,270 MW, respectively) to achieve 5,600 MW of total system storage by 2034. Id.
Witness Tompson testified that Duke modeled a generic LDES technology in
resource planning. Id. at 37–38. While modeling results do not support near-term
commitments or high-cost development activities of any particular LDES, Duke is closely
following the construction and operation of a variety of storage technologies through its
Emerging Technology Assessment Team and Duke’s early activities with candidate
LDES technologies will allow them to more confidently deploy similar non-lithium LDES
when a contender technology achieves commercial viability and cost competitiveness.
Id. at 45–46.
As mentioned above, Duke agreed in the 2023 CPIRP Stipulation to evaluate
surplus interconnection of battery projects at existing solar and fossil sites. In addition to
the information provided in Appendix K, witness Tompson explained how Duke evaluated
the suitability of more than two dozen existing solar sites to bring battery storage onto the
grid using surplus interconnection service. Id. at 36–37. This analysis identified 13 solar
sites where Duke either already has connected storage, or has plans to add it, and four
other sites that warrant further study. 2025 CPIRP App. K at 28. These efforts identified
approximately 200–400 MW of potential battery storage addition and over 100 MW of
new solar generation potential. Id. Duke plans to advance these opportunities through
additional low-risk development activities. Id.
101

Public Staff Direct Testimony
The Public Staff expressed three concerns with the way Duke modeled 1-, 2-, 4-,
and 8-hour BESS resources in EnCompass: (1) an out-of-date BESS resource forecast;
(2) selection of 8-hour batteries in the near-term; and (3) the method used to account for
paired storage resource production. Tr. vol. 18, 223.
With respect to the Public Staff’s first concern, witnesses Weaver and Hill identified
1,454 MW of planned battery resources that were not included in Duke’s forecast because
of new information that became available after the Companies filed the Petition. This
included 980 MW of projects with signed interconnection agreements, as well as 474 MW
in the interconnection queue sought for recovery in Duke’s MYRPs. Consequently, the
Public Staff included this updated information in its modeling forecasts and suggested
that Duke do the same. Id. at 223–24.
Regarding the second concern, Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver made
several arguments. In Duke’s EnCompass modeling, BESS resource availability was
modeled identically for both 4- and 8-hour batteries, resulting in 8-hour batteries being
economically selected as early as 2028 in DEP. Through 2040, 2,800 MW of 8-hour
batteries are selected across DEC and DEP combined. Id. at 228–29.
When the Public Staff asked Duke about this in discovery, Duke stated that it is
not seeking to procure 8-hour batteries and would instead replace these resources with
4-hour batteries. Id. The Public Staff noted that the distinction in operational
characteristics between these two resource types, and the model’s consequent selection
of 8-hour resources, suggests that longer-duration resources provide some additional
benefit to the system in the near-term, and therefore should be seriously considered as a
potential resource. Id. at 229. The Public Staff noted, however, that an 8-hour, 100 MW
BESS facility would be first-of-a-kind. Id.
In addition to this concern, the Public Staff noted that the cost of the LDES resource
type in Duke’s EnCompass files is significantly more expensive than the 8-hour BESS
resource type throughout the modeling period – 112% higher at its highest point. Id. at
242. Yet the LDES resource type was modeled with the same ELCC as the 8-hour BESS.
Id. Given these inputs, the EnCompass model will always select 8-hour BESS before
LDES. The Public Staff Reliability and Operations Panel discussed this dynamic in greater
detail, but this concern informed the Public Staff’s recommendation that Duke continue to
evaluate LDES technologies and monitor the expanding market. Id.
Public Staff witness Ssengonzi additionally noted that Duke represents the capital
cost relationship between 4- and 8-hour batteries as static, whereas the National
Laboratory of the Rockies’ Annual Technology Baseline, the reference dataset used by
Duke, indicates a declining utility-scale 8-hour capital cost trend. Tr. vol. 16, 249.
The Public Staff’s third concern regarding storage resources was that Duke’s
method for estimating paired storage generation for existing and forecasted SPWS
resources underestimates the energy produced. Tr. vol. 18, 232. Within Duke’s
102

EnCompass modeling, battery storage paired with solar is represented as a zero-energy
resource with a static charge-discharge pattern. Id. The Public Staff asserted that this
static charge-discharge relationship does not reflect real-life battery dispatch, as it
underestimates winter morning contributions and fails to account for in-model variations
such as weather or load forecast shifts. Id. at 232–33.
To examine the effect of this static relationship, the Public Staff conducted a
sensitivity in which SPWS batteries were instead dispatched by EnCompass in production
cost modeling. Id. at 233. This sensitivity found that the static charge-discharge
relationship potentially underestimates storage contribution by 5,686 GWh by 2050, the
equivalent output of 75% of a Richmond F-Frame CT in the same time period. Id. Despite
this finding, the Public Staff acknowledged that endogenous dispatch of SPWS resources,
such as that conducted in its sensitivity, was unrealistic for all portfolios. Id. at 235–36.
When considering Duke’s proposed NTAP, the Public Staff expressed concern
about setting battery storage procurement targets for the final years of the NTAP period,
stating that these should instead be addressed in future CPIRP proceedings. Id. at 227.
The Public Staff estimated that standalone BESS projects require a construction timeline
of approximately four years, making standalone procurement targets for 2033 and
2034 unnecessary. However, because the Public Staff recommends Solar RFP targets
through 2027, its SPWS recommendation retains the 2033 in-service year, removing only
the 2034 model-selected projects from the SPWS target. Id. at 227–28.
Combining this delay on SPWS projects with a 2034 in-service target with the
removal of the 2033-2034 standalone projects, the Public Staff’s final recommendation is
that the Commission approve procurement of 1,600 MW of BESS, incremental to
previously approved amounts. Id. at 193–94. The Public Staff further recommended
dividing this amount into 1,200 MW of standalone through 2032 and 400 MW of paired
storage through 2033. Id.
As a result of the concerns noted, the Public Staff recommended the Commission
direct Duke to: (1) re-evaluate its SPWS dispatch model; (2) submit a sensitivity in
rebuttal testimony demonstrating that the SPWS dispatch method does not impact the
model outcome; (3) conduct a Request for Information (RFI) to study 4- and 8- hour BESS
and reevaluate 8-hour BESS availability and cost inputs in the 2027 CPIRP based on the
RFI results; (4) continue to monitor the market availability of LDES and update LDES
model inputs in the 2027 CPIRP; and (5) provide a comprehensive report regarding LDES
resources to be filed contemporaneously with the 2027 CPIRP. Id. at 194.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
AGO witness Burgess recommended that the Commission approve Duke’s
proposed additions of battery storage. Tr. vol. 22, 47. He stated that the Commission
could approve the proposed 2,900 MW of incremental battery resources by 2034 in the
NTAP as a “low regrets” option. Id. at 87. He also recommended that Duke adjust its
Recommended Portfolio to include an additional 2,000 MW of battery storage. Id. at 48.
Witness Burgess testified that Duke should have evaluated surplus interconnection of
103

new resources, such as battery storage, at the coal unit sites Mayo 1 and Cliffside 5, as
well as “the interconnection of solar and BESS resources at existing utility-owned solar,
coal, and CT sites” to comply with the Commission’s directives in the 2024 CPIRP Order.
Id. at 98. Witness Burgess critiqued the storage resources build limits in Duke’s
EnCompass model runs. Id. at 113. He testified that “there is no basis for restricting
battery storage to prohibit selection of utility-scale projects comparable in size to
traditional power plants,” and further stated that “[r]estricting batteries to distributed
generation sizes only serves to disadvantage them economically and explicitly restrict
their availability in the optimization process.” Id. at 116–17.
SACE et al. witness Roumpani similarly requested approval of Duke’s battery
storage amounts. Tr. vol. 20, 297. Witness Roumpani also requested that Duke evaluate
interconnection opportunities at existing coal sites, focusing on surplus at Mayo and
Cliffside 5. Id. at 351.
CCEBA Witness Hagerty recommended that Duke improve its modeling to better
capture the value of battery storage. Tr. vol. 19, 70. He testified that the modeling Duke
currently uses underestimates the value of dispatchable resources, especially battery
storage. Id. at 135. He explained that Duke will need to continue to add battery storage
resources to provide the capacity to maintain reliability during both peak periods and
during winter storms that impact gas availability. Id. at 80.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
Witness Tompson testified that it is reasonable to approve the Duke
Recommended NTAP battery amounts for 2034 at this time because BESS projects take
time to plan, develop, and execute, such that approval at this time is important to ensure
projects can be built over the planning horizon to keep pace with reliability needs and
customer demand. Tr. vol. 22, 491–92. He explained that approving Duke’s
Recommended NTAP battery amounts through 2034 in this docket provides Duke with
optionality to increase storage resource availability in future IRPs and would provide Duke
with confidence in ordering long lead equipment needed to interconnect projects by the
2034 timeframe. Id.
Witness Tompson responded to the Public Staff’s recommendation that Duke
pursue a RFI regarding 4- and 8-hour battery storage. He stated that Duke understands
the Public Staff’s interest regarding 8-hour storage and can and will continue to address
those concerns with in-house expertise, such as the Emerging Technology Office and the
dedicated Energy Storage Department. Id. at 492–93, 530. A storage-specific RFI would
cost customers money, without any guarantee that meaningful and relevant insights
would be achieved in addition to those already deliverable by Duke. Id. at 493. Witness
Tompson stated that for these reasons, the Public Staff’s recommendation should be
dismissed. Id.
Witness Tompson also responded to the Public Staff’s recommendation regarding
LDES monitoring and clarified that Duke already monitors LDES market availability and
will update the inputs in the CPIRP. Id. at 493–94. These updates consider, among other
104

information, market intelligence from various sources and stakeholder engagement
technical sessions to receive input on the most accurate information in its modeling
assumptions and inputs. Id. Accordingly, an update on the LDES market availability will
be available in the next CPIRP, making Public Staff’s request for a separate report
duplicative and unnecessary. Id.
Witness Tompson testified that the LDES resource that SACE et al. modeled is not
reasonable because SACE et al. increased the duration of the generic LDES to 100 hours,
from Duke’s assumption of 10 hours. Id. SACE et al. based these updates on the generic
iron-air storage LDES from PacificCorp’s 2025 IRP. Id. As discussed in Appendix G of
Duke’s 2025 IRP, LDES is still an emerging technology and it is more appropriate to use
Duke’s assumption of a 10-hour LDES, which represents a blend of leading
electrochemical and mechanical LDES technologies that have been demonstrated at
smaller scales. Id. To date, there are no active demonstrations of 100+ hour or multi-day
storage technologies. Id. Swapping Duke’s 10-hour LDES for a resource with lower
technology readiness level could put customer reliability at risk. Id. Moreover, as
explained in response to Public Staff’s LDES recommendation, Duke would adjust LDES
assumptions in future IRPs as it continues to monitor industry developments. Id.
Witness Tompson additionally testified that the 100 MW / 400 MWh generic battery
storage resource unit size that Duke used for modeling are reasonably based on the
current state of storage development in the Carolinas. Id. at 496. In 2025, Duke
interconnected its largest batteries to date, each at 200 MWh. Id. He explained that
generic sizing should always remain rooted in execution realities and with strong
consideration of recent experience. Id.
Witness Tompson defended Duke’s assumptions regarding battery storage
resource availability, arguing that current resource availability assumptions are informed
by the development pipeline. Id. at 496. Duke has only interconnected 250 MW of battery
storage to date, so increased experience in construction will reveal whether higher
resource availability limits are reasonable. Id. Finally, as explained in further detail below,
leveraging surplus interconnection service or generator replacement requests will not
result in incremental capacity resources that would justify greater resource availability
limits. Id.
Finally, Witness Tompson responded to witness Roumpani’s and witness
Burgess’s recommendations regarding evaluations of battery storage at coal sites. Id. at
497–98. Witness Tompson testified that if Duke must choose between building a battery
with net new interconnection capacity or building a battery that provides redundant
capacity at an existing coal site, it would be in customers’ best interest for Duke to build
a battery with net new interconnection capacity. Id. He explained that conducting
evaluations of surplus interconnection service at coal sites would not provide useful
information for capacity expansion modeling, and the evaluation of the generator
replacement request (GRR) at Mayo is already part of the Duke Recommended NTAP in
this case. Id.; 2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 18. Witness Roberts also testified on the utilization of
surplus interconnection and explained that Duke determined that the GRR process, rather
105

than surplus interconnection, would be a more effective use of retiring coal sites. Tr. vol.
23, 41–42.
In the Order Approving Combination, the Commission noted Duke’s agreement to
remove 200 MW of battery storage from its 2025 Recommended NTAP in the event the
combination received all necessary regulatory approvals. Order Approving Combination
at 12–13. The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel confirmed that, because of the
Combination approval, Duke is reducing its Recommended NTAP to remove 200 MW of
BESS resources consistent with the Combination settlement and the
Commission’s Order. Id. at 211.
Discussion and Conclusions
Solar
The Commission finds persuasive Duke’s modeling that fully recognizes solar’s
limited winter capacity value – as well as the impact solar intermittency has on the
system – and yet continues to select solar as part of an overall least-cost portfolio. No
party contended that Duke’s modeling does not account for the costs that solar places on
the system. To the contrary, as Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained in
supplemental testimony, the EnCompass modeling accounts for the impacts of solar’s
variable generation profile with substantial temporal and operational granularity:
In addition to hourly, daily and seasonal variations in load and solar output, the
Companies’ modeling also accounts for system operating reserve requirements
that capture the need for resources with intra-hour flexibility to be available in order
to meet sub-hourly variations in net load described above. To capture the costs of
increased intra-hour operational reserve requirements due to solar sub-hourly
intermittency, the Companies’ modeling includes a Solar Integration Service Cost
(“SISC”). The SISC reflects the costs associated with increased operating reserve
requirements that are necessary to respond to changes in the net load shape and
intra-hour variability as solar is added to the system. Thus, the Companies’
modeling accounts for the costs associated with increased economic commitment
and dispatch on the system to have resources available to meet the necessary
operating reserve requirements.
Tr. vol. 24, 146.
The Commission notes that no parties presented any evidence or testimony in
opposition to the proposed additions of solar, with several intervenors advocating for
additional solar and storage resources beyond the amounts in Duke’s Recommended
NTAP. The Commission concludes that the modest additions of solar resources in the
2026–2028 time period allow for the addition of needed energy resources in the short
term and are necessary to continue to provide reliable service.
Based on the competent, material, and substantial evidence in the entire record,
the Commission concludes that Duke’s plan to procure up to 2,300 MW of solar resources
in 2026–2028 to be placed in service by 2032–2034 is reasonable. The Commission
106

expects Duke to incorporate appropriate ratepayer protection mechanisms into the
procurement process, including the volume adjustment mechanism, the market depth
adjustment mechanism, and the use of an independent evaluator. These mechanisms
should help ensure that solar procurement remains aligned with the modeling results and
prevailing market conditions and that resources are procured at least cost for Duke’s
customers.
Storage
The Commission is not persuaded by the Public Staff that the appropriate
response to uncertainty regarding the duration and configuration of future BESS
resources is to remove the Duke’s remaining storage targets for the 2033–2034
timeframe. The Commission agrees with Duke that storage resources require substantial
development time and that long-lead equipment, including transformers and breakers,
may require three years or longer to procure. Deferring consideration of resources
needed in the 2033–2034 timeframe until the next CPIRP could thus unnecessarily
constrain Duke’s ability to timely develop and interconnect those resources. The
Commission therefore finds it reasonable to maintain the Companies’ BESS targets
through 2034. At the same time, the Commission is persuaded that further evaluation of
the appropriate duration of these resources is warranted. Notably, the Recommended
Portfolio economically selected 200 MW of 8-hour BESS in 2028, while Duke has
indicated that it does not presently intend to procure 8-hour BESS resources. Accordingly,
the Commission directs Duke to file a report with the 2027 CPIRP evaluating the relative
costs, commercial availability, development timelines, operational characteristics, and
reliability benefits of 4-hour and 8-hour BESS and explaining how that evaluation will
inform the duration and configuration of BESS resources procured to meet the storage
targets approved herein. The Commission expects Duke to continue to evaluate
advances in storage technologies and market conditions and to modify the mix and
duration of BESS resources used to meet these targets as technologies develop and
become commercially available and cost-effective.
The Commission finds that a separate comprehensive report regarding LDES filed
contemporaneously with the 2027 CPIRP is unnecessary, as Duke currently monitors
LDES and has stated in rebuttal that it will file an update with its 2027 CPIRP. The
Commission finds that Duke’s current monitoring practice, and its intention to continue to
monitor LDES market availability and include an update in its 2027 CPIRP application,
are reasonable and sufficient. However, the Commission notes that the information
suggested by the Public Staff, in particular requests one through four of its recommended
report, include information which the Commission anticipates will be valuable both for
Duke as well as interested parties in the next CPIRP proceeding. Tr. vol. 18, 243.
Based on the evidence presented on the record and as summarized herein, the
Commission finds it reasonable to approve up to 2,700 MW of additional BESS,
incremental to the 2,700 MW of BESS previously approved by the Commission, for a total
of up to 5,400 MW of total system BESS planned to be online by 2034. The 5,400 MW
total battery storage includes both standalone and storage paired with solar. In making
this finding, the Commission gives significant weight to the evidence presented by Duke
107

and the support from Public Staff for this addition. The Commission also concludes that
Duke complied with the directives from the 2024 CPIRP Order regarding the evaluation
of solar and battery resources at existing utility-owned solar, coal and CT sites, by
performing a surplus interconnection evaluation detailed in Appendix K of the
2025 CPIRP.
The Commission also concludes that Duke’s stakeholder engagement and
development of a new PPA option for QFs with expiring contracts submitted to
the Commission for approval in Duke’s avoided cost proceeding complies with
the Commission’s directive in the 2024 CPIRP Order. As ordered in Docket
No. E-100, Sub 211, the Commission has approved this retrofit program for QFs with
expiring PPAs, finding Duke’s proposal reasonable and appropriate, offering a meaningful
additional negotiated contract option which may contribute to the system through battery
additions.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 36–42
Near-Term Development Activities for Onshore and Offshore Wind
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Chapter 4 and
Appendices C, H, and K of the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke witnesses
Justin LaRoche and Mark McIntire; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s IRP and
Near-Term Actions Panel (Beatty, Quinto, and Snider); the testimony and exhibits of
Public Staff’s Non-Thermal Resources Panel (Hill and Weaver); the testimony and
exhibits of Public Staff witnesses Darrell Brown, Metz, and Michna; the testimony and
exhibits of AGO witness Burgess; the testimony and exhibits of NCSEA witness Glick; the
testimony and exhibits of SACE, et al. witnesses Michael Goggin and Roumpani; and the
entire record.
Onshore Wind
The 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke to take reasonable steps to pursue the
development and procurement of 1,200 MW of onshore wind to achieve commercial
operation by 2033, including at least 300 MW by 2031, and authorized Duke to incur up
to $65.6 million for the development of three annual tranches of onshore wind through
2026 for purpose of putting 1,200 MW of onshore wind generation into service by 2033.
2024 CPIRP Order at 177.
The 2025 CPIRP describes the changes to the market and policy landscape for
onshore wind, since the issuance of the 2024 CPIRP Order, that impact the costs, risks,
and availability of developing this potential resource in the Carolinas. 2025 CPIRP App. H
at 18–19; tr. vol. 12, 126. Duke also states that recent development experience suggests
that the viability and scalability of onshore wind within Duke’s service territory is more
constrained than previously anticipated. 2025 CPIRP App. H at 17–18. Specifically, the
White House issued a Wind Memorandum in January 2025 that prohibits the issuance of
new or renewed approvals, rights of way, permits, leases, or loans for onshore or offshore
wind projects pending completion of additional federal agency reviews. Id. at 18–19. In
108

addition, federal energy tax credits available for onshore wind were halted by the
H.R.1 Reconciliation Bill and any onshore wind projects that have not started construction
by July 4, 2026, are not eligible to receive the production or investment tax credits,
resulting in decreased cost-effectiveness. Id. at 19. The advanced manufacturing tax
credit for wind energy components sold after 2027 was terminated, potentially further
impacting domestic supply chain dynamics for onshore wind. Id. As a result, Duke’s
modeling assumptions for any new onshore wind resource exclude benefits from federal
energy tax credits. Id. In July 2025 the Department of the Interior issued a memorandum
requiring additional review by agencies for solar and wind facilities, creating further
uncertainty regarding the timeline of obtaining federal review or decisions during the
development stages of new onshore wind projects. Id.
Duke states that they have advanced their onshore wind siting and development
efforts, but the extent to which onshore wind can be successfully sited, permitted, and
cost-effectively operated has yet to be demonstrated. Id. at 17. Since the 2024 CPIRP
Order, Duke has: (1) selected, contracted and integrated an onshore wind development
services partner into its development efforts; (2) further evaluated and screened potential
sites identified from the previously completed siting feasibility study for buildability and
viability; and (3) initiated stakeholder and landowner engagement for select identified
potential sites. Tr. vol. 12, 129. Duke was unable to secure site control over any potential
sites in advance of the 2025 interconnection cluster study and therefore did not submit
any interconnection applications into this cluster study. Id. Duke has spent approximately
$1.3 million in development expenses as of July 31, 2025, reflecting a slower pace of
execution in light of significant policy changes, uncertainty in the development and
execution of onshore wind, and Duke’s progress engaging with landowners and other
stakeholders. Id. at 130.
Based on the ongoing execution experience, Duke’s modeling assumptions
reduced the availability of onshore wind to 300 MW per year beginning in 2034, with a
cumulative system capacity of 1,050 MW total for selection throughout the planning
period. Id. at 130–31. Onshore wind was not identified as being needed in near-term in
the Recommended Portfolio and, given the evolving policy environment impacting
onshore wind development, Duke is no longer planning to incur material incremental
development costs in the near term. Id. at 131. Instead, Duke’s Recommended NTAP
proposes limited activities to further evaluate the viability and scalability of onshore wind
within Duke’s service territory, including: (1) continuing limited landowner and stakeholder
engagements and evaluation of the need and benefit of conducting wind resource
campaigns; (2) monitoring federal and state policy environments for impacts to the
potential development of this resource; and (3) continuing to evaluate resource viability,
future availability and timing assumptions in advance of the next Carolinas Resource Plan
and update development activities as part of the 2027 NTAP and Execution Plan to
support those needs. Id.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver opined that Duke’s decision to omit onshore
wind development activities was not based on a comprehensive least-cost or least-regrets
109

approach. Tr. vol. 18, 261. They testified that if no onshore wind development activities
occurred until the 2027 CPIRP, the first available in-service date for onshore wind would
be delayed until 2039, based on Duke’s assumed nine-year development and execution
timeline. Id. at 262–63. Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver recommended Duke
continue moderate development activities to preserve optionality to procure 300 MW of
onshore wind by 2036, including landowner outreach at the remaining categories of sites
identified in the confidential testimony of the Public Staff Non-Thermal Resources Panel,
efforts to establish site control, and meteorological tower installation and operation, with
an authorized spend of no more than $1.8 million through 2028. Id. at 195, 265, 321.
Public Staff witness Metz testified that to the extent that wind profiles are less than what
they are expecting, then the economics of the overall resource in question may no longer
be valid and the Public Staff would need to adjust and try to get energy capacity from a
different resource. Tr. vol. 14, 82–83. Public Staff witness Weaver testified that as Duke
does not have actual measured meteorological wind speed data, development of the
meteorological tower would allow Duke to collect site-specific wind speed data and
provide concrete information as to the viability of onshore wind as a resource. Tr. vol. 18,
322. Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver also recommended the Commission require
Duke to submit quarterly reports on onshore wind development progress and meet
semi-annually with Public Staff to discuss progress. Id. at 195, 278.
Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver explained that onshore wind was selected
within the Base Planning Period in a majority of Duke’s models. Id. at 259. Onshore wind
was selected before 2036 in 48% of Duke’s models, and nearly 70% of sensitivities
selected onshore wind within the planning period. Id. The Public Staff’s modeling selected
150 MW of onshore wind in 2039 for the Public Staff’s Recommended Portfolio. Id. Other
sensitivities selected onshore wind before 2040 suggesting onshore wind is a needed
resource in the near- to intermediate-term. Id at 260. Additionally, Duke’s high load
sensitivity accelerated some onshore wind into the mid-2030s. 2025 CPIRP App. C at 54.
Witness Weaver stated that onshore wind continues to be selected as a modeling
resource, and concluded that to preserve optionality, the steps recommended by the
Public Staff need to be taken to ensure onshore wind is still available in the 2030s.
Tr. vol. 18, 325. Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver expressed concerns around
Duke’s decision to omit onshore wind development activities from Duke’s Recommended
NTAP restricted capacity expansion options, which can expose ratepayers to volatile fuel
pricing. Id. at 261.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
SACE et al. witness Goggin agreed with Duke’s assessment of wind, noting that
onshore wind is highly complementary to other renewable resources and provides a
hedge against fuel price volatility. Tr. vol. 22, 291. He explained that wind and solar
produce output at opposite times of the day and year. Id. Witness Goggin testified that
the 2025 CPIRP overstated the challenges associated with bringing wind generation
online. Id. at 244. He claimed that wind resources can be built in less than a year, and that
there are active third-party onshore wind developers in North Carolina. Id. at 292–93.
Witness Goggin recommended that Duke pursue wind resources in the near term through
110

PPAs with wind plants in North Carolina and contract with wind plants in other regions.
Id. at 294.
SACE et al. witness Roumpani noted that the 2025 NTAP includes approximately
1200 MW less onshore wind relative to the approved 2023 NTAP. Tr. vol. 20, 305. She
testified that portfolios with greater levels of resources such as wind reduce overall
system costs, reduce dependence on volatile fuel prices, and improve system resilience.
Id. at 325–26. Witness Roumpani recommended Duke investigate opportunities to deploy
onshore wind resources within its service territories, and through PPAs in PJM or the
Midcontinent Independent System Operator. Id. at 299. She stated that Duke did not
rigorously follow through on Duke’s stipulated agreement to pursue 1,200 MW of onshore
wind by 2033, with at least 300 MW by 2031, and to explore the feasibility of feasibility of
an additional 600 MW within that timeframe. Witness Roumpani added that “onshore wind
resources were neither deployed, procured, nor even meaningfully planned”, and
determined that Duke had not sufficiently demonstrated it had pursued all reasonable
development activities. Id. at 373–74.
AGO witness Burgess recommended that Duke’s Recommended Portfolio include
an additional 300 MW of onshore wind to be in service by 2040. Tr. vol. 22, 124. He
recommended the NTAP include consideration of non-firm imports, including wind, to
meet the energy needs of large load customers. Id. at 88.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
Given the current policy and market environment, Duke opposed the Public Staff’s
and other Intervenors’ recommendations that Duke pursue additional near-term onshore
wind procurement activities. Tr. vol. 23, 86. Duke witness LaRoche noted that Duke’s
modeling did not select any meaningful amount of onshore wind during the planning
horizon under the Recommended Portfolio or most of the sensitivities. Id. at 87–89.
Regarding Duke’s efforts to pursue onshore wind development as directed by the
Commission in the 2024 CPIRP Order, Duke witness LaRoche testified that Duke’s efforts
to advance onshore wind development indicated substantial challenges to the ability to site,
permit, construct and operate onshore wind facilities in a cost-effective manner in the
Carolinas at this time. Id. at 91. The $1.3 million spent reflects Duke’s efforts in light of
significant policy changes, uncertainty in the development and execution of onshore wind,
as well as Duke’s progress engaging with landowners and other stakeholders. Id. at 91–92.
Duke also opposed the Public Staff’s recommendation for quarterly reporting
requirements and semiannual meetings regarding onshore wind development as overly
burdensome given the limited scope of the proposed near-term activities. Id. at 91. Duke
witness LaRoche testified that it is appropriate to provide updates on onshore wind
evaluation, development considerations, and policy developments in the next CPIRP. Id.

111

Offshore Wind
Duke Direct Testimony
The 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke to issue an offshore wind Acquisition ARFI
for up to 2,400 MW of offshore wind, of which 800 to 1,100 MW should achieve
commercial operation by 2034 and 2,200 to 2,400 MW should achieve commercial
operation by 2035, and: (1) to engage an independent evaluator to support the ARFI;
(2) to work with the Public Staff to develop an asset acquisition reference price for
incorporation into the ARFI to assess the cost competitiveness of offshore wind; and (3) to
incur up to $1.4 million to develop and administer the ARFI. 2024 CPIRP Order at 145,
179. Duke was further directed to file the results of the ARFI with the Commission. If the
definitive pricing submitted in the ARFI resulted in projects that were at or below the asset
acquisition reference price, Duke was to proceed with issuing an RFP for procurement of
offshore wind or file a request with the Commission explaining in detail why the RFP
should not be pursued or should be delayed. Id. at 179. The Commission also directed
Duke, in the Commission’s Order Ruling on Avangrid’s Petition for Declaratory Ruling
issued April 29, 2025, to consider both build-own-transfer and joint ownership proposals
submitted through the ARFI in the 2025 CPIRP modeling. Tr. vol. 24, 283–84.
The 2025 CPIRP explains the shifting federal energy policy and market uncertainty
occurring in the offshore wind industry since the 2023 CPIRP filing, which affect cost,
risks and availability of this potential resource in the Carolinas. 2025 CPIRP App. H at
25–31; tr. vol. 11, 223–27. Specifically, the White House issued a memorandum in
January 2025 that halted all federal permitting activity on existing leases pending
completion of additional federal agency reviews, impeding necessary federal permit
activities specifically for existing North Carolina offshore wind lease areas. 2025 CPIRP
App. H at 26. Offshore wind projects are also exposed to other financial, stakeholder,
schedule and technical uncertainties. Id. Specifically, the H.R.1 Reconciliation Bill altered
federal tax credits available to wind generation, such that any offshore wind projects that
did not start construction by July 4, 2026, or have not been placed in service by end of
July 2027, will not be eligible to receive the production or investment tax credits, resulting
in a decreased cost-effectiveness of non-eligible offshore wind projects. Id.
As a result of the changing federal policy, further development of offshore wind
and the cost-effectiveness of this resource in the near term are limited. Tr. vol. 11, 225.
The 2025 CPIRP described the process to issue and administer the ARFI as
directed by the Commission in the 2024 CPIRP Order, and described the results that were
filed with the Commission on August 11, 2025. 2025 CPIRP App. H at 25, 30–32. Duke
witness McIntire explained that all the ARFI submissions exceeded the ARFI reference
price and therefore Duke did not take any further procurement action, but used the
risk-adjusted ARFI submissions to support the modeling of offshore wind in the
2025 CPIRP. Tr. vol. 11, 229. He noted that these modeling assumptions included an
updated in-service date of 2037. Id. at 230–31; 2025 CPIRP App. H at 32. Witness
McIntire noted that the 2025 CPIRP explained that offshore wind was not identified as
112

part of the most reasonable, least-cost plan during the planning period. Id. at 230;
pet. at 37.
Duke witness McIntire explained that of the $1.4 million authorized by the
2024 CPIRP Order to develop and administer the ARFI, Duke has incurred approximately
$450,000 in ARFI expense. Tr. vol. 11, 231. The Duke Recommended NTAP includes
monitoring the offshore wind industry and continuing to evaluate the resource option in
future CPIRPs, but does not recommend further development activities at this time.
Id. at 230–31.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver supported Duke’s decision not to pursue
offshore wind at this time. Tr. vol. 18, 269. They explained that the Public Staff’s own
modeling of offshore wind did not result in the selection of offshore wind before 2042.
Id. at 271, 273.
Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver testified that they found errors in the Asset
Acquisition Reference Price, which was explained in order to provide the Commission
with a thorough post-analysis report on the ARFI, but despite this error they maintained
their position that offshore wind should not have been procured. Id. at 270. Witnesses Hill
and Weaver testified that multiple ownership structures were not incorporated into the
modeling as directed by the Commission’s ARFI Order. Id. at 272. However, the Public
Staff developed its own inputs for offshore wind based on a subset of ARFI submissions
and found that the effects were minimal and did not change the Public Staff’s
recommendation. Id. at 272–73.
Witnesses Hill and Weaver did not recommend any near-term actions to procure
or develop offshore wind at this time and supported Duke’s proposed NTAP actions and
recommended Duke re-evaluate offshore wind modeling parameters in future CPIRPs
based on the current market and regulatory environment at that time. Witnesses Hill and
Weaver noted that “not pursuing offshore wind at this time will likely reduce the availability
of offshore wind in the future.” Id. at 273–74.
Public Staff witnesses Brown and Michna found Duke’s proposed delay of offshore
wind resources and the removal of offshore wind from Duke’s Recommended NTAP to
be reasonable for planning purposes at this time due to the recent federal policy changes
and the results of the ARFI and recommended the Commission discontinue
additional deferral related to the development of offshore wind. Tr. vol. 12, 221, 229;
tr. vol. 13, 40, 104.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
There were no modeling results nor any testimony provided by any intervenors that
recommended the selection of offshore wind in the NTAP. Tr. vol. 24, 293.

113

Duke Rebuttal Testimony
Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained how Duke complied with the
Commission’s directive to consider build-own-transfer and joint ownership proposals in the
2025 CPIRP modeling by conducting a price sensitivity that considered a 30% reduction
in the installed costs of offshore wind that encapsulated the alternative proposals. Id. at
285, 292. The Low Wind Cost Sensitivity resulted in nearly the same amount of total
resources across the portfolio and did not result in the selection of any offshore wind
through 2040. 2025 CPIRP App. C at 57–59. No modeling conducted by Duke or any other
party resulted in the selection of offshore wind before 2040. Tr. vol. 24, 293.
Discussion and Conclusions
Onshore Wind
The Commission acknowledges the substantial change in policy and market
conditions affecting onshore wind as presented in the 2025 CPIRP and by Duke witness
LaRoche. The Commission also notes that Duke’s modeling does not economically select
onshore wind in the near-term and mostly deploys the resource in the 2040s.
However, the Commission notes the tension in Duke’s proposed near-term actions
for onshore wind. Despite witness LaRoche’s statements that the viability and scalability
of onshore wind within Duke’s service territory is more constrained than previously
anticipated, Duke continues to include onshore wind as a selectable resource in its
modeling exercises. Additionally, Duke proposes to cease any activities that will help it
determine the viability of onshore wind and ascertain whether this is a resource that can
be relied upon in the later years of the planning horizon. The Commission is persuaded
by Public Staff witness Metz’s testimony that Duke must assess the viability of onshore
wind to determine if Duke will need to substitute alternative capacity resources into future
resource plans. The Commission is also persuaded by the Public Staff Non-Thermal
Resources Panel’s testimony that collecting meteorological tower data would allow Duke
to collect site-specific wind speed data to provide concrete information as to the viability
of onshore wind-powered electrical generation. Given the long lead-times for onshore
wind, the Commission believes conducting minimal due diligence at this time to assess
the viability of onshore wind will prevent potentially disruptive changes to modeling
assumptions and resource plans in future iterations of the CPIRP. The Commission does
not find it necessary to direct Duke to meet regularly with the Public Staff to provide
progress updates, but is persuaded that Duke should be required to file semiannual
reports to keep the Commission updated on this directive.
Accordingly, to preserve optionality, the Commission concludes it is appropriate to
direct Duke to conduct moderate development activities to determine the viability of
onshore wind in North Carolina, including outreach activities to the remaining categories
of sites identified in the confidential testimony of the Public Staff Non-Thermal Resources
Panel and securing site control and meteorological tower data collection. The
Commission further finds that Duke should be required to incur those costs necessary to
114

conduct these moderate development activities and that Duke should file semiannual
reports in this docket on moderate development activity progress and costs incurred.
Offshore Wind
The Commission also concludes that Duke substantially complied with the
Commission’s prior order to conduct an ARFI and to consider the ARFI’s offshore wind
build-own-transfer and joint ownership proposals in the 2025 CPIRP modeling by
modeling a Low Wind Cost Sensitivity that captured the potential cost savings of
alternative bids. Both Duke’s modeling and the Public Staff’s modeling confirmed that
offshore wind should not be included as part of a least-cost resource plan at this time.
Based on the competent, material, and substantial evidence presented, the
Commission finds and concludes that it is reasonable to approve Duke’s plans to monitor
regulatory and policy developments and future availability of onshore and offshore wind
and continue to evaluate the cost and availability of wind resource options in
future CPIRPs.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 43–51
Near-Term Development Activities for New and Existing Natural Gas Resources
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Chapter 4 and
Appendix I to the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Natural Gas and
Low-Carbon Fuels Panel (Daniel Donochod, Peter Hoeflich, and H. Lee Mitchell, IV); the
testimony and exhibits of Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel (Beatty, Quinto, and
Snider); the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Reliability and Operational Resilience Panel
(Holeman and O’Connor); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff witness Michna; the
testimony and exhibits of Public Staff’s Thermal Resources Panel (Michna, Nader, and
Lawrence); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff’s System Reliability and Operations
Panel (Ssengonzi and Thomas); the testimony and exhibits of AGO witness Burgess; the
testimony and exhibits of CEBA witness Urlaub; the testimony and exhibits of EDF
witness Patrylak; the testimony and exhibits of NCSEA witnesses Lin and Glick; the
testimony and exhibits of NC WARN witness Powers; the testimony and exhibits of SACE,
et al. witnesses Roumpani and Goggin; the testimony and exhibits of CIGFUR witness
Fitzhenry; and the entire record.
Duke Direct Testimony
New Natural Gas Resources
Duke’s Reliability and Operational Resilience Panel testified that new natural
gas-fired generating resources are necessary for system reliability in all portfolios
modeled by Duke. The Panel explained that Duke fulfills a federal- and state-mandated
obligation to plan and operate its generating fleets to provide reliable power system
operations to their customers 24 hours a day, seven days a week, 365 days per year.
Tr. vol. 7, 111. The Panel testified that as Duke retires aging generation and adds variable
and energy limited resources to the system, the tools available to system operators to
115

ensure system reliability are rapidly changing. Id. at 118–20. The Panel emphasized that
dispatchability becomes more critical as additional complexity is introduced into the grid.
Id. at 135.
The Reliability and Operational Resilience Panel testified that gas CC and CT
resources are a foundational component of reliability in the 2025 CPIRP. Id. at 138. CC
units can provide both baseload energy and load-following capability as needed to match
the variable output profiles of other generation types and the ebb and flow of customer
demands throughout the day. Id. CTs provide essential capacity to meet high loads and
bring fast response capabilities when facing unexpected system events or the
increasingly high ramping needs projected in future years. Id. Both resource types can
provide on-demand output for multiple days at a time if needed. Id. The Panel emphasized
that gas resources form the backbone of new, dispatchable capacity additions in the Duke
Recommended Portfolio and that a diverse resource portfolio gives system operators a
variety of tools and capabilities, thereby avoiding the reliability risks associated with
relying too heavily on any one resource type. Id. at 122.
Similarly, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that new CCs and
CTs are needed to meet near-term load growth and to maintain the reliability of the
system. The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that the incremental system
winter capacity need grows to approximately 12,000 MW by 2034 and approximately
19,000 MW by 2040. Tr. vol. 24, 70. Across the 30 Sensitivity Analysis Portfolios
developed in the Plan, the model selected CC capacity in the range of 5,460 to 8,190 MW
through 2035 and CT capacity in the range of 2,395 to 2,825 MW through 2034,
demonstrating a robust need for natural gas resources across a broad range of planning
assumptions. Id. at 111 tbl. 4. The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that Duke’s
Recommended NTAP includes 2,825 MW of CT capacity through 2034 and 6,825 MW of
CC capacity through 2035. Id. at 121 tbl. 5.
Duke’s Natural Gas and Low-Carbon Fuels Panel testified that natural gas-fueled
generating resources continue to be critical to maintaining system reliability and
managing operational risks, especially during prolonged extreme weather events.
Tr. vol. 9, 129, 151. The Panel testified that Duke assumes a five-year timeline for project
execution of a CT and a six-year timeline for project execution of a CC. Id. at 129.
Considering these timelines, the Panel argued that it is important to adhere to Duke’s
proposed schedule for development of new natural gas resources because these units
need to be in service and available for dispatch prior to the retirement of existing coal
assets. Id. at 130. Any delay in their in-service dates would directly extend coal unit
operations and could constrain Duke’s ability to serve new customers and achieve the
Commission-directed 22% planning reserve margin. Id.
In Appendix I, Duke provided updates to CCs and CTs that had been selected in
the 2023 CPIRP and are currently under development, including CC1, CC2, CC3, CT1,
CT2, CT3, and CT4. 2025 CPIRP App. I at 11–12. Duke also detailed its natural gas fuel
supply strategy for its natural gas fleet, which consists of 447,560 dekatherms per day
(Dth/day) of interstate FT service subscribed to on Transcontinental Gas Pipe Line
Company, LLC (Transco). Id. at 2. Duke described that it has executed additional
116

agreements with Transco for 1 million Dth/day of interstate FT on Transco’s Southeast
Supply Enhancement (SSE) expansion, and with Mountain Valley Pipeline, LLC (MVP)
for 250,000 Dth/day of FT rights on MVP’s Southgate project (Southgate). Id. These
additional FT contracts will provide sufficient natural gas fuel supply for Duke’s entire
natural gas fleet, through the CC3 unit.
In its NTAP for the 2025 CPIRP, Duke requested acknowledgement of 2,730 MW
from two CC natural gas units, identified as CC4 and CC5, and 1,111 MW from CTs,
including one 255 MW F-class CTs, identified as CT5 or the Smith CT, and two 425 MW
advanced J-class CTs, identified as CT6 and CT7. 2025 CPIRP App. at 38–39. Duke
stated in its near-term actions that it intended to file a CPCN application for CT5 in quarter
four (Q4) of 2025 and that it intended to finalize its fuel plans and file CPCN applications
for CC4, CC5, CT6, and CT7 in 2026. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 20. Duke stated that it intends
to place CT5 in-service by 2030, CT6 and CT7 in-service by 2032, CC4 in-service by
2032, and CC5 in-service by 2033. Id.
Duke’s modeling assumes a 35-year operational life for new natural gas-fueled CT
and CC generating resources. 2025 CPIRP App I at 15. Duke has made specification and
design upgrades, as well as included provisions for future expanded hydrogen use, for
the new natural gas resources under development. According to Duke, future utilization
of hydrogen, or carbon capture and sequestration, methane pyrolysis, or some future
method that has not yet been developed, will allow continued use of these critically
important resources after 2050. Id.
Existing Natural Gas Resources
With respect to the existing natural gas fleet, the Natural Gas and Low-Carbon
Fuels Panel testified that Duke has continued the execution of planned uprate projects
intended to expand the flexibility and efficiency of the existing natural gas generation fleet,
consistent with the Commission’s direction in its prior CPIRP orders. Tr. vol. 9, 132;
2025 CPIRP App. I at 13. The Panel testified that four of the seven CC uprate projects
have increased their expected capacity, resulting in an additional 60 MW beyond the
212 MW identified in the previous plan. 2025 CPIRP App. I at 13. The Panel also identified
capacity uprate projects for existing CTs, which result in another 207 MW of incremental
capacity. Id. In total, between the CC and CT uprates, Duke plans to gain approximately
484 MW from the existing natural gas fleet in the 2025–2029 timeframe. Id.
Fuel Security
Pursuant to the 2023 Carbon Plan Stipulation and as ordered in the 2023 Carbon
Plan Order, Duke prepared a fuel security strategy for its natural gas fleet for the 2025
CPIRP. Duke is proposing a fuel security strategy consisting of firm FT contracts and
ELNG. Sufficient gas supply for its natural gas fleet including CC1, CC2, and CC3 would
be obtained through FT contracts at (1) MVP, (2) Transco’s SSE, and (3) MVP Southgate.
Id. at 133. For CC gas unit additions beyond CC3, Duke proposed to achieve fuel security
via ELNG, which would provide both dual-season fuel security and year-round intraday
flexibility for system gas supply balancing. Id. at 140–41. The Panel testified that the
117

dual-season approach would allow ELNG to provide incremental fuel security to manage
both multi-day winter and summer extreme weather events and the that the intraday
flexibility would complement increased intermittent resources on the electric system by
liquifying excess intraday natural gas or vaporizing stored volumes to meet demand on
an hourly basis. Id. at 140.
Prior to the development of modeling inputs and assumptions, Duke conducted
preliminary evaluations to establish base case assumptions for inputs into the modeling
process. 2025 CPIRP App. C at 43. One of these preliminary evaluations is a fuel security
evaluation for the purposes of developing a base assumption for incremental fuel security
costs and strategies for new gas resources. Id. For this evaluation, Duke evaluated two
fuel security options – Enhanced FT 13 or ELNG. Id. The capacity expansion modeling
was performed separately for each fuel security option, allowing the model to select an
optimized portfolio based on the advancing load forecast as well as each fuel security
strategy’s associated fuel security costs and the fuel availability associated with each
option. Id. ; tr. vol. 10, 29–30. The EFT fuel security option was modeled in the capacity
expansion model with the associated fixed cost for applicable new gas resources along
with access to incremental natural gas commodity; ELNG, on the other hand, was
modeled in the capacity expansion model with only the fixed cost associated with ELNG
for new gas resources, without capturing added operational impacts of ELNG to the
system due to the simplified system simulations and the complexity of ELNG as a natural
gas storage resource. 2025 CPIRP App. C at 43–44. Following development of the
optimized portfolios, production cost modeling was conducted for each portfolio utilizing
the specific fuel strategy assumptions for EFT and ELNG to capture the operation of the
system associated with each. Id. at 44. The EFT production cost modeling captured the
fixed costs and access to additional natural gas commodity volumes. Id. The ELNG
portfolio, due to the complexities of the resource, was run through the production cost
modeling in two ways: (1) with the fixed costs along with operational parameters
associated with ELNG and their associated operational costs and impacts to the system;
and (2) using a simplified fuel modeling setup that captures the fixed costs and limits the
system’s access to incremental natural gas supply, without the added operational
parameters. Id. Ultimately, ELNG was determined to be the more cost-effective option
relative to EFT from the Gulf Coast. Id. Duke noted that the IRP models cannot capture
the intraday storage benefits of the ELNG facility explicitly, and therefore the cost
comparison is a conservative estimate of the benefits of ELNG that is based on the fuel
security benefits alone. Id.
Witnesses from the Natural Gas and Low Carbon Fuels Panel provided additional
details on the proposed ELNG fuel security strategy during the expert witness hearing.
They clarified that the six-year timeline assumed for new CCs includes time necessary to
construct ELNG. Tr. vol. 9, 171. They also clarified that the fuel security strategy for CC6
is assumed to be ELNG. Tr. vol. 10, 27–28. Despite confirming that there are no facilities
akin to Duke’s ELNG proposal currently in operation, witness Mitchell stated that Duke

13 Defined as the ability to nominate gas delivery for an increment of hours less than 24 in each

gas day. EFT in this fuel security evaluation assumes Gulf Coast EFT service.

118

would not be a first mover on ELNG because it does not contemplate a technology that
is not in existence today. Tr. vol. 9, 173–74.
Witness Mitchell also testified at the hearing that publicly available Transco
operational capacity data demonstrated that there is sufficient non-peak day natural gas
transportation and supply capacity to support Duke’s proposal for ELNG to provide fuel
security for CCs 4 and 5. Tr. vol. 26, 13–14, 77. Witness Mitchell walked the Commission
through Duke Energy Redirect Natural Gas and Low Carbon Fuels Direct Exhibit 1, which
detailed operational capacity for Transco on May 26, 2026. Witness Mitchell emphasized
that on that May 26, there was over 2.9 BCF of capacity unutilized on Transco’s system.
Id. at 40–41. On that same day, approximately 3.1 BCF was scheduled to come to the
Carolinas, and Duke consumed 1.5 BCF of that scheduled gas, while only holding the FT
rights for a third of that consumption amount. Id. at 41–42. Witness Mitchell explained
that this example shows that on a day like May 26, and for 300-plus days a year, there
would be adequate market capacity to fill the ELNG tanks. Id. at 41–43. Witness Mitchell
emphasized that on a cold January day, this market capacity would not be available and
the ELNG tank could be discharged when Transco is fully utilized. Id. at 41. Witness
Mitchell further explained that the stored ELNG gas provides an additional benefit during
extreme weather days in the form of arbitrage, as the ELNG facility would be able to store
lower-cost gas and time shift its use during higher-priced months. Id. at 63–64.
Regarding hydrogen, witness Hoeflich confirmed that the proposed CTs are
50% hydrogen-capable and that the industry is on track to reach 100% hydrogen-capable
combustors in 2030. Id. at 55. Witness Hoeflich discussed lessons learned from Duke’s
work on its DeBary site, which was converted to 100% hydrogen, including testing that
required that Duke develop a faster hydrogen shut-off valve to prevent potential flameout
in the CT. Id. at 56. Witness Hoeflich also testified that Duke had made progress on NOX
suppression and was able to reduce NOX emissions out of the unit to levels almost
comparable to those of its natural gas units. Id. In response to Commissioner questions
regarding what will be required to spur hydrogen availability, witness Hoeflich testified
that economies of scale may emerge as heavy transportation deploys hydrogen as a fuel
source and as other nations move towards hydrogen production and use. Id. at 57–58. In
response to Commissioner questions on blending, witness Hoeflich stated that Duke has
not yet outlined its plan for blending because it is anticipated to occur far in the future, but
that Duke would likely plan for average blending across its units, with some units starting
at higher blend amounts and others blending more gradually over time. Id. at 59. Witness
Hoeflich clarified that hydrogen cost projections look at market projections of future
hydrogen costs and do not base cost assumptions on the cost of early demonstration
projects or early movers. Id. at 62–63.
The Panel provided an update on the status of the CAA Section 111 Final Rule,
stating that the proposed final rule was submitted and is under review at the Office of
Management and Budget. Id. at 69–70. Witness Donochod stated that there are two
potential proposals – one which removes all Section 111 requirements and a second
which retains the Phase 1 requirements for new gas units. Id. at 69.

119

Public Staff Direct Testimony
The Public Staff, in the direct testimony of witness Michna filed on March 30, 2026,
and as amended during the expert witness hearing, testified that the Public Staff was
aligned with Duke that there is a need for CT3 and CT4 in 2030, CT6 and CT7 in 2032,
CC4 in 2032, and CC5 in 2033, with the exception of CT5. Tr. vol. 14, 13. As of the
hearing, the Public Staff maintained that Duke had not sufficiently demonstrated a need
for CT5. Tr. vol. 13, 11.
On June 30, 2026, the Public Staff filed corrected testimony in the CPCN
proceeding for CT5 pending before the Commission in Docket No. E-2, Sub 1395. Second
Corrected Testimony of Caleb A. Hill, Dustin R. Metz, and Leah M. M. Weaver Public
Staff – North Carolina Utilities Commission, Docket No. E-2, Sub 1395 (Jun. 30, 2026).
In its corrected testimony, the Public Staff withdrew its previous opposition to construction
of CT5 and recommended that the Commission grant DEP a CPCN authorizing
construction of CT5 by 2030. Id. at 4. Specifically, the Public Staff found that there is a
capacity need on the system, and because the Public Staff is not aware of any other
resource that can meet that need in 2030 or 2031, the Public Staff recommends approval
of DEP’s application for a CPCN for CT5. Id. at 53.
The Public Staff Thermal Resources Panel expressed concerns about Duke’s
proposed ELNG fuel security strategy for CCs 4 and 5. Tr. vol. 18, 61–62. In their direct
testimony, the Panel characterized Duke’s ELNG proposal as a first-of-a-kind
implementation, stating that while none of the individual plant components of ELNG are
novel, the combination of those components and the proposed operational plan would be
first-of-a-kind and that ELNG was not an appropriate substitute for interstate FT. Id. at
62–63. During the expert witness hearing, witness Michna stated that Duke’s heightened
load forecast filed in its rebuttal increases reliance on natural gas resources and that
further analysis conducted since the filing of the rebuttal has only increased the Public
Staff’s concerns about ELNG’s ability to replace firm transport gas service. Tr. vol. 13, 12.
The Panel clarified that the Public Staff was not opposed to ELNG as a concept and
recognized that it could bring value to the electricity system; rather, their concern lies in
whether it can fully replace FT. Tr. vol. 18, 145. Witness Michna noted that in its initial
filing, Duke presented ELNG for two CCs that would be energized in 2032 and 2033; the
fuel security strategy for the third CC, CC6, was not necessary at the time of the filing as
that CC was modeled to be placed in service later in the planning horizon. With Duke’s
issuance of an updated load forecast based on its Spring 2026 Large Load Update, CC6
was moved forward in the planning horizon. Id. at 144. Under these new circumstances,
the Public Staff stated that it is unclear whether ELNG can provide fuel security for all
three CCs; if ELNG cannot support CC6, then FT would be required. Id. Because of this,
the Thermal Resources Panel argued that there may be economies of scale of expanding
firm transport in the Carolinas by 2034 and that further analysis is required to ensure that
the fuel security strategy is ultimately least cost to ratepayers. Id. at 145.
In its direct testimony, the Thermal Resources Panel also highlighted that Duke
was unable to provide much detail on the placement, ownership, or operation of potential
ELNG plants. Id. at 68–69. While acknowledging that Duke had provided additional detail
120

in rebuttal, the Public Staff stated that it requires additional information about the impacts
to ratepayers, the configuration of the ELNG facilities and where the facilities will tie into
the gas pipeline system, how the facilities will connect to the electrical system to conduct
liquefaction and vaporization, and how changes in tank size affect the amount of
electricity needed by the facilities, since liquefaction and vaporization will require
significant amounts of electricity. Id. at 151–54.
The Panel likened the analysis needed to the coal pile analysis conducted in the
2019 DEP rate case (Docket E-2, Sub 1142), stating that “inventory stock pipeline
analysis is needed of…the expected draws, the delivery rate, and will the pile survive.”
Id. at 155. On ownership, the Panel stated that it was their understanding that if Duke
owned the ELNG facility and it was considered a part of the overall operation plant, it
would be part of a CPCN process; conversely, if a local distribution company (LDC)
owned the ELNG facility or if it was a standalone ELNG facility, it may not require a CPCN
before the Commission. Id. at 156–57.
Ultimately, the Thermal Resources Panel recommended that Duke submit an
independent third-party analysis to evaluate if Duke’s proposed ELNG fuel security
strategy can provide firm natural gas fuel security. Tr. vol. 13, 12. During the expert
