witness hearing, witness Metz also recommended that Duke should examine potential
natural gas FT and delivery constraints and assess requirements for 1.5 to 2 BCF or more
of natural gas for power generation through 2040. Id. at 15. Witness Metz stated that this
should include, but not be limited to, participation by or even petition of Williams Transco,
a major natural gas pipeline operator, for an open season. Id. Witness Metz specified that
given the change in the load forecast, the Public Staff believed the independent
evaluation should be completed and should, at a minimum, be a part of the CPCN for a
CC that uses ELNG for its fuel security strategy. Tr. vol. 18, 155–56. The Panel also
stated that they believe the independent evaluator’s highest priority is to determine
whether ELNG has the ability to sufficiently replicate firm transport. Id. at 160–61.
In response to Commissioner questions, the Panel expressed uncertainty whether
any evaluator would be willing to conduct the requested analysis. Id. at 168–69. The Panel
stated that Duke does have subject matter experts in-house that could build around the
framework of the intended analysis. Id. at 169. The Public Staff stated that responses to
any bid for an independent evaluator would inform the expected timeline of the analysis,
but that if this analysis is required for a CPCN filed by the end of 2026, the Public Staff
may want this analysis before any actions are taken in the CPCN process, which could
delay the build-out of the natural gas unit and exacerbate already challenging reliability
concerns. Id.
The Public Staff Thermal Resources Panel testified that while hydrogen has
potential as a potential carbon-free fuel source, uncertainty around costs, infrastructure
upgrades, energy use, transportation, and storage makes it a speculative pathway at this
time. Id. at 83–84. The Panel asserted that there is no utility-scale hydrogen marketplace
or distribution network, no precedent or standard for hydrogen transport via pipeline, and
no CCs in commercial operation today that operate on 100% hydrogen fuel, though some
generation units have been able to operate on a blend of hydrogen and natural gas in
121

research settings. Id. at 82–83. The Panel pointed out that Duke’s modeling allows
unreasonable and impractical blending assumptions and can only meet carbon neutrality
if 100% hydrogen blending is permitted. Id. at 88–89. The modeling also assumed that
hydrogen consumed at natural gas plants will be produced using low-carbon energy, but
does not account for the load and new generation required to produce the low-carbon
hydrogen fuel, while also assuming that the hydrogen will qualify for tax credits associated
with burning clean hydrogen. Id. at 81–82. The Panel concluded by stating that through
future iterations of the CPIRP, the realistic specifics of hydrogen will take on increasing
importance because if the level of assumed hydrogen blending does not materialize, other
replacement technologies will need to be substituted in the mid- to long-term. Id. at 90.
Finally, the Panel recommended that the Commission direct Duke to evaluate the
hydrogen landscape for the next iteration of the CPIRP and present an updated,
comprehensive plan for obtaining sufficient hydrogen quantity in the future. Id. at 90–91.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
Several intervenor witnesses raised concerns regarding Duke’s proposed natural
gas buildout. AGO witness Burgess recommended that the Commission decline to
approve CCs 4 and 5 and CTs 6 and 7 and further consider these resources in the next
CPIRP, arguing that the need for those resources was not supported by contracted firm
load growth. Id. at 47, 79. Witness Burgess recommended that the Commission approve
the CT5, as it was selected in the Low Case scenario. Id. at 83. Witness Burgess also
expressed concerns about Duke’s fuel security strategy, arguing that its modeling
assumptions favored a lower FT cost, which in turn favored the selection of new CC units.
Id. at 109–12.
CEBA witness Urlaub testified that Duke’s proposed new natural gas-fueled
resources would exacerbate issues associated with an already constrained natural gas
fuel system. Tr. vol. 20, 126. Specifically, he testified that planning to construct new gas
resources would further increase customer exposure to gas price volatility and commodity
price risks. Id. at 126, 140. Witness Urlaub recommended that the Commission direct
Duke to diversify its resource plan by utilizing more battery storage assets and that the
Commission direct Duke to more fully evaluate the EFT and ELNG strategic options for
natural gas fuel security. Id. at 127, 183.
EDF witness Patrylak evaluated seventeen of Duke’s modeling runs and found that
the first four CCs were consistently selected in the early 2030s, while CC5 was more
sensitive to changes in assumptions and was frequently delayed to 2040. Because of
this, witness Patrylak recommended that the Commission defer consideration of CC5 until
its need was better demonstrated in a future planning cycle. Tr. vol. 22, 139–40, 150.
NCSEA witness Glick conducted alternative modeling which deferred one CC unit
to 2034 and avoided the addition of one CC unit altogether. Tr. vol. 17, 162. Witness Glick
characterized ELNG as an immature technology that would create unnecessary risks to
customers. Id. at 184. NCSEA witness Lin testified that the ELNG concept, while
employing established LNG technology components for fuel security purposes, involves
an intraday flexibility function that is unique and novel. Id. at 264. Witness Lin
122

recommended limiting initial ELNG development to a single pilot or demonstration project
including a minimal increase in tankage size to analyze the enhanced aspect of ELNG
before committing to full-scale deployment. Id. at 258, 260. Witness Lin noted that Duke’s
resource plan assumes its natural gas plants will convert to a hydrogen-based generation
system with accompanying infrastructure. Id. at 270. Witness Lin argued that this
assumption results in a costly capital plan for ratepayers that requires multiple future
developments to succeed in parallel across the planning horizon and creates increased
risk of stranded costs. Id. He concluded that plans to convert all incremental natural gas
assets to hydrogen-firing appear unreasonably costly and risky without additional details
and recommended that Duke present the Commission with cost data for natural gas to
hydrogen conversions. Id. at 270–71.
NC WARN witness Powers contended that Duke’s proposal of new natural gas
units largely hinges on an assumption that these resources will transition to utilize green
hydrogen and that it is unlikely that green hydrogen will be widely available in 2050.
Tr. vol. 22, 217. Instead, witness Powers recommended that Duke substitute its proposed
reliance upon new gas-fired generation with solar plus storage resources. Id. at 223.
SACE et al. witness Roumpani testified that fuel supply infrastructure, cost
escalation, and execution risk are contributing to current affordability concerns and
recommended that the Commission decline to approve construction of CT6, CT7, CC4,
and CC5. Tr. vol. 20, 313-14, 372. SACE et al. witness Goggin testified that Duke’s
proposed increase in natural gas generation in its proposed NTAP poses economic and
reliability risks to ratepayers. Tr. vol. 22, 309. Witness Goggin stated that load shed events
that occurred during Winter Storm Elliott were primarily due to loss of gas generation from
fuel supply interruptions, and that increasing dependence on gas generation exposes
ratepayers to short- and long-term uncertainties in fuel prices. Id. at 321–23. Witness
Goggin also criticized Duke’s calculation of the capacity value of gas generators, which
he claimed did not account for the reliability risks of correlated gas generator outages,
and recommended that Duke calculate the capacity value contributions of conventional
generators using an ELCC or other method. Id. at 316–18.
CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry raised concerns about Duke’s natural gas delivery plans,
including the increasing price of delivery, the price volatility of natural gas, the increasing
utilization rates of the Transco Zone 5 Segment, as well as reliability concerns around the
physical delivery of fuel during system stress. Id. at 37–38. Accordingly, witness Fitzhenry
recommended that Duke include the quantified costs and reliability risks associated with
firm natural gas delivery into its modeling assumptions and adjust the capacity value of
natural gas resources to reflect their ability to operate during periods of peak power and
gas demand. Id. at 40. Witness Fitzhenry expressed concern with Duke’s proposed ELNG
fuel security strategy, stating that LNG is subject to an even greater degree of price volatility
because it is a globally traded commodity. Id. at 39. During the expert witness hearing,
witness Fitzhenry indicated that Duke’s plan to firm up its natural gas pipeline transportation
constraints via ELNG did not rely on proven technology. Id. at 78.
CEBA witness Urlaub, SACE et al. witness Roumpani, NCSEA witness Lin, and
NC WARN witness Powers all raised concerns that new gas resources could become
123

stranded assets if load does not materialize as projected, hydrogen does not develop as
a potential future fuel source, or they are subject to operational constraints imposed by
state and federal law. Tr. vol. 20, 286-87, 369; tr. vol. 22, 218.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified on rebuttal that, considering the
increased load forecast, Duke “need[s] every resource identified in the Duke
Recommended NTAP — and potentially more —to reliably serve current and future
customers.” Tr. vol. 24, 182. Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel further testified
that considering the increased economic development load growth described above,
Duke’s Recommended NTAP is no longer a broadly risk-adjusted set of resources but is
now the floor of what is required to keep the lights on for customers. Id. at 166. The Panel
testified that failure to execute on the Duke Recommended NTAP resources on the
planned timelines will cause the system to be unreliable during a critical period of nearterm load growth. Id. The Natural Gas and Low-Carbon Fuels Panel testified that they
were pleased that Duke and the Public Staff were aligned on the need and timing for CT3,
CT4, CT6, CT7, CC4, and CC5. Tr. vol. 25, 204. The Panel stated that they continue to
support the inclusion of CT5, as it is the only firm dispatchable resource that can deliver
additional capacity by 2030 outside of other resources proposed in the NTAP. Id.
In its rebuttal testimony, Duke’s Natural Gas and Low-Carbon Fuels Panel testified
that fuel security is essential to resource planning because it is necessary to ensure that
dispatchable generation resources are capable of operating when needed to maintain
reliable electric service. Id. at 161. Duke stated that both the Public Staff and the
Commission recognized this due to the directive included in the 2024 CPIRP Order which
directed Duke to provide a firm fuel supply arrangement or other fuel security strategy in
applications seeking CPCNs to construct new CC facilities. Id. at 161–62. Duke stated
that its dispatchable generating units that utilize natural gas rely on interstate pipeline FT,
on-site coal, or diesel dual-fuel capability for fuel security. Id. at 161. The Panel clarified
that Duke is not arguing that ELNG is a substitute for all interstate FT capacity, but that it
is appropriate to diversify the fuel security strategy and optimize the contracted interstate
FT portfolio given the need for dispatchable natural gas-fueled generating facilities. Id. at
163. During the expert witness hearing, witness Mitchell stated that Duke has a high
reliance on Transco and touted both the MVP Southgate project and the proposed ELNG
project as providing needed diversity to that reliance. Tr. vol. 26, 72–73. In response to
Commissioner questions on whether Duke had evaluated shipping LNG into
North Carolina by vessel or train, witness Mitchell stated that Duke did evaluate this
alternative and determined that tankage would still be required to hold any shipped LNG
for time of use and, given the scale of the facility they were evaluating, the solution was
not workable. Id. at 95–96. Witness Mitchell shared that Duke also evaluated expanding
its fuel oil reserves onsite, which was constrained by economic considerations, air
restrictions on the amount of diesel that can be burned, and logistical difficulty of diesel
fuel replenishment. Id. at 97–98.
The Panel argued that ELNG can provide reliability benefits by ameliorating
volatility in Transco’s linepack, which is the amount of natural gas stored inside the
124

pipeline itself due to pressure. Tr. vol. 25, 164–65. The Panel stated that swings in
Transco’s hourly flow rates are increasingly caused by both the electrical load profile and
the profile and intermittency of solar resources, and that ELNG is a superior solution to
adding more pipeline capacity to address these fluctuations. Id. The Panel argued that
when solar generation does not perform as forecasted, gas is typically called upon, which
reduces any linepack available and impacts plans to build linepack for peak hourly usage
within the day. Id. at 170. Witness Mitchell also shared that services provided by ELNG
would not be limited to CC4 and CC5, and that the facilities would help the entire natural
gas fleet. Tr. vol. 26, 74.
The Panel testified that ELNG is not a first-of-a-kind resource and that the
technological components and processes of the assumed ELNG facilities are proven,
well-known, and similar to existing LNG facilities within North Carolina with which the
Commission is familiar. Tr. vol. 25, 150, 157. The Panel explained that multiple utilities
and local distribution companies currently operate LNG facilities in North Carolina,
including Piedmont Natural Gas Company, Inc., Enbridge North Carolina, and other
municipal utilities, though Duke does not participate in the ownership, services, or
operations of any of these facilities. Id. at 159–60. The Panel likened ELNG to a coal pile,
stating that “it can be thought of as the natural gas equivalent of a coal pile at a power
plant that provides in-state fuel security that is not dependent on the interstate pipeline
availability at a given moment in time.” Id. at 153. The Panel explained that the electric
industry is adopting LNG storage for fuel security and that these facilities utilize similar
technological components as the ELNG facilities Duke proposes as a fuel security
solution for CCs 4 and 5. Id. at 154, 161. The Panel argued that while there is currently
no operating LNG facility with an operational plan that precisely matches the proposed
ELNG facility, the essential components are the same as used in LNG facilities in
operation throughout the United States. Id. at 161.
The Panel shared results of a RFI conducted as part of Duke’s due diligence
evaluation to identify LNG liquefaction technologies to implement the ELNG assumption.
Id. at 157. The information collected in this RFI was used to develop Duke’s ELNG
modeling assumptions. Id. at 158.
The Panel responded to intervenor critiques regarding the lack of detail around
ownership, location, and operation of the ELNG facilities, stating that ownership and
site-specific configuration are properly addressed in the CPCN stage for a specific CC
that will utilize ELNG as a fuel security solution. Id. at 168. The Panel also pushed back
against intervenor suggestions that Duke could purchase additional Zone 5 gas during
peak periods, stating that this could create reliability and economic concerns by exposing
customers to locational basis price risk and the real possibility that gas will not be
available in the marketplace at required volumes. Id. at 173. In response to intervenor
concerns that natural gas presents reliability risks due to poor performance during
extreme weather events, the Panel testified that these concerns are inconsistent with
Duke’s operational experience, citing the fact that Duke experienced no FT or fuel supply
curtailments during Winter Storm Fern. Id. at 185. The Panel shared that Duke has
negotiated scarce manufacturing slots for advanced class turbines through 2033 and that
delays in approvals of gas-fueled generation facilities and subsequent delays in planned
125

in-service dates would require renegotiation; Duke warned that there is no guarantee that
Duke would be able to secure slots due to market demand or at previously negotiated
prices. Id. at 193–94. The Panel also argued that delays in approvals of gas-fueled
generation facilities would likely disrupt EPC contractor schedules, resulting in cost
increases and increased execution risks. Id. at 193.
In response to NCSEA witness Lin’s recommendation that the Commission should
limit deployment of the ELNG facility to a pilot or demonstration project, witness Mitchell
disagreed with this recommendation, addressing two points: first, he argued that ELNG
does not use any “new” technology and stated “there is no technology to, quote/unquote,
pilot.” Tr. vol. 26, 78. Second, witness Lin’s testimony discussed upsizing the ELNG tank,
which witness Mitchell stated is not how an ELNG tank works, and that it cannot be
upsized once it is built. Id. at 78–79.
The Panel also expressed concern at the Public Staff’s recommendation that Duke
deploy an independent evaluator for fuel security evaluations to serve CC4 and CC5,
stating that they are concerned that if this evaluation must be prepared in advance and
included with the CC4 CPCN, it may delay the filing and in-service date. Tr. vol. 25, 205.
Further, Duke contended that Duke and the North Carolina LDCs are best positioned to
assess and provide data regarding the availability of capacity to serve the ELNG facility,
and that an independent evaluator would rely on this data without having the expertise of
actually operating within Transco Zone 5. Id. at 208. Duke proposed a collaboration with
the Public Staff in lieu of an independent study. Id. Finally, Duke clarified that despite their
misgivings, they are committed to continuing to engage with the Public Staff and, if
ordered by the Commission, any necessary third parties to evaluate the sufficiency and
cost reasonableness of the ELNG modeling assumption. Id. at 207–08.
In response to witness Metz’s suggestion that Duke assess natural gas transportation
through 2040, witness Mitchell countered that Duke believes there is adequate time to check
and adjust for additional gas supply for any units planned in the mid to late 2030s. Id. at 206.
In response to witness Metz’s suggestion that Duke examine, participate, or petition for
Transco to conduct an open season to determine the availability and pricing for incremental
FT, witness Mitchell stated that Duke already conducted a fuel security evaluation and
provided indicative pricing to the Public Staff. Id. at 206–07. He expressed concern that trying
to address natural gas transportation plans through 2040 could delay Duke from taking
near-term steps crucial for ensuring system reliability. Id. at 206.
Ultimately, the Panel reiterated that, in this proceeding, Duke is not requesting
approval to construct a specific ELNG facility but is rather seeking recognition of ELNG
as a reasonable fuel security planning assumption, subject to further technical review and
approval at the appropriate time in a future CPCN proceeding for CCs 4 and 5. Id. at 200.
However, the Panel did specify that Duke is challenged by the long lead time of CC
construction and that they sought clarity on the ability to use ELNG to serve CC4 and
CC5. Tr. vol. 26, 93–94.
The Panel also provided updates on the progress of securing firm natural gas
transportation capacity for new CC units. The Panel testified that despite appeals filed
126

against Transco SSE and MVP Southgate by certain parties, Duke has confidence that
the projects will ultimately come into service. Tr. vol. 25, 181. The Panel stated that all
intervenors who performed independent modeling showed the need for CC1, CC2, and
CC3, and that any delay in constructing and placing Transco SSE and MVP Southgate
into service will strain Duke’s existing FT capacity and its ability to meet growing demand
in North Carolina with these planned generation assets. Id. at 181–82.
Regarding concerns about Duke’s plans to operate natural gas-fueled resources
after 2050, the IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel testified that there is no requirement to
retire these units in 2050. Id. at 115–16. Instead, Duke sees four pathways for continuing
to operate these resources: utilizing hydrogen, carbon capture and sequestration,
utilization of offset markets, or operating the resources when necessary to ensure system
reliability. Id. at 116. Witness Snider testified that “[t]here’s not going to be a fifth [option]
of let’s just turn them all off . . . That’s not going to happen.” Id.
The Panel testified that the Public Staff’s recommendation that Duke provide a
more detailed plan for future hydrogen utilization in future CPIRPs was well-intentioned
but premature. Id. at 198. The Panel explained that multiple hydrogen production methods
are still currently developing and requiring Duke to describe specific production methods
that will be used over 20-plus years would be a speculative endeavor. Id. Instead, Duke
stated it would continue to monitor and participate in the development of the hydrogen
economy and that future CPIRP iterations would continue to refine CPIRP assumptions
as the landscape matures. Id.
Stipulation on Enhanced Liquefied Natural Gas
On September 18, 2026, Duke and the Public Staff filed an Agreement and
Stipulation regarding the conduct and scope of an independent third-party evaluation of
ELNG. The Stipulation reflects an agreement reached after the evidentiary hearing and
the filing of the parties’ proposed orders concerning the scope of evaluation, supplemental
information Duke will provide to the Public Staff, the filing of the completed evaluation in
this docket, and the timing of future CPCN applications for new natural gas-fired
resources for which ELNG is proposed as a fuel security option.
Discussion and Conclusions
New Natural Gas Resources
The Commission gives substantial weight to the full alignment between Duke and
the Public Staff with respect to the new natural gas-fueled resources in Duke’s
Recommended NTAP. The Commission is also persuaded by the testimony of Duke’s
Reliability and Operational Resilience Panel, that gas CC and CT resources are
foundational components of reliability in the 2025 CPIRP and that all of the Duke
Recommended NTAP resources are needed to maintain or improve system reliability
during this critical period of load growth and coal retirements. The Commission is not
persuaded by the various intervenor critiques of Duke’s modeling related to the selection
of new natural gas resources.
127

Accordingly, the Commission concludes that the new natural gas resources
identified in Duke’s Recommended NTAP are reasonable to be selected in this CPIRP to
meet the future capacity and energy needs of the system.
Based on the foregoing competent, material, and substantial evidence, the
Commission approves the new natural gas-fueled resources identified in Duke’s
Recommended NTAP as reasonable for planning purposes and directs Duke to proceed
with executing the development activities for new CC and CT resources outlined in the
Duke Recommended NTAP. The Commission’s determinations in this proceeding do not
relieve Duke of its obligation to seek the Commission’s issuance of a CPCN authorizing
construction of each new natural gas generating facility. The Commission, the Public
Staff, and other parties will therefore have additional opportunities after issuance of this
2026 CPIRP Order to further evaluate Duke’s plans for each new CC and CT resource
as those plans develop in connection with the applicable CPCN proceedings.
Stipulation on Enhanced Liquefied Natural Gas
The ELNG Stipulation between Duke and the Public Staff was filed after the close
of the evidentiary record. Hence, the Commission does not treat it as evidence supporting
its findings regarding the merits of ELNG. The Commission, nevertheless, recognizes the
agreement reached between Duke and the Public Staff and provides context below on
how that agreement relates to the independent conclusions the Commission reached
based on the evidentiary record.
Fuel Security and Enhanced Liquefied Natural Gas
Regarding fuel supply, the Commission acknowledges the concerns raised by the
Public Staff and other intervenors regarding Duke’s proposed ELNG fuel security strategy
for CCs 4 and 5. In particular, the Commission is cognizant of the Public Staff’s concern
that an additional evaluation of ELNG is necessary given substantial updates to the load
forecast that occurred prior to the expert witness hearing and the potential need for CC6
earlier than anticipated in the planning horizon. The Commission also notes that Duke
revised the proposed configuration and size of the potential ELNG facilities during this
proceeding. Additionally, Duke has indicated in Chapter 4 of the 2025 CPIRP that it
intends to file CPCNs for CC4 and CC5 in 2026. This accelerated timeline has reduced
the time that Public Staff and other intervenors can investigate this potential fuel security
strategy. At the same time, the substantial increases in the load forecast which
necessitated re-evaluations of ELNG tank sizing and the acceleration of CC6 make
further scrutiny of the fuel security strategy imperative. The Commission must ensure that
ELNG is in fact the least-cost option for ratepayers. If Duke intends to submit CPCN
applications in 2026, as it has indicated, and maintains that the time required for an
independent evaluation during the CPCN proceedings could cause in-service delays that
affect reliability, then Duke is effectively asking the Commission to deem ELNG the
selected fuel-security strategy for this CPIRP. Public Staff witness Metz pointed to this
tension, stating during the expert witness hearing that he “did not want to be sitting here
in six months and saying...now we have to do ELNG because...now it takes too long to
get FT so this is the only way you can get gas supply.” Tr. vol. 18, 156. The Commission
128

notes that the ELNG Stipulation states that Duke will not file a CPCN application for an
additional natural gas-fired generation resource for which ELNG is the proposed fuel
security option until more than thirty days after an independent evaluation has been
completed and filed with the Commission.
The Commission agrees with Duke that ELNG facilities do not qualify as a
first-of-a-kind resource and gives significant weight to the Public Staff’s testimony that the
proposal is technically feasible. The Commission also weighs the fact that Duke does not
currently operate any facility with the operational characteristics of ELNG and that there
are no facilities currently in operation in the United States with the operational
characteristics of ELNG. The Commission notes NCSEA witness Lin’s thoughtful
recommendation to approve ELNG contingent on demonstration of the concept via a pilot
project; however, the Commission ultimately agrees with Duke’s rebuttal that
implementation of this recommendation does not appear feasible.
At the same time, as noted by the Commission in several prior proceedings, the
natural gas supply in North Carolina is highly dependent on Transco. A diversification
strategy, such as ELNG, has the potential to reduce that dependency and enhance gaselectric coordination in the State. The Commission is also encouraged by the Public
Staff’s testimony that it is not opposed to ELNG as a concept and acknowledges that LNG
has increasingly been deployed across the country to ensure resource adequacy during
extreme weather events or peak usage days and to mitigate natural gas price volatility.
The Commission gives weight to Duke witness Mitchell’s analysis, which relied in part on
publicly available Transco operational capacity data, that there is enough natural gas
supply available in the marketplace on non-peak days, if needed, to fill the proposed
ELNG tanks.
However, the Commission concludes that further due diligence is necessary to
determine whether ELNG is truly the least-cost fuel security option. The Commission
therefore partially adopts the Public Staff’s recommendation that Duke and the Public
Staff jointly scope, and Duke conduct, an evaluation on whether ELNG can fully replicate
FT or EFT for purposes of providing fuel security for CC4 and CC5, subject to the caveats
discussed below.
Recognizing Duke’s and the Public Staff’s concerns regarding timing and the
willingness of a consultant to respond to a solicitation, the Commission remains flexible
regarding the eventual form of such an evaluation. The Commission directs Duke and
Public Staff to negotiate and jointly agree on the method by which the evaluation will be
conducted. That method may include an RFP for an independent third-party consultant,
a joint effort by Duke and Public Staff, an analysis conducted by Duke with the full
participation and audit of Public Staff, or another mutually agreed-upon approach. The
Commission requires Duke to file the outcome of the resulting discussions with the Public
Staff within 15 days of the issuance of this Order. The Commission further directs Duke
and Public Staff to submit the evaluation within 120 days after issuance of this 2026
CPIRP Order. The Commission encourages Duke and Public Staff to use this 15-day
period to appropriately scope the evaluation and to consider how the filing deadline will
intersect with Duke’s planned CPCN applications for CC4 and CC5. The Commission
129

declines to direct Duke to assess natural gas FT and delivery constraints through 2040
and instead encourages the Public Staff to discuss this within the scope of the evaluation.
The Commission finds its independent conclusion based on the entire evidentiary record
to be generally consistent with the ELNG Stipulation.
Hydrogen
The Commission recognizes that further developments related to hydrogen and
other potential technologies are necessary to determine exactly how new natural gas
resources will be utilized on the system after 2050. At the same time, the Commission
heavily weighs concerns voiced by multiple intervenors around the uncertainties that
surround hydrogen. In particular, the Commission heeds the Public Staff’s testimony that
“through future iterations of the CPIRP, the realistic specifics of hydrogen will take on
increasing importance because if the level of assumed hydrogen blending does not
materialize, other replacement technologies will need to be substituted in the mid- to
long-term.” Tr. vol. 18, 90.
Accordingly, the Commission directs Duke to provide further information for the
2027 CPIRP on how it plans to manage future hydrogen fuel supply, including:
(1) identifying explicit market and blending milestones that must be achieved to supply
the quantity of hydrogen needed; (2) identifying sourcing methodology and load
requirements for producing hydrogen and incorporating those load amounts and costs to
produce and/or source hydrogen into modeling; and (3) determining preliminary pipeline
costs and upgrades or storage costs and equipment needed to achieve blending
milestones, as well as the cost reductions that would be necessary for hydrogen blending
to be economically viable.
Existing Natural Gas-Fueled Resources
No party challenged Duke’s identified CC and CT uprate projects designed to
increase the flexibility and efficiency of these resources. The Commission finds that the
planned uprate projects, which will add approximately 484 MW from the existing natural
gas fleet in the 2025–2029 timeframe while also lowering heat rates, increasing capacity,
and enhancing turndown capability, are reasonable, cost effective, and beneficial to
customers. The Commission finds that Duke should continue execution of the planned
uprate projects at existing natural gas units intended to expand the flexibility and
efficiency of the existing natural gas fleet to maximize the benefits of prior customer
investments in these resources.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 52–56
Near-Term Activities for Bad Creek II
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Chapter 4 and
Appendices H and K of the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s
Renewables and Energy Storage Panel (Farver, Smith, and Tompson); the testimony and
exhibits of Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel (Beatty, Quinto, and Snider); the
testimony and exhibits of Public Staff’s Non-Thermal Resources Panel (Hill and Weaver);
130

the testimony and exhibits of NCSEA witness Glick; the testimony and exhibits of SACE,
et al. witness Roumpani; and the entire record.
Duke Direct Testimony
The 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke to pursue all reasonable development
activities to construct 1,834 14 MW of Pumped Storage Hydroelectric (PSH) at the Bad
Creek II facility to be placed in service by 2034 and to provide annual updates on the
progress of construction of Bad Creek II beginning with the next CPIRP. 2024 CPIRP
Order at 178. The 2024 CPIRP Order further authorized Duke to incur project
development costs up to $165 million for development of Bad Creek II, from 2023 through
2026, to place 1,834 MW of PSH in service by 2034. Id. Finally, the 2024 CPIRP Order
ordered that if Bad Creek II cannot be completed by 2034 or if estimated costs increase
by 15% or more from the estimated resource cost provided, then Duke shall provide an
explanation in the next CPIRP. Id.
The 2025 CPIRP explained that PSH currently provides approximately 2,400 MW
of LDES to Duke’s system. 2025 CPIRP App. H at 14. In prefiled direct testimony, Duke’s
Solar and Energy Storage Panel stated that PSH complements Duke’s nuclear fleet as
well as the increasing additions of solar energy expected on the grid by storing excess
energy during times of low demand and providing energy when demand is high.
Tr. vol. 11, 47. During the expert witness hearing, Duke witness Holman also testified that
Duke’s experience operating the existing PSH shows that it is extremely flexible and
extremely reliable. Tr. vol. 8, 103. The 2025 CPIRP indicated that four similar units would
be added to the existing Bad Creek I facility, essentially doubling the hourly capacity at
the site. 2025 CPIRP App. H at 15. As of the date it filed its 2025 CPIRP, Duke stated it
had spent $36.4 million on development activities for Bad Creek II. Id. at 16.
The 2025 CPIRP also explained that Bad Creek II cannot be completed by
2034 following Duke’s decision in quarter one (Q1) 2025 to withdraw Bad Creek II from
the 2022 DISIS cluster. Id. Duke explained that withdrawal of Bad Creek II from the
2022 DISIS allowed it to bring on more resources that provided needed energy and
capacity to the system on a faster timeline, which resulted in the acceleration of
approximately 675 MW of solar, gas, hydroelectric, and battery resources in the 2022 and
2023 DISIS cluster studies by an average of 47 months, and reduced network upgrade
costs by approximately $358 million. Id.; Tr. vol. 11, 49.
The 2025 CPIRP explained that the earliest projected in-service date for Bad
Creek II is now 2040, following its withdrawal from the interconnection study process.
2025 CPIRP App. H at 16. As a result, the anticipated development costs during the
2026–2028 NTAP period are approximately $19.2 million by the end of 2028. Id.;
tr. vol. 11, 50. This includes approximately $12.3 million to safe harbor the 30% federal
tax credits for storage by purchasing long-lead equipment. Tr. vol. 11, 50. During the
expert witness hearing, Duke witness Smith testified that Bad Creek II is a viable resource
14 Engineering study results produced after the 2023 Resource Plan confirmed expected Bad Creek

II capacity of 1,760 MW. 2025 Carolinas Resource Plan Appendix C at 61.

131

that can bring many benefits to the system and that continued development of Bad Creek
II puts Duke in the best position to save customers money with the federal investment tax
credits. Id. at 112–13.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
In prefiled direct testimony, Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver stated that the
Public Staff considered further development of Bad Creek II to not be in the public interest
due to the minimal PVRR impacts, declining EnCompass modeling usage, risk involved
with projects of this size, potential project delays, increasing costs, and development of
alternative LDES options as well as other resource additions. Tr. vol. 18, 254. Therefore,
witnesses Hill and Weaver stated that the Public Staff disagreed with Duke’s proposal to
defer additional development costs for Bad Creek II. Id. at 253.
Specifically, witnesses Hill and Weaver stated that the impact of including Bad
Creek II in the resource plan showed a less than 1% impact on the PVRR analysis,
especially now that Duke is in a combined utility planning scenario. Id. at 336–37.
Witnesses Hill and Weaver also explained that there have been several cost increases to
Bad Creek II since 2022, and potential future cost increases could lead to less
economically favorable situations. Id. at 337–38. Witnesses Hill and Weaver did not
identify any specific alternative LDES resources to recommend for use instead of Bad
Creek II since most resources classified as LDES have not yet reached commercial
availability. Id. at 256.
Witness Hill argued that it is still in the public interest to continue to analyze Bad
Creek II in the next CPIRP and reiterated that Public Staff does not support the
Commission providing regulatory assurance of cost recovery for additional Bad Creek II
development. Id. at 341–42.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
No other intervenor opposed the continued development of Bad Creek II.
NCSEA witness Glick indicated that Bad Creek II represents a mature LDES
storage resource that can help defer investment in more expensive, riskier technologies
and criticized Duke for failing to pursue reasonable development activities for Bad Creek
II resulting in an unreasonably delayed deployment timeline. Tr. vol. 17, 122–23. He
recommended including Bad Creek II as a resource in the late 2030s in the
Recommended Portfolio. Id. at 123–24.
SACE et al. witness Roumpani stated that in the 2023 CPIRP Duke stipulated that
it would update the Commission promptly in the event that it was determined that the
project could not be completed in the proposed timeline but did not, to her knowledge,
provide such an update to the Commission. Tr. vol. 20, 374. She also disagreed with
Duke’s explanation regarding why it chose to remove Bad Creek II from the 2022 DISIS
queue and described the explanation as being in stark contrast to Duke’s treatment of
other generation resources such as natural gas, nuclear, and coal. Id. at 375–76. Witness
Roumpani noted that LDES can enhance system flexibility and reduce fuel dependence
132

and asserted that Bad Creek II’s omission in this proceeding warrants a more robust
explanation and supporting analysis than that provided by Duke Id. at 376. She
recommended that the Commission require Duke to actively pursue the development of
Bad Creek II on the previously ordered timeline or, in the alternative, as soon as it can be
brought online. Id. at 300, 382.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
On rebuttal, Duke witness Smith opposed the Public Staff’s assertion that
continued development of Bad Creek II is no longer in the public interest, arguing that the
costs of Bad Creek II have not materially increased. Tr. vol. 22, 500. Witness Smith stated
that, despite a delay to Bad Creek II’s in-service date of six years, cost estimates have
increased by approximately 6% since the 2023 CPIRP filing, which is below the
15% reporting threshold from the 2024 CPIRP Order. Id. Witness Smith also disagreed
with Public Staff by noting that the Public Staff’s PVRR analysis was not consistent with
Duke’s PVRR analysis, which showed a $0.1 billion savings through 2050 from the
Preliminary Base Portfolio under a combined utility, and he claimed that this
demonstrates. Duke’s belief that its requested development spend for Bad Creek II by
preserving potential value for customers. Id. at 502. Witness Smith stated that PSH is a
proven technology with a strong operating and safety record and claimed the continued
development of Bad Creek II allows the project to remain a viable future resource option.
Id. at 504. Witness Smith concluded that it is reasonable and prudent for Duke to continue
development of Bad Creek II to maintain future resource optionality and safe harbor tax
credits for the benefit of customers. Id. at 506.
In response to NCSEA witness Glick’s testimony about an earlier availability of
Bad Creek II, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel explained that 2037 is not an
achievable in-service date for Bad Creek II given the time required to re-enter the
interconnection study process, construct the transmission required for reliable
interconnection, develop and construct the project and put into service. Tr. vol. 24, 224.
The panel further indicated that assuming Bad Creek II will be available earlier than 2040,
as suggested by NCSEA witness Glick and SACE witness Roumpani, would create
significant execution and reliability risk and results in a resource plan that would
significantly fall short of meeting system capacity. Id. at 224–25, 232.
Duke witness Bowman explained the rationale behind the decision to remove Bad
Creek II from the 2022 DISIS and noted that the removal allowed other projects that were
waiting on Bad Creek II to come online faster and benefit the system. Tr. vol. 22, 459.
When asked by Commissioner McKissick about notification around the decision to
remove Bad Creek II from the 2022 DISIS, Duke witness Bowman testified that informal
communications regarding the removal of Bad Creek II were given to Public Staff but that
a formal filing with the Commission was not made prior to the 2025 CPIRP filing. Id. When
asked by Chairman Brawley why Duke did not notify the Commission of the removal of
Bad Creek II from the 2022 DISIS, Duke’s IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel claimed that
Duke was very transparent about removing Bad Creek II from the 2022 DISIS in that there
were published filings regarding the removal and restudy of the 2022 DISIS. Tr. vol. 25,
113. The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel conceded that, in hindsight, a notice filing
133

with the Commission may have been warranted but asserted that the removal of Bad
Creek II was made to accelerate other generators and defer hundreds of millions of dollars
of upgrades. Id. The IRP and Near-Term Actions Panel also claimed that by delaying the
Bad Creek II timeline Duke would have better information on competing storage
technologies and would better understand the benefits provided by Bad Creek II relative
to other storage technologies. Id. at 115.
Discussion and Conclusions
As discussed previously in this Order, Duke’s unilateral decision to withdraw Bad
Creek II from the 2022 DISIS without notifying the Commission or requesting a waiver
constituted a clear violation of the 2024 CPIRP Order. That violation, however, does not
alter the Commission’s assessment of the merits of continuing development activities for
Bad Creek II. The Commission finds Duke’s testimony persuasive with respect to the
advances in in-service dates of other generation facilities due to the removal of Bad Creek
II from the 2022 DISIS.
The Commission gives substantial weight to evidence regarding the benefits that
PSH provides as a proven technology on the Duke system and further finds persuasive
the PVRR analysis that shows a benefit to customers of adding Bad Creek II to the system
in 2040 under a combined utility scenario.
While Public Staff has raised legitimate concerns regarding whether Bad Creek II
will ultimately be part of least-cost resource plan, the Commission is nevertheless
persuaded that the limited additional development spend requested by Duke is in the
public interest in order to maintain future resource optionality and safe harbor tax credits
for the benefit of customers. During cross examination by NCSEA, Public Staff witnesses
Hill and Weaver further explained their rationale for requesting the Commission deem
Bad Creek II not in the public interest while agreeing that PSH has been successfully
operated in the Carolinas for several decades, Tr. vol. 18, 335–44. During
cross-examination by SACE et al., the Public Staff also acknowledged that the costs of
Bad Creek II had not deviated by more than 15% from the 2023 cost estimate and had
increased by 6% from the 2023 cost estimate. Id. at 348–49; SACE, et al. Cross Public
Staff Non-Thermal Resources Panel Direct Exhibit 1. Considering this testimony, the
Commission disagrees with Public Staff witnesses Hill and Weaver that Bad Creek II is
not in the public interest and, accordingly, gives that testimony less weight.
The Commission also recognizes the support for – or lack of opposition to – Bad
Creek II by all other intervenors. While the Commission notes its disagreement with the
modeling conducted by SACE et al. and NCSEA, both modeling portfolios that advance
the in-service date of Bad Creek II provide informational insights on the facility’s role in
providing grid flexibility. The Commission also agrees with NCSEA that no party disputes
the capabilities and attributes of such a facility to maintain and improve the adequacy of
the grid. NCSEA Post-Hearing Br. at 22. Accordingly, and based on the competent,
material, and substantial evidence in the record, the Commission concludes that it is
reasonable and prudent for Duke to engage in project development activities as
134

presented in Duke’s Recommended NTAP and proposed Execution Plan to safe harbor
federal energy tax credits for Bad Creek II.
The Commission further concludes that it is reasonable for Duke to incur project
development costs up to $55.6 million through 2028, (which the Commission notes is
significantly less than the $165 million amount approved in the 2024 CPIRP Order) for
development of Bad Creek II, which includes safe harboring federal energy tax credits to
preserve the option of placing 1,760 MW of PSH in service by 2040. The Commission
notes that it will continue to assess Bad Creek II’s resource potential, taking into
consideration the most updated cost and resource expansion analysis, in future CPIRP
proceedings. The Commission’s approval of Duke’s request to incur project development
costs constitutes reasonable assurance of cost recoverability in a future general rate case
subject to the Commission’s review of the reasonableness and prudence of specific costs
incurred in such future proceeding consistent with the Commission’s prior determinations
in the 2024 CPIRP Order.
Finally, the Commission concludes it is appropriate to direct Duke to provide more
timely updates whenever Duke finds it necessary to take an action that represents a
material departure from a prior Commission directive with respect to CPIRP execution
activities, with adequate explanation and justification for such action. Given the two-year
cadence of the CPIRP process and the multiple other regulatory processes (e.g., CPCN
proceedings), the Commission is generally aware of material changes in the execution
plan that occur over time. Recognizing that Duke must retain discretion to adjust
execution activities where necessary for the benefit of customers and must continually
assess a range of interrelated factors when making such decisions, in this case, the
Commission concludes that the decision to withdraw Bad Creek II from the 2022 DISIS
was a violation of the 2024 CPIRP Order and finds that Duke should have notified the
Commission of the decision on a more timely basis, given the timing of the decision
relative to the 2024 CPIRP Order, or requested a waiver from the Order. However, the
Commission finds persuasive Duke’s reasoning for withdrawing Bad Creek II from the
2022 DISIS and the benefits of accelerating approximately 675 MW of new generation to
the system by approximately 47 months (as seen in the figure below) during a period of
rapid load growth and increased demand for new generation, ultimately deferring
hundreds of millions of dollars of upgrades in the near term.

135

Duke Energy Transmission and Interconnection Rebuttal Panel Late-Filed Ex. 1
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 57–60
Near-Term Development Activities for New Nuclear
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in the 2025 CPIRP; the
testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Nuclear Panel (Steven D. Capps and Rounette Nader);
the testimony and exhibits of Duke witness Snider; the testimony and exhibits of Public
Staff’s Thermal Resources Panel (Michna, Nader, and Lawrence); the testimony and
exhibits of CIGFUR witness Bradford D. Muller; the testimony and exhibits of NC WARN
witness Powers; and the entire record.
2025 CPIRP and Duke Direct and Supplemental Testimony
Duke stated in Appendix J of the 2025 CPIRP (Appendix J) that it is working to lay
the groundwork to add advanced nuclear generation to its energy mix, which would
support a diverse resource portfolio. Duke further indicated that it has been actively
evaluating advanced reactors to provide clean, reliable baseload generation that will be
needed to meet growing demand. 2025 CPIRP App. J at 9.
Appendix J explained that SMRs are smaller versions of the light-water reactors in
operation today and rely on the same fuel design and infrastructure. Id. at 11. Appendix
J further stated that like today’s traditional nuclear plants, SMRs will use ordinary water
for core cooling and energy transport and low-enriched uranium for fuel. Id. Duke
anticipated SMRs to be available at scale in the 2030s due to their similarity to existing
light-water reactors currently in operation. Id.
Appendix J also stated that Duke’s existing nuclear fleet is comprised of
Generation II (Gen II) LLWRs. Id. at 3. In comparison, the AP1000®, which is one model
of advanced LLWR, is considered Gen III+. Id. at 10. Appendix J indicated that Gen III+
reactors refer to a class of reactor technologies with key advancements in comparison to
136

previous generations, including more advanced safety features, such as passive core
cooling through natural circulation for extended periods without operator action. Id. at 16.
Gen III+ reactors also use modular construction, building major structural components
off-site and then delivering those components to the site when needed. Id. Moreover,
Appendix J stated that Gen III+ reactors typically have higher thermal efficiency and better
fuel usage than Gen II and Gen III designs. Id. Duke explained that Gen III+ designs
incorporate significant improvements in fuel technology, enhanced safety systems, and
overall design, which results in increased thermal efficiency and better energy production
from the same amount of fuel. Id. Appendix J stated that currently, no Gen III or Gen III+
LLWR projects are under development in North America. The only Gen III+ LLWRs built
in the U.S. are the two AP1000® units at Plant Vogtle in Waynesboro, Georgia. Vogtle
Unit 3 started serving customers in 2023 and Unit 4 in 2024. Id. at 21.
Additionally, Appendix J detailed Duke’s evaluation of non-light water reactors
(Non-LWRs). These Generation IV (Gen IV) reactors may use novel fuels and coolants
that are not currently used by the traditional United States commercial nuclear fleet. Duke
stated that it expects non-LWRs to be available at scale as an option for its system in the
2040s. Id. at 11.
The Execution Plan section of the 2025 CPIRP (Execution Plan) underscored the
importance of adding new advanced nuclear generation to Duke’s future generation
portfolio, stating:
As has been demonstrated in all recent resource plans, and is now yet again
confirmed in the Companies’ modeling in this case (including across nearly
all sensitivities), new nuclear generation is an essential resource to
(1) ensure fuel diversity and reliability for customers and (2) support future
growth and economic development as part of a portfolio that achieves
carbon neutrality by 2050. It is the sole generation technology with a proven
track record in the Carolinas of providing 24/7 clean energy with a capacity
factor greater than 90%.
2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 21.
Summary of the 2024 CPIRP Order Directives on New Advanced Nuclear
In the 2024 CPIRP Order, the Commission directed Duke to “take reasonable
steps [to] develop 300 MW of advanced nuclear generation to be placed into service by
2034 and an additional 300 MW of advanced nuclear generation to be placed into service
by 2035.” 2024 CPIRP Order at 178.
The Commission further directed Duke to update the Commission annually on its
ongoing progress executing the advanced nuclear development activities beginning with
the next CPIRP. Id. at 127. More specifically, the Commission’s directive required Duke
to provide updates to the estimated cost and Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC)
licensing activity on an annual basis as well as updates on the feasibility and revised cost

137

estimates to bring an aggregate 1,200 MW of advanced nuclear generating capacity into
commercial operation by 2036. Id.
The Commission further authorized Duke to incur project development costs for
new advanced nuclear resources, at a system level, up to $75 million through 2024, which
was authorized by the Commission in the Initial Carbon Plan Order, plus an additional
$365 million through 2026. Id. at 128.
Finally, the Commission directed Duke to (1) compile and file, by no later than
March 31, 2025, a comprehensive report detailing the licenses and other infrastructure
held by Duke that could support the development of new LLWRs at all potential known
sites as well as summarizing the key development milestones and a feasible timeline to
develop each potential new large light water reactor, if such resources were to be
economically selected by future CPIRP modeling; and (2) model an LLWR, such as an
AP1000®, as a selectable resource in its next CPIRP filing. Id.
Compliance with the 2024 CPIRP Order
In compliance with the 2024 CPIRP Order, on March 31, 2025, Duke filed its
Report on LLWRs in Docket No. E-100, Sub 190. Further, as explained in the Petition:
Consistent with the Commission’s directive to model [LLWR] technology,
such as an AP1000® unit, as an alternative to [SMRs] that the Companies
modeled in their 2023 CPIRP the Companies conducted a comprehensive
nuclear technology assessment within the modeling platform to establish a
representative, generic nuclear technology for planning purposes. After
evaluating multiple scenarios, the Companies’ Representative Nuclear
Technology Analysis identified LLWR technology as the most reasonable
nuclear technology to generically represent the need for nuclear in the Plan.
Pet. at 16.
The Execution Plan stated that Duke has made significant progress advancing
approved execution activities from previous resource plans. Table 4-1 summarized
Duke’s recent actions to advance the NTAP authorized in the 2024 CPIRP Order for new
nuclear generation. In particular, Table 4-1 explained that, since the issuance of the
2024 CPIRP Order, Duke has continued preparation of the early site permit application
for the Belews Creek site, including completing initial field work, such as groundwater well
installations, core drilling, and environmental studies; installed a meteorological tower and
collected weather data; engaged community, industry, and regulatory stakeholders, and
provided presentations, site tours, and updates during NRC public meetings. 2025 CPIRP
Ch. 4 at 5. Further, since the issuance of the 2024 CPIRP Order, Table 4-1 indicated that
Duke has continued a rigorous technology assessment and due diligence review of SMR
and non-LWR designs, including participating in deep dives with reactor technology
vendors and entering strategic agreements with utilities and vendors. Id. Finally,
Table 4-1 stated that Duke has expanded industry engagement to assess potential for

138

LLWR development and initiated contract development discussions with LLWR vendors
to identify licensing needs. Id.
The Nuclear Panel explained that Duke has worked diligently to pursue licensing
activities for new advanced nuclear plants at various locations and are closely following
the design, licensing, and construction of first-of-a-kind projects currently underway in
North America. Tr. vol. 6, 158. They explained that Duke is engaging in a partnership with
TerraPower on the non-LWR Natrium project in Kemmerer, WY, as part of the United
States Department of Energy’s (DOE) $1.3 billion Advanced Reactor Demonstration
Program. Id. at 158–59. They also noted that Duke is participating in Tennessee Valley
Authority’s application for $800 million in grant funding from DOE’s Generation III+ SMR
Pathway to Deployment program to advance the design and licensing of a promising SMR
technology. Id. at 159. The Nuclear Panel stated that Duke is engaging Westinghouse,
as the reactor designer and constructor, and Southern Nuclear Operating Company, as
the utility applicant and operator, to perform deep dives on the lessons learned at Plant
Vogtle Units 3 and 4, understand the issues incurred during development, and prevent
recurrence, consistent with Duke’s second-mover strategy. Id. They explained that these
activities are “crucial to prepare for and position the Companies to execute on plans for
adding advanced nuclear resources to the Companies’ generation fleet.” Id.
Near-Term Action Plan for New Advanced Nuclear Generation
The Nuclear Panel indicated that there continues to be unique and growing support
at both the state and federal levels for potential new nuclear generation, with several
critical factors coalescing in its favor, such as the potential for efficient recovery of
financing costs under S.L. 2025-78, favorable federal tax policy, and emerging federal
and state policy support. Id. at 167.

139

Table 4-10 of the Execution Plan outlines the Duke Recommended NTAP for new
advanced nuclear generation:

The Nuclear Panel explained that by 2027, Duke intends to select a reactor
technology to be deployed at the first site. The panel argued that deferring the decision to
select an advanced reactor technology to 2027 is a risk mitigation strategy that will allow
time for the designs to mature and front-end engineering and design issues to be resolved.
Tr. vol. 6, 165–66. They stated that Duke will continue to assess whether pursuing LLWRs
or SMRs is the optimal nuclear technology choice for the first project and that Duke will
provide further details to the Commission on their evaluation. Id. at 166.
During the expert witness hearing, Commissioner McKissick questioned the
Nuclear Panel about the timeline for the NRC’s review of the Design Control Document
Revision 20 (DCD20) for Vogtle Unit 4. Tr. vol. 7, 69–70. The Nuclear Panel explained
140

that “by the end of 2026, the industry will have an updated approved design control
document with Vogtle Unit 4 as the reference plant.” Id. at 70. Commissioner McKissick
followed up this discussion by asking the Nuclear Panel whether the approved DCD20
“would put Duke in a better position to evaluate the competing technologies? When I say
that, you know, an AP1000 versus alternative SMR designs that are along the way, and
perhaps make a recommendation as it concerns a preferred technology?” Id. at 71. In
response, the Nuclear Panel confirmed that “having the approved DCD20 for Vogtle Unit
4 is definitely important input,” that could be used to update the two combined licenses
and expedite the approval process for two AP1000®s at the W.S. Lee (Lee) nuclear site
in Cherokee County, SC. Id.
Regarding the projected timeline to bring new advanced nuclear generation online,
the Nuclear Panel indicated in prefiled direct testimony that Duke’s current projections
show the potential to place 600 MW of SMRs or an 1,117 MW LLWR in service by 2037.
Tr. vol. 6, 161.
The Nuclear Panel noted that Duke identified a site in Belews Creek,
North Carolina as the preferred location for deployment of SMRs or non-LWRs. Id. at 159.
They explained that the Belews Creek site is a suitable location for advanced nuclear
deployment due to its water availability from Belews Lake, the existing transmission and
railway infrastructure, the skilled workforce at the existing coal-fired generation plant, and
the eligibility for potential Inflation Reduction Act tax credits when the coal plants are
retired in the 2030s. Id. The Nuclear Panel indicated that Duke could add 600 MW of
advanced nuclear generation by 2037 by pursuing SMR development at Belews Creek.
Id. at 160.
Furthermore, the Nuclear Panel explained that Duke’s near-term actions for 2026–
2028, presented in the Execution Plan, focus on continued licensing activities for both
SMRs and LLWRs. Id. at 165. They stated that DEC is already in possession of approved
combined licenses (COLs) for two AP1000® reactors at the Lee nuclear site. Id. at 160.
They additionally noted that DEP (as Progress Energy Carolinas) has previously
submitted a combined license application (COLA) to the NRC for two AP1000® reactors
at the Shearon Harris (Harris) site in New Hill, North Carolina. Id. The Nuclear Panel
stated that both sites will have significantly less licensing risk and a shorter development
timeline than a site that is starting with no licensing work performed; however, material
amendments and revisions to the approved COLs and application will be required to
incorporate updates since the initial submittals. Id.
Building on these existing licenses for AP1000® reactors, the Nuclear Panel
explained that, in the Duke Recommended NTAP, Duke proposes to develop license
amendments to be filed with the NRC to update the COLs for two Westinghouse
AP1000® units at the Lee site and the COLA for two Westinghouse AP1000® units at the
Harris site. Id. at 166.
The Nuclear Panel clarified that, for LLWRs, Duke is not requesting approval at
this time of the site preparation and procurement activities that would be needed before
the end of 2028 to achieve a 2037 in-service date. Id. at 161. The Nuclear Panel also
141

clarified that, if Duke decides to move forward with LLWRs, it will need to order long-lead
equipment before the end of 2028, and they stated that, prior to ordering long-lead
equipment, Duke will provide detailed information and seek any necessary authorization
from the Commission to proceed. Id. at 163–64. If the decision is made to move forward
with SMRs, the Nuclear Panel explained that Duke will select an SMR reactor technology
by 2027 and develop a construction permit application or a combined license application.
Id. at 165.
Appendix J states that the 2023 Carolinas Resource Plan initially called for 600 MW
of advanced nuclear generation to be in-service at the Belews Creek, North Carolina site
by 2035, with the first SMR achieving commercial operation by 2034. Duke’s additional
evaluation of LLWRs and its need for continued evaluation of reactor technologies that
have not yet been deployed has delayed its in-service timeline for SMRs by two years.
2025 CPIRP App. J at 31. Duke estimates licensing and constructing SMRs or AP1000®
units will take about ten years to bring the first SMR online in 2036 at the Belews Creek
site or to bring the first AP1000® unit online by 2037 at the Lee site. Id. at 31–32.

142

In direct testimony, the Nuclear Panel explained that total projected costs related
to new nuclear generation development activities through year-end 2028
(i.e., $163.7 million through 2026 plus $276.0 million 2027–2028) are $439.7 million.
Tr. vol. 6, 163.
The Commission previously ordered that “pursuant to N.C.G.S. § 62-110.7(b),
Duke is approved to incur $440 million through 2026 to develop advanced nuclear
generation.” 2024 CPIRP Order at 178. The Nuclear Panel stated that Duke’s proposed
near-term actions through 2028 are not projected to exceed the $440 million authorized
through the end of 2026 in the 2024 CPIRP Order. Tr. vol. 6, 163. They indicated that
Duke requests that the Commission extend this prior authorization through 2028 for the
purpose of preserving optionality for selection of either 600 MW of SMRs or 1,117 MW of
LLWRs to be placed in service by 2037. Id. Lastly, the Nuclear Panel noted that if a
decision is made to move forward with LLWRs, long-lead equipment will need to be
ordered before the end of 2028, and long-lead equipment costs are not included in Duke’s
$276 million of estimated future costs. Id. Therefore, the panel stated that Duke will
provide detailed information and seek any necessary authorization from the Commission
prior to ordering long-lead equipment. Id. at 164.
Customer Protections
The Nuclear Panel noted that, notwithstanding the preliminary issue of technology
selection, site preparation and procurement activities for either technology will require
significant additional expenditures. Id. at 161. The Nuclear Panel advised that Duke does
not believe that it is reasonable to make commitments of such magnitude until additional
customer protections can be obtained. Id.

143

The Nuclear Panel explained that Duke relied on the experience gained from past
and recent nuclear construction projects and its experience developing nuclear facilities
to proactively pursue cost-overrun protection for new nuclear construction. Id. at 171.
More specifically, they stated that if new nuclear is ultimately selected, Duke intends to
mitigate cost risk by seeking current federal incentives in the form of the Clean Electricity
Investment Credit, which offer a base credit of 6% for qualifying costs that are part of a
QF and can increase to 30% for facilities that satisfy prevailing wage and apprenticeship
requirements. Id. The Nuclear Panel additionally noted that facilities incorporating
domestic content may be eligible for an extra credit of up to 10%, and those situated in
energy communities, such as a census tract in which a coal-fired electric generating unit
retired after 2009, may receive an additional credit of up to 10%. Id. They explained that
the Clean Electricity Investment Credit can reach as high as 50% of the facility’s cost
including any potential cost overruns and that full tax credits are currently available for
facilities that commence construction prior to 2034, with the value of the credits gradually
phasing out for those beginning construction in 2034 (to 75%) and 2035 (to 50%), and
with the credit completely phased out for facilities beginning construction after 2035. Id.
The Nuclear Panel further explained that although these incentives would serve to
mitigate cost risk, Duke believes that additional measures should be pursued to further
mitigate the unique financial risks posed by the construction of a new nuclear station given
the magnitude of costs associated with such projects. Id. at 172. They stated that Duke
is actively seeking potential opportunities for federal support that could complement
traditional DOE loan guarantees or direct loans with potentially favorable interest rates
from the Loan Programs Office, which is now operating as the Energy Dominance
Financing Program. Id. However, the Nuclear Panel noted that neither a loan guarantee
nor direct lending directly addresses cost overrun risk. Id. Toward that end, they stated
that Duke is exploring three potential federal mechanisms to further address cost overrun
protection for new nuclear developments: (1) direct government funding under the
proposed Accelerating Reliable Capacity (ARC) Act; (2) federal cost overrun insurance
with premium support; and (3) involvement in build-own-transfer models for project
financing and risk sharing. Id. The Nuclear Panel explained that all three financing
mechanisms are currently conceptual opportunities that will require legislative
enactments and are not yet available to support project development. However, they
stated that Duke is committed to pursuing structures, as those structures become
available, that would provide benefit to customers and Duke at the appropriate time.
Id. at 172–74.

Public Staff Direct Testimony
In prefiled direct testimony, the Thermal Resources Panel stated that in the 2023
CPIRP Proceeding, Duke projected that the earliest available date for new advanced
nuclear was expected to be 2034, but in the 2025 CPIRP, the earliest available shifted to
2037. Therefore, the Public Staff did not consider Duke to have met the expected
milestones of new nuclear development previously established by the Commission.
Tr. vol. 18, 91. More particularly, the Thermal Resources Panel stated that “[t]hrough the
145

end of 2026, Duke projects to spend only 63% of the $440 million allocated for new
nuclear development.” Id. at 93. They further asserted that, although the Commission
directed Duke to “move expeditiously” in the 2024 CPIRP Order, the pace of development
has slowed. Id.
The Thermal Resources Panel referred to the prefiled direct testimony of the
Nuclear Panel concerning the need for customer protections. However, they also
referenced Duke earnings calls in which Duke’s CEO discussed the need for “cost
overrun protections” and the need to “protect the balance sheet” before moving forward
with new nuclear, implying these factors were the reason for Duke’s supposed delayed
development of new nuclear resources. Id. at 93–94.
The Thermal Resources Panel contended that no new or unforeseen
circumstances have arisen since the 2024 CPIRP Order to support the delay and further
asserted that the potential cost was known during 2023 CPIRP. Id. They stated that Duke
should have notified the Commission of its decision not to comply with the 2024 CPIRP
Order and received permission to act contrary to the Commission’s directives. Id.
The Thermal Resources Panel further argued that the delay of new nuclear
generation leaves limited mitigation activities and encourages use of natural gas-fueled
generation, which exposes ratepayers to fuel volatility, potentially stranded assets, and
the risk that hydrogen-fueled generation will not be sufficient or able to reach 2050
requirements. Id. at 96–97.
Noting that Duke’s approach continues to identify the need for nuclear resources
with the modeled cost estimate and availability date, the Thermal Resources Panel
contended that Duke’s modeling approach and cost estimates for planning purposes for
new advanced nuclear generation are reasonable, although there is still uncertainty about
project costs and execution ability. Id. at 99.
The Thermal Resources Panel recommended that the Commission direct Duke to
proceed with the proposed course of action in the Duke Recommended NTAP to bring
nuclear generation online by 2037. Id. at 100. Further, to facilitate nuclear generation
efforts, the Thermal Resources Panel recommended that the Commission extend the
Commission’s prior authorization to incur $440 million in development costs for LLWRs,
SMRs, and the nuclear development activities outlined by Duke in Appendix J through
the end of 2028. Id. at 101. The Thermal Resources Panel also recommended that the
authorized funds should continue to be eligible for special treatment under
N.C.G.S. § 62-110.7 but that any exceedance of the $440 million should not qualify under
that statutory provision unless approved by the Commission. Id.
The Thermal Resources Panel recommended that Duke be required to notify the
Commission if it appears that future nuclear generation will not be in service by 2037 or
if the costs materially rise above current estimates and that Duke should describe the
reasons for the delay, provide the new in-service date for new nuclear resources, and
clearly explain which resources should be modified in the Duke Recommended NTAP, if
any, to account for the loss of energy and capacity. Id.
146

Lastly, the Thermal Resources Panel noted that it would “be of value” to open a
dedicated docket for new nuclear resources to have an “open conversation in the
beginning” and reduce future uncertainty as Duke selects its nuclear resource
development pathway. Id. at 164.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
In prefiled direct testimony, CIGFUR witness Muller indicated that nuclear
generation performs “perfectly” during extreme temperature events and praised Duke's
existing nuclear fleet as critical to ensuring reliable power during recent extreme
temperature events. Tr. vol. 20, 97. Nevertheless, he stated that CIGFUR does not
believe that Duke has advanced the development of new nuclear generation with
sufficient urgency. Id. at 93. He recommended that the Commission take a more active
role in the development of new nuclear generation by issuing five directives: (1) that Duke
apply for a CPCN for two AP1000® units at the Lee site within 12 months of an order in
this docket; (2) that Duke request that NRC resume its review of the Harris site COLA;
(3) that Duke work with large customers to develop a tariff that allows large customers to
help support the development of new nuclear generation and propose that tariff to the
Commission no later than six months after the date of an order in this docket; (4) that
Duke apply for federal programs that would aid in the development of new nuclear
generation; and (5) that Duke file regular and frequent reporting so that the Commission
can exercise sufficient oversight to determine whether Duke is pursuing federal funding
with sufficient zeal. Id. at 93–94.
In support of CIGFUR’s recommendations, witness Muller stated that CIGFUR
does not have a preferred nuclear technology type but noted that there is a proven history
of constructing LLWRs in the United States, while the first SMR construction permit was
only recently issued. Id. at 94–95. He explained that, as such, only LLWRs are available
to meet near-term generation needs. Id. at 95. Witness Muller also noted that LLWRs can
be sited at retired coal facilities to utilize existing transmission infrastructure, further
reducing capital costs. Id.
Witness Muller asserted that Duke’s proposed retirement of over 6,000 MW of
baseload generation by 2040 requires “like-for-like” replacement of any retired baseload
generation, i.e., replacement generation must have similar operating characteristics to
the generation being retired and, more specifically, that it have 24/7 availability and
consistent capacity. Id. Witness Muller claimed that nuclear generation is the only
technology that meets these requirements. Id. at 96. Noting that “massive load growth” is
anticipated in North Carolina due to the onshoring of manufacturing, population growth,
and data centers, witness Muller stated that Duke needs a diverse portfolio of new
baseload generation to keep pace and noted that natural gas pipeline capacity limits the
amount of new gas generation that can be installed. Id. He claimed that Duke, for these
reasons, needs to invest in new nuclear generation to meet North Carolina’s needs
regardless of any changes in federal or state policy regarding carbon emissions. Id.
Referencing the 2024 memorandum of understanding between Duke and four
large customers at the White House Summit on Domestic Nuclear Deployment to develop
147

tariffs meant to lower the long-term costs of investing in technologies like new nuclear,
witness Muller indicated that Duke had not proposed any such tariff for the Commission’s
consideration despite the passage of nearly two years. Id. at 105. He also noted that the
recent Ratepayer Protection Pledge Duke made with major data centers included
provisions in which the signatories agreed to build, bring, or buy new generation
resources to meet their needs. Id. Witness Muller stated that agreements between Duke
and large customers, in which large customers bear some of the financial risk related to
new nuclear development, would reduce the exposure of other ratepayers to the financial
risk of new nuclear development, and that such agreements would also derisk Duke’s
own investment, making new nuclear more attractive. Id. at 106.
Witness Muller agreed with Duke regarding the appropriateness of
government-provided financial backstops or other ratepayer protections but stated that
the need for such protections should not be permitted to slow the development of
new nuclear generation since the need for new baseload generation is too great.
Id. at 107–08. He asserted that the Commission can direct Duke to move forward with
developing new nuclear resources subject to existing ratepayer protections while
additional protections are being developed. Id. Additionally, Witness Muller described
various federal efforts implemented through Executive Order related to nuclear
development, before noting that Duke has not, to his knowledge, engaged with any of the
relevant federal agencies. Id. at 108–11. Lastly, he indicated that CIGFUR supports
Duke’s call for the federal government to offer cost overrun insurance. Id. at 111.
In prefiled direct testimony, NC WARN witness Powers argued that new nuclear
power will be very expensive and slow to come online, and that Duke has provided no
evidence that they will be able to avoid the costly errors of other recent nuclear projects,
including but not limited to the widely publicized problems with Georgia Power’s Vogtle
Nuclear Units 3 & 4. Tr. vol. 22, 188.
Witness Powers also noted that Duke already meets a high proportion of its
electricity demand with baseload nuclear power, and the entire existing nuclear fleet will
still be operational in 2050. Id. He argued that, as compared to nuclear development,
solar and storage can be developed quickly and cost-effectively, and claimed this is the
lowest risk alternative to supplement Duke’s existing nuclear power to meet demand
growth and replace coal and gas-fired capacity. He recommended that the Commission
direct Duke to perform new modeling that corrects for Duke’s analytical mistakes and
erroneous assumptions with respect to new nuclear. Id. at 189.
Furthermore, Witness Powers noted that no SMR has yet been commercialized in
the United States and that Duke initiated earlier work on the AP1000® projects at the Lee
and Harris sites, but both were canceled in the design phase after more than $600 million
was spent. Id. at 204–05. Witness Powers stated that South Carolina Electricity and Gas
(SCE&G) led a consortium of utilities financing the construction of two AP1000® reactors
at the V.C. Summer nuclear plant in South Carolina and highlighted that SCE&G was
forced to cancel the project in 2017 after spending $9 billion on the abandoned project
after projected total project costs rose to $25 billion. Id. He noted that Vogtle Units 3 & 4
cost about $36 billion and are the most expensive power plants ever built. He claimed
148

“the Vogtle reactors have widely been characterized as a disaster for ratepayers, although
Georgia Power profited handsomely” Id. at 206. Witness Powers argued that “Vogtle 3 &
4 actually demonstrate that, if financial risk is removed from the nuclear project proponent,
and the project proponent can earn guaranteed profit on mistakes and delays with no cap
on nuclear project cost overruns, an AP1000® project can be completed – albeit at
unreasonable cost and delay.” Id. at 208.
In support of NC WARN’s recommendations, Witness Powers argued that new
