nuclear is not a least cost approach to meeting electricity demand. He noted that “there
are no apparent economies-of-scale in nuclear power plant costs or construction
timelines” for large reactors and contended that “SMRs will be more expensive to build
than large nuclear reactors.” Id. at 212. Instead, he stated that NC WARN recommends
additional solar plus storage resource development as an alternative to building new
nuclear generation. Id. at 218.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
Updates on Progress and Significant Developments
On rebuttal, the Nuclear Panel provided updates on Duke’s progress regarding
new nuclear deployment options. Id. at 558. First, the Nuclear Panel noted that Duke
submitted the Early Site Permit (ESP) application to the NRC on December 30, 2025, for
potential new nuclear development at a 1,000-acre site near the Belews Creek Steam
Station in Stokes County, North Carolina. Id. at 560. They explained that submitting the
ESP application marked the culmination of two years of work that included a rigorous site
selection process, a comprehensive technology assessment, and extensive site and
environmental field studies. Id. The Nuclear Panel explained that the NRC’s 18-month
review process is underway, with permit issuance expected in 2027. Id. They further
indicated that the ESP will confirm the site’s suitability for new SMRs and non-LWRs and
resolve site safety and environmental topics on the front end, reducing the risk of delays
during licensing and construction, if the Belews Creek site is selected to bring 600 MW of
new nuclear energy online by 2037. Id.
Furthermore, the Nuclear Panel explained that the Belews Creek site could
alternatively be evaluated for up to two LLWRs. Id. They stated that if Duke decides to
pursue LLWRs at the Belews Creek site, environmental and site safety data from the
ESP, with additional engineering analysis, could be used to support developing a
combined license application to construct and operate LLWRs at the site. Id.
The Nuclear Panel also discussed Westinghouse’s submission of an updated
AP1000® design to the NRC on March 27, 2026, which provided proposed modifications
to the as-built configuration of Vogtle unit 4 and included more than 200 amendments to
the AP1000® design. Id. at 562. They stated that these changes address some of the key
factors driving the schedule delays and cost increases associated with the Vogtle units.
Id. at 563. The Nuclear Panel further indicated that the NRC’s approval of these
modifications to the as-built design will mitigate those risks, incorporating lessons learned
from Vogtle, and then will be used to update Duke’s COLs for two Westinghouse
149

AP1000® units at the Lee site. Id. The Nuclear Panel stated that the NRC expects to
complete its review of the updated design by August 31, 2026, and then will issue a
rulemaking schedule for the direct final rule. Id. They stated that Duke is already working
to evaluate the changes to the Lee COLs that will result from the revised AP1000® design
and intends to use the updated AP1000® design for the Lee site and plan to submit the
request to amend the Lee COLs to the NRC by early 2027. Id.
The Nuclear Panel indicated that Duke is also evaluating what is needed to revise
the COLA for two AP1000®s at the Harris site, which they explained “is significantly
different from and more complicated than Lee’s regulatory status.” They noted that
although DEP submitted a COLA to the NRC in 2008 for the Harris site, it requested the
NRC suspend its review in 2013 and a COL has not been issued for the facility. To
proceed with nuclear development at the Harris site, The Nuclear Panel indicated that
Duke must develop a new COLA to fully comply with current regulatory requirements,
involving extensive engineering analysis and licensing work to update the environmental
report and site safety analysis report. Id. at 563–64.
The Nuclear Panel stated that Duke is engaging with LLWR vendors to assess
their capacity, requirements, and delivery sequence for major components. They further
noted that Duke is looking for ways to standardize specifications, streamline procurement,
and sequence orders to avoid delays. Id. at 564. The Nuclear Panel indicated that
completing this groundwork before starting construction will improve long-lead
procurement efficiency, support work execution, and mitigate risks of schedule delays
and potential cost overruns. Id.
Furthermore, the Nuclear Panel indicated that Duke’s recent and current actions
and significant industry developments align with Duke’s second-mover strategy to protect
customers by ensuring reactor technology readiness and predictability, increasing
schedule certainty, minimizing risks, and reducing costs. Id. They explained that Duke is
focused on selecting the most predictable and proven technology for customers as a first
step, either SMRs or LLWRs, and noted that the industry is making advances in both
SMR and LLWR technologies, which Duke uses to inform its activities and timeline. Id.
The Nuclear Panel explained that Duke is continuing to monitor first-of-a-kind
projects in North America but that, overall, the industry is experiencing delays consistent
with what would be expected for first-of-a-kind technologies. Id. at 565. The panel noted
the current industry assessment finds an SMR is still at least four years away from being
placed into operation. Id. at 561.
The Nuclear Panel also argued that the Vogtle deployment underscores that even
an NRC-approved design is not a completed design and will continue to evolve during
construction, as first-of-a-kind technologies are only fully complete once they come into
service. Id. at 565. They stated the project ultimately took 15 years to complete and was
delivered significantly over budget. Further, they cautioned that when construction starts,
and equipment and the workforce are mobilized, delays could cost between $3–5 million
a day, underscoring the importance of Duke’s second-mover strategy. Id. However, they
noted that the NRC’s pending approval of the as-built Vogtle design recently submitted
150

by Westinghouse will reduce the number of design changes needed for future
deployments, reducing risk, saving time and lowering costs. Id. at 565–66.
Lastly, the Nuclear Panel provided further updates on the ARC Act, which they
explained was re-introduced in the U.S. Senate on February 10, 2026, with a new
co-sponsor and was rewritten to be technology neutral and is now inclusive of all
advanced nuclear energy technologies. Id. at 566.
Overall, the Nuclear Panel stated that, overall, Duke is focused on safety, reliability
and cost-effective generation to keep costs as low as possible for customers. Id. at 571.
They indicated that the long-term value of nuclear, given that units may operate up to
80 years, necessitates that Duke select a predictable reactor technology that allows for
continued safe, reliable, and cost-effective operations. Id. Additionally, they explained that
Duke must continue to plan for the future to ensure equipment maintenance strategies
and outage planning allow it to achieve and sustain the nuclear fleet’s expected
industry-leading performance throughout a new nuclear unit’s licensed life. Id. They
stressed that, by pairing schedule discipline with a strong emphasis on reliability, Duke is
positioned to deliver new nuclear resources that provide the best overall value for
customers today and over the life of the new nuclear unit, with costs and risks that are
managed through predictable project execution and a continued focus on maintaining
reliable and cost-effective operations post construction. Id.
Response to the Public Staff and Other Intervenors Direct Testimony
The Nuclear Panel responded to CIGFUR witness Muller’s recommendation that
Duke file a CPCN for two AP1000® units within 12 months of a final order in this
proceeding by noting that Duke appreciates the strong support from a critical customer
voice for the benefits of new nuclear generation and stating that if an LLWR is confirmed
to be the preferred technology and will provide the greatest number of customer benefits,
it is feasible to submit a CPCN by the end of 2028. Id. at 575.
The Nuclear Panel also responded to NC WARN witness Powers’ criticism of
Duke’s plans to expand its nuclear fleet by stating that Duke recognizes the risks
associated with pursuing new nuclear generation. Id. They reiterated that risk recognition
informed Duke’s recommended approach to build on essential lessons learned from first
movers, such as the Vogtle project in Georgia. Id. They contend that witness Powers’
testimony, which implies that all AP1000®s are likely to be plagued by the hurdles that
Vogtle had to overcome, is conclusory without differentiating between the unique
characteristics and positions of Vogtle versus Duke’s proposed “second mover” new
nuclear strategy. Id.
Lastly, the Nuclear Panel opposed the Thermal Resources Panel’s contention that
Duke unnecessarily delayed the progress of development activities for new advanced
nuclear resources, and the Public Staff’s assertion that Duke has not moved quickly
enough with respect to new nuclear or failed to comply with the Commission’s directives.
Id. at 558, 567. In response, the Nuclear Panel explained that Duke has made substantial
progress to advance new nuclear generation options and key decision points—progress
151

that is reasonably informed by the magnitude of the investments, evolving industry
considerations and the need to protect customers. Id. at 558. The Nuclear Panel argued
that the Public Staff’s “assertions fail to properly account for a range of risk factors or
acknowledge that premature action could have adverse consequences for customers and
the Companies.” Id. The Nuclear Panel also defended Duke’s action explaining that it
continues to implement a second-mover strategy to deploy new nuclear units after
first-of-a-kind projects have sufficiently progressed and technologies have been proven;
a strategy which allows for the selection of the most predictable reactor technology and
the protection of customers, thereby positioning Duke to successfully execute new
nuclear projects. Id. at 558–59.
The Nuclear Panel clarified that:
One of the core strategy decisions that has yet to be made is selecting a
reactor technology: that is, whether to proceed with SMRs at the Belews
Creek site, and if so which SMR to select, or whether to proceed with
LLWRs, such as the AP1000 at the Lee site. This core decision is
fundamental to the next steps and necessarily guides every subsequent
decision, including the selected vendor, the long-lead-time procurement
contracts, etc.
Id. at 567.
They noted that while the Thermal Resources Panel asserts that Duke should have
moved faster, the Public Staff fails to identify which new nuclear technology it believes
Duke should have already selected. Id. at 568. The Nuclear Panel asserted that this single
decision is a critically important gating decision and not a decision that can be rushed
given the enormous implications and prior experiences in the industry. Id. The Nuclear
Panel cautioned that it is unreasonable to assert that Duke should have moved faster
without identifying which technology Duke should have selected. Id.
The Nuclear Panel also contended that the Thermal Resources Panel further failed
to articulate what steps would have been reasonable for Duke to take. Id. They note that
the 2023 NTAP contemplated only SMRs but the 2024 CPIRP Order directed further
consideration of LLWRs. Id. The Nuclear Panel defended Duke’s progress stating that
Duke has continued its evaluation of LLWRs, while pointing out that if an SMR technology
had been selected to maintain the 2034 timeline from the 2023 CPIRP, Duke would have
needed to commit significant funds in 2026 to lock-in an SMR reactor technology. Id. They
explained that Duke promptly evaluated LLWRs on the heels of Vogtle’s completion, as
directed by the Commission, and determined LLWRs could not meet the 2034 timeline
based on actual information from Vogtle’s construction. Id.
The Nuclear Panel also stated that given continuing uncertainty regarding the
optimal reactor technology and substantial ongoing industry developments, rushing to
commit significant funds to pay initial vendor fees to lock-in an SMR reactor technology
would have been imprudent since SMR designs are incomplete and continuing to evolve
and mature. Id. at 569.
152

Finally, with regard to the Thermal Resources Panel’s testimony, the Nuclear
Panel clarified that notwithstanding the Public Staff’s criticisms, the Public Staff supports
the Duke Recommended NTAP for new nuclear development, including the 2037
projected in-service date for new advanced nuclear, and further supports the request to
extend the Commission’s approval to incur $440 million in new nuclear development costs
through 2028. Id. at 572–73.
Discussion and Conclusions
The Commission gives significant weight to the testimony of the Duke Nuclear
Panel, the Execution Plan, and Appendix J. The Commission also gives significant weight
to the Public Staff Thermal Resources Panel’s endorsement of Duke’s Recommended
NTAP for new advanced nuclear generation and the Panel’s recommendation that the
Commission extend the Commission’s prior authorization to incur $440 million in
development costs for new advanced nuclear resources through 2028. The Commission
notes that Duke and the Public Staff, whose recommendations are founded upon
comprehensive EnCompass modeling, have reached agreement on the appropriate next
steps in the pursuit of new advanced nuclear generation, and the Commission gives
significant weight to this agreement.
The Commission also recognizes the magnitude of the decision to develop new
nuclear resources. Duke witness Snider testified that “those first two nuclear plants come
at a capital cost that is greater than the entirety of our NTAP. 4,000 megawatts solar,
5,600 [MW] of new batteries, seven new CTs, [and] six new CCs are less capital than the
two AP1000®s.” Tr. vol. 25, 19–20. The Commission gives significant weight to this
testimony regarding the magnitude of the capital cost of nuclear.
The Commission does not find the Public Staff Thermal Resources Panel’s
testimony regarding alleged delay on Duke’s part persuasive. Informing this determination
are the Commission’s observations that the panel failed to identify any specific
unreasonable action or inaction on Duke’s part and did not recommend a preferred reactor
technology, which was and remains a core gating item governing the scope and pace of
execution activities. The Commission also notes that Duke’s proposed near-term actions
over the next two years will be necessary regardless of which development pathways are
ultimately selected (as seen below in the figures J-4 and J-5 from Appendix J). However,
the Commission notes that Duke’s proposed 2037 first in-service date in this IRP
proceeding is a two-year departure from the 2035 first in-service date the Commission
approved in its 2024 CPIRP Order. Therefore, though the Commission disagrees with the
Public Staff’s contention that Duke has not made substantive efforts towards evaluating
the development of new nuclear resources, the Commission does not have an adequate
record to determine whether Duke’s new proposed 2037 first in-service date for a new
LLWR or 600 MW of SMR resources is reasonable for planning purposes.

153

Generalized assertions regarding an alleged lack of execution does not sufficiently
demonstrate that Duke has materially failed to progress towards future deployment of
new nuclear generation resources given that it is still in the process of making critically
important technology decisions which cannot and should not be rushed. The Commission
notes that the importance of extensive consideration of technology selection was reflected
in the prudence of more detailed consideration of large light water reactors following
industry developments occurring in 2024 and 2025. The Commission further notes that
the Public Staff neither addressed this dynamic nor did they offer any concrete
recommendation regarding which nuclear technology Duke should have selected or
which specific additional execution activities should have been pursued.

154

Similarly, the Commission is unpersuaded by the recommendations of CIGFUR
witness Muller, as the Commission finds those recommendations to be, on balance,
overly prescriptive and, therefore, gives this testimony little weight. However, the
Commission acknowledges witness Muller’s testimony regarding the importance of
Duke’s current nuclear reactor fleet as a critical baseload generation resource and
concurs with his statements regarding the appropriateness of Duke’s pursuit of
governmental funding and other cost protection measures. The Commission notes that
the detailed and careful modeling process that has been used by Duke, the Public Staff,
NCSEA, and SACE et al., already accounts for the reliability and operational
characteristics of all resources.
The Commission recognizes the concerns raised by NC WARN regarding
historical cost increases in nuclear reactor development. The Commission finds that
Duke’s proposed second-mover nuclear development strategy is informed by and
addresses these concerns. Further, the Commission is not persuaded by NC WARN’s
recommendation that solar plus storage resources can sufficiently replace the modeled
capacity which new nuclear generation has been selected to provide and notes that NC
WARN did not provide any modeling to support its analysis. The Commission gives the
testimony of NC WARN in this regard no weight.
The Commission recognizes the magnitude of the investment, the inherent timing
impacts of the technology selection, and the need to protect customers as being critical
factors in Duke’s technology selection and execution activities. The Commission
concludes that selecting a reactor technology is a threshold question requiring meaningful
evaluation that is beyond the scope of the CPIRP modeling process but must occur prior
to preparation of a CPCN application. Further, as previously noted, the Commission
cannot conclude Duke’s proposed 2037 first in-service date for a new LLWR or 600 MW
of SMR resources is reasonable. To determine the earliest and most prudent timeframe
that Duke could bring a new nuclear reactor into service, and to provide for timely
reporting of the nuclear reactor selection and development process, the Commission will
open a new docket for the purpose of receiving additional information regarding new
nuclear development and advanced reactor resource selection (Nuclear Development
Docket). The Commission concludes it is appropriate to require Duke to file the following
informational updates quarterly in this Nuclear Development Docket:
1.
selection;

material milestones reached in advanced reactor technology design and

2.
material updates in all NRC proceedings, including updates on permit
submission, timeline, and disposition for Duke-owned facilities as well as "first mover"
facilities that are anticipated to affect Duke's permitting timelines;
3.
an evaluation of the earliest and most prudent timeframe to place an
additional nuclear resource into service, and any developments that cause this proposed
first-in-service date to change by more than one year. In the event in-service dates are
delayed, Duke shall detail in that report how it will meet anticipated load if the new nuclear
generation is unavailable; and
155

4.
material developments in Duke’s efforts to engage with federal initiatives to
reduce risk surrounding financing, supply chain, and labor necessary to construct new
nuclear units.
The Commission will issue an Order by June 30, 2027, to schedule a technical
conference in the Nuclear Development Docket. This technical conference will solicit a
wide array of perspectives to evaluate the parameters identified in Appendix J that are
necessary to support a well-informed decision to build a new nuclear plant:
1.

capital required to finance a new nuclear generation project;

2.

project execution risks;

3.

resources needed to build a new nuclear generation project at scale;

4.

state of the supply chain for major equipment;

5.

engineering, procurement, and construction market readiness;

6.

NRC licensing of different technologies;

7.

customer, community, and investor perspectives;

8.

siting options and requirements;

9.

use of sites available in the Duke Energy service territories;

10.

federal tax credits and supporting legislation;

11.

exposure to import tariffs;

12.

North Carolina and South Carolina regulatory and legislative perspectives; and

13.

industry consortium possibilities.

In its next CPIRP submission, Duke shall consider the findings from this technical
conference in its analyses and shall update the Commission on decisions made by peer
utilities pertaining to new nuclear development as well as the “first mover” projects it is
evaluating to inform its development strategy. Duke shall also report on the state of SMR
development and the state of the new nuclear supply chain as it pertains to its anticipated
technology choice or choices.
Duke is also directed to inform the Commission on the material steps towards new
nuclear development it has taken at each of its Lee, Harris, and Belews Creek sites, as
well as any additional identified site candidates, and its proposed next steps to be taken
in support of its identified earliest prudent first-in-service date in its next CPIRP filing.
Based on the competent, material, and substantial evidence in the record, the
Commission concludes that it is reasonable to approve Duke’s plans to continue
156

development activities in 2026–2028 to support the future availability of SMRs and
LLWRs. The Commission further concludes that Duke’s proposed project development
activities for new advanced nuclear generation, as presented in the Duke Recommended
NTAP and proposed Execution Plan, are reasonable and prudent steps in executing the
2025 CPIRP and are necessary to enable development and future selection of either
SMRs or LLWR units. Accordingly, the Commission extends the prior authorization for
Duke to incur up to $440 million pursuant to N.C.G.S. § 62-110.7 for the development of
new advanced nuclear resources through 2028 for the purpose of progressing initial
development of both SMRs and LLWRs. Consistent with the Commission’s prior
determinations in the 2024 CPIRP Order, the Commission’s approval of Duke’s request
to incur project development costs constitutes reasonable assurance of cost
recoverability in a future general rate case subject to the Commission’s review of the
reasonableness and prudence of specific costs incurred in such future proceeding. In the
event these long lead time resources are ultimately determined not to be necessary to
achieve the energy transition and comply with the carbon reduction provisions of the
Carbon Plan Statute, such project development costs will be recoverable through base
rates over a period of time to be determined by the Commission at the appropriate time.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 61–62
Near-Term Development Activities for Existing Nuclear Fleet
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in the 2025 CPIRP; the
testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Nuclear Panel (Capps and Nader); the testimony and
exhibits of Public Staff witness Michna; the testimony and exhibits of CIGFUR witness
Muller; the testimony and exhibits of NC WARN witness Powers; and the entire record.
Duke Direct Testimony
In prefiled direct testimony, the Duke Nuclear Panel stated that Duke operates the
nation’s largest regulated nuclear fleet through 11 units at six sites in the Carolinas, which
produces about 51% of the energy needs and over 96% of the clean energy produced for
our customers in the Carolinas. Tr. vol. 6, 151–52. They explained that the nuclear fleet is
highly reliable, achieving a fleet capacity factor of 94.8% in 2024 and marking the 26th
consecutive year with a capacity factor greater than 90%. They further noted that nuclear
is the only clean energy source that can operate 24/7 (except during outages) regardless
of weather conditions. Id. at 152. The Nuclear Panel stated that Duke is taking reasonable
steps towards extending the operational life of the existing nuclear fleet through subsequent
license renewals (SLRs), adding 245 MW of capacity to the existing fleet by pursuing
extended power uprate (EPU) and measurement uncertainty recapture (MUR) projects,
and reducing the number of refueling outages through 24-month fuel cycle projects. Id.
The Execution Plan noted that “[e]xtending the operational life of the Companies’
existing nuclear fleet is a foundational assumption for the Plan, providing for the
continuation of the only source of reliable, clean, and cost-competitive energy that is
always on 24 hours a day regardless of weather conditions.” 2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 14.
157

The Execution Plan also noted that Duke’s nuclear fleet provides 10,819 MW of clean
energy, enough to power more than eight million homes. Id.
SLRs
The Execution Plan further noted that ensuring that the nuclear fleet continues to
operate through midcentury requires federal regulatory approval of SLR applications for
all nuclear units, extending the operational life by an additional 20 years, from 60 years
of operation up to 80 years. Id. The Execution Plan also explained that the current
operating licenses start to expire in 2030, and the regulatory process may take up to four
years per SLR application. Id.
In prefiled direct testimony, the Nuclear Panel stated that, in accordance with the
2024 CPIRP Order, on March 31, 2025, the NRC approved DEC’s SLR application for
the three units at the Oconee Nuclear Station (Oconee) in Seneca, South Carolina,
making Oconee the first site in Duke’s nuclear fleet to obtain an extension to operate the
facility for up to 80 years. Tr. vol. 6, 152. The Execution Plan noted that with an approved
SLR, Oconee’s three units may operate through 2053–2054. 2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 14.
The Nuclear Panel also indicated that on April 1, 2025, DEP submitted the next
SLR application to the NRC for Robinson Nuclear Plant (Robinson) in Hartsville, South
Carolina, to operate for up to 80 years, beginning the comprehensive review process.
Tr. vol. 6, 152–53. They explained that DEP is currently working to prepare the next SLR
application for Brunswick Nuclear Plant (Brunswick) in Southport, North Carolina. Id. at
153. The Execution Plan also stated that Duke plans to develop and submit an SLR
application for each of the four remaining nuclear sites in the 2027–2035 timeframe.
2025 CPIRP Ch. 4 at 14.
EPU, MUR, and 24-Month Fuel Cycle Projects
In addition to extending the operating licenses at each site, the Execution Plan
explained that Duke continues to leverage its existing infrastructure and optimize the use
of EPUs, where cost-effective, to increase the total capacity of the nuclear fleet. Id. The
Execution Plan noted that several of the nuclear facilities (e.g., Brunswick, Harris, and
Robinson) have already been uprated, while the remaining facilities (e.g., Catawba,
McGuire, and Oconee) have available design margin and are being evaluated for
modifications to increase their power output. Id. at 14–15. Furthermore, Duke reported in
the Execution Plan that design studies and engineering evaluations are underway for
Catawba Unit 1 and McGuire Units 1 and 2, while license amendment requests (LARs)
are being prepared for submittal to the NRC for review and approval. Id. at 15. The
Execution Plan indicated that, when implemented at Catawba and McGuire, the EPU
projects will add about 225 MW of clean, round-the-clock capacity to the system. Id. The
Execution Plan also stated that Duke will continue to look for further opportunities,
including the evaluation for an EPU project at Oconee that includes investigating the
required component replacements, costs, and timing. Id.

158

The Execution Plan explained that MUR power uprate projects allow more
accurate measurements of parameters in the plant, resulting in additional incremental
power output. Id. The Execution Plan reported that a MUR project at Oconee was
completed in 2024 and resulted in an additional 46 MW (i.e., approximately 15 MW per
unit) of capacity and that Duke is also pursuing an MUR project at Brunswick, which is
expected to increase power output by about 20 MW overall, or about 10 MW per unit. Id.
The Nuclear Panel indicated that the EPU and MUR projects are all scheduled to be
implemented by year-end 2031. Tr. vol. 6, 154.
Finally, the Execution Plan explained that 24-month fuel cycle projects are being
planned for Catawba (Units 1 and 2), Harris (Unit 1), and McGuire (Units 1 and 2) to
extend their fuel cycle length from 18 months to 24 months and noted that this work has
already been completed for the Brunswick, Oconee, and Robinson units. 2025 CPIRP
Ch. 4 at 15. The Execution Plan explained that extension of fuel cycle length reduces the
number of refueling outage days during the remaining life of the plants, thereby increasing
the capacity factor and total energy produced during the remaining life of the plant. Id.
NTAP for Existing Nuclear Fleet
The Execution Plan stated that SLR, EPU, MUR, and 24-month fuel cycle projects
will allow Duke’s existing nuclear fleet to continue to provide clean and cost-effective
baseload power to the Carolinas well into the midcentury. Id. Table 4-5 from the Execution
Plan outlines the Duke Recommended NTAP for existing nuclear:

159

Table J-2 from Appendix J provides the expansions for each plant, including the estimated
MW, timeline, and costs:

Request for Relief
Finally, the Nuclear Panel indicated in prefiled direct testimony that they support
request for relief (4) detailed in Duke’s Petition and recommended that the Commission
approve Duke’s proposed actions with respect to existing nuclear resources, including
continued disciplined pursuit of power uprate projects for Duke’s existing nuclear fleet as
described in the Execution Plan and Appendix J. Tr. vol. 6, 151–52.
Intervenor Direct Testimony
CIGFUR witness Muller stated that Duke 's existing nuclear fleet has been critical
to ensuring reliable power during recent extreme temperature events. Tr. vol. 20, 97.
NC WARN witness Powers indicated that Duke’s existing nuclear fleet is “[v]ery
substantial” and that more than half of electricity used by Duke customers in the Carolinas
is produced by nuclear power plants. Tr. vol. 22, 202. He also noted that there are 11
operational nuclear reactors at six different sites in North Carolina and South Carolina
with a total capacity of 10,819 MW. Id. Witness Powers stated that Duke is pursuing
160

20-year SLRs for all 11 of the reactors in its operational nuclear fleet in the Carolinas and
that the SLRs will extend the operating lifetime of Duke’s nuclear fleet in the Carolinas
from an original 40 to 80 years, which means that all of Duke’s existing reactors will still
be operational in 2050. Id. at 202–03. He also noted that over 10,000 MW of Duke’s
existing 10,819 MW nuclear fleet will be online through 2053 and over 7,000 MW will be
online until 2055 and that Duke’s nuclear fleet produced approximately 90,000 GWh in
2024, which satisfied about 60% of Duke’s total demand for the year. Id. at 203. Finally,
he characterized Duke’s existing nuclear fleet as being Duke’s backbone baseload
resource and stated that the nuclear fleet had a combined capacity factor of 94.8% in
2024, meaning that the nuclear fleet operated at full power round-the-clock for nearly the
entire year in 2024. Id. at 203–04.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
On rebuttal, the Nuclear Panel advised the Commission that, on April 23, 2026,
the NRC extended the Robinson license an additional 20 years from 2030 to 2050, which
was the fastest license renewal review in NRC history and the first application completed
under new federal reforms aimed at accelerating nuclear regulatory approvals. Tr. vol. 22,
559. They stated that with Robinson’s SLR, Duke has now completed license renewal
projects for four of 11 units at two of six sites. Id. The Nuclear Panel also noted that Duke
is continuing the work needed to submit an SLR application for Brunswick Nuclear Plant
units 1 and 2 by year-end 2026, which are meant to extend those unit licenses an
additional 20 years — unit 1 from 2036 to 2056 and unit 2 from 2034 to 2054. Id.
Discussion and Conclusions
The Commission concludes that Duke is successfully operating the largest nuclear
fleet in the nation, providing customers in the Carolinas access to affordable energy 24/7.
The Commission gives significant weight to the testimony of the Duke Nuclear Panel, the
Execution Plan, and Appendix J and further notes that no party opposes the Duke
Recommended NTAP for its existing nuclear fleet. Based on the competent, material, and
substantial evidence in the record, the Commission concludes that it is reasonable to
approve proposed actions with respect to existing nuclear resources, including continued
disciplined pursuit of power uprate projects for Duke’s existing nuclear fleet as described
in the Execution Plan and Appendix J.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 63–67
Transmission Planning and Interconnection Considerations
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Chapter 4 and
Appendix K of the 2025 CPIRP; the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Transmission and
Interconnection Panel (Roberts and Shi); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff’s
Transmission Panel (Lawrence and Ssengonzi); the testimony and exhibits of SACE, et
al. witnesses Roumpani and Goggin; the testimony and exhibits of AGO witness Burgess;
the testimony and exhibits of CCEBA witness Hagerty, and the entire record.

161

Duke’s Direct Testimony and Supplemental Testimony
Duke’s transmission and interconnection planning and execution are detailed in
the transmission-related portion of Chapter 4 and Appendix K of the 2025 CPIRP. The
2025 CPIRP addressed several key transmission and interconnection planning and
execution issues and provided a comprehensive overview and update on the
transmission system transformation and expansion needed to execute the Duke
Recommended NTAP and enable execution of mid- to long-term plans presented in the
2025 CPIRP.
In prefiled direct testimony, Duke witness Roberts indicated that Duke has made
significant progress in the areas of strategic transmission planning, transmission planning
processes, interconnection processes, utilization of the Federal Energy Regulatory
Commission (FERC)-accepted generation replacement and provisional interconnection
service processes, and planned compliance with FERC Order No. 2023 cluster study
requirements. Tr. vol. 12, 19–20. As part of this progress, Duke utilized a MVST Study
that provides a forward-looking approach to arrive at “least regrets or no regrets proactive
transmission projects.” Id. at 76. Duke witness Shi described Duke’s response to
generator interconnection challenges caused by significant anticipated CPIRP resource
additions needed to accommodate forecasted economic development, ensure reliability,
and connect new resources to replace retiring generation. Id. at 38–39.
Compliance with the 2024 CPIRP Order
Witness Roberts stated that Duke has taken specific steps to address the
Commission’s directives in the 2024 CPIRP Order. Id. at 24. He noted that the 2024 CPIRP
Order directed Duke to “continue to align and integrate its IRP for generation with
transmission planning to efficiently and cost-effectively meet the supply-side generation
resource additions identified in the 2025 CPIRP, including by engaging in the CTPC and
working with the Public Staff and other interested stakeholders on a 2024 MVST Study
aligned with the CPIRP execution plan approved” therein. Id.
Witness Roberts explained that the 2024 MVST Study considered three different
scenarios and several sensitivities studied under industry standard credible contingency
considerations to identify transmission system impacts and least-regrets strategic
transmission solutions. Id. at 25, 29, 65. He stated that Duke collaborated with the Public
Staff through CTPC Transmission Advisory Group Stakeholder meetings to discuss
2024 MVST Study results and respond to Public Staff questions. Id. at 25–26. During the
expert witness hearing, witness Roberts testified that Duke modified the portfolios under
consideration in the MVST Study process to incorporate stakeholder feedback. Id. at 67.
Duke also took stakeholder recommendations to look at corridor zone needs versus
individual needs, which witness Roberts stated allowed for solutions that solved more
than one need. Id. at 80–81. During cross-examination, Witness Roberts also stated that
Duke developed a production cost model over the period of the MVST study that they
used to calculate production cost benefits of the study, but that Duke would need more
run time with the model to ensure it is accurate. Id. at 88–89.
162

In prefiled direct testimony, Witness Roberts stated that Duke has and will continue
to file updates on the CTPC process and RZEP construction in Docket
No. E-100, Sub 205 in compliance with the 2024 CPIRP Order. Id. at 28.
In Appendix K, Duke provided an update on the Southeast Energy Exchange
Market (SEEM), which is a bilateral trading platform that allows the 23 SEEM entities to
buy and sell power on a non-firm, sub-hourly basis. 2025 CPIRP App. K at 45. Duke
reported that from inception through July 2025, Duke entered into 81,000 SEEM trades
with an estimated benefit of over $4 million. Id. at 46. In response to Commissioner
questions on SEEM, witness Roberts stated that Duke has seen a good increase
associated with SEEM purchases and sales above the number provided in Appendix K.
Tr. vol. 12, 116.
In prefiled direct testimony, witness Roberts noted that the 2024 CPIRP Order
directed Duke to “continue developing a local transmission plan that focuses on
identifying least-regrets transmission system upgrades, including the consideration of
[Grid-Enhancing Technologies], that is (1) beneficial across a range of future scenarios,
including system stress scenarios (such as extreme weather), and (2) supports the
delivery of multiple future resource additions in a manner that maintains or improves the
reliability of the grid.” Id. at 28–29; 2024 CPIRP Order at 180 (Ordering ¶ 48). He pointed
to Duke’s compliance with this directive through the MVST Study process, which
evaluated and prioritized the transmission needs resulting from studying three future
scenarios of possible resource portfolios with various sensitivities. Tr. vol. 12, 25. Witness
Roberts elaborated that alternative solutions considered to mitigate the identified and
prioritized transmission needs include greenfield transmission, right-of-way optimization,
grid-enhancing technologies (GETs), advanced conductors, Remedial Action Schemes,
and battery storage. Id. at 29.
Witness Roberts also noted that the Commission directed Duke to address GETs
in its next CPIRP filing, including “(1) a description of GETs that have been deployed on
Duke’s systems at the time of the filing; (2) a description of GETs under development at
the time of the filing; (3) a summary of the costs and benefits of GETs that Duke has
already deployed and are under development; and (4) an explanation of Duke’s decision
to not implement any GETs that it evaluated.” Id. at 29–30. Duke has shown, he
contended, that it has evaluated and utilized GETs as alternative solutions to identified
transmission needs and will continue to evaluate and utilize these potential alternative
solutions where they are determined to be a sustainable and reliable solution for
addressing identified transmission needs. Id. at 30; 2025 CPIRP App. K at 15–18.
Witness Roberts noted that the Commission directed Duke to “continue to explore
and develop options for earlier interconnection of generators on Duke’s systems through
the stakeholder process held in conjunction with the annual solar procurement process.”
Tr. vol. 12, 30. He stated that Duke has invested substantial time and resources into
developing a robust provisional interconnection service process that is compatible with
the cluster study framework for FERC- and state-jurisdictional interconnection customers.
Id. at 31. Since obtaining regulatory approval, witness Roberts noted that Duke
implemented the improved and expanded process and began accepting provisional
163

interconnection service requests from new solar and BESS resources as well as natural
gas uprate resources in the state- and FERC-jurisdictional interconnection queues. Id. He
additionally noted that in the most recent July 2025 FERC Order No. 2023/2023-A state
interconnection procedures alignment filings Duke proposed to add, based on
stakeholder feedback, a new option to allow certain qualifying customers to request an
Engineering and Procurement Agreement to authorize Duke to begin engineering and
procurement of long-lead materials necessary for interconnection prior to execution of the
associated Interconnection Agreement (IA). Id. at 31–32. Witness Roberts indicated that
this additional optionality will provide flexibility to solar developers who wish to accelerate
engineering and procurement activities. Id. at 32.
As noted by witness Roberts in his prefiled direct testimony, the 2024 CPIRP Order
also directed Duke to “utilize any information obtained from use of provisional
interconnection service and study ERIS as another interconnection option.” Id.;
2024 CPIRP Order at 180 (Ordering ¶ 51). In Appendix K, Duke shared that there are
several reliability concerns with connecting high volumes of resources under ERIS
interconnection service without limitations. 2025 CPIRP App. K at 24. Witness Roberts
indicated that Duke implemented Provisional Interconnection Service, which allows
generator resources to undergo an ERIS evaluation and interconnect to the grid by
utilizing “as available” transmission service for the period needed to construct and
place in-service network upgrades necessary for firm deliverability of the resources’
output to reliably serve native load. Tr. vol. 12, 32–33, 41, 95–96, 98; 2025 CPIRP App. K
at 24–25. As more resources request and start using Provisional Interconnection Service,
witness Roberts explained, Duke will continue to evaluate information obtained from the
use of such service to further study ERIS as another interconnection option. Tr. vol. 12,
33.
Witness Roberts further noted that the 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke to
“evaluate the interconnection of solar and battery storage resources at existing
utility-owned solar, coal, and CT sites; assess whether to include these opportunities in
its near-term procurement efforts to reduce costs and development timelines; and provide
an update on its findings, including the impact on interconnection limits, in the next
CPIRP.” Id. at 34–35; 2024 CPIRP Order at 177 (Ordering ¶ 23). Appendix K described
Duke’s evaluation of the potential for new solar and battery storage resources at
existing generating sites and presented the results of this assessment. 2025 CPIRP App.
K at 25–29, Table K-7–K-10. Based on this analysis, Duke concluded that limited
opportunity exists to materially impact longer-term interconnection limits over the CPIRP
planning horizon and that surplus interconnection opportunities identified in its analysis
were already factored into resource availability assumptions. Tr. vol. 12, 29. Witness
Roberts indicated that Duke is leveraging five standalone solar sites to accommodate
surplus storage and evaluating four additional future sites. Id. at 35. He explained that
Duke has determined that generation replacement would be utilized in conjunction with
planned coal retirements. Id. Lastly, he reported that one CT site was identified where
surplus solar is already planned and sited while Duke will continue to evaluate another
site with potential suitable land for surplus solar capability. Id.

164

Witness Roberts noted that the 2024 CPIRP Order directed Duke to provide
“substantial justification for any delays to the coal unit retirement schedule [approved in
the CPIRP Order] in the next CPIRP proceeding.” Id. at 37; 2024 CPIRP Order at 175
(Ordering ¶ 6). In the 2025 CPIRP, Duke showed that the Belews Creek generation
location, along with its active and reactive power capability, fulfills a critical voltage
support role in DEC’s Triad area of its system. 2025 CPIRP App. K at 40. Witness Roberts
stated that this active and reactive capability must be replaced prior to the retirement of
Belews Creek generation. Tr. vol. 12, 37. He explained that Duke plans to replace the
retiring Belews Creek coal generation with CT generators and nuclear generation by
2040, thereby replacing the capability while ensuring reliability is maintained or improved
for Triad area customers. Id. Witness Roberts further explained that this schedule for
replacement informs the updated retirement schedule for these supercritical coal units,
which are also capable of 50% natural gas co-firing. Id. at 37–38. He noted that the CTs
were evaluated in the 2024 DEC DISIS study and would be placed in service prior to the
coal units. Id. at 38. Witness Roberts lastly indicated that Duke evaluated the combination
of CT generators and nuclear generation as replacement generation for Belews Creek
coal generation in the 2024 MVST Study. Id.
Witness Roberts stated that Duke introduced a second phase of proactive local
transmission planning projects with the seven RZEP 2.0 projects in the North Carolina
Transmission Planning Collaborative (NCTPC) 2024–2034 Local Transmission Plan. Id.
at 34. He noted that the 2023 NCTPC Public Policy Study, which analyzed transmission
needs for solar and other generator resource types represented in the SPA P3 Fall Base
Case Portfolio, validated the need for the RZEP 2.0 projects. Id. During
cross-examination, witness Roberts noted that in addition to the 2023 Public Policy Study,
the recent MVST Study confirmed the need for the RZEP projects. Id. at 73.
Impact of the Interconnection Queue on Transmission Development
In prefiled direct testimony, witness Shi stated that Duke has advanced cluster
study interconnection policy improvements at the federal and state level, implemented
provisional interconnection service, and leveraged the generator replacement process to
replace retiring generators with new generation resources that interconnect at the same
switchyard. Id. at 41–42. She testified that Duke also continues to assess potential
opportunities to utilize fast track, cost-effective surplus interconnection service. Id. at 42.
During the expert witness hearing, witness Shi shared that as of the hearing, 48 solar and
solar plus storage projects requested provisional interconnection service and
approximately half were still active. Id. at 71. She noted that, of those projects, three had
executed agreements. Id. at 71–72. Witness Shi also expressed that she considered the
provisional interconnection process to be successful and that Duke intends to proceed
and continue with provisional interconnection service. Id. at 72.
Witness Shi noted, in prefiled direct testimony, that Duke has further improved its
interconnection process since the 2024 CPIRP Order by improving efficiencies as
detailed on pages 32–34 of Appendix K, which include optionality for ordering materials,
developing standard interconnection engineering designs, enhancing workflow
automation, and other key steps to shorten interconnection timelines to meet resource
165

plan objectives. Id. at 42. She continued by stating that Duke developed Inverter-Based
Resources (IBR) Interconnection Requirements to ensure reliable interconnection and
parallel operation of IBRs to its transmission systems. Id. at 43. Witness Shi stated that
Duke has also progressed in implementing new requirements for transmission-connected
IBR resources, including incorporating new IBR requirements and milestones into the IAs
of Transitional and 2022 DISIS Clusters’ state and FERC projects. Id. at 45.
Witness Shi additionally stated that Duke has taken steps to mitigate the risk of
late-stage withdrawals in the cluster study process. First, she noted that Duke’s transition
from a serial study process to a cluster study process serves as an initial mitigant;
additionally, Duke introduced increasing readiness criteria and withdrawal penalties as
projects proceed through the study phases. Id. at 52. Second, witness Shi indicated that
Duke began a separate cluster study process in 2023 (the Resource Solicitation Cluster)
to mitigate any impact that a large generator may have had on solar procurement
generator interconnection requests. Id. at 52–53. Third, she noted that Duke recently
revised its North Carolina Interconnection Procedures to align with FERC Order 2023,
which includes higher financial disincentives for late withdrawals. Id. at 53. Fourth,
witness Shi stated that Provisional Interconnection Service and the Engineering and
Procurement Agreement process are now available as options for all cluster participants,
which allows interconnection projects to progress while any restudies occur and thus
minimizes any schedule delay impacts from changes to network upgrades or contingent
facility assignments. Id. at 53–54. Finally, she noted that Duke streamlined analyses and
restudies to issue Restudy Reports in a shortened timeline. Id. at 54.
Public Staff Direct Testimony
The Public Staff Transmission Panel stated that Duke’s transmission planning
processes are adequate and that the incorporation of MVST planning into the
transmission planning process provides the opportunity for a more robust, holistic study
process. Id. at 185. The panel reflected that Duke’s transition from a serial study process
to a cluster study process has yielded benefits, including shortened study times,
decreased speculative projects, and a shortened period that each project spends in the
interconnection queue. Id. at 195. The Transmission Panel provided an update on the
22 RZEP projects (inclusive of RZEP 1.0 and 2.0), including that the five projects
completed at the time of their testimony were completed at a combined total cost that was
$22.59 million lower than projected. Id. at 209–10.
The Transmission Panel expressed concern regarding increasing energy transfers
from DEP to DEC and explained that incorporating a transmission transfer rate (TTR) into
Duke’s modeling would address this concern. Id. at 200–01, 211. However, the panel did
not ultimately recommend a TTR in the present proceeding largely “due to optimism that
the [DEC and DEP] Merger will be approved and help resolve this cost causation issue
over the long term.” Id. at 201, 211.
Finally, the Transmission Panel stated that Duke’s EnCompass modeling inputs
for system upgrade costs for interconnection of generation, the intertie capacity limit for
166

energy transfers between DEC and DEP, and the cumulative interconnection limits are
reasonable for planning purposes. Id. at 204, 211.
Other Intervenor Direct Testimony
CCEBA witness Hagerty indicated that Duke has made significant improvements
to its transmission planning and interconnection processes over the past several years,
including the implementation of cluster studies in the interconnection process,
identification and construction of RZEP projects, and completing its initial MVST planning
process through the CTPC. Tr. vol. 19, 86. In response to Commissioner questions, he
stated that his conversations with developers had led him to believe that provisional
interconnection service has been a success used by developers and has allowed
resources to come online several years faster than if they had to wait for upgrades.” Id.
at 116. On cross-examination, witness Hagerty testified that the MVST process has
“definitely been a success over the past two years” that has enabled Duke to conduct
integrated resource and transmission planning. Id. at 119–20.
In prefiled direct testimony, witness Hagerty recommended that the next MVST
scenarios include at least two projections of future load growth. Id. at 97. He also
recommended changes for the role of the CTPC in the next MVST study, including
recommendations that CTPC should: (1) summarize constraints in the base case
production cost model that result in uneconomic dispatch and the costs of the binding
constraints during the needs assessment phase, similar to the results shared for the
reliability models, and provide a summary to stakeholders for their review; (2) validate its
production cost model by replicating the load, generation resource mix, renewable energy
generation profiles, and natural gas prices of a recent historical year and comparing key
outputs from the model, including generation mix, renewable curtailments, and total
production costs, against their actual system in order to identify how well the model
reflects the real-world system; and (3) present, in more detail, its analysis of each of the
benefits so that stakeholders can understand the impacts of each proposed solution to
the system and the drivers of benefits. Id.
Witness Hagerty also recommended that the Commission direct Duke to study the
effectiveness of SEEM to enable cost-saving transactions with neighboring systems,
including current limitations to the market framework, improvements that can be made to
SEEM to increase its effectiveness, and comparisons of the effectiveness of SEEM to a
regional Southeast energy imbalance market. Id. at 70. During the expert witness hearing,
witness Hagerty provided further comments on SEEM, recommending that Duke account
for SEEM transactions in its resource planning and that Duke increase transparency of
SEEM by providing an assessment of the transactions that have occurred and the
estimated benefits that have occurred since SEEM’s inception, as well as how it is
improving. Id. at 129, 133. Witness Hagerty also stated if SEEM is not accounted for in
Duke’s resource planning, then Duke is also not accounting for the fact that it can reduce
curtailments. Id. at 129.
In prefiled direct testimony, SACE et al. witness Roumpani disagreed with Duke’s
decision not to conduct a site-by-site analysis of the potential for surplus interconnection
167

at existing coal-generating facilities. Tr. vol. 20, 344. She asserted that Duke’s choice to
wait for the full GRR process to be complete, rather than interconnecting at already
existing coal sites, indicates that “Duke is delaying opportunities to add clean energy
through transmission infrastructure that already exists.” Id. at 346. Witness Roumpani
recommended that Duke be required to evaluate opportunities to interconnect new
resources at existing coal sites using surplus interconnection service. Id. at 354.
SACE et al. witness Goggin criticized Duke’s implementation of the MVST process
in his prefiled direct testimony. He stated that the MVST process has failed to live up to
its potential because it is focused on reactively responding to near-term reliability
problems and has not used economic tools to examine a broader set of needs and identify
more optimal solutions. Tr. vol. 22, 250. He recommended multiple reforms to the MVST
Study process, including using a 20-year outlook similar to Order 1920 for the CTPC
process, identifying transmission needs based on economics and not just reliability, better
utilizing tools like nodal production cost modeling to identify both transmission needs and
optimal solutions that can deliver the lowest-cost power to ratepayers, and transparently
evaluating a broader range of solutions, including higher-voltage and greenfield
transmission, interim solutions like GETs and strategically-sited batteries, and expanded
transmission ties with neighbors and sharing the estimated benefits associated with these
alternatives. Id. at 242–43, 256. Witness Goggin stated that absent these reforms, Duke’s
transmission planning process will continue to follow reactive reliability planning, which
does not anticipate needs and leads to a grid that expands too slowly. Id. at 243. He also
contended that Duke overstates the challenges associated with interconnecting new
generating resources, arguing that other grid operators are successfully interconnecting
new generation and storage resources at a significantly faster rate. Id. at 244. Finally,
witness Goggin recommended that the Commission require Duke to evaluate the
incremental net benefits of creating an energy imbalance market in the Southeast relative
to SEEM. Id. at 335.
In prefiled direct testimony, AGO witness Burgess indicated that Duke identified
proactive transmission projects already under way for the purpose of enabling greater
solar buildout. Id. at 115. However, he contended that Duke could have taken further
steps to address transmission capacity issues, including: (1) the use of surplus
interconnection at existing coal and natural gas facilities and (2) the use of flexible “energy
only” interconnection. Id. at 116. Witness Burgess recommended that the Commission
require Duke to complete an evaluation of surplus interconnection at existing coal sites
that mirrors the analysis Duke has already completed for its CT sites within 90 days of
the date on which the Commission issues its order in this proceeding. Id. at 135.
Duke Rebuttal Testimony
In prefiled rebuttal testimony, Witness Roberts stated that Duke completed the
first-of-its-kind 2024 MVST Study in 2025 and published the results in the 2025–2035
CTPC Local Transmission Plan. Tr. vol. 23, 17. He noted that the 2024 MVST Study
produced five MVST projects for inclusion in the 2025 Collaborative Transmission Plan,
two of which are in the “planned” stage and three of which are “conceptual” and will be
168

monitored for addressing transmission needs identified in subsequent MVST studies.
Id. at 18.
Additionally, witness Roberts explained that Duke’s RZEP process, designed to
proactively address identified transmission constraints with local transmission projects for
the benefit of all customers, resulted in two rounds of projects: RZEP 1.0 and RZEP 2.0.
Id. Of the 15 projects included in RZEP 1.0, he stated that 14 are expected to be in-service
during 2026. The fifteenth project, the Robinson – Rockingham 115 kV upgrade, is
estimated to be in service in 2027. Id. Witness Roberts concluded that RZEP 2.0 projects
are moving through the project management phases which include engineering and
construction. Id.
Witness Roberts disagreed with witnesses Hagerty and Goggin that nodal
production cost modeling is a best practice for identifying transmission needs in the
Carolinas. Id. at 23. He explained that the MVST process already evaluates transmission
needs across multiple future scenarios and sensitivities, providing a comprehensive view
of potential overloads, and benchmarks power flow results against transmission planning
and operations to ensure legitimacy of identified needs. Id. at 23–24. Witness Roberts
argued that the nodal production cost model should not be relied upon as a standalone
modeling resource, as it can be sensitive to underlying modeling assumptions for
neighboring systems. Id. at 24. He noted that Duke did develop and use nodal production
cost modeling for benefit quantification with the 2024 MVST Study. Id.
Witness Roberts agreed that witness Hagerty’s suggestion to include at least two
scenarios with different load growth projections has merit and that it can be considered
for the 2026 MVST Study. Id. at 25. He further agreed with witness Hagerty that validating
production cost models is prudent. Id. Witness Roberts further clarified that a TAG
stakeholder meeting dedicated to providing details on the MVST benefits analysis was
held on February 27, 2026, consistent with witness Hagerty’s recommendation to provide
additional details. Id. at 25–26. He explained that Duke already incorporated witness
Hagerty’s recommendation to summarize the constraints in the base case production cost
model that result in uneconomic dispatch, resulting in 299 BA interchange, transmission
limits, and individual branch constraints modeled for DEC and DEP in the MVST nodal
study cases. Id. at 25.
With regard to witness Goggin’s suggestions regarding a 20-year planning horizon
for MVST study process, witness Roberts stated that the CTPC plans to transition to a
20-year model to align with FERC Order No. 1920, but he clarified that this transition will
be undertaken cautiously in light of the uncertainty of resource type, size, and location to
avoid advocating for projects that are not ultimately needed. Id. at 23, 26. Regarding
witness Goggin’s assertion that Duke’s planning failed to adequately evaluate greenfield
and higher-voltage solutions, witness Roberts explained that the MVST Study showed that
greenfield solutions had limited effectiveness for addressing identified needs. Id. at 27.
Witness Roberts clarified, in response to witness Goggin’s assertion that RZEP
2.0 projects meet only 11% of identified transmission needs, by stating that the 11%
quantity represents transmission lines or line segments that were overloaded by study
169

results – i.e., 11% by number only, not utilization. Id. at 30. He noted that contrary to
witness Goggin’s implications, the 2023 Public Policy Study validated the choice of “no
regrets” RZEP 2.0 projects. Id. Witness Roberts agreed with witness Goggin that the
RZEP projects have increased interconnection rates. Id.
Witness Roberts did not share the Public Staff’s concern regarding projected
DEP-East to DEC transfers based on EnCompass modeling. Id. at 31. He noted that,
holding all else equal, if resources are sited in these legacy DEC and DEP-East areas as
predicted by the Utility Combination sensitivity, then higher DEP-East to DEC power
transfers could be realized in the future. Id. at 32. Witness Roberts further noted that the
Public Staff’s analysis shows the maximum power transfer from DEP-East to
DEC occurring during the net demand peak evening hours between 8:00 pm and
10:00 pm, revealing that the Utility Combination sensitivity has chosen the majority of the
BESS to be located in the DEP-East area. Id. Witness Roberts explained that this
modeling nuance results from the inputs in this specific scenario and that, in execution,
the BESS resources will be dispersed across the system versus all being located in the
DEP-East area. Id.
In response to recommendations from witness Goggin, Charlotte, and Asheville
and Buncombe regarding GETs and ATTs, witness Roberts explained that, as addressed
in Appendix K of the 2025 CPIRP, there are four primary GETs in the industry. Id. at 34.
He noted that Appendix K of the 2025 CPIRP describes Duke’s engagement with a variety
of GETs to gain valuable insights on their sustainability and reliability. Id. Witness Roberts
indicated that Duke continues to prudently pursue the use of GETs where deemed reliable
and cost effective for interim and long-term solutions to identified transmission needs. Id.
He stated that Duke’s evaluation procedures are consistent with Commission Rule
R8-60A and the 2024 CPIRP Order. Id. at 35.
Responding to witness Goggin’s assertion that GETs can increase Duke’s ability
to interconnect new resources, witness Roberts explained that Duke has utilized GETs
as alternative solutions to identified transmission needs and will continue to evaluate and
utilize these potential alternative solutions where they are determined as sustainable and
reliable solutions as part of the generator interconnection studies. Id. He noted that DEC
and DEP have fully transitioned to the FERC Order 2023 Cluster Study process, which
requires evaluation of ATTs/GETs as potential alternatives to traditional transmission
upgrades within the cluster study framework. Id. at 35–36.
Witness Roberts disagreed with witness Goggin’s description of SERTP’s regional
transmission planning process as “siloed” and therefore unable to efficiently drive
proactive transmission development. Id. at 37. He explained that SERTP’s process
implements FERC Order’s 890 and 1000 in accordance with each transmission planner’s
open access transmission tariff approved by FERC. Id. Witness Roberts stated that
implementation of FERC’s recent Order 1920 will include some of the same attributes of
the current CTPC MVST Study process. Id. at 37–38. He asserted that the
implementation of Order 1920 at a regional level should lead to the identification of “no
regrets” regional transmission projects. Id. at 38. In response to witness Goggin’s
recommendation that Duke advocate for a cost allocation mechanism for transmission
170

projects in SERTP, witness Roberts explained that Duke is already participating in a
process to develop a cost allocation mechanism as required by FERC Order 1920. Id.
Witness Roberts rebutted witness Goggin’s recommendations regarding
interconnections with neighboring utilities, noting that there are currently no identified
projects for which the benefits of such interconnection have been shown to outweigh
costs. Id. at 39. He explained that if needs and associated projects are identified through
subsequent iterations of NERC’s Interregional Transfer Capability Study or through FERC
Order 1920 interregional transmission planning coordination, Duke will engage in
assessment of these projects to ensure benefits outweigh costs and there are no better
alternative solutions for Duke’s customers in North and South Carolina. Id.
Witness Roberts explained that evaluation of the incremental net benefit of
creating an energy imbalance market in the southeast as an alternative to SEEM as
suggested by witnesses Hagerty and Goggin is not appropriate at this time. Id. at 40. He
further explained that market functions and their benefits are very regional in nature and
depend on resource mix and inherent incremental cost spreads. Id. As a result, witness
Roberts noted that approaches that may provide benefit in one region will not necessarily
deliver benefits in other regions. Id. Furthermore, he stated that SEEM has a stakeholder
process and can evolve naturally based on needs of the members, as there are currently
23 members in SEEM operating in 12 different states. Id. Witness Roberts clarified that
to form an energy imbalance market, a centralized dispatch platform would be required
with significant upfront cost and complexity to implement over many years with alignment
from all members and states. Id. During the expert witness hearing, he shared that over
the last three and a half years, there had only been approximately 95 hours of solar
curtailment due to excess energy, which would be the only curtailment that would take
advantage of an energy imbalance market. Id. at 67.
Witness Shi testified in response to witness Roumpani’s and witness Burgess’s
recommendation for consideration of the use of surplus interconnection at coal sites that
Duke has chosen not to use battery for surplus interconnection at coal sites because
doing so would limit its ability to replace the full interconnection capacity of a coal unit
upon its retirement. Id. at 41–42. While surplus interconnection provides for short-term
benefits in certain situations, witness Shi explained that permanent coal retirement sites
are best preserved for long-term full replacement of capacity resources to maximize the
use of existing interconnection facilities and transmission infrastructure. Id. at 42.
Discussion and Conclusions
Based upon competent, material, and substantial evidence in the record, the
Commission concludes that Duke has complied with the directives in the 2024 CPIRP
Order with regard to transmission planning and interconnection optionality and reliability
considerations. The Commission acknowledges Duke’s work to provide a provisional
interconnection service option to interconnecting resources and accords significant
weight to both Duke witness Shi and CCEBA witness Hagerty’s testimony regarding the
success of the provisional interconnection service so far.
171

Specifically, based on the evidence contained within the 2025 CPIRP and the
testimony presented, the Commission concludes that the additional steps Duke has taken
to enhance its transmission planning processes, including through the MVST, are
appropriate and that the MVST planning process has provided for a more comprehensive
study process. The Commission acknowledges Duke’s efforts with respect to the MVST
process and gives significant weight to the Public Staff’s description of the MVST as
providing “for a more robust, holistic study process.” Tr. vol. 12, 216. Furthermore, the
Commission concludes that the frequent status updates and progress reports filed with
the Commission have provided valuable information to the Commission and parties and
concludes it is reasonable to direct Duke to continue filing these updates.
The Commission notes the lack of transparency identified by intervenors with
regard to SEEM, its transactions, and its benefits. The Commission further acknowledges
that with increased need for capacity on Duke’s system, SEEM may be a potential
resource allowing the purchase of additional power from neighboring utilities. Accordingly,
the Commission finds it appropriate to direct Duke to file semi-annual reports detailing the
size of Duke’s SEEM transactions, when these transactions occur, and an estimation of
benefits accrued over the reporting period. These reports should be filed in Docket E-100,
Sub 205 in conjunction with the semi-annual CTPC update reports.
Finally, the Commission concludes that Duke’s continued evolution of
interconnection policies to remain in compliance with federal interconnection regulations
while adopting new timeline improvement options has been reasonable and appropriate.
Overall, the Commission concludes that the transmission and interconnection planning
that Duke conducted to be reasonable for planning purposes.
EVIDENCE AND CONCLUSIONS FOR FINDINGS OF FACT NOS. 68–71
Customer Affordability
The evidence supporting these findings of fact is contained in Duke’s 2025 CPIRP;
the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Carolinas Utility Operations Panel (Bateman and
Goettsch); the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Bill Impacts and Large Load Benefits
Panel (Bateman and Beveridge); the testimony and exhibits of Duke’s Load Forecast and
Economic Development Panel (Passty, Stillman, and Tate); the testimony and exhibits of
Public Staff’s Load Panel (Patrick and Thomas); the testimony and exhibits of Public Staff
witness Brown; the testimony and exhibits of AGO witness Burgess; the testimony and
exhibits of CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry; the testimony and exhibits of SACE, et al. witness
Roumpani; and the entire record.

of additional sensitivities, as shown in Tables C-47 and C-51 in Appendix C (Quantitative
Analysis). Tr. vol. 12, 170–71. Witness Bateman described how the rate impacts in the
plan are calculated, and provided the average annual rate percentages through 2035 and
2040 for the Preliminary Base and Recommended Portfolios as shown in the table below.
