Id. She noted that the model also provides the impact of the portfolios as adjusted for
inflation, or in “real” dollars, using an annual inflation rate of 2.5%. Id. at 171.

Public Staff and Intervenor Testimony
CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry expressed concern over Duke’s bill impact estimates.
Tr. vol. 20, 18, 25. Witness Fitzhenry recommended that Duke be required to provide allin bill impacts for residential, industrial, and commercial customers in future CPIRP
proceedings. Id. at 18, 29. Witness Fitzhenry argued that this comprehensive reporting
must incorporate all components of a customer’s bill, including transmission and
distribution costs, non-production operations and maintenance expenses, and additional
regulatory charges such as storm securitization and renewable energy adjustments. Id. at
29, 49.
In addition, witness Fitzhenry argued that Duke should be required to provide
specific bill impact estimates for the commercial and industrial classes and file annual
reports benchmarking Duke’s industrial rates against other utilities. Id. at 18, 29. He then
recommended that the Commission implement an enforceable rate mitigation mechanism
that would be triggered if Duke’s industrial rates fell out of the top quartile for two
consecutive years. Id. at 18, 30, 50.
Public Staff witness Brown stated that Duke assumed inclusion of construction work
in progress (CWIP) in rate base in its bill impact calculations and argued that it is premature
to allow CWIP to be included in rate base for the activities Duke has proposed. Tr. vol. 12,
223. Witness Brown also asserted that inclusion of CWIP in rate base should only be
considered on a case-by-case basis during a general rate case proceeding. Id. at 224.
Finally, several intervenors noted concerns about bill impacts in the event the
forecasted economic development load fails to materialize. CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry
noted that “if the forecasted [load] growth is reduced by even small amounts, the rate
impacts for remaining customers would substantially increase.” Tr. vol. 20, 25. Similarly,
AGO witness Burgess expressed concern that “if these uncontracted loads fail to
materialize, bills for retail customers could increase by 38% rather than the 21% increase
Duke is already projecting by 2035.” Tr. vol. 22, 79. SACE et al. witness Roumpani
likewise noted that “Duke’s advancing load scenario produces higher rate increases than
its moderate load scenario in the near-term. If Duke builds out the infrastructure its
recommended portfolio requires and the projected load does not materialize, the outcome
for existing ratepayers would be worse still.” Tr. vol. 20, 444.
173

Duke Rebuttal Testimony
Impact of Large Loads on Customer Affordability
In their rebuttal testimony, Duke witnesses Bateman and Beveridge testified that
DEC and DEP can meet their obligations to serve large load customers, using current
regulatory constructs and principles, in a manner that lowers costs for all customers.
Tr. vol. 21, 48. Witnesses Bateman and Beveridge claimed that large load customers in
North Carolina will not only pay for the costs required to serve them but will also contribute
additional revenue to lower costs for all other customers. Id. at 49.
Witnesses Bateman and Beveridge explained that Duke prepared two analyses
that demonstrate how large load customers drive down costs for all customers. Id. at 49.
The first analysis shows that large load customers in North Carolina will contribute
additional revenue totaling $3.6 billion to lower costs for all ratepayers. Id .at 49, 52. This
analysis looks at 16 currently executed ESAs and: (1) assumes that those projects will
achieve 95% of the contract demand; (2) includes only each customer’s contribution to
fixed (non-variable, non-fuel) costs based on representative demand rates and load ramp
rates; (3) includes demand charges based on Schedule HLF; (4) assumes that the
incremental resources necessary to integrate these loads are additional CCs and CTs
based on resource plans from the Advancing Development Forecast; and (5) utilizes
financial estimates aligning with the CPIRP where possible, inclusive of DISIS estimates,
AFUDC, CWIP, and an assumption of no regulatory lag. Id .at 50–52. Witnesses Bateman
and Beveridge conclude that this analysis demonstrates that the net benefits from large
load customers will lower costs for all other customers and that criticism that large load
customers will drive up costs for other customers is unfounded. Id .at 49, 54.
The second analysis utilizes Duke’s standard approach to determining bill impacts
and concludes that the average residential customer’s bill impact is lower under the High
Load Portfolio than it is under the Preliminary Base or Recommended Portfolios. Id. at
49–55. Witness Bateman testified that the High Load Portfolio bill impact analysis shows
on average a reduction to residential customer monthly bills of $4 to $6 through 2040
when compared to the Preliminary Base and Recommended Portfolios and provided a
comparison of the CAGR for all three portfolios in the table below. Id. at 55–56.

During the evidentiary hearing, witnesses Bateman and Beveridge provided
testimony supporting the two analyses. Witness Beveridge testified that the large load
impact analysis compares the incremental capital costs of serving large load customers
(including generation for base load and reserve margin, transmission upgrades, and
interconnection costs) against the revenues those customers generate. Id. at 141–42. He
174

reiterated that his analysis showed a net benefit of approximately $3.6 billion over
15 years, which would flow to other customers by offsetting shared system costs. Id. at
108. Witness Bateman’s bill impacts analysis uses the same methodology applied across
all 2025 CPIRP portfolio scenarios, allocating total system costs (fixed and variable,
including fuel) across all customers. This analysis concluded the large load sensitivity
produced lower rates for customers compared to scenarios without the large load
additions. Id. at 143–44.
During cross-examination, witnesses Beveridge and Bateman agreed that for
analysis to be as accurate as possible, it should rely on the most accurate or realistic
inputs available. Id. at 120–21. Witness Beveridge conceded that 15 of the 16 projects
included in the large load benefits analysis did not meet the 100 MW threshold
requirement to qualify for an ESA that would have the large load protections contained
within its pro forma large load customer ESA, including minimum contract demand, term
lengths, enforcements, or restrictions. These 15 projects represented about 13 percent
of the total load within his analysis. Id. at 101–05. Witnesses Beveridge and Bateman
acknowledged that Duke’s large load benefits analysis only considered the costs of
incremental generation from CCs and CTs added to the system when large load
customers take service, and not the costs of the existing system as well. Id. at 108–10.
They conceded that their large load benefits analysis did not account for the costs of other
incremental generation that Duke is planning to build, including new nuclear which is more
costly on a per kW basis. Id. at 111–13. Witnesses Bateman and Beveridge admitted that
their analysis makes revenue calculations based on Schedule HLF when the majority of
the ESAs they included in their analysis will initially take service on other rate schedules
including OPT-V. Id. at 125–26. When asked whether it was reasonable for Duke to
assume that every ESA customer it included in its analysis would achieve a 95% load
factor for the entirety of the time period in the analysis – 15 years – witnesses Bateman
and Beveridge argued that this reflected Duke’s experience with data centers and what
they expect data centers to do going forward. Id. at 127. They acknowledged that their
large load benefits analysis did not attempt to account for regulatory lag or the timing
mismatch between when costs are incurred and put into rates, such as with CWIP, and
when revenues from these large load customers are expected. Id. at 131–33. Witness
Bateman testified that the timing of customer benefits and when costs are incurred
compared to when revenues are received are better considered in a general rate case.
Id. at 134.
Large Load ESA Financial Protections
Witness Tate explained that Duke’s large load pro forma LA and ESA each require
large load customers to make performance and financial commitments intended to protect
Duke’s existing customers. Tr. vol. 20, 542. The LA requires payment of refundable
capital to cover the cost of interconnection facilities and non-network upgrades. While this
amount is refundable based on actual customer performance, it is not refundable based
on termination. Similarly, both the LA and ESA contain a 75% minimum billing demand
requirement. Witness Tate explained that this means large load customers must pay for
75% of their service and contracted demand over the entire 15-year term of the contract
whether they use it or not. Id. at 543–44. The ESA also contains a liquidated damages
175

provision to protect the system and all ratepayers if a large load customer departs before
its commitment is fully satisfied. Id. at 544. Finally, the ESA requires large load customers
to provide credit security for the entire term of the ESA to secure its payment and
performance obligation. The credit security acts as a source of funds for Duke in the event
of customer termination or default on the contract. Witness Tate explained that, together,
these contractual commitments ensure that large load customers seeking service from
Duke are progressing through escalating and significant commitments to protect existing
customers from the risk that the load may not materialize. Id.
Response to Public Staff and Intervenor Testimony
Witness Bateman explained that the purpose of the bill impacts provided in her
direct testimony is to evaluate the effect of resource planning activities captured within
the scope of the CPIRP, and they are not intended to represent an all-inclusive bill impact
analysis. Tr. vol. 21, 57. In response to CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry’s recommendation that
Duke should be required to provide all-in bill impacts for residential, industrial, and
commercial customers in future CPIRP proceedings, witness Bateman noted that the
Commission rejected this same recommendation in the 2022 Carbon Plan Order and
argued that it should be rejected again in this proceeding for the same reasons. Id.
Witness Bateman added that the projected rate impacts were never intended to try to
predict exactly what a customer’s all-in rate will be, but instead to be a tool to compare
alternative resource plans. Id. at 58. Witness Bateman stated that all-in bill impacts are
neither feasible nor necessary in the CPIRP context. Id. at 59.
In response to CIGFUR witness Fitzhenry’s recommendation that Duke provide bill
impacts for industrial and commercial customers, witness Bateman asserted that Duke
has already provided bill impacts for industrial and commercial customers in discovery.
Id. at 59 Additionally, Duke witnesses Bateman and Beveridge disagreed with witness
Fitzhenry’s suggestion that DEP’s industrial rates are higher than the national average
and that Duke should be required to file annual reports comparing those rates against
other utilities. Id. at 21, 59–60. Witnesses Bateman and Beveridge argued there is no
legal basis to require that Duke’s industrial rates remain in the top 25% and therefore no
basis for requiring Duke to file such an annual report. Id. at 60. Additionally, witnesses
Bateman and Beveridge testified that there is nothing in the Public Utility Act that would
allow the implementation of a “rate mitigation trigger” that would somehow adjust rates
outside of a rate case nor anything that would allow the Commission to arbitrarily cap
Duke’s industrial rates based on an apples-to-oranges comparison with industrial rates in
other states. Id. at 61.
Witnesses Bateman and Beveridge described the concept of CWIP in rate base
and explained that Duke only assumed CWIP in rate base treatment for baseload
investments or long-lead time investments. Id. at 62–64. In response to Public Staff
witness Brown’s assertion that inclusion of CWIP in rate base should be considered on a
case-by-case basis during a general rate proceeding, witnesses Bateman and Beveridge
argued it is appropriate for the Commission to approve the general treatment of CWIP in
rate base at the time it approves the construction of the facilities given the necessity of
176

CWIP in rate base treatment to Duke’s ability to construct the referenced long lead time
facilities. Id. at 64–65.
During the evidentiary hearing, Duke witnesses Bateman and Beveridge noted that
commercial and industrial customers are much more diverse than residential customers
and explained that there is no “typical bill” for such customers. Id. at 95–96. They further
testified that for an industrial or commercial customer to understand the impacts on their
bill depends on the individual customer’s usage and load profile. Id. at 96. A commercial
or industrial customer could look at the CAGR and apply that to the bill using their own
spreadsheet to estimate the bill impact. Id. Further, witness Bateman noted that no other
utility provides all-in bill impact projections over a 15-year period. Id. at 98–99.
Low-Cost Financing for New Generation
Witness Bowman testified that Duke initiated the U.S. Department of Energy
(DOE) Energy Dominance Financing loan application process in May 2026 by submitting
an initial, enterprise level pre-application. According to witness Bowman, this
pre-application is an early screening step intended for the Office of Energy Dominance
Financing to begin assessing Duke’s general eligibility and readiness to proceed to the
next phase of the loan application process. Upon final approval and meeting all required
terms and conditions, Duke would receive a guaranteed loan to provide valuable, low-cost
financing for the development of new generation that should lower the interest costs for
customers over the length of the loan. Tr. vol. 22, 438.
Discussion and Conclusions
Based upon competent, material, and substantial evidence in the entire record, the
Commission concludes that the bill impact calculations provided by Duke in this
proceeding are reasonable for planning purposes and provide a helpful tool to compare
the relative benefits of the different portfolios. The Commission notes that the focus of
CPIRP proceedings is on evaluating various portfolios in order to determine most
reasonable least-cost path towards achieving the State’s long term carbon neutrality
planning goal by 2050, while maintaining or improving reliability. With respect to
CIGFUR’s desire for Duke to provide all-in bill impacts that present the total cost of
electricity that customers will pay as Duke implements the CPIRP, the Commission gives
significant weight to the testimony of Duke witness Bateman that there are substantial
uncertainties associated with projecting all-in costs for an extended future period. As
such, projected all-in bill impacts are unlikely to be accurate or provide meaningful
information that customers can rely upon. Further, neither Duke nor any other party was
aware of any other utilities providing such all-in forecasts. As the Commission decided in
the Initial Carbon Plan Order and affirmed in the 2024 CPIRP Order, the bill impact
analyses provided by Duke are sufficient for evaluating and comparing the relative
benefits of the various portfolios Duke presents in its 2025 CPIRP proposal.
The Commission further declines to require Duke to file annual reports comparing
Duke’s industrial rates to those of other investor-owned utilities nationwide. The
Commission concludes that there is no legal basis to require that Duke’s industrial rates
177

remain in the top quartile and therefore no basis for requiring Duke to file such an annual
report. The Commission also declines to implement an enforceable rate mitigation trigger
if Duke fails to be in the top quartile with respect to industrial rates. The Commission notes
that it is required to set base rates in accordance with the statutory framework and
process laid out in N.C.G.S. §§ 62-133 and 62-133.16 rather than setting rates based on
a comparison to rates of other investor-owned utilities nationwide. Moreover, there is
nothing in the Public Utility Act that would allow the implementation of a rate mitigation
trigger that would adjust rates outside of a rate case, nor anything that would allow the
Commission to cap Duke’s industrial rates based on the industrial rates in other states.
The Commission accepts Duke’s large load analysis and acknowledges that it is
subject to a variety of inputs, which could potentially change in future CPIRP cycles.
However, the Commission also recognizes the underlying thrust of Duke’s analysis that
large load customers could provide a benefit to existing ratepayers by driving up average
utilization and thus spreading the fixed costs of the system over a greater volume of
electricity sales. The Commission takes note of the financial protections in Duke’s pro
forma LA and ESA. Witness Tate testified that the LA’s refundable capital requirement,
the 75% minimum billing demand requirement under both the LA and ESA, and the ESA’s
liquidated damages and credit security provisions together impose escalating
commitments intended to protect existing customers from the risk that anticipated large
load fails to materialize or departs prematurely. The Commission remains cautious about
the risk to existing customers given the scale of the expected growth. It is critical that the
Commission maintain visibility into Duke’s large load contracting process. Accordingly,
the Commission finds it appropriate to direct Duke to file its pro forma LA and ESA in
Docket No. E-100, Sub 208A within 30 days of this 2026 CPIRP Order and to file any
future updates to the pro forma LA and ESA within 30 days of completion.
Finally, the Commission recognizes that the DOE Office of Energy Dominance
Financing offers valuable, low-cost financing that could lower the cost of building new
generation that is needed to retire aging resources and serve growing load. Accordingly,
the Commission concludes it is appropriate to direct Duke to continue pursuing financing
through the Energy Dominance Financing Program. The Commission further concludes
it is reasonable to direct Duke to report on its efforts to obtain a loan through the Energy
Dominance Financing Program in the 2027 CPIRP proceeding.
